## **B_0d8b548c. Evaluate Genetic Conditions and Evolutionary Implications**

**Core move:** Analyze descriptive genetic information, including recessive/dominant traits and heterozygote advantage, to evaluate statements about allele frequency, disease prevalence, and selective pressures in a population.

**Seen in / context:**
- Genetic diseases with a clear mode of inheritance (recessive, dominant).
Scenarios involving heterozygote advantage (balancing selection) such as sickle cell anemia and malaria, or cystic fibrosis and cholera.
Questions that require understanding how genetic traits persist or change in a population over generations.
Interpreting the effects of natural selection on allele frequencies.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing individual Punnett square probabilities with overall population allele frequencies.
Underestimating or ignoring the impact of heterozygote advantage on maintaining a detrimental allele in a population.
Assuming that a recessive allele will always be rare or eliminated from a population.
Failing to connect the genetic information (e.g., carrier status) with broader evolutionary pressures (e.g., resistance to disease).
Misinterpreting the meaning of 'recessive' or 'carrier' in the context of disease expression and survival.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions typically present a detailed biological scenario about a genetic condition.
The task is to evaluate conceptual statements based on the provided genetic and evolutionary context, rather than performing direct numerical calculations.
Emphasize the interplay between genetics (genotypes/phenotypes) and population dynamics/evolutionary forces.
Ensure that the provided information clearly supports or refutes the statements to be evaluated.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q56_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_0d90450b. Calculate Enthalpy Change from Bond Energies**

**Core move:** Apply Hess's Law using mean bond energies to determine the enthalpy change of a reaction or an unknown bond energy.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating standard enthalpy of reaction (ΔHrxn) from given mean bond energies.
Determining an unknown bond energy when ΔHrxn and other bond energies are known.
Understanding the energy changes involved in breaking and forming chemical bonds during a reaction.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying bonds broken vs. bonds formed.
Using the wrong sign convention for bond breaking (endothermic) and bond forming (exothermic) if not using the standard formula Σ(bonds broken) - Σ(bonds formed).
Failing to account for the number of each type of bond (e.g., multiple C-H bonds).
Mathematical errors in calculation.
Confusing bond energies with other enthalpy terms like enthalpy of formation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Always draw out the full structural formulas of reactants and products to accurately identify and count all bonds.
The formula ΔH = Σ(Bond energies of bonds broken) - Σ(Bond energies of bonds formed) is crucial, where all bond energies are positive values representing the energy required to break a bond.
Pay attention to the state symbols, as bond energies typically refer to gaseous species.
This is a core concept in chemical thermodynamics, not biology.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q54_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q53_NSAA_Sec`: The question explicitly asks for the 'overall enthalpy change for the reaction' and provides 'bond enthalpies', which requires applying Hess's Law using mean bond energies to calculate the enthalpy change of the reaction, directly matching the core move of schema B_0d90450b.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q50_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires using the provided enthalpy change of a reaction and several known bond energies to calculate an unknown mean bond energy (C-H). This directly applies Hess's Law using mean bond energies, which is the core move of schema B_0d90450b.

## **B_118f9bea. Unit Conversion and Proportional Calculation**

**Core move:** Apply proportional relationships and unit conversion factors to determine a physical quantity in a specified unit.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating dimensions or distances across different scales (e.g., atomic to macroscopic).
Converting between various unit prefixes (e.g., nano, micro, milli, kilo, femto).
Determining quantities based on relative proportions (e.g., scaling up/down values).
Problems involving orders of magnitude.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the scaling factor (e.g., multiplying instead of dividing for 'times smaller').
Errors in unit conversion, especially with powers of ten or the direction of conversion.
Forgetting to perform all necessary unit conversions.
Confusing similar scientific prefixes (e.g., pico vs. femto).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly states the scaling factor and direction (e.g., 'times larger' or 'times smaller').
Use common SI unit prefixes, providing non-standard conversion factors if necessary.
Can involve multiple sequential unit conversions.
Vary the physical context (e.g., astrophysics, cellular biology, chemical bond lengths) while maintaining the core mathematical skill.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q54_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q51_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires several steps of proportional calculation and unit conversion: first, scaling up the amount of chlorine per dm³ to the total daily volume of water; then, converting the mass of chlorine from mg to g, g to moles using molar mass, and finally moles to volume using the molar volume at room temperature and pressure. This aligns perfectly with applying proportional relationships and unit conversion factors to determine a physical quantity in a specified unit.

## **C_13978330. Calculate Total Energy Change from Stoichiometry**

**Core move:** Apply the given molar energy change and reaction stoichiometry to calculate the total heat released or absorbed for a specific amount of reactant consumed or product formed.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculations involving enthalpy of reaction (ΔH)
Determining energy changes in chemical processes based on extent of reaction
Relating moles of substances in a balanced equation to energy changes

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Using initial moles of reactants instead of the moles that actually reacted (or were produced)
Incorrectly applying the stoichiometric ratio when converting between moles of substances
Confusing 'heat released' with the sign of ΔH (e.g., if ΔH is negative, heat is released; the question asks for the positive magnitude of heat released)

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the balanced chemical equation is correctly used for stoichiometric ratios.
Pay attention to whether the energy change is given per mole of a specific reactant/product or per mole of reaction as written.
The final answer should be in appropriate energy units (e.g., kJ).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q54_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **B_1625e63a. Evaluate Cellular Processes and Mechanisms**

**Core move:** Assess statements for accuracy regarding the steps, conditions, or outcomes of fundamental cellular processes.

**Seen in / context:**
- Cell division (mitosis, meiosis, binary fission)
Respiration (aerobic, anaerobic)
Photosynthesis
Protein synthesis
Active transport, diffusion, osmosis

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing similar processes (e.g., mitosis vs. meiosis, binary fission vs. mitosis)
Misinterpreting the role of organelles or cellular components
Incorrectly identifying inputs, outputs, or conditions for a process
Applying characteristics of one organism type to another (e.g., eukaryotic division to prokaryotic)

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should focus on core biological mechanisms.
Statements can include comparisons, functions, locations, or requirements.
Options should test common misconceptions or distinctions between related processes.
Ensure the process is clearly defined in the question or options.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q64_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_31431176. Compare Relative Reactivity via Electrochemical Cells**

**Core move:** Relate the measured potential difference (voltage) in an electrochemical cell to the relative reactivity (tendency to form positive ions) of different metals.

**Seen in / context:**
- Reactivity series of metals
Galvanic/voltaic cells
Standard electrode potentials
Redox reactions

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the anode and cathode based on reactivity
Incorrectly associating the sign of the electrode with its tendency to oxidize/reduce
Misinterpreting voltage as directly proportional to mass change or current, rather than a measure of potential difference
Assuming the direction of electron flow without considering relative reactivity

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions might involve predicting electron flow, identifying anode/cathode, or ranking metals based on observed voltages.
Emphasize the concept that a greater tendency to form positive ions means a metal is more easily oxidized and will act as the negative electrode (anode) in a galvanic cell with a less reactive metal.
The diagram shows a voltmeter, implying a measurement of potential difference which is a direct measure of the relative tendency.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q58_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_35930931. Relate Atomic Mass to Isotopic Composition**

**Core move:** Integrate data from formula mass, ionic charge, and subatomic particle counts to determine an element's average atomic mass or isotopic abundances.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating the relative atomic mass (Ar) of an unknown element from the Mr of its compound and known elements.
Deducting the atomic number (proton count) of an element from the electron count of its ion.
Determining mass numbers of isotopes given proton and neutron counts.
Calculating the average atomic mass from isotopic mass numbers and relative abundances.
Solving for unknown isotopic abundances given the average atomic mass and isotopic mass numbers.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly determining the charge of an ion from a compound's formula.
Confusing the number of electrons in an ion with the number of electrons in a neutral atom.
Neglecting to add protons to neutrons when calculating an isotope's mass number.
Mathematical errors in weighted average calculations for average atomic mass.
Overlooking the contribution of other known elements to the compound's Mr.

**Notes for generation:**
- This pattern often involves sequential application of several chemical principles.
Can be extended to incorporate mass spectrometry data.
Clear distinction between atomic number (Z) and mass number (A) is crucial.
Provide necessary relative atomic masses for common elements if not standard knowledge.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q56_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q53_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the atomic number of element X from the total electron count in a molecule (XF3), then using this information, along with given isotopic relationships (neutrons vs protons) and relative abundances, to determine the average relative atomic mass of element X. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of integrating data from subatomic particle counts and isotopic composition to determine an element's average atomic mass.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q52_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the relative abundances of two isotopes given their mass numbers and the element's relative atomic mass, which directly matches the core move of determining an element's isotopic abundances from average atomic mass and subatomic particle counts (mass numbers).

## **C_4a905fc5. Quantitative Analysis via Titration Stoichiometry**

**Core move:** Apply stoichiometric principles derived from balanced chemical equations to calculate unknown solution concentrations from titration data, including necessary unit conversions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Acid-base titrations to determine unknown concentrations of acids or bases.
Redox titrations to determine concentrations of oxidizing or reducing agents.
Precipitation titrations and complexometric titrations.
Calculations involving equivalence points in neutralization reactions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to write or correctly balance the chemical equation, leading to an incorrect mole ratio between reactants.
Not converting volumes to consistent units (e.g., cm³ to dm³) before calculating moles.
Incorrectly converting between molar concentration (mol dm⁻³) and mass concentration (g dm⁻³) using molar mass, or vice-versa.
Mixing up the volumes and concentrations of the known and unknown reactants.
Ignoring the stoichiometric coefficients from the balanced equation when calculating moles of the unknown substance.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the reaction type (e.g., acid-base, redox, precipitation) is clearly defined or inferable.
Provide all necessary molar masses (Mr) if conversion to mass concentration is required.
Can vary the unknown quantity to be calculated (e.g., volume, concentration in mol dm⁻³, or g dm⁻³).
Can involve mono-, di-, or triprotic acids/bases or different redox reactions to test understanding of complex stoichiometry.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q52_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q52_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires using titration data (neutralization of excess HCl with NaOH) to determine the amount of unreacted HCl. This information is then used, along with stoichiometry from balanced chemical equations, to calculate the amount and subsequently the mass of the initial reactant (CaCO3). This process is a classic application of quantitative analysis involving titration stoichiometry, even if the final output is a mass rather than a concentration.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q52_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires using titration data (volume and concentration of NaOH, volume of the unknown HCl solution) to determine the concentration of HCl, which then allows for the calculation of the initial amount of SiCl4 based on reaction stoichiometry, ultimately leading to percentage purity. This directly matches the core move of applying stoichiometric principles from balanced equations to calculate unknown concentrations from titration data.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q50_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating moles of acid and base, applying stoichiometric principles from a balanced neutralization equation to determine the excess reactant, calculating its final concentration in the mixture, and then deriving the pH. This process directly aligns with the core move of applying stoichiometric principles to calculate solution concentrations, which is fundamental to quantitative analysis like titration.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q56_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying stoichiometric principles to titration data to determine the initial amount of a reactant. Specifically, it involves calculating the excess reactant via titration and then using that to find the amount of the limiting reactant, which perfectly aligns with quantitative analysis using titration stoichiometry, even if the final quantity is a mass rather than a concentration.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q54_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires using titration data (volumes and concentrations of acid and base) to apply stoichiometric principles to determine a molar ratio (moles of acid to moles of base), which allows for deduction about the acid's proticity. This directly aligns with the 'Core Move' of applying stoichiometric principles derived from balanced chemical equations from titration data.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q57_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires applying stoichiometric principles to calculate the unknown volume of a reactant solution needed for complete reaction, given the amount of another reactant and the solution's concentration. This is a direct application of the quantitative analysis and stoichiometric calculations described in the core move, including necessary unit conversions, even if it's not presented as a classical 'titration to find unknown concentration'.

## **P_4febad60. Calculate Temperature Change from Thermal Energy**

**Core move:** Apply the specific heat capacity formula (Q = mcΔT) to determine the temperature change or final temperature of a substance given its mass, specific heat capacity, and absorbed or released thermal energy.

**Seen in / context:**
- Heating or cooling of objects
Calorimetry experiments
Energy transfer in systems without phase change

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly rearranging the formula (e.g., Q = mc/ΔT)
Forgetting to add/subtract the initial temperature to find the final temperature
Confusing specific heat capacity with specific latent heat
Errors in unit conversion (e.g., kg to g)

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary variables (Q, m, c, T_initial) are either provided or can be derived.
Questions can involve solving for any variable in the Q = mcΔT equation.
The specific heat capacity can be given in J kg⁻¹ °C⁻¹ or J g⁻¹ °C⁻¹; pay attention to mass units.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q54_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_53939452. Stoichiometry of Gas-Producing Reactions**

**Core move:** Apply stoichiometric relationships derived from a balanced chemical equation to calculate the volume of a gaseous product from a given mass of solid reactant.

**Seen in / context:**
- Reactions where a gas is produced or consumed and its volume needs to be determined.
Calculations at specified temperature and pressure conditions (e.g., STP, RTP) where molar gas volume is given.
Quantitative analysis of chemical reactions in laboratory settings.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to write or correctly balance the chemical equation.
Incorrectly calculating the number of moles of the reactant (e.g., using molecular mass instead of atomic mass, or vice versa).
Using an incorrect mole ratio from the balanced equation to relate reactant to product gas.
Applying the wrong molar gas volume or making errors in unit conversions (e.g., dm³ to cm³).
Confusing moles of reactant with moles of gas produced directly without considering stoichiometry.

**Notes for generation:**
- Always provide relevant atomic/molecular masses and the molar gas volume at the specified conditions.
Vary the type of reaction (e.g., metal + acid, decomposition, metal + water).
Include excess reactants to simplify calculations to a single limiting reactant.
Ensure the question clearly states the units required for the final answer, often requiring conversion (e.g., dm³ to cm³).
The options should include common miscalculation results to test for understanding.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q51_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q50_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires applying stoichiometric relationships derived from the balanced chemical equation (Mg + 2HCl → MgCl2 + H2) to convert between the volume of gaseous product (H2) and the mass of solid reactant (Mg). Although it asks for a 'rate' (mass/time), the core mathematical thinking involves the conversion factors between moles, mass, and volume, which is central to the 'Stoichiometry of Gas-Producing Reactions' schema. The time component is a simple division after the stoichiometric conversion is performed.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q57_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires applying stoichiometric relationships derived from a balanced chemical equation to determine the relative amounts (masses) of hydrogen gas produced from equal masses of different reactants. Although the schema mentions 'volume' of gas, the core thinking pattern of using stoichiometry to quantify a gaseous product from a given mass of reactant is identical, leading to a comparison of mass loss.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q53_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires determining the stoichiometric relationship between a solid reactant (copper) and a gaseous product (gaseous oxide of nitrogen) from a balanced chemical equation, and then using this relationship along with molar volume to calculate the volume of the gas. This directly matches the core move of applying stoichiometric relationships from a balanced equation to calculate the volume of a gaseous product from a given amount of solid reactant.

## **C_5b7d0491. Deduce Unknown Product Formula from Reaction Stoichiometry**

**Core move:** Apply stoichiometric principles, including electron transfer and mass balance, to deduce the empirical formula of an unknown product from given reactant quantities and known products.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining the formula of an oxide, halide, or other simple compound formed in a redox reaction.
Problems where a reactant undergoes partial reduction/oxidation to form a new product, while other parts remain unchanged (e.g., nitric acid acting as both oxidant and acid source).
Quantitative analysis to determine the composition of an unknown compound formed in a reaction.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the moles of reactants.
Failing to account for the multiple roles of a reactant (e.g., nitric acid as both oxidizing agent and source of nitrate ions).
Errors in assigning oxidation states or calculating the total electron transfer.
Not correctly allocating atoms (e.g., nitrogen, oxygen) to known and unknown products.
Confusing empirical formula with molecular formula, or making calculation errors in finding the simplest mole ratio.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure sufficient stoichiometric information is provided for reactants.
May require balancing redox half-equations or understanding changes in oxidation states.
The nature of the unknown product (e.g., 'oxide of nitrogen') should be specified to guide the deduction.
Questions can vary by providing total mass or volume, requiring prior calculation of moles.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q58_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_62cda313. Calculate Moles and Identify Components in Gas Mixtures**

**Core move:** Apply gas laws and molar relationships (Avogadro's number, molar volume) to determine the amount in moles of individual components within a gas mixture and identify them based on their relative proportions or known compositions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing the composition of air or other gas mixtures.
Calculations involving partial pressures and mole fractions.
Determining the amount of a substance given number of particles or volume at specified conditions (e.g., RTP/STP).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing number of particles with moles directly without using Avogadro's number.
Using incorrect values for Avogadro's number or molar volume.
Misremembering or incorrectly applying the typical composition of common gas mixtures (e.g., dry air).
Failing to convert all given quantities (particles, volume) into moles before performing calculations on the mixture.
Performing arithmetic errors when summing or subtracting mole amounts.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary constants (Avogadro's number, molar volume at RTP/STP) are explicitly provided in the question.
The problem should require multiple steps of conversion (e.g., particles to moles, volume to moles) and subsequent arithmetic.
The identification of components should rely on general scientific knowledge (e.g., composition of air) or the calculated relative proportions within the mixture.
Emphasize unit consistency throughout the calculations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q52_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_637723b8. Separating Liquid Mixtures by Physical Properties**

**Core move:** Select and justify appropriate separation techniques for liquid mixtures based on the distinct physical properties of their components.

**Seen in / context:**
- Separation of miscible liquids (e.g., distillation based on boiling points).
Separation of immiscible liquids (e.g., using a separating funnel based on density).
Separation of soluble solids from liquids (e.g., evaporation, crystallization).
Separation of insoluble solids from liquids (e.g., filtration, decantation).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing physical separation techniques with chemical reactions.
Incorrectly applying a separation method suitable for one type of mixture to another (e.g., using a separating funnel for miscible liquids).
Failing to identify the relevant physical property (e.g., using density instead of boiling point for miscible liquids).
Not considering the practical aspects or limitations of the chosen method.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide clear information about the components of the mixture and their relevant physical properties (e.g., boiling points, densities, solubilities).
May require describing the apparatus or process of the chosen separation technique.
Can involve evaluating and comparing the effectiveness or efficiency of different separation methods.
Ensure the problem explicitly states or implies that the components do not react chemically.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q50_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_7a9e4059. Comparing Strong and Weak Acids**

**Core move:** Distinguish between strong and weak acids by analyzing their ionization behavior and resulting chemical properties at equivalent concentrations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Predicting pH values for equimolar strong and weak acid solutions.
Explaining differences in electrical conductivity between strong and weak acid solutions.
Comparing reaction rates of strong and weak acids with reactive metals or carbonates.
Interpreting titration curves involving strong vs. weak acids.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing acid strength (degree of ionization) with acid concentration (moles per unit volume).
Assuming a weak acid will always have a higher pH than a strong acid, regardless of concentration.
Not understanding that strong acids fully ionize in water, while weak acids only partially ionize.
Incorrectly predicting that a weak acid will react slower because it's weaker, without linking it to the lower concentration of H⁺ ions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often provide equimolar solutions to isolate the effect of acid strength.
Focus on the *implications* of full vs. partial ionization on observable properties.
May involve identifying the nature of an unknown acid based on experimental data.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q49_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q54_NSAA_Sec`: The question presents ionization information for phosphoric(V) acid (a weak, polyprotic acid) and the pH of hydrochloric acid (a strong acid) at a given concentration. The core task, 'Which of the following statements about these acids is/are correct?', inherently requires distinguishing between strong and weak acids based on their ionization behavior and chemical properties, directly matching the schema's core move.

## **C_7c9a508f. Deduce Unknown Product from Partial Stoichiometry**

**Core move:** Apply the principle of conservation of mass to determine the elemental composition of an unknown product, given partial stoichiometric information for a balanced chemical equation.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chemical reactions with unknown products or reactants.
Stoichiometry problems where specific coefficients are given, constraining the balancing process.
Problems testing understanding of balancing chemical equations and conservation of atoms.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming a common product without verifying it fits the given stoichiometric constraints.
Attempting to balance the equation from scratch without incorporating the given coefficients, leading to a different, but still valid, balanced equation that doesn't match the specific problem constraints.
Mistakes in counting atoms during balancing.
Incorrectly writing chemical formulas for known compounds.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem explicitly provides some coefficients for the balanced equation.
The unknown compound's formula should be deducible uniquely from the elemental balance.
The options for the unknown compound should include distractors that are plausible products but do not fit the specific stoichiometric constraints.
The question might specify properties of the unknown (e.g., 'does not contain copper or hydrogen') to eliminate ambiguous options or guide the deduction.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q46_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

**Seen in / context:**
- Evaluating the economic viability or environmental impact of different industrial chemical processes.
Comparing the efficiency or yield of alternative synthesis pathways.
Analyzing redox properties and reaction conditions for parallel reactions.

Incorrect calculation of molar masses or conversion between mass and moles.
Misunderstanding the function of non-reacting components (e.g., catalysts, inert gases, solvents).
Incorrectly applying definitions or trends related to redox agents or reactivity series.

Requires both quantitative (stoichiometry) and qualitative (chemical principles) reasoning.
Often includes options that test common misconceptions about reaction conditions or properties.

**Seen in / context:**
- Separation of immiscible liquids using a separating funnel.
Separation of miscible liquids by distillation.
Interpreting density to determine liquid layering.
Interpreting boiling points for distillation.

## **B_8ee2a647. Evaluate Fundamental Biological Concepts**

**Core move:** Evaluate multiple biological statements for accuracy based on fundamental knowledge of cells, tissues, organs, and physiological processes.

**Seen in / context:**
- Multiple-choice questions assessing broad biological understanding across different levels of organization (cell, tissue, organ).
Questions requiring recall of definitions, functions, and processes related to common biological systems.
Assessments of basic cell biology, histology, and introductory human physiology.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing similar but distinct biological terms (e.g., adult vs. embryonic stem cells, meiosis vs. mitosis, endocrine vs. exocrine).
Misattributing functions to incorrect structures or processes (e.g., chemical vs. electrical impulses, enzyme vs. hormone secretion).
Overgeneralizing specific biological rules or exceptions (e.g., 'any adult stem cells').
Lacking precise knowledge of tissue types and their roles within organs.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure distractors are biologically plausible but fundamentally incorrect based on precise definitions or functions.
Questions should cover a variety of foundational topics to test comprehensive understanding.
The correct option often requires a precise and accurate understanding of a specific biological fact or process.
