## **C_9abb7e35. Limiting Reactant Stoichiometry Calculation**

**Core move:** Apply stoichiometric relationships derived from a balanced chemical equation to identify the limiting reactant and calculate the maximum theoretical yield of a product, given initial quantities of multiple reactants.

**Seen in / context:**
- Reactions involving two or more reactants with known initial quantities
Calculations involving reactants and products in different states (solid, liquid, gas)
Determining the maximum amount of product that can be formed from a reaction

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to balance the chemical equation correctly
Incorrectly converting between mass, volume, and moles for different reactants
Failing to identify the limiting reactant, or identifying it incorrectly
Calculating the product yield based on the excess reactant instead of the limiting reactant
Using incorrect molar masses or molar volumes

**Notes for generation:**
- Always start by writing and balancing the chemical equation.
Ensure all given quantities of reactants are converted to moles before comparing them stoichiometrically.
The limiting reactant determines the maximum amount of product that can be formed.
Questions can involve reactants in different states (solid, liquid, gas), requiring appropriate conversion factors (molar mass, molar volume at given conditions, density).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q53_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_a7eaca0a. Calculate Molar Enthalpy Change from Calorimetry Data**

**Core move:** Apply experimental calorimetry data (mass, specific heat capacity, temperature change) and reactant stoichiometry to determine the molar enthalpy change of a reaction.

**Seen in / context:**
- Experimental determination of enthalpy changes (e.g., neutralization, dissolution, combustion)
Calorimetry experiments in chemical reactions
Stoichiometric calculations combined with thermochemistry

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to identify the limiting reactant when calculating the molar enthalpy change
Incorrectly assigning the sign of the enthalpy change (positive for endothermic, negative for exothermic)
Using the mass of only one reactant instead of the total mass of the solution for Q = mcΔT
Errors in unit conversions (e.g., J to kJ, cm³ to dm³)
Confusing the specific heat capacity of the solution with that of pure water if different values are given

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the sign of the enthalpy change accurately reflects whether the reaction is exothermic or endothermic based on the observed temperature change.
Pay close attention to unit consistency throughout the calculation, especially for volume, mass, and energy.
The density and specific heat capacity of the solution are often assumed to be similar to that of water unless stated otherwise.
This pattern frequently involves a multi-step calculation combining calculations of moles, heat transfer (Q), and then molar enthalpy change (ΔH).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q57_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_a8db39d8. Stoichiometry of Electrolysis Products**

**Core move:** Apply stoichiometric principles derived from half-equations to relate quantities of products formed at different electrodes during electrolysis.

**Seen in / context:**
- Electrolysis of aqueous solutions (e.g., salts, acids, bases).
Electrolysis of molten ionic compounds.
Calculations involving molar volume of gases at specified conditions (e.g., RTP, STP).
Quantitative analysis of electrochemical reactions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the products formed at each electrode (e.g., confusing H₂ with Na, or O₂ with Cl₂ in aqueous solutions).
Using incorrect stoichiometric ratios between different products based on the balanced half-equations.
Confusing the cathode (negative electrode) with the anode (positive electrode) when assigning products.
Errors in converting between moles, mass, and volume (e.g., incorrect use of molar mass or molar gas volume).
Ignoring the concentration of electrolytes when determining preferential discharge.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure that all necessary molar masses and molar gas volumes (at specified conditions) are provided or are common knowledge.
Vary the initial given product (mass or volume) and the requested product to test comprehensive understanding.
Can be extended to include calculations involving charge (Faraday's laws) if current and time are given.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q47_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q53_NSAA_Sec`: The question presents half-equations for electrolysis and asks to make deductions from them, which requires applying stoichiometric principles to compare or relate the products and other species formed at the electrodes, directly aligning with the core move of schema B_a8db39d8.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the mass of a product (aluminium) formed during electrolysis, given the rate of electron flow. This directly involves applying stoichiometric principles from the reduction half-equation (Al³⁺ + 3e⁻ → Al) to relate the quantity of electrons to the quantity of aluminium produced, which is the core move of the 'Stoichiometry of Electrolysis Products' schema.

## **C_b2055da6. Factors Influencing Reaction Rate**

**Core move:** Predict changes in reaction rate based on alterations to concentration, temperature, surface area, or catalyst properties.

**Seen in / context:**
- Industrial chemical process optimization.
Understanding enzyme activity in biological systems (qualitative).
Designing and interpreting chemical kinetics experiments.
Explaining everyday phenomena like food preservation or rusting.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the role of a catalyst with changing the equilibrium position or overall yield.
Incorrectly assuming a linear relationship between concentration/temperature and reaction rate without considering reaction order or activation energy.
Neglecting the importance of surface area for solid reactants or catalysts.
Misinterpreting temperature's effect as solely increasing kinetic energy, rather than increasing the proportion of successful collisions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions can ask for identifying conditions that increase/decrease rate, comparing rates, or explaining observed changes.
Ensure options clearly manipulate distinct variables (e.g., temperature, concentration, surface area, catalyst amount/state).
Qualitative understanding is often sufficient, but quantitative relationships (e.g., Q10 rule for temperature, general impact of concentration) may be implied.
Include scenarios where multiple factors are changed simultaneously to assess understanding of their combined effects.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q50_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q67_NSAA_Sec`: The question describes an experiment investigating how pH, an environmental factor affecting enzyme (catalyst) properties, influences the rate of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction. The production of phenolphthalein, indicated by color change, is a direct measure of the reaction rate. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of predicting or understanding changes in reaction rate based on alterations to catalyst properties (enzymes are biological catalysts) or environmental factors affecting them.

## **B_b2e6087b. Calculate DNA Base Composition**

**Core move:** Apply Chargaff's rules and DNA complementary base pairing to determine the proportions of each nucleotide in a DNA molecule.

**Seen in / context:**
- Understanding DNA structure (double helix, complementary strands)
Problems involving genetic material quantification
Molecular biology calculations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming A=C or T=G instead of A=T and C=G
Not accounting for both strands in a double helix when given a percentage for the entire molecule
Miscalculating percentages or absolute numbers based on the total number of bases

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions can specify total number of bases, total length in base pairs, or just percentages.
Clarify if the DNA is single-stranded or double-stranded, as Chargaff's rules apply to double-stranded DNA.
Ensure the provided information is sufficient to unambiguously calculate all base proportions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q63_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **B_b9061248. Analyze Inheritance Patterns in Pedigree Charts**

**Core move:** Infer genotypes and inheritance pathways by interpreting familial pedigree charts for specific genetic conditions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining the mode of inheritance (autosomal dominant, autosomal recessive, X-linked, etc.)
Predicting the probability of offspring inheriting a trait or condition
Identifying carriers of recessive traits
Explaining the presence or absence of a trait in individuals across generations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing dominant and recessive inheritance rules (e.g., assuming an affected individual with a dominant trait must have two affected parents, or that unaffected parents cannot have affected offspring in a dominant condition)
Misinterpreting symbols on the pedigree chart (e.g., affected vs. unaffected, male vs. female)
Failing to trace the trait through multiple generations to identify potential carriers or ancestors
Incorrectly applying X-linked inheritance rules, especially for males
Assuming complete penetrance when it might not be specified

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly state the mode of inheritance (e.g., autosomal dominant, recessive, X-linked) or provide enough information in the pedigree for students to deduce it.
Ensure the pedigree chart is clear, unambiguous, and can be solved with the given genetic rules.
Questions can involve identifying individuals with specific genotypes, calculating probabilities, or explaining observed patterns in a family.
Consider scenarios where new mutations might arise, though this is less common in introductory pedigree problems unless explicitly stated.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q65_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **B_b9245b43. Mechanism of Organismal Response to Stimuli**

**Core move:** Explain the step-by-step mechanism by which an organism responds to an environmental stimulus, identifying the key internal factors and cellular processes involved.

**Seen in / context:**
- Plant tropisms (e.g., phototropism, gravitropism)
Animal endocrine responses (e.g., blood glucose regulation, stress response)
Nervous system responses (e.g., reflex arcs)
Immune system responses to pathogens

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the stimulus with the ultimate response without detailing intermediate steps.
Omitting crucial internal signaling molecules (e.g., hormones, neurotransmitters) or cellular changes.
Incorrectly describing the effect or distribution of the regulatory factors (e.g., auxin's effect on cell elongation).
Failing to connect the cellular/molecular mechanism to the observed macroscopic behavior.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly define the stimulus and the observed organismal response.
Answers typically require knowledge of specific biological pathways, including reception, transduction, and effector mechanisms.
Can involve scenarios where a particular factor (e.g., hormone) is absent or altered to test understanding of its role.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q61_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **B_ba3774f0. Calculating Mendelian Inheritance Probabilities**

**Core move:** Determine the probability of specific offspring genotypes or phenotypes by applying Mendelian inheritance principles, often deducing parental genotypes from pedigree information.

**Seen in / context:**
- Autosomal recessive inheritance
Autosomal dominant inheritance
X-linked inheritance
Monohybrid crosses
Analyzing family pedigrees
Calculating combined probabilities of independent events (e.g., gender and trait)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly deducing parental genotypes from pedigree charts, especially for carriers of recessive traits.
Confusing dominant and recessive alleles when assigning genotypes.
Errors in setting up or interpreting Punnett squares.
Forgetting to combine probabilities of independent events (e.g., gender and specific trait).
Assuming unaffected individuals are always homozygous dominant for recessive conditions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the type of inheritance (autosomal, sex-linked, dominant, recessive) is clearly identified.
Often requires a two-step process: first deduce parental genotypes, then calculate offspring probabilities.
Questions may involve combining probabilities from two or more independent events (e.g., probability of a specific gender AND a specific phenotype).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q64_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_bcd4a7ef. Quantitative Analysis of Mixtures by Selective Precipitation**

**Core move:** Use the mass of a selectively precipitated product to determine the initial mass of a specific component within a mixture through stoichiometric calculations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Gravimetric analysis experiments.
Determining the purity of a substance.
Analyzing the composition of alloys or unknown samples.
Reactions where only one component of a mixture forms an insoluble product.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming all components of the mixture react to form the precipitate.
Incorrectly balancing the chemical equation or using incorrect mole ratios.
Using the Mr of the wrong substance in calculations.
Failing to identify the non-reacting components or ignoring the total mass of the mixture.
Errors in converting between mass, moles, and vice versa.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem clearly states which component forms a precipitate and which does not.
Provide Mr values or atomic masses for all relevant species.
The excess reactant should be clearly stated to ensure the limiting reactant is the component from the mixture.
Calculations should involve multiple steps (mass to moles, moles of product to moles of reactant, moles of reactant to mass of reactant, then using total mixture mass).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires determining the initial mass of an impure mixture (bauxite) needed to produce a target mass of a specific product (aluminium). This involves working backward from the product mass, applying stoichiometric calculations (molar masses and mole ratios) to find the mass of the pure component within the mixture (hydrated aluminium oxide), and then using the given mass percentage to find the total mass of the initial mixture. This aligns well with the core move of using a product's mass and stoichiometry to analyze components in a mixture, even if the product is obtained by extraction rather than selective precipitation.

## **B_c88e9f9b. Inferring Origin of Genetic Anomalies**

**Core move:** Infer the likely origin and timing of a genetic mutation or chromosomal abnormality based on observed inheritance patterns and phenotypic expression.

**Seen in / context:**
- De novo mutations
Germline vs. somatic mutations
Mosaicism vs. uniform genotype
Non-Mendelian inheritance patterns
Recurrence risk in families

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming all conditions must be inherited directly from parents.
Confusing somatic mutations (affecting only some cells) with germline mutations (passed to offspring or affecting all cells if early zygotic).
Ignoring the timing aspect of mutation occurrence (e.g., in gamete vs. early embryo).
Overlooking the possibility of incomplete penetrance or variable expressivity in parental phenotypes when parental genotypes are not explicitly stated as clear.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often present contradictory information (e.g., condition present in child but not parents) that points to de novo events.
Pay close attention to whether 'all cells' are affected (suggesting germline or very early embryonic mutation), or if it's 'mosaic' (later somatic mutation).
Consider the implications of the mutation's origin for future offspring recurrence risk in similar family scenarios.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q66_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **B_cf1b2bda. Calculating Population Size with Fixed Ratios Across Generations**

**Core move:** Determine the size of a specific sub-population in a future generation by iteratively calculating total population growth based on reproductive output and applying a given fixed ratio for a specific characteristic.

**Seen in / context:**
- Breeding experiments tracking population growth over multiple generations.
Estimating the number of individuals with a specific trait (e.g., sex, genotype) in a growing population.
Problems involving exponential or geometric population increase with a constant reproductive rate.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the number of breeding individuals (e.g., using total population instead of only females).
Errors in calculating the generational increase in population size (e.g., miscounting generations or applying the growth factor incorrectly).
Confusing the generation number with the exponent in power calculations.
Applying the fixed ratio too early or too late in the calculation, or incorrectly to a non-total population count.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure clear identification of the starting population and the mechanism of growth per generation.
Pay attention to whether all individuals, or only a subset (e.g., females), contribute to the next generation's offspring.
The ratios provided are typically deterministic expectations, not probabilities that require complex statistical methods.
Questions often involve a constant multiplication factor for population growth.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_d2a3d95c. Determine Stoichiometric Coefficients in Equations**

**Core move:** Apply the principle of conservation of atoms and charge to determine the stoichiometric coefficients of reactants and products in a chemical equation.

**Seen in / context:**
- Balancing simple chemical reactions
Balancing combustion reactions
Balancing acid-base neutralization reactions
Balancing redox reactions using oxidation states or half-equations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly counting atoms, especially within polyatomic ions
Failing to balance charge in ionic equations
Not recognizing the need for redox balancing techniques for complex reactions
Assuming a coefficient for one species fixes others without proper balancing

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions can ask for any coefficient in the balanced equation.
Vary complexity by changing the type of reaction (e.g., simple combination vs. redox).
Ensure that the final balanced equation has the smallest whole-number ratio of coefficients.
The method for balancing (inspection, algebraic, half-reaction) is implicitly tested.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q51_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q55_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires using the principle of conservation of atoms (C, H, O) to first deduce the formula of the alkane (CnH2n+2) from the given gas volume ratios (which represent mole ratios), and then to determine the stoichiometric coefficients 'a', 'b', and 'c' for oxygen, carbon dioxide, and water, respectively. The core move of determining stoichiometric coefficients by applying conservation principles is directly aligned.

## **P_d4225699. Synchronization Frequency from Multiple Delays**

**Core move:** Calculate individual delay times for multiple events and then determine the fundamental period required for all delays to be integer multiples of this period, finally converting the fundamental period to frequency.

**Seen in / context:**
- Echo problems with multiple reflecting surfaces.
Situations requiring synchronization of periodic events with different, but related, timings.
Problems involving sound, light, or other wave propagation and reflection.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting the '2d' factor when calculating echo travel time (sound travels to the reflector and back).
Confusing frequency with period, or miscalculating their reciprocal relationship.
Incorrectly identifying the greatest common divisor (GCD) of delay times for the fundamental period, or the least common multiple (LCM) for the fundamental frequency.
Ignoring one of the specified echo times or misinterpreting the condition 'each echo coincides'.
Misinterpreting the given distance(s) without a diagram, especially when only one distance is provided for a 'multiple echoes' scenario, leading to incorrect assumptions about the individual reflection distances.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary distances or relationships between distances are either explicitly stated or are common implicit assumptions for the problem type (e.g., one reflection distance being half of another).
The phrase 'gradually increased from zero until...' implies finding the largest possible fundamental period (and thus the lowest frequency) that allows all events to synchronize.
Pay careful attention to units (meters, seconds, Hertz) throughout calculations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q48_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **B_d9242dec. Ecological Population Estimation from Samples**

**Core move:** Apply standard ecological sampling formulas to estimate population parameters from collected quadrat or transect data.

**Seen in / context:**
- Estimating the abundance or frequency of species using quadrats or transects.
Analyzing species distribution in a given habitat.
Comparing population characteristics between different sampling sites or species.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing ecological terms like 'frequency' with 'density' or 'abundance'.
Errors in summing individuals or quadrats from raw data.
Not dividing by the correct total number of sampling units (quadrats/transects) or total area.
Misinterpreting the definition of the requested ecological parameter.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide raw counts of individuals per sampling unit (quadrat/transect).
Clearly specify which ecological parameter (e.g., frequency of occurrence, density, mean abundance) needs to be calculated.
Can involve comparing multiple species or different sampling locations.
Ensure the total number of sampling units and their size (if density is relevant) are provided.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q57_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q58_NSAA_Sec`: The question describes an investigation involving repeated sampling (net sweeps) to count organisms (tadpoles) in a specific area/volume. This data would then be used to estimate the larger population or its density, which directly aligns with the core move of applying ecological sampling formulas to estimate population parameters from collected data, analogous to quadrat or transect methods.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: The question describes the methodology for an ecological survey using quadrats to estimate population size in a larger area, which directly aligns with the 'Core Move' of applying standard ecological sampling formulas to estimate population parameters from collected quadrat data.

## **B_dba04f99. Counting Specific Alleles in a Population**

**Core move:** Calculate the total count of a specific allele within a population by summing contributions from individuals of known genotypes.

**Seen in / context:**
- Mendelian genetics problems involving allele frequencies.
Population genetics calculations prior to applying Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium.
Analysis of genetic trait prevalence based on genotype distribution.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting that homozygous individuals contribute two copies of an allele.
Incorrectly identifying the genotypes for homozygous recessive, heterozygous, or homozygous dominant individuals.
Excluding heterozygous carriers when counting recessive alleles.
Confusing the number of individuals with the number of alleles.
Failing to account for all relevant genotypes in the population (e.g., only counting affected individuals and ignoring carriers).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly defines the genetic condition (recessive/dominant) and the specific allele to be counted.
Provide counts for affected individuals, carriers, and potentially unaffected non-carriers, or enough information to derive them.
The task typically involves basic multiplication and addition.
Can be a precursor to calculating allele frequencies.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **B_e3e9c5c0. Tracking Gene Copies in Molecular Biology**

**Core move:** Trace the number of copies of a specific gene or DNA sequence through stages of a genetic engineering process, applying knowledge of cell biology and molecular genetics.

**Seen in / context:**
- Genetic modification/engineering workflows
Recombinant DNA technology
Understanding gene copy number in different organisms (e.g., human somatic cells vs. bacteria) and genetic elements (e.g., chromosome vs. plasmid)
Distinguishing between DNA and its protein product

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing DNA copies with protein copies
Incorrectly assuming the copy number of genes in diploid human cells or plasmids
Not recognizing that the base sequence (DNA) is not present in the final protein product
Assuming bacterial plasmids always have multiple copies when the question implies a specific single transfer

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often involve stages of gene cloning, expression, or transformation.
Requires careful reading to identify what exactly is being counted (gene, specific sequence, protein) and at what stage.
Pay attention to specific phrases like 'one copy of gene removed' or 'one modified plasmid moved'.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q62_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **B_e620b36b. Explaining Adaptation via Natural Selection**

**Core move:** Explain how environmental selective pressures drive the survival and prevalence of organisms possessing advantageous heritable traits.

**Seen in / context:**
- Evolution and natural selection
Environmental change and species adaptation
Population genetics and allele frequency shifts

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing individual adaptation with population evolution.
Attributing adaptation to conscious effort or acquired characteristics (Lamarckian thinking).
Misunderstanding the role of random mutation in providing variation.
Assuming all individuals of a species adapt equally or simultaneously.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should present an environmental challenge and observable changes in a population.
Focus on the mechanism (variation, selection, inheritance) rather than just the outcome.
Can involve different types of selective pressures (e.g., predation, disease, pollution, climate).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q55_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q56_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires understanding the relative frequencies of different types of mutations (beneficial, harmful, neutral) in a population, which is a fundamental concept underpinning how natural selection operates and drives adaptation. The proportions are determined by the rarity of beneficial mutations, the commonality and often detrimental nature of harmful mutations, and the high frequency of neutral mutations, all of which are critical inputs to explaining adaptation via natural selection.

## **C_f08f1192. Determine Molar Mass from Elemental Mass Fractions and Stoichiometric Atom Count**

**Core move:** Exploit known mass fractions of elements and a stoichiometrically determined atom count for one specific element to calculate the relative molar mass of a compound.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating molecular formulas and molar masses from elemental analysis data.
Problems where the number of atoms of a specific element in the product can be directly inferred from reactants and their stoichiometry.
Quantitative analysis of reaction products in organic and inorganic chemistry.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing empirical formula mass with molecular formula mass by misinterpreting the implied atom count from stoichiometry (e.g., 1 Br atom instead of 2 from Br2).
Arithmetic errors in converting mass fractions to relative numbers of moles or vice versa.
Failing to correctly use atomic mass values in calculations.
Ignoring the stoichiometric information that defines the absolute number of atoms for a given element in the product molecule.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem provides mass fractions (or percentages) for all constituent elements or implies the sum is 100%.
The number of atoms of at least one element in the product must be explicitly given or derivable from reaction stoichiometry.
Problems can be varied by making the stoichiometric inference more complex or by requiring multiple steps to find the target element's atom count.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **B_f2359591. Analyze Genetic Mutations and Protein Synthesis**

**Core move:** Apply the genetic code to translate DNA/mRNA sequences into amino acid sequences and predict the effect of specific base mutations on the resulting protein.

**Seen in / context:**
- Molecular genetics problems involving DNA, RNA, and protein synthesis.
Understanding the central dogma of molecular biology.
Analyzing the consequences of point mutations (silent, missense, nonsense).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly grouping bases into codons (e.g., starting at the wrong base, reading in the wrong direction).
Misinterpreting the genetic code table (e.g., using DNA codons as mRNA codons without conversion, misreading abbreviations).
Failing to identify the correct mutated base position.
Not recognizing the degeneracy of the genetic code, leading to overestimating the impact of a mutation.
Overlooking the significance of a 'Stop' codon or the effect of a mutation creating one.

**Notes for generation:**
- Always provide the relevant genetic code (either DNA or mRNA codons, clearly specified).
Clearly indicate the type and location of mutations (e.g., substitution, insertion, deletion, specific base numbers).
Questions can vary in complexity by including silent mutations, missense mutations, nonsense mutations, or frameshift mutations.
Ensure the provided sequence length is manageable for manual translation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q70_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q65_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks what aspects of a protein may remain unchanged after a gene mutation, which directly requires applying knowledge of the genetic code and predicting the effect (or lack thereof) of base changes on the resulting amino acid sequence and protein, matching the core move of analyzing genetic mutations and protein synthesis.

## **B_fab78c80. Predicting Water Movement by Osmosis**

**Core move:** Predict the direction of net water movement across a partially permeable membrane based on differences in solute concentration or water potential.

**Seen in / context:**
- Cell biology (e.g., animal cells in different solutions)
Plant cells (turgor, plasmolysis)
Physiology (e.g., kidney function, fluid balance)
Transport across membranes

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing solute concentration with water potential
Believing solutes move across the partially permeable membrane instead of water
Incorrectly identifying the direction of water movement (e.g., water moves from higher solute concentration to lower)
Assuming active transport instead of passive diffusion

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often involve comparing percentage concentrations or molarities of solutions.
May require linking water movement to visible changes in cell structure (e.g., crenation, lysis, plasmolysis, turgidity).
Emphasize the role of the partially permeable membrane.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q62_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_05432d25. Calculate Relative Atomic Mass from Isotopic Data**

**Core move:** Compute the weighted average of isotopic mass numbers, after determining these numbers from subatomic particle counts and given relative abundances.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining the average atomic mass of an element from details about its isotopes.
Problems involving mass spectrometry data where isotopic masses and abundances are provided or inferred.
Understanding the relationship between atomic number, mass number, protons, neutrons, and electrons in the context of isotopes and ions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly determining the number of protons from an ion's electron count and charge.
Incorrectly calculating the mass number (protons + neutrons) for an isotope.
Forgetting to convert percentage abundances to decimal form or using incorrect proportions.
Simply averaging the mass numbers without considering abundance.
Confusing relative atomic mass with the mass number of a specific isotope.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem provides enough information to uniquely identify the atomic number (e.g., from an ion's electron count or direct mention of the element).
Isotopic masses are often approximated by their mass numbers (sum of protons and neutrons).
Abundances must sum to 100%.
The calculation is a weighted average, so the result should typically be a non-integer value, unless all isotopes have the same mass or abundances are such that it works out to an integer.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q52_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the percentage abundance of isotopes given the relative atomic mass and individual isotopic masses. This is an application of the weighted average principle described in the core move, where the unknown is the abundance rather than the overall average.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q43_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires determining the relative atomic mass (Ar) by computing the weighted average of the isotopic masses, using the relative abundances provided in the chart. This directly matches the core move of calculating a weighted average from isotopic data.

## **C_05b89da3. Comparing Product Yields from Stoichiometric Equivalence**

**Core move:** Compare the quantitative yield of a common product across multiple chemical reactions by applying stoichiometric principles to identical initial reactant amounts.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing precipitation reactions with common products.
Evaluating the impact of different counter-ions on reaction yield.
Comparing outcomes of experiments with identical molar starting conditions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming the identity of spectator ions affects the mass of the precipitate.
Overlooking that 'same concentration' and 'same volume' mean 'same moles' of reactants.
Failing to recognize the 1:1 stoichiometric ratio between the limiting reactant and the common product.
Incorrectly considering the molar mass of the initial reactants instead of the product when comparing yields.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the multiple reactions produce the *same* common product.
Provide clear conditions for identical initial reactant amounts (e.g., same concentration, same volume).
Can vary the nature of spectator ions or other non-reactive components to test understanding.
Questions should focus on the *comparison* of quantities (mass, moles, volume) rather than absolute calculations of a single reaction.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q44_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_05d6f963. Interpreting Data for Fractional Separation**

**Core move:** Order components of a mixture for separation by fractional distillation using given boiling point data.

**Seen in / context:**
- Separation of components from liquefied air
Fractionation of crude oil into different products
Separation of ethanol and water mixtures
Purification of organic compounds in laboratories

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing melting point with boiling point data for separation
Incorrectly ordering negative boiling points (e.g., thinking -196°C is higher than -183°C)
Misunderstanding that the component with the lowest boiling point vaporizes and is collected first
Attempting to use melting points instead of boiling points for fractional distillation

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure a clear set of boiling points is provided for multiple components.
The question should explicitly or implicitly reference fractional distillation or a similar process relying on boiling point differences.
Can vary the number of components to be separated.
Can ask for order of vaporization, condensation, or collection.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q41_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q41_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires ordering the collection of gases by fractional distillation using their given boiling points, which directly aligns with the core move of ordering components for separation by fractional distillation based on boiling point data.

## **C_11b180e1. Deriving Balanced Net Ionic Equations**

**Core move:** Derive and balance net ionic equations for chemical reactions by identifying spectator ions and ensuring conservation of atoms and charge.

**Seen in / context:**
- Precipitation reactions
Acid-base neutralization reactions
Redox reactions in solution (though the provided example is not redox)
Identifying soluble and insoluble compounds using solubility rules
Stoichiometry of reactions in solution

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to correctly identify and cancel spectator ions.
Incorrectly balancing charges or atoms in the final equation.
Writing ionic forms for solid precipitates, liquids (e.g., water), or gases.
Incorrectly determining the chemical formulas of ionic compounds based on ion charges.
Not correctly identifying the states of matter (aqueous, solid, liquid, gas) for reactants and products.
Confusing molecular, complete ionic, and net ionic equations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Always provide clear information about the reactants and products, including their states of matter (e.g., 'precipitate of').
Vary the complexity by including different polyatomic ions or various charge combinations for the reacting ions.
Questions can also be designed for acid-base neutralization or gas evolution reactions.
Multiple-choice options should include common mistakes such as unbalanced equations, incorrect formulas, or inclusion of spectator ions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q43_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_1294369e. Calculating Total Moles of Ions in Solution**

**Core move:** Calculate the total moles of ions produced by the dissolution of a given amount of an ionic compound in solution, considering its stoichiometry.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating ion concentrations in solutions
Stoichiometric calculations involving dissolved ionic compounds
Understanding the dissociation of salts in water

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to convert volume units (e.g., cm³ to dm³)
Incorrectly writing the chemical formula of the ionic compound
Incorrectly balancing the dissociation equation, leading to wrong stoichiometric ratios for ions
Only calculating moles of the compound or moles of one type of ion, not the total moles of ions
Mistaking moles of the compound for moles of ions

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize correct chemical formula derivation for ionic compounds.
Highlight the importance of writing and balancing the dissociation equation.
Ensure units are consistent (e.g., volume in dm³ for mol dm⁻³ concentration).
Clearly distinguish between moles of the compound and moles of ions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q45_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_14b510b2. Characterizing Strong Acid Solutions**

**Core move:** Deduce the chemical and physical characteristics of a strong acid solution based on its concentration and definition.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating pH, [H+], and [OH-] for strong acid solutions.
Predicting reaction products and stoichiometry involving strong acids.
Describing electrical conductivity and other colligative properties of strong acid solutions.
Analyzing the effects of dilution on strong acid properties.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing strong acids with concentrated acids (strength vs. amount).
Treating a strong acid as if it partially dissociates (like a weak acid).
Incorrectly applying definitions of pH or Kw to calculate [H+] or [OH-].
Overlooking the complete ionization of strong acids in solution.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly state the acid's strength (strong/weak) and its initial concentration.
Can involve calculations (e.g., pH, concentration after dilution, reacting volumes) or qualitative descriptions of properties (e.g., conductivity, reactivity).
Often requires applying the concept of complete dissociation for strong acids.
Can be extended to strong bases as well, requiring similar deductive reasoning.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q39_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_1537b1f8. Identifying Organic Reaction Products**

**Core move:** Predict the major organic product(s) by recognizing the functional groups, reagents, and reaction conditions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Addition reactions (e.g., electrophilic addition to alkenes)
Substitution reactions (e.g., SN1, SN2, electrophilic aromatic substitution)
Elimination reactions (e.g., E1, E2)
Oxidation and reduction of organic compounds (e.g., alcohols to carbonyls, carbonyls to alcohols)
Condensation reactions (e.g., esterification, amide formation)
Hydrolysis reactions (e.g., esters, amides)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing reaction mechanisms or types (e.g., addition vs. substitution).
Incorrectly applying regioselectivity rules (e.g., Markovnikov's, Zaitsev's).
Misinterpreting the role of specific reagents (e.g., strong oxidant vs. mild oxidant).
Ignoring or misinterpreting reaction conditions (e.g., temperature, solvent, catalysts).
Incorrectly predicting stereochemistry (e.g., syn vs. anti addition, inversion vs. retention).
Failing to recognize common functional group interconversions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly state reactants and specific conditions/reagents.
The correct product(s) should be unambiguous and based on standard organic chemistry principles.
Can be multiple choice, requiring selection of the correct product, or open-ended, requiring drawing the product structure.
May involve multi-step syntheses or named reactions as part of the context.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q49_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_17f28fe3. Identifying Unknowns through Reaction Sequences**

**Core move:** Deduce the identity of unknown chemical substances by analyzing a series of described reactions, their products, and characteristic observations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Qualitative analysis problems involving inorganic compounds.
Questions combining knowledge of element properties, acid-base chemistry, precipitation reactions, and gas tests.
Deductive reasoning problems in descriptive chemistry.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting descriptive terms (e.g., 'white precipitate' for cream or blue).
Overlooking specific constraints (e.g., 'two products only').
Failing to connect observations to specific chemical species (e.g., Mg(OH)₂ is white, Cu(OH)₂ is blue).
Not systematically testing all given options against all conditions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should require step-by-step elimination based on characteristic reactions.
Observations should be specific enough to narrow down possibilities but may contain slight ambiguities (like 'white' for 'cream') to test careful reasoning.
Ensure a unique answer is derivable from the given information.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q47_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_19cbc1d0. Calculate Average Speed of Oscillating Particle**

**Core move:** Apply the definition of average speed to an object undergoing simple harmonic motion, using its amplitude and frequency.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing particle motion in transverse or longitudinal waves.
Problems involving simple harmonic oscillators (e.g., mass on a spring, pendulum at small angles).
Distinguishing the speed of a wave from the speed of particles in the medium.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the average speed of a particle in SHM with the wave speed (v = fλ).
Calculating the maximum speed of the particle (v_max = Aω) instead of the average speed.
Incorrectly calculating the total distance traveled by the particle (e.g., using displacement instead of 4 times the amplitude over one period).
Failing to convert units consistently (e.g., cm to m).

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize that the average speed of a particle over an integer number of periods of oscillation is 4Af, where A is amplitude and f is frequency.
Ensure students understand that wave velocity is the speed at which the disturbance propagates, while particle velocity is the speed at which individual points in the medium oscillate.
Questions can vary by providing amplitude, frequency, period, or angular frequency and asking for the average speed or a related quantity.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q36_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_1ab35d9c. Analyze Organic Reaction Mechanisms**

**Core move:** Deduce reaction pathways, intermediate structures, and products by applying principles of electron movement and and stability.

**Seen in / context:**
- Explaining observed reaction outcomes
Predicting products of multi-step syntheses
Identifying catalysts or reagents needed for specific transformations
Understanding selectivity (regio-, stereo-) in reactions

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assigning electron movement (e.g., arrows starting from positive charges or bonds moving to negative charges)
Overlooking resonance stabilization of intermediates or transition states
Failing to consider steric hindrance or electronic effects of substituents
Misidentifying nucleophiles, electrophiles, or leaving groups

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often involve drawing curly arrows to show electron flow
May require knowledge of named reactions and their mechanisms
Can be combined with spectroscopic data interpretation to confirm structures
Ensure all formal charges and lone pairs are correctly represented

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q31_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q37_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires deducing the products of an organic addition reaction (HBr with pent-2-ene), which involves applying principles like Markovnikov's rule, based on carbocation stability and electron movement, thus aligning with the core move of analyzing organic reaction mechanisms to determine products.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q46_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires identifying the repeating unit of a polymer, which is the product of an addition polymerization reaction from its monomer. This involves deducing the structure of a reaction product by applying the principles of how a double bond breaks and forms new single bonds in an organic reaction, aligning with the core move of deducing reaction products.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires deducing the structure of an addition polymer, which is a product formed through an organic reaction mechanism (addition polymerization). This involves understanding the breaking of the C=C double bond and the formation of new C-C single bonds between monomers, aligning with the core move of deducing products by applying principles of electron movement.

## **P_1d9567bf. Analyze Motion with Changing Mass and Constant Force**

**Core move:** Apply Newton's Second Law to determine the instantaneous acceleration and its rate of change for an object experiencing a constant net force while its mass changes over time.

**Seen in / context:**
- Rocket propulsion problems where fuel is consumed
Objects losing or gaining mass during motion (e.g., melting ice, collecting debris)
Problems involving constant thrust with varying vehicle mass

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming acceleration remains constant because the force is constant.
Forgetting to account for the change in mass over time.
Confusing the rate of change of acceleration with the acceleration itself.
Incorrectly applying the inverse relationship between acceleration and mass (e.g., if mass decreases, acceleration decreases).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly states whether the force is constant or variable.
Specify the nature of the mass change (constant rate, variable rate, instantaneous loss/gain).
Can be extended to include calculus for determining the rate of change of acceleration (da/dt).
Often requires understanding the inverse relationship: a = F/m, so if F is constant and m decreases, a increases. If m decreases at a constant rate, then m decreases faster relative to the remaining mass, leading to an increasing rate of acceleration increase.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q39_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_24a7e618. Calculating Ideal Transformer Properties**

**Core move:** Apply the ideal transformer equations relating voltage, current, and turns ratio to determine an unknown electrical property in a transformer circuit.

**Seen in / context:**
- Power transmission and distribution systems.
Analysis of step-up and step-down transformers.
Circuits involving ideal (100% efficient) transformers.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the turns ratio to voltage or current (e.g., using Np/Ns = Ip/Is instead of Vp/Vs = Is/Ip).
Confusing primary and secondary values for voltage, current, or turns.
Failing to apply the principle of power conservation (P_primary = P_secondary) for ideal transformers.
Mixing up the roles of multiple transformers in a series (e.g., step-up output becoming step-down input).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all relevant equations (Vp/Vs = Np/Ns, Vp/Vs = Is/Ip, P = VI) are considered.
Emphasize the 'ideal' condition as it simplifies calculations by eliminating efficiency factors.
Questions can involve a single transformer or a cascade of transformers, requiring sequential application of the principles.
Units must be consistent throughout the calculation (e.g., Volts, Amperes, dimensionless turns).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q34_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_25ef6348. Identifying Compounds by Physical and Chemical Properties**

**Core move:** Classify or identify a chemical compound by integrating its physical state properties (melting/boiling points) with its electrical conductivity behavior in solid, liquid, and aqueous solution phases.

**Seen in / context:**
- Differentiating between ionic, metallic, simple molecular (polar/non-polar), and giant covalent structures.
Predicting suitability of a substance for a specific application based on its properties.
Understanding the link between bonding and macroscopic properties.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing molten conductivity with aqueous solution conductivity for ionic compounds.
Not considering the possibility of molecular compounds ionizing in water (e.g., strong acids).
Misinterpreting low melting/boiling points as exclusively indicating non-polar molecular compounds, ignoring polar molecular compounds or gases that ionize.
Overlooking one property in favor of others (e.g., focusing only on MP/BP and ignoring conductivity).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure a comprehensive set of properties is given to allow for unambiguous identification.
Include options that represent different bonding types (ionic, simple molecular non-polar, simple molecular polar that ionizes, possibly metallic or giant covalent for harder questions).
Vary the specific properties (e.g., solubility, density, state at room temperature).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q43_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_27001b74. Calculate Distance/Time from Differential Speeds**

**Core move:** Apply kinematic equations to determine an unknown variable (distance or time) when two entities travel the same distance at different constant speeds, resulting in a known time difference in their arrival.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving two vehicles or objects traveling between the same two points.
Analysis of different wave types (e.g., sound, seismic) with varying speeds and detection times.
Scenarios where two events occur at the same location but propagate at different rates to a detection point.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly setting up the relationship between the two travel times.
Failing to recognize that the distance traveled by both entities is the same.
Confusing which entity is faster or arrives earlier.
Making errors in algebraic manipulation when solving simultaneous equations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all units (distance, speed, time) are consistent throughout the calculation.
Problems can be varied by asking for time difference, speed, or distance.
Can be extended to scenarios where entities start at different times or from different locations, but the core idea of relative motion and time difference remains.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q39_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_30453dc9. Analyzing Thermal Energy Transfer Mechanisms**

**Core move:** Identify the primary modes of thermal energy transfer (conduction, convection, radiation) applicable to given objects based on their properties, temperature, and environment, and compare their relative rates.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing heat loss/gain for objects in different environments (air, vacuum, liquids).
Comparing cooling rates for objects with different surface characteristics (e.g., dull vs. shiny, dark vs. light).
Understanding how insulation works to reduce specific types of heat transfer.
Predicting which object in a set will cool/heat fastest.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misidentifying the conditions necessary for convection (e.g., assuming convection in a vacuum or within a solid).
Incorrectly ranking the rate of thermal radiation, especially by neglecting the strong dependence on absolute temperature (T⁴) or surface emissivity.
Confusing heat transfer by conduction with heat transfer by convection.
Overlooking that all objects above absolute zero emit thermal radiation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often present multiple objects with varying properties (temperature, surface color/texture, size) and environments.
The comparison of rates (e.g., 'greatest rate') requires understanding the factors affecting each mode.
It's important to distinguish between losing and gaining thermal energy.
Common setups involve comparing dull black vs. shiny silver surfaces, or objects in air vs. a vacuum.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_321432b7. Interrelating Cuboid Physical Quantities**

**Core move:** Apply the relationships between mass, density, volume, force (weight), pressure, and surface area of a cuboid, including geometric properties, to solve for an unknown quantity.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving cuboids or other regular solids resting on surfaces.
Calculating pressure, force, area, mass, or density of a cuboid.
Situations requiring unit conversions between different systems (e.g., g/cm³ to kg/m³, N/cm² to Pa).
Using the geometric relationship V² = A₁A₂A₃ for a cuboid with face areas A₁, A₂, A₃.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrect unit conversions, leading to calculation errors (e.g., mixing g/cm³ with kg mass without conversion).
Confusing mass with weight (force).
Incorrectly calculating volume or area from given properties.
Not recognizing or misapplying the geometric relationship between the product of face areas and the volume of a cuboid.
Assuming pressure is directly proportional to area instead of inversely proportional (P=F/A).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all units are consistent before performing calculations (e.g., convert all quantities to SI units or cgs units).
Gravitational field strength (g) is essential for converting mass to force (weight).
The problem often requires working backwards from pressure to area, then using volume/density relationships to find missing information.
Highlight the importance of understanding the inverse relationship between pressure and area when force is constant.
This type of problem frequently appears in introductory physics or engineering contexts, even if presented in a broader 'Natural Sciences' assessment.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_3a9dad0f. Predicting Constituent Element Collection via Electrolysis**

**Core move:** Determine the products formed at the anode and cathode during the aqueous electrolysis of a compound, then evaluate if these products correspond to the constituent elements of the original compound, considering the reactivity series and competitive discharge.

**Seen in / context:**
- Electrolysis of molten salts
Electrolysis of aqueous solutions (dilute vs. concentrated)
Electroplating
Purification of metals

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Ignoring the presence of water in aqueous solutions, leading to incorrect cathode products (e.g., producing Na instead of H2).
Ignoring concentration effects for halide ions at the anode, leading to incorrect anode products (e.g., producing O2 instead of Cl2 from concentrated NaCl).
Not considering the reactivity series for cation discharge.
Confusing the products of electrolysis with the constituent elements of the compound, missing the specific requirement to collect elements of the original compound.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the competition between ions and water at both electrodes.
Specify conditions like 'concentrated' or 'dilute' for aqueous solutions.
Ensure the question clearly defines what needs to be 'collected' (e.g., specific elements, or any product).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q47_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_4140fef9. Deducing Subatomic Particle Relationships for Isoelectronic Species**

**Core move:** Infer the relative numbers of protons and electrons for isoelectronic atoms and ions based on their charge and classification.

**Seen in / context:**
- Comparing atoms, monatomic cations, and monatomic anions with the same electron configuration.
Questions involving the periodic table position of isoelectronic species.
Understanding the effect of gaining/losing electrons on the number of protons relative to electrons.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming 'same electron configuration' implies same element or same group.
Confusing the total number of electrons with the number of valence electrons.
Incorrectly relating charge to the number of protons (e.g., thinking a cation has more electrons than protons).

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize that 'same electron configuration' implies the same *total number of electrons*.
Remind that an atom has protons = electrons, an anion has protons < electrons, and a cation has protons > electrons.
The concept of atomic number (number of protons) determining the element is crucial.
Questions often ask for 'always correct' statements, requiring general understanding rather than specific examples.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q44_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_4787eeca. Optimize Reaction Conditions for Yield and Rate**

**Core move:** Evaluate various experimental conditions to determine the setup that achieves a specified product yield in the shortest possible reaction time.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chemical kinetics experiments exploring factors affecting reaction rate (surface area, concentration, temperature, catalyst).
Stoichiometry problems involving limiting reactants and theoretical yield calculations.
Experimental design questions where multiple variables are manipulated to optimize an outcome.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to perform stoichiometric calculations to ensure the target yield can actually be met by the given reactant quantities.
Incorrectly applying factors affecting reaction rate, such as assuming higher surface area or concentration always leads to faster reactions without considering the limiting reactant.
Confusing factors that influence reaction rate with those that influence the total yield of a reaction.
Ignoring one aspect (yield or rate) of the question when optimizing the conditions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide a balanced chemical equation and relevant molar masses.
Multiple experimental variables (e.g., reactant concentration, surface area, temperature, reactant mass/volume) should be presented.
The task requires identifying conditions that simultaneously satisfy a yield requirement and a rate optimization goal.
Ensure clear distinction between conditions that affect rate and conditions that affect maximum yield.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q53_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_4bcc248e. Molar Mass Calculation in Condensation Reactions**

**Core move:** Determine the molar mass of an unknown reactant or product by applying the mass change associated with a condensation reaction.

**Seen in / context:**
- Esterification reactions
Amidation reactions
Formation of ethers or acetals via condensation
Polymerization involving condensation steps

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to account for the loss of the small molecule (e.g., water, HCl) produced during the condensation.
Incorrectly calculating the molar mass of known components (e.g., propanol).
Misidentifying the functional groups or connectivity in the reactants or products.

**Notes for generation:**
- The question should explicitly state the reaction type (e.g., esterification) or provide enough information for it to be deduced.
Always provide atomic masses for calculation unless explicitly stated they are not needed.
The calculation typically follows the pattern: Mr(Product) = Sum(Mr(Reactants)) - Sum(Mr(By-products)).
Can be extended to include isomers of alcohols/carboxylic acids, but the carbon count remains critical.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q49_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_4db6391e. Circuit Analysis with Ohm's and Power Laws**

**Core move:** Apply Ohm's Law and the power formula, combined with rules for series and parallel connections, to determine unknown electrical quantities within a DC circuit.

**Seen in / context:**
- Simple DC circuits (series, parallel, or mixed configurations)
Calculations involving voltage, current, resistance, and power of components or the entire circuit
Problems requiring algebraic manipulation, potentially including quadratic equations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying series/parallel rules for voltage, current, or resistance
Using an incorrect form of the power formula (P=VI, P=I²R, P=V²/R)
Misattributing component-specific values (e.g., voltage across one resistor) to total circuit values in formulas
Mathematical errors when solving equations

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure consistent units (e.g., volts, amps, ohms, watts)
Problems can be scaled by adding more components or different circuit configurations
May require solving systems of equations for more complex circuits (e.g., Kirchhoff's laws, though not required here)

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q38_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_576bd07d. Interpreting Exam Instructions**

**Seen in / context:**
- Beginning a standardized test
Understanding exam structure and scoring
Identifying allowed and prohibited resources

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Overlooking crucial details such as time limits or allowed materials
Misinterpreting scoring rules (e.g., penalties for incorrect answers)
Failing to understand the choice of sections or number of questions per section

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the instructions provide unambiguous rules and expectations.
Questions should test the ability to identify key constraints and opportunities (e.g., 'no penalties' encourages attempting all questions).
This schema applies across different subjects, not exclusively to Chemistry.

**Exemplar questions:**

- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q60_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text consists of exam instructions, and the core move of schema C_576bd07d is to 'Extract and synthesize critical procedural and logistical information from exam instructions to formulate a test-taking strategy', which directly applies to analyzing these instructions.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q60_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text consists of exam instructions, and the schema 'Interpreting Exam Instructions' (C_576bd07d) specifically addresses the core move of extracting and synthesizing information from such instructions to formulate a test-taking strategy.

## **C_5885c264. Predict Effects on Equilibrium and Rate**

**Core move:** Predict how changes in temperature, pressure, concentration, or the addition of a catalyst affect the equilibrium position, yield, and/or reaction rate of a reversible reaction.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chemical equilibrium problems (Le Chatelier's Principle)
Reaction kinetics questions (factors affecting rate)
Industrial processes optimization
Thermodynamics of reversible reactions

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the effect on equilibrium position with the effect on reaction rate.
Incorrectly applying Le Chatelier's principle, especially regarding pressure for reactions with equal moles of gas.
Misinterpreting the sign of ΔH when predicting temperature effects on equilibrium.
Assuming a catalyst changes the equilibrium position or overall yield.
Forgetting to consider the state of matter when predicting pressure effects (only applies to gases).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure reactions are reversible for equilibrium analysis.
Vary the stoichiometry of gaseous reactants/products to test pressure effects.
Include both exothermic and endothermic reactions for temperature effects.
Differentiate between effects on forward/reverse rates versus net equilibrium shift.
Can include scenarios involving concentration changes of reactants/products.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q38_NSAA_Sec`: The question directly asks to identify changes that will alter the position of equilibrium for a reversible reaction, which is precisely what the core move of schema C_5885c264 describes: 'Predict how changes in temperature, pressure, concentration, or the addition of a catalyst affect the equilibrium position, yield, and/or reaction rate of a reversible reaction.'
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q45_NSAA_Sec`: The question describes a reversible reaction in the Contact process, a classic context for analyzing equilibrium and reaction rates. The core move of predicting how changes in conditions (temperature, pressure, concentration, catalyst) affect equilibrium position, yield, and reaction rate for such reactions directly matches the likely continuation of this question fragment.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q41_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks to predict how a change in pressure affects the rate of a chemical reaction involving gaseous reactants and to provide the reason. This directly aligns with the core move of schema C_5885c264, which is to 'Predict how changes in ... pressure ... affect the ... reaction rate'.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q75_NSAA_Sec`: The question describes the use of the temperature coefficient (Q10) to 'analyse the effect of increasing temperature on the rate of DNA replication'. This directly aligns with the core move of schema C_5885c264, which is to 'predict how changes in temperature...affect the...reaction rate'. Q10 is a quantitative tool used for this analysis and subsequent prediction.

## **C_5ff5623b. Constructing Ionic Compound Formulas**

**Core move:** Derive the chemical formula of an ionic compound by recalling common ion charges and balancing them to achieve electrical neutrality.

**Seen in / context:**
- Nomenclature of inorganic compounds
Ionic bonding and structure
Predicting reaction products and stoichiometry

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the charge of a common polyatomic ion
Failing to use parentheses correctly when multiple polyatomic ions are present
Not balancing the total positive and negative charges to zero
Confusing valency with number of atoms in a polyatomic ion

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should include both monoatomic and polyatomic ions to test comprehensive knowledge.
Common charges for main group elements can often be derived from their group number.
Charges for polyatomic ions and common transition metals usually need to be recalled from memory or a reference table.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q46_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_66f83de2. Mixture Composition by Mass and Volume**

**Core move:** Calculate the percentage of a component in a mixture by mass, given its percentage by volume and the densities of all components, or vice versa.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing the composition of alloys and other solid mixtures.
Determining concentrations of solutions when density data is available.
Problems involving the mixing of materials with different densities.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing mass percentage with volume percentage, assuming they are the same.
Incorrectly applying the density formula (e.g., density = volume / mass).
Failing to account for the total mass or total volume correctly.
Making algebraic errors when simplifying expressions with fractions and percentages.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage assuming a convenient total volume or mass (e.g., 100 units) to simplify calculations when dealing with percentages.
Ensure all units are consistent (e.g., g/cm³ for density, cm³ for volume, g for mass).
Can be extended to mixtures with more than two components.
Questions can be reversed, asking for volume percentage from mass percentage.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q34_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_71f3cf3e. Calculating Gas Volume Change from Stoichiometry at RTP/STP**

**Core move:** Calculate the change in gas volume for a reaction by determining the change in moles of gaseous substances and applying the molar volume at the specified temperature and pressure.

**Seen in / context:**
- Stoichiometric calculations involving gaseous reactants and products.
Determining the volume of a gas produced or consumed in a reaction.
Questions set at standard temperature and pressure (STP) or room temperature and pressure (RTP).
Applying Avogadro's Law and the concept of molar volume (e.g., 24 dm3/mol at RTP, 22.4 dm3/mol at STP).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying which substances are gases (ignoring state symbols).
Forgetting or misremembering the molar volume value for a gas at RTP or STP.
Failing to calculate the net change in moles of gaseous substances (moles of gaseous products - moles of gaseous reactants).
Not using a balanced chemical equation to determine stoichiometric mole changes.
Confusing the volume of an individual gas with the overall change in volume of the gas mixture.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the chemical equation is balanced before determining mole ratios.
Pay close attention to the state symbols (g) for gases.
The specific molar volume (e.g., 24 dm3/mol or 22.4 dm3/mol) is crucial and depends on the given conditions (RTP vs STP).
The 'change in volume' refers to the difference in total gaseous volume from reactants to products.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q47_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q51_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires using stoichiometric ratios from a balanced chemical equation to determine the volumes of gaseous reactants consumed and products formed, and then summing them to find the total final gas volume. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of 'calculating the change in moles of gaseous substances and applying the molar volume at the specified temperature and pressure', as gas volumes are directly proportional to moles under the given conditions.

## **C_725309e1. Calculate Chromatographic Retention Factors and Distances**

**Core move:** Apply the concept of retention factor (Rf) constancy to calculate unknown distances moved by components or the solvent front in chromatography.

**Seen in / context:**
- Paper chromatography
Thin-layer chromatography (TLC)
Predicting spot positions or solvent front positions over time or different chromatograms
Quantitative analysis of separation

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming the distance moved by the spot is directly proportional to time (i.e., simple linear extrapolation without considering Rf)
Incorrectly calculating the total solvent front distance in the second scenario
Miscalculating the Rf value (e.g., inverting the ratio)
Not understanding that the Rf value for a substance is constant under fixed chromatographic conditions

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure questions provide sufficient initial data to calculate the Rf value.
Vary which parameter (spot distance, solvent front distance, or Rf) is the unknown.
Can involve a single component or multiple components to assess understanding of selective separation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q49_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_7deb363a. Stoichiometry in Neutralization Reactions**

**Core move:** Apply stoichiometric principles to calculate an unknown quantity (mass, moles, concentration, or volume) of a reactant or product in an acid-base neutralization reaction.

**Seen in / context:**
- Titration calculations to determine unknown concentrations or volumes.
Quantifying the amount of a specific substance in a sample via acid-base reaction.
Determining the purity of an acidic or basic substance.
Calculating excess reactants or products after an acid-base reaction.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly balancing the neutralization equation, especially for polyprotic acids or polybasic bases.
Failing to use the correct mole ratio derived from the balanced chemical equation.
Errors in unit conversion (e.g., cm³ to dm³ for molarity calculations, grams to moles via Mr).
Confusing molarity (concentration) with molar mass (Mr value).
Not distinguishing between moles of an acid/base and moles of H+/OH- ions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Always start by writing and balancing the chemical equation for the neutralization reaction.
Ensure all volumes are converted to dm³ if concentrations are given in mol dm⁻³.
Pay close attention to the stoichiometry (mole ratios) in the balanced equation.
This pattern often involves multi-step calculations (e.g., moles from concentration/volume, then mole ratio, then mass from moles).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q50_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q53_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the volume of an acid needed to neutralize a base, which directly involves applying stoichiometric principles (mole ratio from the diprotic acid nature, and concentration-volume relationship) in an acid-base neutralization reaction to find an unknown quantity.

## **C_7ed108c3. Identifying Unknown Product via Stoichiometric Balancing**

**Core move:** Deduce the identity of an unknown chemical product in a reaction by applying conservation of atoms and given stoichiometric ratios to balance the chemical equation.

**Seen in / context:**
- Reactions with incomplete information about products.
Balancing complex inorganic reactions.
Determining empirical or molecular formulas of unknown substances.
Questions involving elemental analysis when combined with reaction stoichiometry.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying stoichiometric ratios given in the problem statement.
Making errors in counting atoms for balancing elements across the reactants and products.
Not fully utilizing all given constraints about the unknown substance (e.g., its elemental composition or lack thereof).
Attempting to guess the product from options rather than systematically balancing.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the balanced equation reflects the conservation of all atoms.
The unknown product's identity might be an element or a compound.
The question typically provides specific clues about the unknown substance's properties or a key stoichiometric relationship to aid in balancing.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q52_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_885cfd41. Identifying Essential Data for Molecular Formula Determination**

**Core move:** Recognize the general formula of a compound from its structural description and identify the minimum specific quantitative information required to determine its exact molecular formula.

**Seen in / context:**
- Hydrocarbons (alkanes, alkenes, alkynes) where only a structural class is given.
Organic compounds with functional groups where the class is known but specific chain length or molecular mass is missing.
Problems where the empirical formula is known, and the molecular formula requires an additional data point like molecular mass.
Questions about determining the degree of polymerization (n) from monomer information.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to correctly derive the general formula from the structural information (e.g., misinterpreting 'one double bond').
Suggesting qualitative data (e.g., 'reacts with bromine water') instead of necessary quantitative data.
Providing redundant information or information that can be derived from other given data.
Not understanding that relative atomic masses are usually given or assumed when determining formulas.
Confusing empirical formula with molecular formula and forgetting the need for molecular mass to convert between them.

**Notes for generation:**
- The general formula (e.g., CnH2n) is typically derivable from the compound's class (e.g., alkene with one double bond).
The 'minimum additional information' usually pertains to a single quantitative value that fixes the variable 'n' (e.g., number of carbon atoms, total molecular mass, or a related property like vapor density).
Emphasize that the information must be quantitative to determine the exact number of atoms.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q44_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_8d4a2ec9. Interpret Chromatograms for Component Identification**

**Core move:** Deduce the presence and identity of components in a mixture by comparing their retention positions on a chromatogram with those of known pure substances.

**Seen in / context:**
