- Paper chromatography (PC)
Thin-layer chromatography (TLC)
Qualitative analysis of mixtures (e.g., food dyes, drugs, plant extracts)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assuming two spots at different heights represent the same substance.
Failing to recognize that substances in a mixture separate into individual spots.
Misinterpreting the solvent front or baseline.
Assuming a pure substance would produce multiple spots.

**Notes for generation:**
- The chromatogram should clearly show baseline, solvent front, and distinct spots.
Questions can involve identifying known components, determining purity, or identifying unknown components based on comparison.
Can be extended to include calculations of Rf values.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q41_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_8ffbc902. Conservation of Energy in Spring-Mass Systems**

**Core move:** Apply the principle of conservation of mechanical energy to determine kinematic variables or system properties in systems involving elastic potential energy, gravitational potential energy, and kinetic energy.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing vertical or horizontal spring-mass oscillations
Calculating speeds or displacements in systems with springs and gravity
Problems where resistive forces are negligible, allowing for mechanical energy conservation

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly defining the spring extension (x) versus the total length of the spring.
Forgetting to include all forms of energy (kinetic, gravitational potential, elastic potential) in the conservation equation.
Choosing an inconsistent reference point for gravitational potential energy.
Assuming the spring is at equilibrium when it is only momentarily passing through it.
Mistakes in algebraic manipulation or unit conversions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly define the initial and final states of the system, including all relevant variables (height, extension, speed).
Establish a consistent datum (zero potential energy level) for gravitational potential energy.
Remember that spring potential energy depends on the *extension* (ΔL) from the unstretched length, not the total length.
Ensure all forces are conservative or that non-conservative work is accounted for if present.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_98345ef3. Predicting Electrolysis Products**

**Core move:** Predict the products formed at the anode and cathode in electrolytic cells by comparing the ease of oxidation and reduction of species present.

**Seen in / context:**
- Electrolysis of molten ionic compounds
Electrolysis of aqueous solutions (e.g., brine, dilute acids, metal salt solutions)
Understanding reactivity series and standard electrode potentials in the context of electrolysis
Identifying the role of water in aqueous electrolysis

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing which species is oxidized and which is reduced at each electrode.
Incorrectly prioritizing species for discharge in aqueous solutions (e.g., ignoring water, or misapplying electrochemical series).
Failing to differentiate between molten and aqueous conditions regarding available species.
Confusing anode and cathode reactions (oxidation vs. reduction).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should vary the electrolyte (molten, aqueous, different ions, different concentrations).
Can ask for half-equations, overall reactions, observations, or identification of specific products.
Ensure clarity on whether electrodes are inert or reactive if relevant.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q50_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q39_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks to identify the overall process at the cathode in the electrolysis of an aqueous solution. This requires comparing the ease of reduction of the species present (Na+ and H2O/H+) to predict the product formed at the cathode, which directly aligns with the core move of 'Predicting Electrolysis Products'.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires predicting the products formed at the anode and cathode during the electrolysis of concentrated aqueous sodium chloride (H2, Cl2, and OH- ions), which directly determines the pH of the remaining solution and the identity of the gases collected. The subsequent tests on these gases depend entirely on the correct prediction of these electrolysis products, aligning perfectly with the schema's core move.

## **C_98529288. Stoichiometry from Electrolysis Products**

**Core move:** Apply stoichiometric principles to deduce the empirical formula of a compound from quantitative electrolysis product data.

**Seen in / context:**
- Electrolysis of molten ionic compounds
Determining chemical formulas from experimental masses/volumes of products
Relating gas volumes to moles at specified conditions (e.g., RTP)
Interpreting electrical conductivity data to infer bonding types

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly using molar volume (e.g., using STP value for RTP)
Forgetting oxygen is diatomic (O₂) when converting moles of gas to moles of oxygen atoms in the compound
Calculation errors in mole conversions or ratios
Misinterpreting the significance of solid vs. molten conductivity for bonding type

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary constants (e.g., molar mass, molar volume at RTP) are either provided or standard knowledge for the target audience.
Vary the types of product measurements (e.g., mass of solid, volume of gas).
Can be extended to include Faraday's laws if charge/current is involved, but this specific question focuses on product ratios.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q38_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_a0e94807. Explaining Physical Properties via Intermolecular Forces**

**Core move:** Explain observed differences in physical properties (e.g., boiling point, solubility, viscosity) between molecular compounds by analyzing their molecular structure, polarity, and the types and strengths of intermolecular forces.

**Seen in / context:**
- Comparing boiling points of similar molecular compounds (e.g., alkanes, alcohols, haloalkanes)
Explaining solubility in different solvents (e.g., polar vs. non-polar)
Relating physical state at room temperature to molecular structure
Discussing viscosity of liquids

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing intermolecular forces with intramolecular covalent bonds
Incorrectly assigning polarity to molecules (e.g., assuming a molecule with polar bonds is always polar overall due to molecular geometry)
Overlooking the role of molecular size/surface area in London dispersion forces
Ignoring hydrogen bonding when applicable
Attributing differences to ionic or metallic bonding when dealing with molecular compounds

**Notes for generation:**
- Focus questions on differences between two or more molecular compounds.
Ensure the compounds allow for clear distinctions in IMFs based on common structural differences (e.g., branching, presence of polar groups, molecular size).
May require knowledge of VSEPR theory for molecular geometry and polarity.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q46_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_a3b2cf10. Deducing Compound Properties from Periodic Table**

**Core move:** Deduce the formula, bonding type, and acid-base character of simple compounds by applying periodic trends based on the given element's atomic number.

**Seen in / context:**
- Predicting formulas of simple binary compounds (e.g., oxides, halides)
Determining bonding types (ionic, covalent) from elemental properties
Classifying oxides as acidic, basic, or amphoteric based on element type and position

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the element's group or common oxidation state from its atomic number
Confusing criteria for ionic vs. covalent bonding (e.g., metal-nonmetal vs. nonmetal-nonmetal)
Misremembering the acid-base character trends for metal vs. non-metal oxides

**Notes for generation:**
- Vary the element's atomic number to cover different groups (e.g., alkali metals, alkaline earth metals, non-metals)
Focus on simple binary compounds (e.g., oxides, chlorides, hydrides)
Ensure options contain plausible distractors related to errors in formula, bonding, or acid-base character

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q44_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires deducing various physical and chemical properties (boiling point, electrical conductivity, reactivity, melting point, bond structure) of both elements and simple compounds by applying knowledge of bonding types (metallic, ionic, covalent, hydrogen bonding) and periodic trends based on the elements' positions in the Periodic Table. This aligns directly with the core move of 'Deduce the formula, bonding type, and acid-base character of simple compounds by applying periodic trends based on the given element's atomic number', as well as the schema's title 'Deducing Compound Properties from Periodic Table'.

## **P_a9a3eea7. Calculating Terminal Velocity with Drag and Buoyancy**

**Core move:** Apply Newton's first law (equilibrium of forces) to determine the terminal velocity of an object falling through a fluid, considering its weight, buoyant force, and a given drag force model.

**Seen in / context:**
- Fluid dynamics problems involving objects moving through viscous media.
Calculating terminal velocity for objects falling or rising.
Problems where densities and volumes are used to determine weight and buoyant force.
Applications of Stokes' Law or similar empirical drag force equations.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly setting up the force balance equation (e.g., adding drag force in the wrong direction or neglecting upthrust).
Using the wrong density for calculating the object's weight (e.g., liquid density instead of object density).
Using the wrong density for calculating the upthrust (e.g., object density instead of liquid density).
Algebraic errors when rearranging the force balance equation to solve for terminal velocity.
Misinterpreting the condition for 'terminal speed' (i.e., not setting the net force to zero).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all forces (weight, upthrust, drag) are correctly identified and their directions are properly accounted for in the force balance.
Emphasize the definition of upthrust as the weight of the fluid displaced by the object.
The form of the drag force equation (e.g., linear with velocity, quadratic with velocity) will be provided and must be incorporated correctly.
Questions should require quantitative calculation and algebraic manipulation to solve for an unknown variable like velocity.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q38_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_b2a7e461. Deduce Reactivity Order from Chemical Properties**

**Core move:** Infer the relative order of chemical reactivity for a set of substances based on their observed reactions and extraction methods.

**Seen in / context:**
- Reactivity series of metals or non-metals
Displacement reactions
Extraction methods of elements (e.g., electrolysis vs. reduction by carbon)
Ease of ion formation (for metals) or electron gain (for non-metals)
Reactions with water, acids, oxygen, or other elements

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting extraction methods as indicators of reactivity (e.g., confusing carbon reduction with requiring high reactivity)
Confusing ease of ion formation with being less reactive (for metals)
Incorrectly applying the rules of displacement reactions
Failing to synthesize all pieces of information into a consistent and complete order
Confusing reactivity with position in the periodic table without considering specific reaction data

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure multiple distinct clues are provided that need to be logically combined to establish the full order.
Clues should cover different segments of the reactivity scale.
Clearly specify the direction of the required order (e.g., most reactive to least reactive).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q41_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q41_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires inferring the relative order of reactivity of four metals by analyzing a set of observed chemical reactions, which directly aligns with the core move of schema C_b2a7e461.

## **C_bb0a9270. Adjusting Solution Concentration for Hydration**

**Core move:** Recalculate the solution concentration based on the actual mass of the active component when a different form (e.g., hydrated instead of anhydrous) was mistakenly used.

**Seen in / context:**
- Stoichiometry problems involving solution preparation.
Calculations related to hydrated salts and their anhydrous forms.
Analyzing errors in chemical synthesis or solution preparation.
Determining the purity of a substance based on its hydration state.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to calculate the molar mass correctly for both the anhydrous and hydrated forms.
Using the molar mass of the intended compound instead of the compound actually used for the given mass.
Incorrectly converting between moles of hydrated and anhydrous forms (e.g., assuming a non-1:1 ratio when it is actually 1:1 for the active component).
Errors in volume unit conversion (e.g., cm³ to dm³).
Arithmetic errors in multi-step calculations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly states which substance was intended and which was actually used, along with the given quantity (mass or moles).
Provide all necessary relative atomic mass values (Ar).
Vary the compounds used (different hydrated salts, different hydration numbers).
The mistake could also be the reverse: intended hydrated, used anhydrous.
Can extend to scenarios where the active component is not 1:1 in the hydrated compound (e.g., complexes).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q47_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_bd5cd4eb. Calculate Gas Property Change from Applied Pressure**

**Core move:** Apply the ideal gas law and force-area relationship to determine a gas property (e.g., density, volume) after a pressure change caused by an added force, assuming constant temperature.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving pistons compressing or expanding gases in cylinders.
Scenarios where external mechanical forces (e.g., weight, spring force) alter the pressure on an enclosed gas.
Isothermal processes for ideal gases where density or volume changes are tracked.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to account for initial atmospheric pressure when calculating the total final pressure.
Incorrectly applying gas laws, especially for non-constant temperature processes or if the mass of gas changes.
Errors in calculating pressure from force and area (e.g., unit conversion, algebraic mistakes).
Not recognizing the inverse relationship between volume and density for a constant mass of gas, leading to an incorrect ideal gas law application (e.g., P1V1=P2V2 vs P1/ρ1=P2/ρ2).

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly specify whether the process is isothermal, adiabatic, or isobaric.
Ensure the mass of the gas remains constant in the system.
Questions can be varied by asking for initial/final pressure, initial/final density, volume change, or the properties of the object causing the pressure change (e.g., piston mass/area).
Can incorporate other forces acting on the piston (e.g., friction, spring force).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q38_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: This question applies Boyle's Law (a special case of the ideal gas law for constant temperature) to determine the volume of an ideal gas. The pressure changes are caused by hydrostatic pressure due to varying depth, which is an application of the force-area relationship (P = F/A, where F is the weight of the water column over a given area, resulting in P = ρgh) added to atmospheric pressure.

## **C_c28e3682. Maximize Molecular Mass from Isotopic Data**

**Core move:** Calculate the maximum possible relative molecular mass of a compound by selecting the heaviest available isotope for each constituent element based on provided isotopic data.

**Seen in / context:**
- Questions involving isotopes and molecular formulae.
Scenarios where a 'maximum' or 'minimum' value is sought instead of an average.
Analysis of mass spectrometry data for specific applications (e.g., tracking specific molecules).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Calculating the average relative atomic mass for each element instead of selecting the heaviest isotope.
Ignoring the stoichiometry of the compound.
Misreading the isotopic masses from the charts.
Confusing percentage abundance with the actual isotopic mass when selecting the heaviest.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the chemical formula of the compound is provided or easily derivable.
Isotopic data (relative mass and abundance) should be clearly presented, often graphically.
The question should explicitly ask for a maximum or minimum value, guiding the selection of isotopes.
Avoid ambiguity in identifying the heaviest/lightest isotope from the charts.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_c592878a. Calculate Forces in Physical Systems**

**Core move:** Apply Newton's laws of motion and principles of statics or dynamics to determine unknown forces.

**Seen in / context:**
- Forces in equilibrium (e.g., tension in ropes, support forces)
Dynamics problems (e.g., F=ma)
Gravitational forces or friction

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misidentifying forces acting on a system.
Incorrectly applying vector addition or resolution.
Forgetting to account for all relevant forces (e.g., friction, gravity).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure appropriate free-body diagrams are drawn.
Distinguish between static and kinetic situations.
Pay attention to significant figures and units.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q75_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_c68d86d5. Calculating Temperature from Reaction Energy Transfer**

**Core move:** Calculate the final temperature of a substance by first determining the total energy released or absorbed from a chemical process and then using that energy with the substance's mass and specific heat capacity.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calorimetry experiments (e.g., combustion, dissolution, neutralization)
Thermochemical calculations involving enthalpy changes
Heat transfer calculations using specific heat capacity (Q=mcΔT)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly converting between moles and mass for reaction energy calculation
Errors in unit conversion (e.g., Joules to kilojoules, grams to kilograms)
Misidentifying which mass or specific heat capacity value to use in Q=mcΔT
Forgetting to add the calculated temperature change to the initial temperature
Not accounting for heat loss or incomplete transfer if specified (though often assumed ideal in such problems)

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary molar masses, specific heat capacities, and enthalpy values are provided.
Explicitly state any assumptions about heat transfer efficiency (e.g., 'all heat is transferred').
Questions can involve a variety of chemical reactions (combustion, dissolution, acid-base, etc.) as the energy source.
Can be extended to include phase changes if latent heat values are provided.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q54_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_c8df3bc3. Calculating Isotope Ratios after Radioactive Decay**

**Core move:** Apply the concept of half-life to determine the remaining amount of a radioactive isotope and the amount of its stable decay product over a given time.

**Seen in / context:**
- Nuclear chemistry problems involving radioactive decay
Calculations for radioactive dating (e.g., carbon dating)
Determining the composition of samples over time due to decay

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the number of half-lives passed
Forgetting to account for the initial amount of the stable isotope
Confusing the remaining amount of the parent isotope with the amount that has decayed
Applying the decay formula incorrectly (e.g., using A = A0 * e^(-λt) instead of just halving for half-life problems)

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the decay process is clearly defined (e.g., single stage to stable product).
Specify initial conditions for all isotopes involved (e.g., equal numbers, pure sample).
Vary the required output, such as ratio of parent:daughter, parent:total, or daughter:total.
Choose half-lives and time periods that result in a whole number of half-lives for simpler calculations, or explicitly state that calculators are allowed for non-integer half-lives.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q36_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_c99fbfe6. Determining Subatomic Particle Counts in Nuclides and Ions**

**Core move:** Calculate the absolute number of protons, neutrons, and electrons for any given atomic or ionic species using its atomic number, mass number, and charge.

**Seen in / context:**
- Interpreting nuclide notation (e.g., A/Z X or AX^c)
Comparing isotopes, isobars, and ions
Mass spectrometry interpretation (to understand the species being detected)
Basic atomic structure questions

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing mass number (protons + neutrons) with atomic number (protons only).
Incorrectly calculating the number of electrons for ions (e.g., adding electrons for positive ions or subtracting for negative ions).
Assuming isotopes of an element have different numbers of protons.
Forgetting that the number of protons defines the element.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly provide the element symbol, mass number, and ionic charge (if applicable).
Often involves comparing properties of multiple species (e.g., different isotopes or ions).
This foundational skill is a prerequisite for understanding topics like isotopic abundance calculations or mass spectrometry principles.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_ca3ed7f6. Identifying Redox Species via Oxidation States**

**Core move:** Determine if a substance is oxidized, reduced, or neither by calculating the oxidation states of relevant elements in reactants and products.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing chemical equations for redox processes
Identifying oxidizing and reducing agents
Understanding the role of substances in industrial processes (e.g., extraction of metals)
Balancing redox equations (though not directly in this question, it's a related skill)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assigning oxidation states to elements in compounds or ions.
Confusing oxidation (increase in oxidation state, loss of electrons) with reduction (decrease in oxidation state, gain of electrons).
Mistaking decomposition or acid-base reactions for redox reactions when no change in oxidation state occurs.
Failing to check the oxidation states of all relevant elements within a compound.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide clear chemical equations.
Include a mix of reactions that are redox and those that are not to test comprehensive understanding.
Focus on elements that can exhibit multiple oxidation states.
Ensure the problem requires the fundamental application of rules for assigning oxidation states.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q43_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_ca474f82. Determining Subatomic Particle Counts**

**Core move:** Calculate the number of protons, neutrons, and electrons for an atom or ion using its mass number, atomic number, and charge.

**Seen in / context:**
- Identifying and comparing isotopes and ions.
Understanding the meaning of atomic number, mass number, and ionic charge.
Relating periodic table information to atomic structure.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing mass number (A) with atomic number (Z).
Incorrectly calculating the number of electrons for an ion (e.g., adding electrons for a cation, subtracting for an anion).
Mistaking the number of protons for neutrons or vice versa.
Forgetting that the atomic number (number of protons) defines the element.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary information (mass number, atomic number, charge) is either provided or inferable (e.g., from periodic table for Z).
Questions can involve a direct calculation or a comparative task (e.g., 'same number of neutrons as X').
Can include isotopes, isobars, and allotropes as distractors.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q37_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q37_NSAA_Sec`: The question presents atoms and ions with their mass numbers, atomic numbers, and charges. To evaluate any potential statements about these species (e.g., number of protons, neutrons, electrons, or isotopic relationships), one must first be able to calculate the counts of these subatomic particles, which directly aligns with the core move of the C_ca474f82 schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q37_NSAA_Sec`: The question directly asks to determine the number of protons, neutrons, and electrons for a given ion, utilizing its mass number, atomic number, and charge, which perfectly aligns with the core move of schema C_ca474f82.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the number of protons, neutrons, and electrons for an ion using its atomic number (x), mass number (2x + 2), and charge (-2), which precisely matches the core move of determining subatomic particle counts.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q48_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the number of protons, neutrons, and electrons for an ion given its mass number, atomic number (expressed algebraically), and charge. This directly matches the core move of determining subatomic particle counts.

## **C_cca6b2f6. Analyze and Compare Experimental Rate Data**

**Core move:** Interpret and compare quantitative experimental data, typically presented in tables, to identify trends, evaluate rates, or draw conclusions about a process.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing results from experiments in biology (e.g., transpiration rates, enzyme activity)
Interpreting chemical reaction kinetics data (e.g., initial rates)
Evaluating efficiency or performance metrics in engineering or physics experiments
Comparing different trials or conditions in scientific investigations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting the units of the measured rate or quantity.
Failing to account for or identify constant variables (controls).
Incorrectly identifying the independent and dependent variables.
Drawing conclusions that are not supported by the presented data.
Confusing absolute values with relative changes or trends.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the data presented allows for clear comparisons or trend identification.
Questions can involve identifying the highest/lowest rate, optimal conditions, or describing relationships between variables.
Can require calculations such as averages, differences, or simple ratios.
The 'process' can be biological, chemical, or physical.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q67_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_cd763d5d. Inferring Element Properties from Periodic Position**

**Core move:** Infer an element's characteristic physical and chemical properties based on its group and period in the Periodic Table.

**Seen in / context:**
- Predicting reactivity trends within a group or period
Identifying common physical states and appearances for elements
Describing typical bonding behaviors and common oxidation states
Comparing properties of elements in the same group (e.g., boiling points of halogens, metallic character)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying periodic trends (e.g., misidentifying increasing vs. decreasing reactivity down a group)
Confusing properties between different groups (e.g., assuming halogens are noble gases)
Overgeneralizing without considering specific element nuances or common exceptions (e.g., hydrogen as an alkali metal)
Mixing up physical and chemical properties

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should specify the group/period or provide enough information to deduce it.
Focus on fundamental properties that are direct consequences of an element's electron configuration and atomic structure.
Can involve true/false statements, multiple-choice options, or direct descriptive questions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q48_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires inferring the properties of Lithium (Group 1) and Bromine (Group 17) based on their periodic table positions. These inferred elemental properties (e.g., tendency to form ions, electronegativity differences) are then used to deduce the nature and properties of the compound lithium bromide, which directly aligns with the core move of inferring characteristics from periodic position.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q47_NSAA_Sec`: The question first requires identifying element Q's position in the periodic table from its electron shell description. Then, it asks to deduce the properties and characteristics of elements immediately surrounding Q (above, below, left, right) by applying knowledge of periodic trends and the structure of the periodic table. This directly aligns with inferring element properties based on their group and period.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q46_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires inferring silicon's characteristic structure (giant covalent) and bonding type from its periodic position (directly below carbon) to explain its high melting point. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of inferring element properties from periodic position, which is a prerequisite for correctly identifying the type of bonds that need to be broken on melting.

## **C_d26f3956. Calculate Initial Acceleration of Displaced Floating Object**

**Core move:** Apply Archimedes' principle and Newton's second law to determine the initial acceleration of a floating object displaced from its equilibrium position.

**Seen in / context:**
- Buoyancy problems involving objects partially submerged in liquids.
Problems where an object is displaced from its floating equilibrium and a dynamic response (acceleration) is sought.
Situations that can lead to simple harmonic motion for floating objects.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the submerged volume after displacement.
Confusing the density of the object with the density of the fluid when calculating mass or buoyant force.
Failing to correctly identify the net force acting on the object (difference between buoyant force and weight).
Forgetting to convert units or using inconsistent units (e.g., g cm⁻³ with N kg⁻¹).
Assuming the buoyant force remains constant or equal to the object's weight after displacement.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the change in buoyant force directly proportional to the change in submerged volume due to displacement.
Ensure the mass used in F=ma is the mass of the floating object.
Questions can be adapted by changing the shape of the object or the properties of the liquid.
Can be extended to calculate parameters for simple harmonic motion (e.g., period or frequency) if the displacement is small.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q39_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_d83414aa. Distinguishing Ionic Compounds Using Qualitative Tests**

**Core move:** Select a single chemical test or physical property to uniquely identify and differentiate between a set of given ionic compounds.

**Seen in / context:**
- Identifying unknown salts.
Distinguishing between visually similar white solids or colorless solutions.
Practical chemistry examinations involving qualitative analysis.
Understanding characteristic reactions of common cations and anions (e.g., carbonate, sulfate, chloride, calcium, potassium, sodium).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Choosing a test that gives the same result for two or more of the compounds.
Choosing a test that gives no observable change for all compounds.
Not considering the full range of possible observations (e.g., precipitate, gas, color change, dissolution).
Confusing tests for solid state vs. solution state properties.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure there is a unique observable outcome for each compound with the chosen test.
Common distinguishing tests include adding acid (for carbonates), adding barium chloride/nitrate (for sulfates), adding silver nitrate (for halides), and flame tests (for characteristic cations).
The question specifies 'on its own', emphasizing a single, comprehensive test.
The initial state (e.g., 'white solids') implies visual appearance is not a distinguishing factor initially.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q43_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_d971743a. Quantifying Wave Propagation in Different Media**

**Core move:** Apply the relationships between speed, wavelength, frequency, and number of oscillations to solve problems involving wave propagation through different materials.

**Seen in / context:**
- Light passing through optical elements (lenses, prisms, glass slabs).
Sound waves traveling through different substances (air, water, solids).
General wave phenomena in physics.
Calculating optical path difference.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming frequency changes when a wave moves from one medium to another (frequency remains constant, wavelength and speed change).
Incorrectly applying the speed-wavelength-frequency relationship (v = fλ).
Confusing the number of oscillations with the wavelength.
Calculation errors when dealing with ratios of speeds or wavelengths.

**Notes for generation:**
- Vary the type of wave (e.g., light, sound) and the media involved.
Provide different initial conditions (e.g., refractive index instead of speed ratio, wavelength in one medium).
Ask for different unknowns (e.g., refractive index, wavelength in a medium, number of oscillations, phase difference).
Can be extended to scenarios with multiple layers of different materials or to optical path difference concepts relevant to interference/diffraction.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q35_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_dc623dcf. Assigning Oxidation States in Complex Structures**

**Core move:** Calculate the individual oxidation state for each atom of a specified element within a complex ion or molecule, considering its specific bonding environment.

**Seen in / context:**
- Polyatomic ions containing multiple atoms of the same element (e.g., polythionates, peroxides)
Molecules where different atoms of the same element have distinct bonding patterns
Species where a simple average oxidation state calculation is insufficient or misleading

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Calculating only the average oxidation state for the element in question
Assuming all atoms of the same element in a molecule or ion have identical oxidation states
Incorrectly applying general rules (e.g., O is always -2) without considering exceptions or specific bonding arrangements (e.g., peroxide linkages, S-S bonds)
Ignoring the overall charge of the ion when assigning oxidation states

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the need to consider the molecule's or ion's structure (e.g., a Lewis structure or known connectivity) to correctly identify bonding environments.
Remind students that atoms bonded to identical atoms (like S-S bonds) often have an oxidation state of 0 within that bond.
Questions should guide students to select from lists of possible oxidation states rather than just a single average.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q48_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_ddcea784. Determining Stoichiometry via Redox Balancing**

**Core move:** Determine stoichiometric coefficients of reactants and products by applying conservation of mass and charge principles in redox reactions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Balancing complex inorganic redox reactions in acidic, basic, or neutral media
Calculations involving quantitative analysis (e.g., titrations) based on redox stoichiometry
Understanding electron transfer in electrochemical cells

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assigning oxidation states to elements
Failing to balance both atoms (mass) and charge simultaneously across the equation
Errors in balancing electrons transferred between half-reactions
Arithmetic mistakes when summing coefficients or during the balancing process
Overlooking spectator ions or species that do not change oxidation state

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question involves a clear redox reaction with identifiable changes in oxidation states.
Vary the complexity of the reactants and products, including polyatomic ions.
Can ask for individual coefficients or the sum/difference of several coefficients.
The reaction conditions (acidic/basic) should be specified if H+ or OH- are involved in balancing.
The equation should have a unique set of smallest whole-number integer coefficients.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q48_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_debfd5cf. Calculating pH after Stoichiometric Acid-Base Reaction**

**Core move:** Determine the final pH of a solution by performing stoichiometric calculations for an acid-base reaction, identifying the limiting reactant or exact neutralization, and then using the resulting concentrations (or neutrality) to calculate pH.

**Seen in / context:**
- Titration problems where the equivalence point or endpoint needs to be determined.
Reactions involving strong acids/bases and weak acids/bases.
Calculating the pH of salt solutions formed from neutralization reactions.
Problems where the complete consumption of a reactant implies a specific stoichiometric outcome.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Ignoring the stoichiometric coefficients in the balanced chemical equation.
Incorrectly identifying the limiting reactant or assuming excess of one reactant when the problem implies complete reaction/neutralization.
Forgetting to consider the hydrolysis of salt ions if a weak acid or base is involved in the formation of the salt.
Not accounting for dilution effects on concentration before calculating pH.
Confusing moles of reactant with moles of H+ or OH- ions produced/consumed.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary reaction equations are balanced.
Always consider the nature of the reactants (strong/weak acid/base) to accurately determine the pH of the resulting solution or salt solution.
Pay close attention to phrases like 'completely reacted' or 'neutralized' as they are critical clues for inferring stoichiometric relationships.
If the initial amount of one reactant is not given, assume a stoichiometric reaction leading to neutrality or a simple acidic/basic state if it's the only way to make the problem solvable given the options.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q90_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_e2e3b327. Deducing Salt Formula from Tests and Calculating Molar Mass**

**Core move:** Identify the constituent cation and anion of a salt based on qualitative chemical tests, deduce its chemical formula, and then calculate its relative molar mass using given atomic masses.

**Seen in / context:**
- Identification of unknown inorganic salts.
Stoichiometry problems involving compounds identified through characteristic reactions.
Practical chemistry exercises for ion detection.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly interpreting the results of qualitative tests (e.g., misidentifying the oxidation state of iron, confusing sulfate with sulfite/carbonate).
Failing to correctly balance charges to derive the chemical formula of the ionic compound.
Arithmetic errors when summing the relative atomic masses to calculate the relative molar mass.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the qualitative tests described are specific enough to unambiguously identify both the cation and the anion.
Provide all necessary relative atomic masses, or indicate where they can be found (e.g., periodic table).
The compound should be a common, simple ionic salt whose formula can be readily derived once the ions are known.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q45_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_e34cd17b. Deduce Formula from Precipitation Stoichiometry**

**Core move:** Apply stoichiometric principles to determine the empirical formula of a compound based on quantitative precipitation reaction data.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining the formula of ionic compounds using gravimetric analysis.
Analyzing reactions where a precipitate's mass is used to find an unknown quantity.
Stoichiometric calculations involving halide precipitation with silver ions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the moles of the precipitate.
Failing to correctly establish the mole ratio between the initial compound and the precipitated product.
Confusing the moles of the compound with the moles of the specific ion involved in precipitation.
Assuming a 1:1 molar ratio without calculation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure molar masses are provided or easily derivable.
Often involves calculating moles of precipitate, relating it to the moles of the specific ion, and then comparing that to the moles of the original compound.
The problem may extend to identifying element X based on its atomic mass.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q47_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_e3e71bb9. Quantitative Stoichiometry of Electrolysis Products**

**Core move:** Apply stoichiometric ratios derived from the balanced electrolysis equation to determine the mass or moles of one product given the mass or moles of another.

**Seen in / context:**
- Electrolysis of water or aqueous solutions to produce gases
Calculations involving the quantitative yields of products from electrolysis
Relating product amounts at different electrodes

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly balancing the overall electrolysis reaction (e.g., using atomic H and O instead of H₂ and O₂).
Confusing mass ratios with mole ratios directly.
Using incorrect molar masses for diatomic gases.
Failing to convert mass to moles before applying stoichiometric ratios.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the balanced chemical equation for the overall decomposition (e.g., 2H₂O → 2H₂ + O₂) is correctly identified.
Emphasize the conversion between mass and moles as a critical first step for stoichiometric calculations.
Questions can be extended to include gas volumes (using molar volume) or charge passed (using Faraday's laws).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q46_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q57_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the mass of different elements produced per mole of electrons transferred using the stoichiometric ratios derived from their balanced half-equations and then comparing these calculated masses. This aligns with applying stoichiometric ratios to determine the mass of products relative to a common electron transfer.

## **C_e4ea9c22. Balancing Chemical Equations**

**Core move:** Systematically apply the principles of conservation of mass and charge to determine the stoichiometric coefficients in an unbalanced chemical equation.

**Seen in / context:**
- Balancing simple molecular equations by inspection.
Balancing redox reactions using oxidation states or half-reaction methods.
Balancing ionic equations in acidic or basic conditions.
Predicting products and then balancing the overall reaction.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assigning oxidation states in redox reactions.
Failing to conserve all elements (especially hydrogen and oxygen when water is involved).
Not balancing the total charge on both sides of an ionic equation.
Attempting to balance elements randomly without a systematic approach.
Not reducing coefficients to the smallest whole number ratio.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions can range from simple inspection balancing to complex redox balancing requiring calculation of oxidation states.
Ensure the final coefficients are the smallest whole numbers.
Can be applied to molecular, ionic, or redox equations.
Emphasize the step-by-step process of balancing each element.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q45_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q50_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires determining the stoichiometric coefficients in a given unbalanced chemical equation by applying the principles of conservation of atoms (mass) and charge. This directly matches the core move of systematically balancing chemical equations.

## **C_f20b2e1e. Calculating Enthalpy Change from Bond Energies**

**Core move:** Calculate the net energy change of a chemical reaction by summing the energies required to break reactant bonds and the energies released by forming product bonds.

**Seen in / context:**
- Estimating enthalpy changes for reactions where standard enthalpy of formation data is unavailable.
Understanding the energy profile of chemical reactions (endothermic vs. exothermic).
Comparing the relative stability of different molecules based on bond strengths.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing bonds broken (energy input, positive) with bonds formed (energy released, negative contribution, subtracted).
Incorrectly counting the number of each type of bond in molecules, especially in polyatomic or complex structures.
Failing to account for stoichiometric coefficients of reactants and products in the balanced chemical equation.
Using incorrect bond energy values from the table (e.g., C-O single bond instead of C=O double bond).
Sign error in the final calculation, leading to an incorrect exothermic/endothermic classification.

**Notes for generation:**
- Provide a balanced chemical equation for the reaction.
Ensure all necessary bond energies for the bonds present in the reactants and products are listed in the data table.
Include molecules with various bond types (single, double, triple) to test careful bond identification and counting.
The bond energies provided are typically average bond enthalpies, so the calculated value is an estimate.
Units for energy change should be clearly specified (e.g., kJ mol⁻¹).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q55_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_fc361c48. Analyzing Physico-Chemical Influences on Biological Rates**

**Core move:** Predict the impact of changes in physical or chemical environmental parameters on the rates or efficiencies of biological processes.

**Seen in / context:**
- Effects of temperature, pH, solute concentration, and gas partial pressures on enzyme activity or cellular processes.
Influence of water potential, vapor pressure, and osmosis on osmoregulation, fluid balance, and transpiration.
Role of specific chemical molecules (e.g., hormones, inhibitors, nutrients) in modulating biological pathways or physiological responses.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Ignoring the directionality or magnitude of gradients (e.g., water potential, concentration).
Confusing the effect of a factor on a process with its effect on an organism's overall survival or fitness.
Overlooking the principle of Le Chatelier's principle or steady-state dynamics in biological systems.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often require integrating concepts from physical chemistry, biochemistry, and biology.
Answers typically involve identifying directional changes (increase, decrease, stay the same) based on cause-and-effect relationships.
The 'chemical' aspect can stem from the nature of the substance involved (e.g., a protein, water) or the physical chemistry of the environment (e.g., humidity, concentration).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q65_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **C_fcce3d6d. Calculate Empirical Formula from Mass Composition**

**Core move:** Determine the simplest whole-number ratio of atoms in a compound by converting elemental masses to moles and finding their ratio.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining empirical and molecular formulas from analytical data
Stoichiometry calculations involving compound composition
Analysis of unknown inorganic or organic compounds

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to calculate the mass of the second element (e.g., oxygen from total compound mass)
Using atomic numbers instead of relative atomic masses (Ar) for mole calculations
Failing to divide by the smallest number of moles to find the ratio
Incorrectly rounding mole ratios before ensuring they are whole numbers (e.g., mistaking 1.5 for 1 or 2 instead of multiplying by 2)
Not multiplying fractional ratios by an integer to achieve whole numbers

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary atomic masses (Ar values) are provided.
The input masses should lead to simple, small whole-number ratios for the empirical formula.
Can be extended to finding molecular formulas if the compound's molar mass is also given.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q41_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q43_NSAA_Sec`: The question provides the total mass of a compound and the mass of one element within it, along with atomic masses. The core task is to determine the simplest whole-number ratio of atoms (empirical formula) by calculating the moles of each element and finding their ratio, which perfectly aligns with the 'Calculate Empirical Formula from Mass Composition' schema.

## **C_fed9c0c1. Kinetic Energy Distribution in Recoil**

**Core move:** Apply conservation of momentum and the definition of kinetic energy to distribute total decay energy among recoil products based on their masses.

**Seen in / context:**
- Nuclear decay (e.g., alpha emission, fission)
Two-body recoil problems in classical mechanics (e.g., gun recoil, explosion of an object into two pieces)
Situations where an initially stationary object splits into two parts

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming kinetic energy is distributed equally among products.
Assuming velocities are equal for the products.
Incorrectly applying the inverse relationship between kinetic energy and mass (for a given momentum, lighter particle has more KE).
Using the ratio of masses of the products (M1/M2) directly multiplied by the total energy (E), rather than the ratio of the mass of the *other* product to the *total* product mass (M2/(M1+M2)). This leads to a partial energy being greater than the total energy.
Confusing which particle's mass belongs in the numerator vs. denominator when calculating the fractional share of kinetic energy.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the initial state (stationary) to establish zero initial momentum.
Ensure the problem clearly states that the given energy is the *total* kinetic energy produced.
Mass numbers can be used as proxies for actual masses for ratio calculations.
The sum of the individual kinetic energies of the products must equal the total energy.
The kinetic energy of a product is given by E * (mass_of_other_product) / (sum_of_product_masses).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q35_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_0125439b. Percentage Change in Substance Flow**

**Core move:** Determine the percentage change in the rate of a specific substance flowing through a system by first calculating its initial and final flow rates from given concentrations and volume flow rates.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analysis of substance transport in biological systems (e.g., nutrient absorption, waste excretion).
Material balance calculations in chemical processes.
Environmental studies involving pollutant dispersion or concentration changes in water/air bodies.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Mistaking volume flow rate for the substance's mass/molar flow rate.
Failing to account for concentration changes when calculating the substance's flow rate.
Incorrectly calculating the percentage change (e.g., dividing by the final quantity instead of the initial, or subtracting in the wrong order).
Confusing reabsorption (removal of substance from flow) with an increase in concentration due to water removal without considering the total flow.

**Notes for generation:**
- Problems often involve a biological or chemical context that requires additional domain-specific knowledge (e.g., active transport mechanism for ions).
Emphasize the understanding that the total amount of substance flowing per unit time is the product of its concentration and the volume flow rate.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q55_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_03af253d. Classifying Interspecies Ecological Relationships**

**Core move:** Classify the type of ecological relationship between organisms (e.g., mutualism, parasitism, producer-consumer) based on their described interactions and the resulting effects on each organism.

**Seen in / context:**
- Ecology questions involving food webs or specific species interactions
Analyzing descriptions of symbiotic relationships
Understanding roles in an ecosystem (producer, consumer, decomposer)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the definitions of different ecological relationships (e.g., mutualism vs. commensalism, parasitism vs. predation)
Misinterpreting the net effect of the interaction on one or more organisms
Failing to identify all interacting parties and their roles (e.g., ignoring the impact on the tree)

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the description clearly outlines the benefits or harms to each interacting organism.
Include options that test understanding of key ecological terms (e.g., primary producer, parasite, mutualist).
Avoid ambiguity in the description of the interactions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q61_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_048feceb. Calculating Time in Electrolysis**

**Core move:** Determine the duration of an electrochemical process by interconverting mass, moles, charge, and current using Faraday's laws of electrolysis and fundamental electrical relationships.

**Seen in / context:**
- Electroplating calculations (mass deposited/removed, time, current)
Determining current required for a specific rate of reaction
Calculating the amount of substance produced/consumed given charge or time

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly determining the number of electrons transferred per atom/mole of substance
Using the wrong oxidation state for the metal ion
Confusing Faraday's constant with the charge of a single electron
Incorrect unit conversions (e.g., coulombs to seconds, minutes to seconds)
Not using the correct molar mass (e.g., using the formula mass of the compound instead of the element being deposited)

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide atomic masses and Faraday's constant (or allow its derivation).
Clearly specify the substance being deposited or reacted and its initial/final oxidation states.
Vary the unknown quantity (mass, current, time).
Ensure units for current (A), charge (C), and time (s) are consistent before final conversion.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_0686c68f. Solving Exponential Equations by Equating Exponents**

**Core move:** Transform an exponential equation by expressing all terms with a common base, then equate the exponents to form an algebraic equation and solve for the unknown.

**Seen in / context:**
- Equations involving powers of integers or rational numbers.
Problems requiring application of exponent rules (e.g., (a^m)^n = a^(mn), a^m * a^n = a^(m+n), a^m / a^n = a^(m-n), a^(-n) = 1/a^n).
Equations where variables primarily appear in the exponents.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying exponent rules.
Failing to identify the common base or attempting to use an incorrect common base.
Making algebraic errors when solving the linear or polynomial equation derived from equating exponents.
Not recognizing that 1/a^n = a^(-n) or other reciprocal forms.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure that the initial equation is truly transformable into base^A = base^B. If there are terms where the variable is the base (e.g., x^k), it's a different type of problem unless x is a specific power of the common base.
Common bases are usually small prime numbers (2, 3, 5, 7) or simple composites (10).
Questions can involve fractional or negative exponents, adding complexity to the algebraic manipulation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q9_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q81_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires solving an exponential equation by recognizing that it can be transformed into a quadratic equation through substitution of a common base raised to the power of the unknown variable (e.g., let y = 5^x, turning 2 * 5^(2x) - 5^x - 3 = 0 into 2y^2 - y - 3 = 0). This constitutes forming an algebraic equation and solving for the unknown, which aligns with the core move of transforming an exponential equation by expressing terms with a common base to form an algebraic equation, even if it doesn't always directly involve 'equating exponents' in the final step but rather solving a derived polynomial.

## **M_0730f376. Hydrostatic Pressure in Layered Liquids**

**Core move:** Calculate the height of each liquid layer using volume and cross-sectional area, determine their relative order based on density, and then apply the hydrostatic pressure formula P = ρgh to find the pressure at a specified depth.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving immiscible liquids forming distinct layers in containers.
Calculating pressure at various depths in a liquid column where fluid properties change with height.
Engineering applications in fluid storage and civil engineering.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to convert units (e.g., cm to m, g/cm³ to kg/m³ or vice-versa) consistently.
Using the total height of all liquids instead of the height of the liquid(s) above the specific point of interest.
Incorrectly summing pressure contributions from different layers or overlooking a layer.
Misinterpreting which liquid settles at the bottom when densities are provided, leading to an incorrect layer order.
Neglecting to use the provided gravitational field strength or using an incorrect standard value.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary physical parameters (liquid densities, volumes, container dimensions, gravitational acceleration) are available or can be derived.
For layered liquids, pressure at a point is the sum of pressures due to all liquid columns above that point.
The cross-sectional area of the container is crucial for determining liquid heights from given volumes.
The formula P = ρgh gives gauge pressure; atmospheric pressure needs to be added for absolute pressure if requested.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q80_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_0901b105. Deducing Oxidation States from Redox Stoichiometry**

**Core move:** Determine the change in oxidation state of an element in a redox reaction by quantitatively analyzing the stoichiometric ratio of reactants and the known oxidation state changes of other species.

**Seen in / context:**
- Titration experiments involving redox reactions
Quantitative analysis of metal ion concentrations
Balancing complex redox equations
Determining the fate of transition metal ions in reactions

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating moles of reactants
Failing to correctly identify the oxidation state of known species (e.g., oxalate, sulfate)
Miscalculating the number of electrons transferred by the known redox species
Incorrectly applying the stoichiometric ratio to the electron transfer
Confusing the initial oxidation state with the final oxidation state without considering electron gain/loss

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary quantitative information (volumes, concentrations) is provided.
Problems should typically involve one known redox species whose electron transfer can be easily determined.
Focus on elements that commonly undergo changes in oxidation state (e.g., transition metals, non-metals in polyatomic ions).
Calculations should ideally lead to clear integer changes in oxidation states.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q34_NSAA_Sec`: The core of this problem involves using the stoichiometric data from the reaction of lutetium with oxygen to first deduce the oxidation state of lutetium. This oxidation state is then used to write the correct chemical formula for lutetium sulfate and subsequently perform mass-to-mass conversions for the hydrated salt, directly matching the 'Deducing Oxidation States from Redox Stoichiometry' core move.

## **M_09226618. Classifying Biological Processes from Multi-Attribute Data**

**Core move:** Deduce the identity of biological processes by synthesizing multiple descriptive attributes and applying relevant domain knowledge.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing experimental results to identify cell types or biological molecules.
Categorizing diseases based on symptoms and causal factors.
Matching physiological responses to stimuli based on characteristics.
Identifying organisms based on morphological and behavioral traits.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Focusing on only one or two attributes instead of all relevant ones.
Misinterpreting the meaning of attributes due to insufficient domain knowledge.
Confusing correlation with causation when interpreting attributes.
Failing to systematically compare options against derived characteristics.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide a clear table or list of attributes.
The options for classification should be distinct and well-defined biological concepts.
The problem should require integrating several pieces of information for a correct deduction.
Ensure that the attributes provided are unambiguous and directly lead to one unique classification when combined with correct biological knowledge.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q78_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_093ce2ff. Applying Kinematic Equations for Constant Acceleration**

**Core move:** Select and apply the appropriate kinematic equation (SUVAT) to solve for an unknown variable in a scenario involving constant acceleration.

**Seen in / context:**
- Free-fall problems (on Earth or other celestial bodies).
Motion along a straight line with constant acceleration (e.g., cars accelerating/decelerating).
Analyzing vertical or horizontal components of projectile motion separately.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Using the wrong sign for acceleration (e.g., positive 'g' when moving against gravity).
Confusing initial and final velocities.
Incorrectly choosing a kinematic equation (e.g., using one that requires time when time is not known).
Forgetting that final velocity is zero at maximum height in vertical motion.
Including irrelevant information (like mass in this case) in the calculation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all known variables are clearly identified with their correct signs and units.
Emphasize understanding the physical situation (e.g., what happens at maximum height or when an object stops).
Questions can vary by providing different combinations of three knowns to find a fourth unknown, requiring selection of the appropriate equation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q84_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: The problem describes objects moving at constant speeds, which is a specific case of constant acceleration where acceleration is zero. The solution requires applying the fundamental kinematic relationship 'distance = speed × time' (d=vt), which is the appropriate kinematic equation (derived from SUVAT equations when a=0) to solve for an unknown variable (Sam's covered distance) within this constant acceleration scenario. The core move of selecting and applying the appropriate kinematic equation is central to solving the problem.

## **M_0a37d879. Integrating Spatially Varying Properties**

**Core move:** Calculate a total physical quantity by integrating a differential element that is defined by a spatially varying property or density, over the relevant dimension.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating total mass from a variable density function (linear, surface, or volume density).
Determining total charge from a variable charge density.
Finding total resistance from a material with variable resistivity along its length.
Computing total force or pressure from a varying pressure distribution over an area.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming the property/density is constant and using a simple multiplication formula (e.g., R = ρL/A directly without integration).
Incorrectly setting up the differential element, such as using the wrong variable for integration or incorrect units.
Errors in performing the definite integral, including incorrect antiderivatives or improper evaluation at the limits.
Confusing constants with variables that change over the integration dimension.

**Notes for generation:**
- The problem typically provides the functional form of how the property varies with position (e.g., ρ(x), σ(r)).
Identify the appropriate differential element (e.g., dR, dM, dQ, dF) and its relationship to the varying property and the differential length/area/volume.
Ensure the integration variable and limits correctly correspond to the physical setup of the problem.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q0_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_0adb94e8. Solving Quadratic Roots for Algebraic Expressions**

**Core move:** Apply the quadratic formula to find the roots of a quadratic equation, differentiate between the roots based on a given condition, and substitute them into a target algebraic expression to evaluate its value.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems requiring the exact numerical values of quadratic roots.
Questions combining equation solving with algebraic evaluation and simplification.
Scenarios where roots are irrational or involve radicals.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Errors in applying the quadratic formula (e.g., sign errors in discriminant or numerator).
Incorrectly assigning 'p' and 'q' based on an inequality condition (e.g., p > q).
Algebraic errors during the substitution and simplification of the final expression.
Attempting to use Vieta's formulas when direct root calculation and substitution is simpler or necessary for the specific expression.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the quadratic equation is in standard form ax^2 + bx + c = 0 before applying the formula.
Emphasize careful simplification of square roots and fractions.
The complexity of the target algebraic expression (e.g., Ap + Bq) can be varied.
Always check the inequality condition for distinguishing between roots.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q8_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_0aff7927. Solving Trigonometric Equations with Identities**

**Core move:** Apply trigonometric identities and algebraic manipulation to solve equations involving trigonometric functions for specific variable values.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding angles or trigonometric ratios that satisfy a given trigonometric relationship.
Simplifying complex trigonometric expressions.
Problems involving transformations between different trigonometric functions (e.g., tan, sin, cos).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying trigonometric identities or algebraic rules.
Dividing by a variable expression without considering cases where it might be zero, which could lead to loss of solutions or undefined terms.
Forgetting the periodicity of trigonometric functions when general solutions are required.
Failing to convert all terms to a single trigonometric function or its related inverse function.
Errors in solving the resulting algebraic equation (e.g., quadratic formula, factorization).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often require the use of fundamental identities such as `sin^2(x) + cos^2(x) = 1`, `tan(x) = sin(x)/cos(x)`, etc.
The equation frequently needs to be transformed into a polynomial equation (often quadratic) in terms of a single trigonometric function (e.g., `cos(x)` or `tan(x)`).
Solutions obtained from the algebraic equation must be checked for validity within the original trigonometric domain and definition (e.g., `cos(x)` must be between -1 and 1).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q81_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q75_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires solving two distinct trigonometric equations (sin(x) = 1/2 and cos(2x) = 1/2), determining common solutions ('k'), and then finding all solutions within a specified range, which directly aligns with the core move of applying trigonometric manipulation to solve equations involving trigonometric functions.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q77_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires using trigonometric identities (tan x = sin x / cos x, sin^2 x + cos^2 x = 1) and algebraic manipulation to transform the given equation into a solvable form (a quadratic in terms of cos x), then solving for cos x and subsequently finding the possible values of tan x. This directly matches the core move of applying trigonometric identities and algebraic manipulation to solve trigonometric equations.

## **M_0cc81c22. Probability of Independent Events and Complements**

**Core move:** Calculate the probability of multiple independent events occurring simultaneously or sequentially, often using the complement rule to simplify 'at least one' scenarios.

**Seen in / context:**
- Dice rolls or coin flips over multiple trials
Drawing items with replacement from a fixed set
Success/failure rates in a series of independent trials
Problems involving 'at least one' outcome

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly adding probabilities for independent events instead of multiplying them.
Forgetting to use the complement rule and directly calculating all 'at least one' cases, leading to complex enumeration.
Confusing independent events with mutually exclusive events.
Miscalculating the probability of a single simple event (e.g., P(even) on a die).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often involve 'at least one' which is a strong indicator for the complement rule.
Ensure all events considered are truly independent.
The 'success' and 'failure' probabilities for each individual trial should be clearly defined and calculated.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q16_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks for the probability of an individual possessing two characteristics simultaneously: being male and being unaffected. In genetics problems, the probability of being male (typically 1/2) is generally independent of the probability of inheriting an autosomal trait (unaffected status). The solution involves calculating the probability of being male and the probability of being unaffected (derived from the pedigree, which is a biology step), and then multiplying these two independent probabilities, which directly aligns with the core move of calculating the probability of multiple independent events occurring simultaneously.

## **M_0eae8ac9. Stoichiometric Calculation for pH Adjustment**

**Core move:** Apply concentration definitions, pH relationships, and reaction stoichiometry to calculate the amount of a substance needed to adjust a solution's pH to a target range.

**Seen in / context:**
- Acid-base titrations and neutralizations
Buffer solutions and their preparation
Environmental chemistry problems involving pH control
Pharmaceutical formulations requiring specific pH ranges

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly converting between pH and [H+] concentration
Failing to account for the stoichiometry of the reaction (e.g., 1:1, 1:2 ratios)
Using initial volume for final concentration calculation, or vice-versa, without considering volume changes (if significant)
Not properly calculating the moles of acid/base *remaining* after partial neutralization, or moles *to be neutralized*
Ignoring the difference between initial moles and moles to be neutralized to reach the target pH.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all units are consistent (e.g., cm³ to dm³ for concentration calculations).
Pay attention to the significant figures implied by the given data and pH values.
The target pH often represents a *range*, requiring calculations for both ends of the range to find an appropriate answer choice.
Consider if the added substance significantly changes the total volume; for small additions, this is often negligible.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q35_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q33_NSAA_Sec`: The question presents initial concentration and pH values for an acid, followed by a dilution step, all in preparation for disposal. This directly aligns with the core move of applying concentration definitions, pH relationships, and subsequent reaction stoichiometry to adjust the solution's pH to a target range (implied for safe disposal).

## **M_10456965. Integrating Linear Kinematics with Rotational Motion**

**Core move:** Determine a rotational parameter (e.g., angle, angular velocity) by first calculating the time taken from an independent linear motion described by constant acceleration kinematics.

**Seen in / context:**
- Objects falling onto or interacting with rotating platforms/discs/gears.
Timing events where a linear process triggers or is synchronized with a rotational outcome.
Problems requiring the conversion between linear time and angular displacement.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to correctly calculate the time of the linear event using appropriate kinematic equations.
Incorrectly converting rotational frequency (revolutions s⁻¹) to angular speed in desired units (degrees s⁻¹ or radians s⁻¹).
Mixing units (e.g., using radians for an answer required in degrees, or vice versa) without proper conversion factors.
Assuming the rotational object stops or changes speed without information to support it.

**Notes for generation:**
- Highlight the two distinct phases: linear motion (often constant acceleration) to find time, and rotational motion using that time.
Emphasize unit consistency and the necessity of converting between revolutions, degrees, and radians as required by the question.
Ensure all necessary parameters for both linear kinematics (h, g, u) and rotational motion (f) are provided or can be inferred.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_106950e8. Problem Identification and Domain Mapping**

**Core move:** Identify the core mathematical or physical domain of a problem statement to prepare for method selection, even if no explicit problem is given.

**Seen in / context:**
- Initial scan of exam papers
Categorizing unknown problems
Determining the scope of a question within a multi-disciplinary section

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming a problem exists when only header information is provided
Misinterpreting context information as part of a mathematical problem
Failing to recognize that specific numerical or textual information for a question is missing

**Notes for generation:**
- This schema applies when the input is context or meta-information rather than a solvable problem.
It emphasizes the preparatory step of understanding what *kind* of problem is presented (or should be presented).
In this specific instance, the 'question' provided is actually a section header and page number, not a mathematical problem.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q71_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_11082a0a. Understanding Exam Paper Conventions**

**Core move:** Recognize and correctly interpret non-problem elements of an exam paper, such as instructions, structural markers, or placeholders, to effectively navigate the examination.

**Seen in / context:**
- General instructions at the start of a paper.
Pages explicitly marked for rough working or notes.
Copyright notices, page numbers, and exam codes.
Section headings or advisories regarding question types.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Mistaking non-problem pages for actual questions requiring a mathematical solution.
Ignoring crucial instructions about timing, answering format, or forbidden items.
Wasting time on blank or instructional pages instead of proceeding to substantive questions.

**Notes for generation:**
- The focus is on meta-cognition regarding the exam format and rules, not content-specific problem-solving.
Questions often test comprehension of instructions or general awareness of exam structure.
Can involve identifying the purpose or implication of specific pages or sections within the paper.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_130d60b7. Calculating Potential Difference using Resistivity**

**Core move:** Apply the definition of resistance based on resistivity and dimensions, followed by Ohm's Law, to determine the potential difference across a conductor.

**Seen in / context:**
- Basic circuit analysis problems involving uniform wires or resistive elements.
Determining voltage drop across a component given its physical properties and current.
Calculations in material science related to electrical conductivity.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing resistivity (ρ) with resistance (R).
Incorrectly applying units or failing to convert to SI units (e.g., using mm instead of m, or cm² instead of m²).
Forgetting or incorrectly recalling the formula for resistance (R = ρL/A) or Ohm's Law (V = IR).
Swapping variables in the formulas (e.g., using V = I/R or R = A/ρL).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all given quantities are in consistent SI units before performing calculations.
Questions can vary by asking for R, ρ, L, A, I, or V, requiring rearrangement of the formulas.
The conductor is typically assumed to have uniform cross-sectional area and resistivity.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q78_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_1318b323. Calculating Allele Counts from Population Data**

**Core move:** Calculate the total number of dominant and recessive alleles within a population by first determining the absolute counts of each genotype from given phenotypic or genotypic proportions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Population genetics problems involving Mendelian traits.
Questions requiring quantification of genetic traits within a defined population.
Preliminary step for Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium calculations.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the number of individuals with a specific genotype with the number of alleles they contribute.
Forgetting that each individual carries two alleles for a diploid gene.
Miscalculating the number of heterozygous individuals from given homozygous dominant and recessive counts/percentages.
Incorrectly converting percentages to absolute numbers of individuals.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all genotype categories (homozygous dominant, heterozygous, homozygous recessive) are accounted for.
Emphasize the step-by-step process: percentages -> individual counts -> allele counts.
