The total number of alleles for a given gene in a population will always be twice the number of individuals.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q44_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_132df403. Deducing Resultant Force from Kinematics**

**Core move:** Apply Newton's Second Law (F=ma) to deduce the resultant force on an object based on its described kinematic state (e.g., constant velocity, at rest, or accelerating).

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems where an object moves at a constant speed in a straight line.
Scenarios where an object is at rest or in equilibrium.
Calculations involving net force given acceleration.
Conceptual questions about force and motion relationships.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming a non-zero resultant force simply because multiple forces (e.g., thrust, drag, weight, lift) are acting.
Confusing individual forces acting on an object with the resultant (net) force.
Misinterpreting 'constant speed in a straight line' as implying a non-zero resultant force.
Failing to apply the direct consequence of zero acceleration (a=0) to Newton's Second Law (F=ma).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly states or implies the kinematic state (velocity/acceleration).
Focus on the direct relationship between acceleration and resultant force.
Can include scenarios where resultant force is non-zero due to acceleration for broader application.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q76_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires the application of Newton's Second Law (F=ma) to determine the acceleration of the ball based on the given forces (part b). Furthermore, part c leverages the concept of terminal velocity, which is a specific kinematic state (zero acceleration/constant velocity), to deduce the relationship between the forces and solve for speed, directly aligning with the core move of deducing resultant force or acceleration from a described kinematic state.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: The question describes a mass in an accelerating lift. To determine the force exerted by the spring (and thus its extension), one must first deduce the resultant force acting on the mass based on its acceleration (kinematic state) using Newton's Second Law, which perfectly matches the core move of this schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the acceleration from a given velocity-time relationship (kinematics) and then applying Newton's Second Law (F=ma) with the given resultant force to determine the object's mass. This directly aligns with the core move of applying F=ma based on the object's kinematic state.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying Newton's Second Law (F=ma) to analyze the forces on a mass in an accelerating lift. This allows for the deduction of the effective force exerted by the spring or the change in force due to acceleration, directly matching the core move of determining resultant forces based on kinematic state.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q12_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires deducing the resultant force on the particle at a specific time. This involves first using the given kinetic energy function (E=kt) to derive the particle's velocity and then acceleration as a function of time. Subsequently, Newton's Second Law (F=ma) is applied to determine the resultant force. This sequence of steps directly aligns with the core move of applying F=ma based on the object's kinematic state (acceleration derived from time-dependent energy).

## **M_132f5a8d. Solving Parameters via Intersecting Functions and Root Properties**

**Core move:** Establish a combined algebraic equation from intersecting functions, identify its roots as characteristic points, and utilize properties of these roots (e.g., Vieta's formulas) along with given relationships to determine unknown function parameters.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding intersection points of parabolas and lines.
Problems involving the distance or difference between x-coordinates of intersection.
Determining coefficients of quadratic equations based on properties of their roots.
Analyzing the number of intersection points (discriminant usage could be related, though not central to this specific problem).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly setting up the quadratic equation for intersection.
Errors in applying Vieta's formulas (e.g., sign errors for sum of roots).
Algebraic mistakes when solving the system of equations for the roots and the unknown parameter.
Forgetting that the roots of the combined equation are the x-coordinates of the intersection points.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the combined equation is in standard form (e.g., ax^2 + bx + c' = 0) before applying Vieta's formulas.
The given relationships between roots can involve sum, product, difference, or other algebraic expressions.
The parameter to be found can be any coefficient of the quadratic or linear equation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q11_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_13b28059. Identifying Insufficient Information**

**Core move:** Determine if the provided problem statement contains all necessary information to derive a solution.

**Seen in / context:**
- Mathematical problems with omitted variables or parameters
Physics problems lacking initial conditions
Chemistry problems with missing reagent quantities or reaction conditions
Any problem where critical explicit questions are absent

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming values for missing information
Attempting to solve an under-defined problem
Misinterpreting a lack of information as a subtle hint about a property (e.g., assuming a value is zero if not given)
Not explicitly stating the problem's incompleteness

**Notes for generation:**
- This pattern applies when the problem text itself is demonstrably incomplete.
The goal is to articulate what information is missing, not to guess it.
Questions generated for this schema would explicitly present an incomplete problem and implicitly ask the solver to identify the missing pieces.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q37_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q50_NSAA_Sec`: The problem asks for the maximum mass of substance X transferred into the aqueous layer at equilibrium. To calculate the equilibrium distribution of a solute between two immiscible solvents, the partition coefficient (distribution coefficient) for the solute between those solvents, or the solubility limits of the solute in each solvent, must be known. None of these critical pieces of information are provided in the problem statement, rendering it unsolvable without additional data.

## **M_145e2e97. Analyzing Kinematic Extremes in Dynamic Systems**

**Core move:** Employ conservation laws (energy, momentum) or dynamic equations (Newton's Laws) to determine extreme kinematic values (e.g., maximum speed, minimum displacement, zero velocity) at specific points within a system experiencing non-constant forces.

**Seen in / context:**
- Bungee jumping or objects attached to springs (elastic forces)
Projectile motion where drag force varies with velocity
Vertical motion under gravity where other forces (e.g., fluid resistance, tension) are present and variable
Systems involving oscillatory motion

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming constant acceleration throughout the motion when forces are variable.
Failing to identify all forces acting on the object at different points in its trajectory.
Incorrectly applying conservation of energy, e.g., neglecting elastic potential energy or work done by non-conservative forces.
Confusing the point of maximum displacement (where velocity is zero) with the point of maximum speed (where net force/acceleration is zero).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should involve identifying specific critical points in motion (e.g., highest point, lowest point, point of maximum speed).
The forces acting on the object should either be variable or lead to a non-constant acceleration.
Solutions often involve setting up energy conservation equations or applying Newton's second law (F=ma) and then integrating/differentiating to find kinematic quantities.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks to determine the 'maximum speed' of the lorry, which is an extreme kinematic value. The solution involves applying dynamic equations (P=Fv and Newton's Second Law for zero acceleration at maximum speed) to a system with a non-constant resistive force (proportional to speed squared), directly aligning with the core move of analyzing kinematic extremes in dynamic systems.

## **M_14c2bff5. Calculating Overall Average Rate from Segment Data**

**Core move:** Compute the overall average rate for a multi-segment journey or process by summing the total quantity (e.g., distance, production units) and dividing by the total duration (e.g., time), after deriving the duration for each segment using its specific rate and quantity.

**Seen in / context:**
- Multi-stage journeys with varying speeds or rates
Calculating average production rates over different periods or stages
Determining overall efficiency from individual process steps
Problems involving average speed, average current, or average flow rate

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Averaging the individual rates directly (e.g., arithmetic mean of speeds) instead of using the total quantity/total duration formula, which is a common error in weighted averages.
Not converting units consistently across all segments (e.g., mixing meters and kilometers, or hours and minutes/seconds).
Failing to recognize that 'several complete repetitions' does not change the average rate of a single repetition.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the fundamental principle: Average Rate = Total Quantity / Total Duration.
Ensure all units (distance/quantity, time, rate) are consistent before performing calculations.
Questions often require unit conversions, as segment data might be given in different units (e.g., meters and km/h).
This pattern is distinct from calculating an *unknown segment rate* when the *overall average rate* is given.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q11_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_167e520d. Relating Regular Polygon Angles and Sides**

**Core move:** Employ the relationship between the number of sides and interior/exterior angles of regular polygons to solve for unknown properties or compare different polygons.

**Seen in / context:**
- Comparing interior/exterior angles of multiple regular polygons
Finding the number of sides given angle differences or ratios
Problems involving sums or differences of angles of regular polygons

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the interior or exterior angle formula
Assuming a linear relationship between the number of sides and the angle
Algebraic errors when solving the derived equations
Confusing interior and exterior angles

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often require setting up and solving algebraic equations based on the angle formulas.
The exterior angle formula (360/n) can simplify algebraic manipulation compared to the interior angle formula ((n-2)*180/n).
Ensure the number of sides calculated is an integer and at least 3.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_17867722. Identifying Reproductive Strategies in Organisms**

**Core move:** Classify organisms by associating them with their primary cellular mechanisms of reproduction.

**Seen in / context:**
- Introductory Biology
Cell Biology
Genetics and Reproduction
Diversity of Life

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing asexual reproduction methods (e.g., binary fission vs. mitosis)
Misunderstanding the role of mitosis in asexual vs. sexual reproduction
Incorrectly linking organisms to their primary reproductive processes (e.g., assuming all eukaryotes use meiosis for reproduction)
Lacking knowledge of specific organisms' reproductive modes

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide a list of organisms or scenarios for classification.
Clear definitions of reproductive processes (mitosis, meiosis, binary fission, budding, fragmentation) should be implicitly or explicitly tested.
Can be extended to include genetic variation implications of different reproductive modes.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q27_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_18372be2. Applying Mendelian Ratios in Statistical Tests**

**Core move:** Calculate expected phenotypic frequencies from a given genetic cross, then use these and observed frequencies to populate a statistical test formula.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chi-squared tests in genetics
Monohybrid or dihybrid crosses
Calculating expected counts for Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium tests

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly deducing parental genotypes from the initial description
Miscalculating expected phenotypic ratios (e.g., 1:1 instead of 3:1)
Incorrectly assigning observed values to the correct phenotype category
Errors in applying the statistical formula (e.g., (O-E)/E instead of (O-E)^2/E)
Mixing up observed and expected values in the formula

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question provides enough information to deduce parental genotypes.
The statistical formula will typically be provided, so the focus is on deriving the input values.
Often involves a 'single gene with dominant and recessive alleles' scenario.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_19d9b071. Identifying Null Problem Statements**

**Core move:** Determine if a given problem text contains sufficient mathematical content to pose a solvable question, or if it is effectively empty or administrative in nature.

**Seen in / context:**
- Exam papers with blank or administrative sections
Automated problem parsing systems
Initial review of question sets

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Attempting to interpret administrative text (like page numbers or copyrights) as mathematical data
Assuming implicit mathematical problems when no explicit one exists
Searching for hidden patterns in blank spaces

**Notes for generation:**
- This schema applies when the *absence* of a clear problem statement is the primary feature.
Questions designed to invoke this pattern would deliberately contain minimal to no mathematical content, or purely administrative text.
It's about problem validation rather than problem-solving.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q3_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is an administrative content listing from an exam paper and does not contain an explicit problem statement or a solvable mathematical question. This matches the core move of determining if the problem text is sufficient to pose a solvable question or if it is administrative in nature.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q60_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is clearly administrative instructions for an exam, not a mathematical problem. It lacks any mathematical content or an explicit question to be solved, directly matching the core move of identifying null or administrative problem statements.

## **M_19f7cfc3. Interconverting Moles, Mass, Volume, and Density**

**Core move:** Calculate an unknown quantity (moles, mass, volume, or density) by sequentially applying the relationships between them using molar mass and known values.

**Seen in / context:**
- Stoichiometric calculations in chemistry
Determining concentrations of solutions
Characterizing physical properties of substances (e.g., specific gravity)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Using incorrect units or unit conversions for density, volume, or molar mass
Confusing mass with moles or volume (e.g., using volume where mass is required for density calculation)
Incorrectly calculating molar mass from the chemical formula
Not rearranging formulas correctly (e.g., density = mass/volume vs. volume = mass/density)
Misinterpreting given values (e.g., total solution volume vs. solute volume)

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure unit consistency throughout the calculation process.
Calculations often involve multiple steps and formula rearrangements.
May require prior calculation of molar mass from the chemical formula.
Carefully distinguish between solute and solvent quantities when dealing with solutions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q32_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the moles of the initial monomer from its given volume, then converting these moles to mass. This mass then represents the total mass of plastic, which is conserved for the new biodegradable cups. Finally, the same mass is used to determine the required mass of the new monomer, effectively interconverting between volume, moles, and mass using molar mass and molar volume in sequence.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires identifying the reactant, writing a balanced chemical equation, calculating the moles of the initial reactant, using stoichiometry to determine the moles of gaseous products (mass lost), and finally converting these moles back into mass using molar mass. This directly aligns with the core move of interconverting moles and mass using molar mass and stoichiometry to calculate an unknown quantity.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q37_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the mass of taurine added from a magnesium taurate solution by sequentially applying relationships: first, determining moles from given volume and concentration; then, using stoichiometry to find moles of taurine; and finally, converting these moles to mass using the given molar mass (Mr). This directly aligns with the core move of interconverting moles, mass, volume, and concentration.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q46_NSAA_Sec`: The core mathematical task in this question is to calculate the mass of enzyme from its given volume and density, which requires interconverting these quantities and performing unit conversion. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of calculating an unknown quantity (mass) by applying relationships between volume and density.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q32_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires writing a balanced chemical equation, using volume ratios to infer mole ratios (due to the ideal gas assumption), calculating molar masses, and then converting moles of reactants to their respective masses to determine a mass ratio. This is a sequential application of relationships between moles, mass, and volume using molar mass and known values (stoichiometry, composition by volume).
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q35_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires calculating the mass of iron within sodium feredetate trihydrate using its molar mass and then relating this mass to a given volume of solution. The final step involves scaling this relationship to find the volume corresponding to a different target mass of iron, which aligns directly with calculating unknown quantities by applying relationships between mass, volume, and molar mass.

## **M_1b77e338. Configuring Power Sources for a Load**

**Core move:** Determine the optimal series and parallel arrangement of identical power sources, each with internal resistance, to deliver specific voltage and power to a given load while utilizing all available components.

**Seen in / context:**
- Designing solar panel arrays
Configuring battery packs for desired voltage and current output
Matching power source characteristics to a specific electrical load

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the equivalent voltage or resistance for series/parallel combinations of sources with internal resistance.
Failing to ensure all given components are used in the final configuration.
Neglecting to consider both voltage and power requirements for the load simultaneously.
Confusing the total current supplied by the array with the current flowing through an individual cell or branch.

**Notes for generation:**
- The problem often involves a system of equations relating the number of series (N) and parallel (n) components, total available components (N*n = total), and the load characteristics.
It typically requires calculating the load resistance first from the given power and voltage.
The solution usually involves finding integer values for N and n.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q11_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_1b7947b2. Analyzing Forces on Inclined Planes with Friction**

**Core move:** Apply Newton's Laws and force resolution to determine unknown forces, friction, or acceleration for objects on rough inclined planes.

**Seen in / context:**
- Objects moving or at rest on inclined surfaces.
Scenarios involving kinetic or static friction.
Problems requiring the calculation of a coefficient of friction.
Situations where net force is zero (constant velocity) or non-zero (constant acceleration).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly resolving weight components (e.g., swapping sin and cos for parallel/perpendicular to the plane).
Misidentifying the direction of the friction force (e.g., always opposing motion vs. opposing tendency of motion).
Omitting significant forces such as normal reaction or friction.
Failing to distinguish between conditions for constant velocity (net force = 0) and constant acceleration (net force = ma).
Errors in algebraic manipulation when combining force equations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should involve at least two distinct phases of motion (e.g., initial constant velocity, then accelerated motion) to necessitate multiple applications of Newton's Laws.
Vary the angle of inclination, applied forces, and direction of motion.
Ensure the question clearly states whether the object is sliding or about to slide to determine the type of friction.
The gravitational field strength 'g' is typically left symbolic in the answer.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_1bf60554. Analyzing Circuit Behavior with a Variable Component**

**Core move:** Apply fundamental circuit laws (Ohm's Law, power equations) to calculate electrical quantities for a variable component across a specified range of values.

**Seen in / context:**
- Series or parallel circuits containing a variable resistor, thermistor, or LDR.
Problems requiring the generation of a table of results or characteristic curve data.
Investigating the relationship between a component's resistance and other circuit parameters (e.g., current, voltage, power).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to recalculate total circuit current for each new value of the variable component.
Incorrectly applying Ohm's Law or power equations (e.g., using source voltage instead of component voltage for power calculation).
Errors in combining series/parallel resistances.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the circuit diagram or description is clear, especially fixed resistance values and source voltage.
The task typically involves repetitive calculations suitable for a tabular presentation.
Often used to explore how a circuit parameter changes as a control variable (like resistance) is altered.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the total electrical energy transferred to a component, which is an electrical quantity. The current in the component is variable (I = kt^2), and the calculation involves integrating the power (P=VI) over a time interval (0 to TF), aligning with the core move of applying fundamental circuit laws and power equations for a variable component across a specified range of values.

## **M_1ce7a2f9. Translating Proportional Comparisons**

**Core move:** Convert verbal descriptions of multiplicative relationships between quantities (e.g., "X times higher than Y", "Z percent less than A") into accurate algebraic equations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Financial calculations involving percentage increases or decreases.
Comparing experimental results with factors or ratios.
Scaling quantities in physics or engineering problems.
Interpreting growth or decay rates in biological contexts.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting 'X times higher than Y' as 'X = Y * factor' instead of 'X = Y + (Y * factor)' or 'X = Y * (1 + factor)'.
Confusing the base quantity when calculating percentage changes or multiplicative factors.
Incorrectly applying 'percent increase' as a simple addition rather than a multiplier.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure clear phrasing of the comparative language (e.g., 'times higher/lower', 'percent more/less', 'fraction of').
The quantities being compared should be easily identifiable.
Can be a foundational step for multi-part problems involving quantitative analysis.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_1ef961ec. Deriving Tangent Equations for Circles**

**Core move:** Apply geometric properties of circles and lines, along with algebraic manipulation, to determine the equation of a tangent at a given point on a circle.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding the equation of a tangent line to a circle at a specific point.
Problems involving the perpendicular relationship between a radius and a tangent.
Calculations of gradients and equations of straight lines in coordinate geometry.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the gradient of the radius or tangent.
Forgetting or misapplying the negative reciprocal rule for perpendicular lines.
Algebraic errors when substituting values into the line equation (y - y1 = m(x - x1)) or rearranging it.
Confusing the equation of the circle with the equation of the tangent line.

**Notes for generation:**
- The centre of the circle may not always be at the origin, requiring a different radius gradient calculation.
The given point is assumed to be on the circle, but in some problems, this might need to be verified first.
The final equation of the tangent should be presented in a standard form (e.g., y=mx+c, Ax+By+C=0) unless otherwise specified.
Variations can include finding the point of tangency given the tangent's equation and the circle's equation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_233028cd. Analyzing Biological Experimental Setups**

**Core move:** Identify the aim, variables, controls, and expected outcomes of a biological experiment based on its description.

**Seen in / context:**
- Interpreting preliminary experimental descriptions in biology
Predicting biological observations based on given conditions
Identifying the components and purpose of an experimental setup

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing independent and dependent variables in the setup
Overlooking crucial control conditions (e.g., 'in the dark')
Misinterpreting the role of different solutions or cell types
Failing to connect the experimental conditions to underlying biological principles (e.g., osmosis, enzyme kinetics)

**Notes for generation:**
- The question provides a description of an experiment or an experimental setup and may not include results or a specific question yet.
Requires careful reading to extract all relevant conditions and components.
Often sets the stage for follow-up questions about data analysis, conclusions, or further experimentation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q77_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q74_NSAA_Sec`: The question describes a biological investigation, detailing the independent variable (protease concentration), the dependent variable (rate of protein breakdown), and explicitly stating that 'all other variables were kept constant' (controls). This aligns perfectly with the core move of identifying the aim, variables, and controls of a biological experiment from its description.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q74_NSAA_Sec`: This question describes a biological experiment, including its setup, variables, controls, and results (presented in a graph). To answer 'Which of the following statements is/are correct?', the student must identify and analyze the aim, independent/dependent variables, control variables, and interpret the experimental outcomes from the provided graph, which directly matches the core move of analyzing biological experimental setups.

## **M_236b636e. Multidisciplinary Analysis of Biological Transport Systems**

**Core move:** Quantify various parameters of a biological transport system, including flow velocity and structural volume, by performing necessary unit conversions and applying geometric formulas, and interpret how environmental factors influence its function.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems in biology that require calculations involving geometric shapes (e.g., cylinders, spheres).
Scenarios where fluid dynamics or transport rates in biological systems are analyzed.
Questions that integrate physics/maths concepts (units, geometry, kinematics) with biological principles (physiology, environmental responses).
Examinations testing a broad range of scientific and mathematical skills within a single question.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Inaccurate or incomplete unit conversions, especially across different orders of magnitude (e.g., cm to µm, m/hr to µm/s).
Errors in applying standard geometric formulas (e.g., volume of a cylinder).
Misidentifying relevant physical dimensions (e.g., using diameter instead of radius).
Incorrectly predicting the impact of environmental changes on biological processes (e.g., transpiration rate, enzyme activity).
Overlooking specific details or constraints given in the problem statement.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage systematic breakdown of the problem into distinct calculation and reasoning steps.
Stress the importance of checking units at every stage of calculation.
Questions may contain 'distractor' information or require careful selection of relevant values.
The biological component often requires recall of fundamental physiological processes and their environmental sensitivities.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q53_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_25887cc3. Deriving and Comparing Properties of Parallel Lines**

**Core move:** Apply the properties of parallel lines (equal gradients) to establish the equation of a new line and determine specific characteristic points such as intercepts.

**Seen in / context:**
- Coordinate geometry problems involving finding equations of lines.
Determining intercepts or other specific points of lines.
Analyzing relationships between multiple lines in a plane.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the gradient from the standard form of a linear equation.
Confusing the condition for parallel lines with that for perpendicular lines.
Arithmetic errors when substituting points or solving for intercepts.
Errors in applying the point-slope form or y=mx+c form for a new line.

**Notes for generation:**
- Vary the initial information provided for the lines (e.g., two points, point and gradient, intercept and gradient).
Can extend to finding x-intercepts or intersection points instead of y-intercepts.
Ensure calculations remain manageable without a calculator for test settings.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_2781e557. Stoichiometry of Redox Reactions with Half-Equations**

**Core move:** Calculate the required or produced amounts (mass, volume, moles) of reactants or products in a redox reaction by combining and balancing electron transfers from provided half-equations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Titration calculations involving redox reactions.
Determining limiting reagents in redox processes.
Calculating theoretical yields in redox synthesis.
Electrochemical cell stoichiometry calculations.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly balancing the total electron transfer between the oxidation and reduction half-equations.
Errors in stoichiometric mole conversions (e.g., miscalculating moles from mass/Mr, or volume from moles/concentration).
Ignoring one of the reaction steps if the overall process involves multiple half-equations (e.g., ethanol to aldehyde, then aldehyde to acid).
Using an incorrect mole ratio between the reactants due to an unbalanced overall redox equation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary quantitative information (Mr values, concentrations, masses) is provided.
Problems should clearly define the starting and ending compounds for multi-step redox processes.
Calculations typically involve multiple steps: initial mole calculation, balancing electrons between half-equations, determining the mole ratio, and final quantity calculation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q36_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q35_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the total volume of gaseous products from multiple redox reactions. This involves determining the stoichiometric ratios between reactants and products, which is derived from balancing the electron transfers in the redox half-reactions, and then applying these ratios to given masses to find the moles and ultimately the volume of gases, aligning perfectly with the core move of this schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q34_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires identifying the redox half-reactions occurring at the cathode and anode during different stages of electrolysis, calculating moles of substances (mass to moles, volume to moles), and using the stoichiometry of electron transfer (balancing electron transfers) to determine the amounts (volumes of gas) of products formed at each electrode.

## **M_2859f9c5. Calculating Young's Modulus from Material Properties**

**Core move:** Apply the definition of Young's Modulus, relating stress, strain, force, area, extension, and original length, to determine a material's elastic modulus.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analysis of tensile or compressive force-extension or stress-strain graphs.
Problems involving stretching wires, rods, or other materials under load.
Understanding and quantifying material stiffness and elastic behavior.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing stress with force or strain with extension.
Incorrectly identifying original length or cross-sectional area from diagrams or descriptions.
Using inconsistent units (e.g., mixing mm with m, or cm² with m²).
Misinterpreting the gradient of a Force-Extension graph (gradient is k, not E directly).
Applying the formula for Young's Modulus outside the material's elastic limit.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often provide a force-extension graph, requiring the gradient or specific points to be extracted.
Cross-sectional area might be given indirectly (e.g., diameter of a circular wire).
Stress (Force/Area) should be in Pascals (N/m²), Strain (Extension/Original Length) is dimensionless, and Young's Modulus is in Pascals (Pa).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_2b30f9b0. Calculating Total Probability of a Conditional Event**

**Core move:** Apply the Law of Total Probability by summing the products of initial event probabilities and their respective conditional probabilities to determine the overall likelihood of a subsequent event.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving sequential random selections where the outcome of the first selection influences the probabilities of the second (e.g., choosing a box then drawing an item).
Scenarios where an event can occur through several mutually exclusive preceding conditions.
As a component for the denominator in Bayes' Theorem calculations.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the probability of the initial events (e.g., probability of choosing a specific box).
Confusing conditional probability P(A|B) with the joint probability P(A and B).
Averaging the conditional probabilities without weighting them by the probability of the preceding condition.
Failing to consider all possible mutually exclusive paths leading to the desired outcome.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the initial events (conditions) are mutually exclusive and collectively exhaustive.
Questions can vary the mechanism for selecting the initial condition (e.g., coin toss, die roll, spinner, random selection).
Can be extended to problems requiring Bayes' Theorem by using this total probability as the denominator.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q15_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_2dd5c01c. Chemical Composition and Oxidation State Analysis**

**Core move:** Analyze chemical species to determine their empirical formulas from elemental composition and the oxidation states of constituent elements.

**Seen in / context:**
- Elemental analysis for organic compounds (determining empirical/molecular formula)
Stoichiometric calculations in chemical reactions
Assigning oxidation states in inorganic compounds and polyatomic ions
Fundamentals of redox chemistry

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Errors in converting mass percentages to mole ratios for empirical formula.
Incorrectly simplifying mole ratios to the simplest whole numbers.
Misapplying rules for assigning oxidation states (e.g., for oxygen, hydrogen, or group 1/2 metals).
Forgetting to account for the overall charge of an ion when calculating oxidation states.
Confusing oxidation state with valency in all contexts.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure sufficient data (e.g., mass percentages or direct masses) is provided for empirical formula calculation.
Include a variety of species for oxidation state determination, such as neutral compounds, polyatomic ions, and compounds with transition metals.
Questions can vary in complexity by requiring prior calculations (e.g., from combustion analysis data) before determining empirical formula.
Can serve as a foundational step for further questions on redox reactions or organic structure elucidation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_2e4e0d71. Simplifying Complex Algebraic Expressions**

**Core move:** Apply algebraic rules and identities to transform and reduce complex expressions involving fractions, powers, and multiple variables into their simplest equivalent forms.

**Seen in / context:**
- Simplifying rational expressions
Factoring and expanding algebraic terms
Combining or splitting algebraic fractions
Working with exponents and roots
Manipulating expressions in pre-calculus and calculus problems

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the order of operations (PEMDAS/BODMAS)
Making sign errors during expansion or factoring
Attempting to cancel terms across addition or subtraction signs incorrectly
Misapplying rules for exponents or roots (e.g., (x+y)^2 != x^2+y^2)
Failing to identify common factors or suitable algebraic identities

**Notes for generation:**
- The question typically provides an initial complex expression to be simplified or an equation to be solved, with the options representing different forms of the simplified result or solution.
Options should include common incorrect simplifications to act as distractors.
The complexity of the expressions can be varied by increasing the number of variables, terms, or the degree of the polynomials/exponents involved.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q0_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_2e91cf27. Determining Geometric Series Parameters and Sum**

**Core move:** Apply the formulas for terms and sum of a geometric series, using given conditions to solve for unknown parameters and derive the required sum.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems requiring the calculation of a specific term or the sum of a finite or infinite geometric series.
Questions where conditions such as convergence, positivity, or reality of terms are provided to constrain solutions.
Scenarios involving finding the common ratio and first term from given information about the series.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the formula for the nth term (e.g., using ar^n instead of ar^(n-1)).
Failing to check or misinterpreting the convergence condition for an infinite series (|r| < 1).
Ignoring or misusing additional conditions (e.g., 'term is positive' or 'term is real') to narrow down possible values for the common ratio.
Making algebraic errors when solving for the common ratio or rationalizing denominators for the final sum.
Confusing properties or formulas of arithmetic series with those of geometric series.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure there are enough independent pieces of information to uniquely determine all unknown parameters (e.g., first term 'a' and common ratio 'r').
Vary the types of given information, such as the value of specific terms, the sum of a certain number of terms, or relationships between terms.
Include constraints (e.g., 'real and positive', 'convergent') that require careful consideration of multiple solutions for 'r' and lead to a unique valid answer.
The final answer often requires rationalization of denominators or simplification of surds.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q77_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q83_NSAA_Sec`: The question provides the first term and the sum to infinity of a geometric progression, with the common ratio given in terms of an unknown variable 'x'. The core task is to apply the formula for the sum to infinity of a geometric series to solve for the common ratio and subsequently for 'x', which directly aligns with the schema's core move of applying formulas to solve for unknown parameters.

## **M_2ea64c88. Analyzing Chemical Equilibrium with Temperature Changes**

**Core move:** Calculate the equilibrium constant at a given temperature and predict its shift based on the reaction's enthalpy change and Le Chatelier's principle when temperature changes.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chemical reactions at equilibrium (Kc, Kp)
Stoichiometry and mole calculations (ICE tables)
Thermodynamics of reactions (exothermic/endothermic, ΔH)
Application of Le Chatelier's principle to temperature changes

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating initial or equilibrium moles/concentrations using stoichiometry
Misinterpreting the sign of ΔH (exothermic vs. endothermic)
Incorrectly applying Le Chatelier's principle (e.g., believing increasing temperature shifts exothermic equilibrium to products)
Failing to account for the physical states of reactants/products when formulating Kc (e.g., including solids or pure liquids incorrectly)
Algebraic errors in solving for Kc

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure a clear understanding of initial, change, and equilibrium (ICE) calculations for mole determination.
Emphasize the direct relationship between ΔH and the direction of equilibrium shift with temperature change.
Stress the importance of using correct molar masses and unit conversions.
Questions can vary the external factor (temperature, pressure, concentration, catalyst) but this schema focuses on temperature effects.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q29_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_2f9c8ef5. Equating Geometric Properties to Solve for Unknowns**

**Core move:** Construct and solve an algebraic equation derived from comparing specific geometric properties of related figures.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving angles (interior, exterior) of regular or irregular polygons.
Problems relating dimensions or areas of similar or related geometric shapes.
Scenarios where a change in a figure's property affects another related figure's property.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly recalling or applying the relevant geometric formula.
Making algebraic errors when simplifying rational expressions or solving the resulting quadratic equation.
Failing to consider constraints on the variable (e.g., number of sides 'n' must be an integer > 2).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should require the application of a specific geometric formula (e.g., exterior angle = 360/n, sum of interior angles = (n-2)x180).
The problem setup should lead to an equation that often involves rational expressions and typically resolves into a quadratic equation, requiring more than basic linear solving.
Ensure the context allows for a valid solution (e.g., 'n' for number of sides must be a positive integer).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q11_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q83_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires identifying geometric properties (radius from the circle's equation and the given tangent length) which, with the distance from the circle's center to point P, form a right-angled triangle. The Pythagorean theorem, an algebraic equation, is then constructed and solved using these geometric properties to find a key unknown distance (from the center to P). This process of using an algebraic equation derived from the relationships of geometric properties to find an unknown aligns with the core move of M_2f9c8ef5.

## **M_304971f1. Analyzing Periodic Trends and Chemical Properties**

**Core move:** Relate an element's position on the periodic table to its chemical properties and explain property variations using periodic trends and intermolecular forces.

**Seen in / context:**
- Predicting maximum and minimum oxidation states based on group number.
Explaining trends in electronegativity, ionization energy, or atomic radius within groups and periods.
Comparing physical properties like boiling points or melting points by identifying and explaining the dominant intermolecular forces (e.g., hydrogen bonding, van der Waals forces).
Discussing the reactivity of elements based on their position in the periodic table.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing trends across a period with trends down a group (e.g., electronegativity increasing down a group).
Incorrectly assigning maximum or minimum oxidation states for an element.
Failing to identify the strongest intermolecular force present (e.g., overlooking hydrogen bonding in H2O).
Providing the correct trend or property comparison without a clear, scientific explanation.
Ignoring the impact of molecular size or number of electrons on the strength of van der Waals forces.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should require explanations for observed trends and property differences, not just factual recall.
Can involve comparing properties of elements within the same group or period, or simple compounds formed by these elements.
Often requires knowledge of fundamental chemical principles such as electron configuration, polarity, and types of intermolecular forces.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q17_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_3312e959. Area of a Region Defined by Lines**

**Core move:** Determine the vertices of a polygonal region by finding the intersection points of its bounding linear equations, and then compute its area.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding the area of a triangle or quadrilateral formed by given lines
Problems involving systems of linear equations and inequalities where the feasible region is a polygon
Geometric interpretation of algebraic equations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly solving simultaneous equations to find vertices
Misidentifying the enclosed region, especially if lines intersect at multiple points or form multiple regions
Errors in applying area formulas (e.g., incorrect base/height, or arithmetic mistakes in the shoelace formula)
Not sketching the lines to visualize the region, leading to incorrect vertex selection

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the lines provided unambiguously enclose a single, finite region.
Coordinates of intersection points should be reasonably simple to work with for area calculation.
Typically involves finding three intersection points for a triangle, or four for a quadrilateral.
Encourage sketching the graph as a primary step to verify the enclosed region and its vertices.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q8_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q73_NSAA_Sec`: The question explicitly asks to find the area of a shape bounded by linear equations. The core move of schema M_3312e959 involves determining the vertices of the polygonal region formed by these lines and then calculating its area, which is precisely the required thinking pattern to solve this problem.

## **M_335792d4. Analyzing Forces in Coupled Systems**

**Core move:** Apply Newton's Second Law to individual components of a mechanically coupled system to derive and solve simultaneous equations for unknown forces or kinematics.

**Seen in / context:**
- Systems with multiple masses connected by cables over pulleys.
Objects moving on inclined planes (rough or smooth).
Problems involving tension, friction, and normal forces.
Atwood machines (a specific type of pulley system).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly resolving forces into components (especially on inclined planes).
Confusing the direction of forces (e.g., friction opposing motion, tension pulling).
Forgetting to include all relevant forces (e.g., normal force for calculating friction).
Assuming different magnitudes of tension or acceleration for connected objects via a light, inextensible cable over a frictionless pulley.
Incorrectly assigning signs to forces in the F=ma equation based on the chosen positive direction.

**Notes for generation:**
- Always draw a free-body diagram for each object in the system.
Choose a consistent positive direction for acceleration for all connected objects.
The tension in a light inextensible cable over a frictionless pulley is uniform throughout the cable.
The magnitude of acceleration is the same for all connected objects in the system.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q90_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_33ad8815. Transforming Algebraic Expressions to Standard Forms**

**Core move:** Transform an algebraic expression into a specified standard form by using algebraic manipulation, and subsequently extract a particular constant or coefficient.

**Seen in / context:**
- Converting quadratic expressions from general form to vertex form (completing the square).
Rearranging polynomial expressions to identify specific coefficients or roots.
Manipulating expressions to simplify graphing or analysis.
Expressing rational functions in partial fraction form.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Errors in expanding binomials (e.g., (a+b)^2 = a^2 + b^2).
Incorrectly distributing negative signs when subtracting expressions.
Algebraic errors when completing the square (e.g., forgetting to subtract the squared term multiplied by the leading coefficient).
Misidentifying the specific constant or coefficient requested in the target form.

**Notes for generation:**
- The target form (e.g., p(x+q)^2+r) must be clearly defined in the question.
The initial expression can involve various polynomial degrees or types of algebraic terms.
Questions should focus on identifying specific coefficients or constants after the transformation, rather than just the transformation itself.
Can be adapted for expressions requiring factorization, expansion, or a combination of both.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q9_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: Part (iv) of the question directly requires transforming a previously derived algebraic expression (from part ii, not shown) into a specified standard form, and then extracting integer values for the coefficients a, b, and k. This perfectly matches the core move of schema M_33ad8815.

## **M_34e83b94. Calculating Unknowns from Descriptive Statistics Relationships**

**Core move:** Formulate and solve an algebraic equation by expressing the mean and median of a partially defined dataset in terms of an unknown variable, then applying a given relationship between these statistics.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving a list of numbers where one or more are algebraic expressions.
Questions that state a relationship (e.g., equality, difference, ratio) between common descriptive statistics like mean, median, mode, or range.
Scenarios where the order of elements for median calculation might depend on the range of the unknown variable.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the mean (summing terms incorrectly or dividing by wrong count).
Incorrectly determining the median, especially when the list contains algebraic terms and its order needs careful consideration based on the variable's range.
Algebraic errors when setting up or solving the equation.
Not checking if the derived value of the unknown falls within specified constraints.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the importance of sorting the data (or at least understanding its relative order) to correctly identify the median terms.
When the unknown variable is part of the numbers used to find the median, ensure the assumption of order holds across the variable's possible range.
Vary the type of descriptive statistics involved (e.g., mode, range, quartiles) and the relationship between them.
Introduce scenarios where the exact ordering for the median calculation might change based on the value of the unknown, requiring case analysis.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q8_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires formulating and solving a system of algebraic equations derived from the definitions of mean and median for a partially defined dataset. The values of the mean and median are given, which allows for the calculation of unknown variables ('x' and 'y') within the dataset, ultimately leading to the determination of the mode. This directly matches the core move of schema M_34e83b94.

## **M_36f743f9. Calculating Combined Genetic and Gender Probabilities**

**Core move:** Apply Mendelian inheritance principles and independent event probability rules to determine the likelihood of specific offspring characteristics, including sex.

**Seen in / context:**
- Monohybrid crosses with dominant/recessive alleles
Autosomal recessive genetic conditions
Calculating probabilities for multiple independent events (e.g., genetic trait and gender)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly determining parental genotypes from descriptions (e.g., carrier vs. affected)
Errors in constructing or interpreting Punnett squares for offspring genotypes/phenotypes
Forgetting to incorporate the probability of gender (usually 1/2 for male/female)
Confusing heterozygous with homozygous dominant or recessive genotypes
Miscalculating the probability for a 'carrier' (heterozygous) state

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the inheritance pattern (dominant/recessive, autosomal/sex-linked) is clearly stated or inferable.
Define parental conditions that allow their genotypes to be unambiguously determined.
The questions should require combining genetic probability with other independent probabilities like gender.
Probabilities should result in simple fractions to avoid complex arithmetic and focus on the conceptual understanding.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q65_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q72_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying Mendelian inheritance principles to interpret a given phenotypic ratio (2:1) and explain its deviation from the standard 3:1 ratio expected from a monohybrid cross of two heterozygotes. This involves understanding how factors (e.g., lethal alleles) can modify the likelihood of specific offspring characteristics, aligning with the core move of determining likelihoods based on genetic principles.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q79_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires applying Mendelian inheritance principles to determine offspring genotypes and phenotypes, and then calculating the expected proportion (probability) of a specific characteristic (brown fur) under different parental genotype scenarios. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of applying Mendelian inheritance principles and probability rules to find the likelihood of offspring characteristics.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q68_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires applying Mendelian inheritance principles to determine the genotypes and phenotypes of offspring from given crosses, which is central to calculating the likelihood of specific offspring characteristics. The three described crosses (V, W, Y) directly test understanding of monohybrid crosses and the resulting phenotypic ratios.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q41_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying Mendelian inheritance principles to determine the probability of a child inheriting each recessive condition from the given parental genotypes. Since the conditions are on different chromosomes, the independent event probability rule is then applied to calculate the likelihood of the child having both conditions.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires applying Mendelian inheritance principles to determine the probability of a specific genotype in an offspring, which aligns with the core move of calculating the likelihood of specific offspring characteristics using probability rules.

## **M_37a04af7. Applying Domain-Specific Formulas to Solve for Unknowns**

**Core move:** Apply a known formula or equation from a specific scientific or mathematical domain, substituting given values to calculate one or more unknown quantities, or to find values that satisfy a given condition.

**Seen in / context:**
- Physics problems (e.g., kinematics, optics, electricity, waves)
Chemistry problems (e.g., stoichiometry, gas laws, reaction kinetics)
Engineering applications (e.g., stress-strain calculations, fluid dynamics)
Geometric calculations (e.g., area, volume, perimeter formulas)
Financial calculations (e.g., compound interest, loan repayments)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Using the incorrect formula for the given scenario.
Failing to convert units consistently before calculation.
Algebraic errors when rearranging the formula to isolate the unknown.
Misidentifying variables or substituting values into the wrong places.
Ignoring contextual constraints, such as physical impossibility (e.g., negative length) or specific conditions (e.g., 'first minimum').

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly implies the use of a specific formula or requires knowledge of a standard formula within the relevant domain.
All necessary parameters for the formula should either be provided or deducible from the question context.
The unknown quantity should typically be a single variable, or a set of discrete values that satisfy a condition (as in part f).
Questions can involve multi-step calculations or require solving for multiple unknowns sequentially.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q11_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires the application of domain-specific formulas (Snell's Law, critical angle formula, and a given refractive index formula) from the field of optics to derive an expression for an unknown (θ1) and subsequently calculate its numerical value under specified conditions. This directly aligns with the core move of applying known formulas to solve for unknowns or satisfy conditions.

## **M_38573e2f. Differentiating Atomic Species by Subatomic Particles**

**Core move:** Classify atoms or ions based on their subatomic particle counts by applying definitions of element, isotope, and ion.

**Seen in / context:**
- Identifying unknown elements, isotopes, or ions from given subatomic particle data.
Comparing atomic species based on proton, neutron, and electron numbers.
Understanding the relationship between atomic number, mass number, and charge.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the role of protons, neutrons, and electrons in defining an element, isotope, or ion.
Misremembering the definition of an isotope (e.g., thinking it's different element, same mass number).
Incorrectly calculating the number of electrons for an ion based on its charge.
Not recognizing that the number of protons uniquely identifies an element.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often require a direct application of definitions to numerical data (e.g., a table of protons, neutrons, electrons).
Ensure the question clearly states which properties are to be compared (e.g., same element, different isotope).
Can be extended to involve calculating mass number or atomic number.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q0_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q23_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires identifying the isotopes for each element in the molecule (C, H, O), applying the definition of an isotope to determine the number of neutrons for each specific isotope (e.g., Tritium, 3H; 12C; 16O), and then summing these subatomic particle counts across the entire molecule. This directly aligns with the core move of classifying atomic species by their subatomic particle counts using the definition of isotopes.

## **M_3a25988d. Calculating Area/Perimeter of Regular Polygons**

**Core move:** Apply established geometric formulas and properties for regular polygons to interconvert dimensions (e.g., side length, apothem, radius) and compute the area or perimeter.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving regular triangles, squares, pentagons, hexagons, octagons, etc.
Questions where one dimension (like apothem, side length, diagonal, or distance between opposite sides) is given, and another (like area, perimeter, or another internal length) is required.
Problems that often involve knowledge of special right triangles (e.g., 30-60-90 for hexagons) or basic trigonometry.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the apothem (distance from center to midpoint of a side) with the radius (distance from center to a vertex) or side length.
Incorrectly applying or recalling area formulas for regular polygons or their constituent triangles.
Errors in algebraic manipulation, especially with square roots and fractions, when deriving dimensions.
Assuming properties of one regular polygon apply to another (e.g., radius equals side length is only true for a hexagon).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure questions can be solved by decomposing the regular polygon into simpler shapes (e.g., isosceles or equilateral triangles).
Vary the initial given dimension (e.g., apothem, side length, radius of circumscribed/inscribed circle, shortest/longest diagonal).
Vary the target calculation (e.g., area, perimeter, internal distance).
Consider including scenarios where the polygon is inscribed or circumscribed within a circle.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires applying the geometric properties and formulas specific to a regular polygon (equilateral triangle) to interconvert its given side length into the circumradius, and then using this radius to calculate the circumference (a type of perimeter) of the circumcircle. This directly aligns with the core move of schema M_3a25988d.

## **M_3a692ce3. Distributing a Total into Categories**

**Core move:** Allocate a total quantity into distinct, non-overlapping categories based on given criteria or proportions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Surveys and population distribution problems
Resource allocation among different options
Analyzing data for charts and graphs (e.g., pie charts, bar charts)
Simple probability questions involving mutually exclusive events

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Double-counting items or individuals in multiple categories
Misinterpreting exclusivity (e.g., 'each chose one activity')
Assuming an even distribution across categories when not specified
Failing to account for the entire total quantity across all categories

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the sum of quantities in all categories equals the initial total quantity.
Questions often require subsequent calculations of percentages, fractions, or ratios for each category.
This pattern forms the basis for many data handling and statistical problems.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q12_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires breaking down a total number of students (100) into various distinct and non-overlapping categories (gender and subject), and then using given algebraic relationships between these categories to deduce an expression for a specific sub-total. This perfectly aligns with the core move of allocating a total quantity into distinct categories based on given criteria.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q12_NSAA_Sec`: The question provides a total sum for three numbers and their ratios. The core task is to combine these ratios to establish proportions and then allocate the total sum into the respective categories (p, q, and r) to find their individual values, which perfectly aligns with distributing a total into categories based on proportions.

## **M_3c2d3d96. Calculating Distance, Speed, or Time with Geometric Paths**

**Core move:** Apply the fundamental relationship (Distance = Speed × Time) to determine an unknown variable, often after calculating the total distance from given geometric properties of the path.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving travel around defined geometric shapes (squares, circles, rectangles).
Scenarios requiring the calculation of a perimeter or length as the total distance.
Basic kinematics problems where motion is at a constant speed.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the total distance of the path (e.g., using side length instead of perimeter).
Mixing units without conversion (e.g., m/s and km/h).
Dividing distance by time when speed is required, or vice versa.
Assuming constant speed when acceleration might be implied, or vice versa.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly defines the path's geometry and relevant dimensions.
Questions can ask for distance, speed, or time, requiring rearrangement of the formula.
Unit consistency is crucial; prompt for conversions if necessary.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_3de6ec2f. Multi-stage Mechanics: Forces, Kinematics, and Momentum Conservation**

**Core move:** Integrate calculations involving forces, constant acceleration kinematics, and the principle of conservation of momentum to solve problems involving sequential events.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving objects undergoing acceleration due to forces before a collision.
Inelastic and elastic collisions where momentum is conserved.
Scenarios where initial conditions for a collision are determined by forces acting over time.
Systems with multiple interacting objects over different time periods.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to correctly calculate the pre-collision velocity (e.g., kinematic errors, F=ma errors).
Mistaking elastic for inelastic collisions (or vice-versa) regarding energy conservation.
Incorrectly applying the conservation of momentum equation (e.g., forgetting masses, wrong directions, using impulse instead of momentum).
Mixing up initial and final states or objects in the equations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize breaking down the problem into distinct stages (e.g., acceleration phase, collision phase).
Highlight the importance of accurately determining the state of the system (especially velocities) at the transition points between stages.
Reinforce that momentum is conserved in collisions if no net external forces act *during the collision*.
Encourage consistent use of a sign convention for vector quantities like velocity and momentum.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q82_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_3ded199f. Calculating Component Values in a Circuit**

**Core move:** Apply Ohm's Law and relevant circuit rules (series/parallel) to determine the current, potential difference, or resistance of a specific component within a circuit.

**Seen in / context:**
- Simple series circuits
Simple parallel circuits
Mixed series-parallel circuits where individual component values are sought
Problems involving variable resistors or specific components

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing total circuit values with values specific to a single component
Incorrectly applying series rules (e.g., current is the same, voltage divides) or parallel rules (e.g., voltage is the same, current divides)
Misidentifying which V, I, or R corresponds to the specific component being analyzed when using Ohm's Law
Assuming values not given or derivable from the circuit context

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question provides or implies all necessary information (e.g., supply voltage, other resistor values, total current/voltage) either in the question text or an accompanying diagram/preceding parts.
The question should clearly specify which component's values are being requested.
Calculations should primarily involve direct application of Ohm's Law (V=IR) and basic circuit principles.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q8_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the resistance of individual wire strands using the resistivity formula (determining a component value) and then applying the rules for resistors in parallel to find the total resistance of the cable, which is a specific circuit component.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q11_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying the rules for combining series and parallel resistors to deduce the resistance of an individual component (R) from the given total resistances of different arrangements. This directly aligns with the core move of determining the 'resistance of a specific component' using 'relevant circuit rules (series/parallel)'.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the equivalent EMF and internal resistance of the battery pack using series and parallel rules, then applying Ohm's Law to find the total current in the circuit, and finally calculating the power dissipated in the external resistor using the derived current and its resistance. This perfectly matches the core move of applying circuit rules and Ohm's Law to determine values within a circuit.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks for the current at a specific point 'P' within a circuit, given an initial current of 10 mA. This requires applying fundamental circuit rules (such as Kirchhoff's Current Law for current division or series current rules) to determine a specific component's current, which is the core move of schema M_3ded199f. The options suggest a current division scenario where 10 mA splits into two branches, e.g., 3.0 mA and 7.0 mA.

## **M_3ed5cf63. Applying Energy and Power Principles with Efficiency**

**Core move:** Apply the definitions of power, energy, and efficiency, along with relevant physical formulas, to determine an unknown quantity in a system involving energy transformations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Hydroelectric power generation
Efficiency calculations for machines (motors, generators)
Systems involving potential energy conversion (e.g., lifting objects, water flow)
Thermodynamic efficiency in engines

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the efficiency factor (e.g., multiplying by efficiency when dividing is needed, or vice versa)
Failure to convert units correctly (e.g., MW to W, minutes to seconds)
Confusing input power with output power
Using an incorrect formula for potential energy or density
Calculation errors when dealing with large numbers and powers of ten

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often require multiple steps, working backward or forward through energy transformations.
Emphasis on consistent unit usage (e.g., SI units).
Students must understand the relationship between power, energy, mass, density, and height.
Can involve fluid dynamics concepts like volume flow rate.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q74_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q88_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating strain energy, which is a form of stored energy. The core move involves applying fundamental physical formulas (force, stress, strain, Young's modulus, and the strain energy formula) to determine an unknown energy quantity within a system where work done by a load is transformed into elastic potential energy. This aligns with 'Applying Energy and Power Principles with ... relevant physical formulas, to determine an unknown quantity in a system involving energy transformations', despite the specific 'efficiency' component not being directly applicable in this problem.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires applying the definitions of electrical current (I=Q/t) and power (P=VI) to determine the power dissipated in a resistor. This aligns with the schema's core move of applying 'energy and power principles' and 'relevant physical formulas' to determine an unknown quantity in a system involving energy transformation (electrical energy to heat).
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating initial and final kinetic energy, determining the total change in kinetic energy, and then computing the average rate of loss of kinetic energy (average power) by dividing the energy change by the given time. This directly applies the definitions of kinetic energy and average power, and uses the impulse-momentum theorem, fitting the core move of applying energy and power principles with relevant physical formulas to determine an unknown quantity.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating energy from a given power function over a time interval (involving integration), and then applying the concept of efficiency to determine the wasted energy. This directly aligns with the core move of applying definitions of power, energy, and efficiency to find an unknown quantity in an energy transformation context.

## **M_3fce3961. Simplifying Rational Algebraic Expressions**

**Core move:** Factor polynomials in numerators and denominators to simplify individual terms, and then combine rational expressions over a common denominator.

**Seen in / context:**
- Adding and subtracting algebraic fractions
Simplifying complex algebraic fractions
Preparing expressions for partial fraction decomposition
Solving equations involving rational expressions

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Errors in factoring quadratic or other polynomials
Incorrectly finding the least common multiple of denominators
Distributing negative signs incorrectly when subtracting fractions
Cancelling terms that are not common factors (e.g., cancelling 'x' from 'x+a' and 'x+b')
Arithmetic mistakes during numerator expansion and collection of like terms

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions typically involve polynomials of degree up to 2 or 3.
The simplification might result in a single rational expression or a sum/difference of simpler rational expressions (partial fraction form).
Constraints on variables (e.g., x>1) should be noted, but often don't affect the algebraic process itself, mainly the validity of steps like cancellation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_41ab00ac. Determine Young's Modulus from Load and Deformation**

**Core move:** Combine geometric analysis and force equilibrium to derive stress and strain, then compute Young's modulus.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving a material under tension or compression.
Scenarios where deformation leads to a change in geometry (e.g., a suspended mass causing a rope to sag).
Situations where Hooke's Law is obeyed.
Calculations involving wires, ropes, or rods under load.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the total extension with the extension of a single segment.
Incorrectly identifying the original length for strain calculation (e.g., using the stretched length instead of the unstretched length).
Failing to resolve forces correctly (e.g., equating total weight to tension in one rope segment without considering angles, or forgetting to account for multiple segments).
Using the wrong force in the stress calculation (e.g., using the total weight instead of the tension along the rope).
Arithmetic errors in Pythagorean theorem or force resolution.
Units conversion errors.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize careful geometric analysis, often involving trigonometry and the Pythagorean theorem.
Stress is defined as Force/Area, where Force is the internal tensile/compressive force acting perpendicular to the cross-sectional area.
Strain is defined as Extension/Original Length. The original length is the unstretched length of the portion undergoing extension.
Clearly distinguish between the total load and the tension in an individual component.
Ensure all quantities are in consistent SI units.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the extension of the wire using geometric analysis (Pythagorean theorem) and determining the tension in the wire using force equilibrium (resolving forces vertically). These derived values for extension and force are then used to calculate stress and strain, ultimately leading to the Young's Modulus. This perfectly aligns with the core move of combining geometric analysis and force equilibrium to derive stress and strain, then computing Young's modulus.

## **M_41db1444. Determining Extent of Gas Dissociation from Volume Change**

**Core move:** Calculate the initial moles of reactant, set up an ICE table to express equilibrium total moles in terms of the extent of dissociation, and equate this to the total moles derived from the given equilibrium volume and molar volume.

**Seen in / context:**
- Gas phase equilibrium reactions where the number of moles changes (e.g., dissociation or association).
Problems providing equilibrium total volume or pressure, alongside molar volume or gas constant.
Calculations of percentage dissociation or extent of reaction without an explicit equilibrium constant (Kc or Kp).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly setting up the 'change' row in the ICE table, particularly regarding stoichiometric coefficients.
Failing to recognize that the total number of moles changes due to the reaction.
Incorrectly converting between mass, moles, and volume units (e.g., cm³ to dm³).
Assuming the initial volume or pressure is the same as the equilibrium volume or pressure without accounting for dissociation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Highlight the importance of the stoichiometric coefficients in determining the change in total moles for the reaction.
Questions may provide total pressure instead of total volume, requiring the use of PV=nRT or partial pressures to find total moles.
Ensure all units are consistent throughout the calculation (e.g., volume in dm³ if molar volume is in dm³/mol).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q39_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_447b11ba. Applying Laws of Indices**
