**Core move:** Apply the fundamental laws of exponents to combine and simplify terms in an algebraic expression.

**Seen in / context:**
- Simplifying complex algebraic fractions involving powers.
Solving equations with variables in exponents (exponential equations).
Manipulating expressions in calculus (differentiation, integration of power functions).
Physics problems involving relationships with powers (e.g., inverse square laws, scaling).
Chemistry calculations involving concentrations or rates.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing (a^m)^n with a^m * a^n.
Incorrectly distributing exponents over addition or subtraction (e.g., (a+b)^n != a^n + b^n).
Errors with negative exponents (e.g., a^(-n) vs -a^n or 1/a^n).
Misinterpreting fractional exponents as division rather than roots (e.g., a^(1/n) as a/n).
Arithmetic mistakes when adding, subtracting, or multiplying fractional exponents.
Ignoring the order of operations within the expression.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should feature a clear algebraic expression with multiple variables and exponents.
Include both integer and fractional exponents, and potentially negative exponents.
Incorporate nested powers and divisions/multiplications to require the application of several exponent rules.
Distractor options should reflect common errors in applying exponent laws.
Can be structured as 'simplify' or 'which is equivalent to'.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_45fbb56f. Making a Variable the Subject in Rational Expressions**

**Core move:** Rearrange an algebraic equation, typically involving rational expressions and potentially quadratic terms, to isolate a specific variable.

**Seen in / context:**
- Making a variable the subject of a formula in algebra.
Solving for parameters in scientific or engineering equations that are rational functions.
Finding the inverse function (f⁻¹(x)) when f(x) is a rational expression.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Errors in cross-multiplication or distributing terms.
Failing to correctly group all terms containing the target variable on one side of the equation.
Algebraic errors when factoring out the target variable.
Sign errors when moving terms across the equals sign.
Forgetting the '±' sign when taking a square root.

**Notes for generation:**
- The target variable typically appears in both the numerator and denominator, or multiple times within the expression.
The final form often involves a square root or other inverse operations.
Vary the complexity by changing the powers of the variables or the number of terms.
Ensure the question explicitly states which variable to make the subject.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q9_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q15_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires rearranging an algebraic equation that contains rational expressions to make 'y' the subject, which perfectly aligns with the core move of isolating a specific variable in rational expressions.

## **M_49102f46. Analyzing Oxidation States and Redox Products**

**Core move:** Apply rules for assigning oxidation states to elements in compounds and ions, and deduce products of common redox reactions given specific reagents and conditions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining the charge of an atom in a molecule or ion.
Identifying the oxidizing and reducing agents in a reaction.
Balancing redox equations using changes in oxidation states.
Predicting reaction outcomes in inorganic chemistry.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assuming standard oxidation states for elements in exceptions (e.g., oxygen in peroxides or fluorine compounds, hydrogen in metal hydrides).
Miscalculating the overall charge of a species.
Failing to consider reaction conditions (e.g., acidic vs. basic medium for redox products).
Confusing oxidation with reduction.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should specify whether the solution is acidic or basic if it influences the products.
Compounds and ions for oxidation state calculations should vary in complexity.
Common oxidizing/reducing agents should be featured (e.g., H2O2, MnO4-, Cr2O72-, halogens, metals).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q39_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires a multi-step analysis. First, the quantity of iodine produced must be determined using titration stoichiometry. This quantity, along with the initial amount of reactant, is then used to deduce the effective stoichiometric ratio of lanthanum to ion Q in the final product. Finally, applying the given oxidation states for iodine (+7) and oxygen (-2) in ion Q, combined with the deduced charge of ion Q from its combination with La³⁺, allows for the deduction of ion Q's chemical formula. This entire process aligns with applying rules for assigning oxidation states and deducing the formula of a redox product based on specific reaction conditions and quantitative data.

## **M_49723874. Solving Simultaneous Equations for Combined Costs**

**Core move:** Formulate a system of linear equations from given conditions, solve for the individual unit costs, and then compute a specified combined cost.

**Seen in / context:**
- Word problems involving different item types with distinct unit prices.
Cost analysis in business for varying product combinations.
Determining unknown values in scenarios with multiple interacting variables.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly translating the word problem into algebraic equations.
Making algebraic errors during the elimination or substitution process.
Failing to answer the final question by only finding the individual unit costs instead of the requested combination.
Assuming a direct linear relationship between costs without proper system solving.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem provides a sufficient number of independent equations to solve for all unknown variables.
Design the numerical values such that individual unit costs are manageable (e.g., integers or simple fractions).
The final requested quantity should require the use of the solved individual unit costs.
Vary the complexity by changing the number of items or the coefficients in the equations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_4b294c20. Calculate Cumulative Percentage Change**

**Core move:** Compute the overall effect of a repeated percentage change applied sequentially over multiple periods.

**Seen in / context:**
- Financial depreciation or appreciation (compound interest)
Population growth or decay
Biological growth rates
Sequential discounts or markups

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Applying the percentage change to the original value for each period instead of the current value (simple interest error)
Calculating the final value instead of the total change (or vice versa) as requested by the question
Incorrectly adding when depreciation/decay occurs, or subtracting when appreciation/growth occurs
Calculating a single percentage equivalent for multiple periods (e.g., 20% over 2 years is not 40%)

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly distinguish between 'final value' and 'total change in value'.
Vary the number of periods and the percentage rate.
Include scenarios for both increases and decreases.
Ensure the numerical values are manageable for mental arithmetic or typical calculator use.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q11_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the overall effect of two sequential percentage changes (an increase of 125% followed by a decrease of 40%) to determine the final relationship between the initial and final values. This directly matches the core move of computing the cumulative percentage change.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the volume after a 60% decrease is applied sequentially over 3 weeks, which directly matches the core move of computing the overall effect of a repeated percentage change over multiple periods.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the net effect of two sequential percentage changes (a 25% reduction followed by a 25% increase) to determine the relationship between the original price and the customer's incorrectly calculated price. This directly matches the core move of computing the overall effect of repeated percentage changes applied sequentially.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires understanding and applying sequential percentage changes to work backwards from a final value to an initial value. This directly aligns with the core move of computing the overall effect of repeated percentage changes, as the overall multiplicative factor must first be determined or conceptualized to solve the problem, whether working forwards or backwards.

## **M_4b4199dd. Applying Defined Physical/Chemical Formulas**

**Core move:** Apply a given mathematical formula by correctly identifying, substituting, and manipulating variables and their units to calculate a specific outcome.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculation of physical quantities using explicit equations (e.g., forces, energies, concentrations, rates).
Estimation or comparison of values using empirical or theoretical models provided within the problem.
Solving problems where the relationship between variables is explicitly given.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misidentifying which numerical values correspond to which variables in the formula.
Errors in unit conversion or using inconsistent units without proper conversion.
Algebraic mistakes during substitution or rearrangement of the formula.
Ignoring specific definitions or conditions associated with the formula's variables (e.g., absolute value, number of ions).

**Notes for generation:**
- The formula should be explicitly provided within the question or be common knowledge relevant to the exam level.
All necessary input values or a clear path to derive them should be available in the problem statement.
Questions can involve single calculations, comparisons between different scenarios, or analysis of how changes in variables affect the outcome based on the formula.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_4c72332d. Deducing Organic Structures from Reaction Outcomes**

**Core move:** Deduce the structural formulas of organic molecules by interpreting the number and types of products formed from specific chemical reactions, applying knowledge of isomerism and reactivity patterns.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining unknown parent alkanes, alkenes, or other homologous series members based on chlorination/bromination counts or hydrogenation/hydration products.
Identifying the specific positions of functional groups or branches in molecules from reaction information.
Problems involving counting structural isomers for a given molecular formula or reaction product set.
Applying knowledge of specific reaction mechanisms (e.g., free radical substitution, addition reactions) to predict product diversity.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying or counting unique hydrogen environments in alkanes, leading to an incorrect number of chloroalkane isomers.
Failing to consider all possible structural isomers for a given molecular formula.
Errors in drawing skeletal or full structural formulas, such as incorrect valencies or bond representations.
Confusing constitutional isomers with stereoisomers (if stereoisomers are implicitly excluded or not relevant to the question).
Misinterpreting the outcome or conditions of a chemical reaction.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often require a systematic approach to list and compare potential structures and their reaction outcomes.
Emphasis is placed on the ability to translate verbal descriptions of chemical properties into visual structural representations.
Complexity can be increased by using larger carbon chains, multiple functional groups, or multi-step reaction sequences.
Careful attention to IUPAC nomenclature can help in identifying unique isomers.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q23_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires deducing the structure of an intermediate formed during a reaction (part g) and suggesting the structure of an alkene isomer that yields the same product (part h). Both tasks directly involve interpreting reaction outcomes, applying knowledge of isomerism (explicitly in part h), and understanding reactivity patterns to deduce structural formulas, which aligns precisely with the core move of schema M_4c72332d.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q33_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires deducing which structural isomers (alcohols of C5H12O) will exhibit specific chemical reactions (yellow precipitate with alkaline aqueous iodine, and silver mirror with Tollens' reagent after oxidation). This directly aligns with the core move of interpreting reaction outcomes (products) to deduce structural formulas and count them based on reactivity patterns and isomerism.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q36_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires deducing the structural formulas of organic compounds X and Y by interpreting their reactions (production of CO2 from X, combustion products from Y), their reactivity patterns (acidic nature of X, reaction of Y with sodium but not litmus), and then predicting the stoichiometry of their subsequent reaction based on these deduced structures. This aligns perfectly with the core move of schema M_4c72332d.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires identifying all structural isomers of C5H12O (alcohols), predicting the oxidation product for each based on its structure and reactivity patterns (primary/secondary/tertiary alcohols), and then determining which of these products would give a specific reaction outcome (yellow precipitate from iodoform test). This directly aligns with the core move of deducing structural formulas (of the products) by interpreting reaction outcomes and applying knowledge of isomerism and reactivity.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires identifying the type of organic reaction (condensation), the specific organic product (a disaccharide like maltose) based on the reactants (implied monosaccharides), and then deducing a structural feature (number of hydroxyl groups) of the formed product. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of 'Deduce the structural formulas of organic molecules by interpreting the number and types of products formed from specific chemical reactions, applying knowledge of isomerism and reactivity patterns', as determining the disaccharide and its hydroxyl groups necessitates understanding its structural formula derived from the reaction.

## **M_4d1ced66. Calculating Relative Atomic Mass from Isotopic Data**

**Core move:** Calculate the average relative atomic mass of an element or determine the natural abundances of its isotopes by applying the weighted average formula that considers the mass and relative abundance of each isotope.

**Seen in / context:**
- Introductory chemistry for understanding atomic structure and isotopes.
Problems involving average atomic mass and isotopic composition.
As a foundational step for understanding mass spectrometry results.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Simple averaging of isotopic masses without considering their abundances.
Incorrectly setting up the weighted average equation (e.g., using percentages without converting to decimals, or incorrect sums for abundances).
Confusing mass number with relative atomic mass or isotopic mass.
Misinterpreting given data, such as assuming neutron count is directly the mass number.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly states what needs to be calculated (e.g., average mass or isotopic abundances).
Provide clear isotopic mass values, or information to derive them (e.g., number of protons and neutrons).
The sum of relative abundances (as fractions or decimals) must always equal 1 (or 100% if working with percentages).
Be aware that some questions might use integer mass numbers as approximations for isotopic masses.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q44_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_4d734405. Calculating Mendelian Probabilities Across Generations**

**Core move:** Apply Mendelian inheritance principles and conditional probability to determine the likelihood of specific genotypes or phenotypes appearing in offspring across multiple generations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Genetic counseling scenarios for inherited conditions.
Predicting the probability of traits in offspring given parental and ancestral information.
Analyzing pedigrees to infer genotypes and probabilities.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to account for the probabilistic genotype of an intermediate generation (e.g., not calculating the chance the daughter is a carrier).
Misinterpreting the definitions of dominant/recessive alleles or homozygous/heterozygous genotypes.
Incorrectly applying conditional probability, especially when a phenotype is known.
Making errors in constructing Punnett squares or calculating allele probabilities from crosses.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly state whether traits are dominant or recessive and specify gene locations (autosomal, X-linked).
Often involves calculating probabilities for at least two generations.
Requires a clear understanding of genotype-phenotype relationships.
Can include scenarios where an individual's genotype must be inferred probabilistically from their parents and phenotype.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q80_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_4daafb55. Characterizing and Ordering Spectrum Components**

**Core move:** Recall and apply knowledge of the order and characteristic properties (e.g., frequency, wavelength, energy ranges) of components within a physical spectrum.

**Seen in / context:**
- Physics: Electromagnetic spectrum (radio, micro, IR, visible, UV, X-ray, gamma)
Physics: Sound spectrum (infrasound, audible, ultrasound)
Chemistry: Spectroscopic techniques (NMR, IR, UV-Vis, Atomic Absorption)
Biology: Vision and light sensitivity, effects of various radiations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the order of components (e.g., placing UV before IR in terms of increasing frequency).
Mixing up inversely related properties like frequency with wavelength (e.g., thinking a higher frequency means a longer wavelength).
Incorrectly recalling orders of magnitude for numerical ranges associated with different spectrum components.
Misinterpreting the question regarding whether it asks for a range between two components, a range containing components, or the specific properties of individual components.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly specify the units for numerical values (e.g., Hz, m, eV) or imply them through context.
Can test knowledge of the entire spectrum or specific sections.
Can involve comparing properties (e.g., 'which has higher energy?').
Can involve identifying common applications for different parts of the spectrum.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q8_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks to identify which part of the electromagnetic spectrum a calculated vibration frequency corresponds to. This directly requires recalling and applying knowledge of the characteristic properties and order of components within the electromagnetic spectrum.

## **M_4db1cf44. Manipulating Rates and Ratios for Totals**

**Core move:** Calculate an absolute quantity or a related ratio by correctly applying the definition of a rate or density (amount per unit of another amount).

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining total cost from unit price and quantity.
Calculating total mass from density and volume.
Finding total energy consumed from power and time.
Deriving total protein mass given surface area and surface area per unit protein.
Comparing relative efficiencies or concentrations based on given ratios.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Inverting the ratio (e.g., dividing when multiplication is needed).
Mismatched units leading to incorrect calculations or interpretations.
Confusing intensive properties (per unit) with extensive properties (total).

**Notes for generation:**
- Problems often involve unit analysis to guide the calculation (e.g., (A/B) * B = A).
Can involve a simple direct calculation or comparison between two different sets of values.
Focus on the 'per unit' aspect and what it implies for the calculation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q43_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_517ff042. Interpreting Problem Statements**

**Core move:** Extract all relevant numerical information, explicit questions, and implicit constraints from a given problem text.

**Seen in / context:**
- Word problems in any mathematical domain (algebra, geometry, calculus, statistics).
Understanding exam instructions or problem setups.
Identifying the objective of a proof or derivation.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Ignoring keywords that indicate operations or relationships.
Failing to identify all given data points.
Misinterpreting the actual question being asked.
Assuming information not explicitly stated.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure problem statements contain both necessary and potentially extraneous information to test discernment.
Vary the linguistic complexity of problem descriptions.
Design questions where the 'real' question is embedded within context.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q3_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q80_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is an instruction page for an exam. To 'analyze' it, one must interpret its content by extracting relevant numerical information (e.g., time limit, number of parts, questions per part) and implicit constraints (e.g., no calculators, mandatory sections, marking scheme). This process directly aligns with the core move of 'Interpreting Problem Statements' to understand the setup and parameters of a situation.
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q36_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is an incomplete question from an exam paper. The core move of 'Interpreting Problem Statements' is applied by attempting to extract relevant information, explicit questions, and constraints, which in this case reveals that there is no mathematical problem content to analyze.
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: The provided 'QUESTION' section is empty, containing no mathematical problem. Therefore, the only relevant thinking required is to attempt to interpret the problem statement and extract information, which is the core move of schema M_517ff042. While no information can be extracted, the act of interpreting the (empty) problem text is still engaged.
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text does not contain a mathematical question. Applying the 'Interpreting Problem Statements' schema, one attempts to extract relevant numerical information, explicit questions, and implicit constraints, but finds none in this case. The core move of interpretation is performed, even if the result is an empty set of extracted information.
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q32_NSAA_Sec`: The 'question' consists of the number '32' followed by blank lines. The core move of schema M_517ff042 is to 'Extract all relevant numerical information, explicit questions, and implicit constraints from a given problem text'. In this case, the number '32' is the only numerical information present in the 'problem text', and the process of identifying it (and the absence of an explicit question or constraints) directly aligns with interpreting the problem statement, however minimal it may be.

## **M_51b2d9d0. Interpreting Exam Paper Structure**

**Core move:** Extract information about the subjects, sections, and page allocations from an exam paper's content listing.

**Seen in / context:**
- Navigating multi-part examinations
Understanding the scope and layout of an assessment
Identifying the start pages for specific subject components

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the listed page number with the number of questions or marks
Misinterpreting section labels or subject allocations
Assuming content is sequential without checking page numbers

**Notes for generation:**
- This pattern primarily involves data extraction and comprehension rather than problem-solving.
It is crucial for test-takers to effectively manage time and locate relevant sections during an exam.
Questions based on this pattern might ask for total pages in a section, or which section starts on a given page.


## **M_529070f5. Analyzing Chromosome and DNA Content During Meiosis**

**Core move:** Apply the principles of meiosis to determine the chromosome number and DNA strand content in gametes produced from a given diploid cell.

**Seen in / context:**
- Cell biology questions involving cell division (mitosis/meiosis)
Genetics problems concerning gamete formation
Reproduction and inheritance scenarios

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the outcome of mitosis with meiosis (e.g., number of cells produced, ploidy of daughter cells)
Incorrectly accounting for DNA replication before meiosis I, leading to errors in the number of DNA strands per chromosome
Failing to differentiate between chromosome number (n or 2n) and the number of chromatids (DNA strands) per chromosome at different stages of meiosis
Not understanding that haploid cells (gametes) contain single chromatid chromosomes after meiosis II

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly specify the starting cell type (diploid/haploid) and its chromosome number (2n or n).
Specify the type of cell division (mitosis or meiosis).
Ask about the state (chromosome number, DNA strands) of specific daughter cells (e.g., sperm cells, egg cells, somatic cells) or at particular stages (e.g., after meiosis I, after meiosis II).
Can be extended to include different organisms or different starting chromosome numbers.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q67_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q53_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying knowledge about the differential DNA content between X and Y sperm (gametes), which is a direct consequence of chromosome segregation during meiosis. This aligns with the core move of determining DNA strand content in gametes produced from a diploid cell.

## **M_55b867c7. Multi-Step Unit Conversion and Algebraic Expression Construction**

**Core move:** Construct a multi-variable algebraic expression by sequentially applying unit conversion factors and rates to transform an initial quantity into a target quantity with different units.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating total cost or quantity based on multiple rates and unit conversions.
Problems involving fuel efficiency, speed, density, or other compound units where multiple conversions are required.
Deriving formulas for real-world scenarios by combining given proportional relationships.
Questions requiring careful dimensional analysis to ensure the final units are correct.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly inverting conversion factors (e.g., multiplying instead of dividing by 'x' when converting miles to km, or vice versa).
Confusing numerators and denominators when multiplying or dividing by fractional rates.
Omitting one or more steps in the conversion chain (e.g., forgetting currency conversion).
Making arithmetic errors with the variables.
Not keeping track of units throughout the calculation, leading to an expression with incorrect final dimensions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage students to write down the units at each step to verify the dimensional consistency of their calculations.
Questions should typically involve at least three distinct conversion or rate application steps.
The variables should represent specific quantities, and the answer should be an algebraic expression, not a numerical value.
Distractor options should include common mistakes, such as inverted terms, missing terms, or incorrect multiplication/division by constants like 100.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_564a115a. Identifying Non-Content Exam Paper Elements**

**Core move:** Distinguish administrative or structural pages from actual problem-solving questions within an exam paper.

**Seen in / context:**
- Familiarization with exam paper layouts and conventions
Recognizing section breaks, instructions, and placeholder pages
Efficient navigation through test booklets

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Attempting to solve or interpret a blank page as a hidden problem
Confusing administrative notes with mathematical questions
Overlooking the explicit statement that a page is left blank

**Notes for generation:**
- This schema applies to any non-question content in an exam, such as cover pages, instructions, blank pages, or specific answer sheet instructions.
Questions testing this schema would present an image or text of such a page and ask for its function or classification.
The focus is on interpreting the structure and purpose of the document rather than mathematical content.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q3_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is an exam paper's table of contents or structural overview, not a problem-solving question. This matches the core move of distinguishing administrative or structural pages from actual problem-solving content.

## **M_5662fe5f. Applying Exponent Laws**

**Core move:** Apply exponent laws (multiplication, division, power of a power, negative exponents) to simplify algebraic expressions containing powers of variables and constants.

**Seen in / context:**
- Simplifying complex algebraic fractions or expressions
Solving equations involving exponential terms
Preparing expressions for differentiation or integration in calculus
Formulas in physics or engineering involving powers of quantities

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting negative exponents (e.g., x^-n as -x^n instead of 1/x^n)
Incorrectly applying the power of a power rule (e.g., (x^a)^b = x^(a+b) instead of x^(ab))
Failing to apply exponents to all terms within parentheses (e.g., (ab)^n as a b^n instead of a^n b^n)
Adding exponents when bases are different, or multiplying when adding terms
Errors in combining numerical coefficients with exponential terms

**Notes for generation:**
- Include a mix of positive, negative, and potentially zero or fractional exponents.
Vary the number of variables and constants to adjust complexity.
Design expressions that require sequential application of multiple exponent rules.
Ensure that common misapplications of exponent rules lead to plausible incorrect answer options.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_566b137b. Recognizing Missing Problem Content**

**Core move:** Identify the absence of a clear problem statement, relevant numerical data, or an explicit question to be answered within the provided text.

**Seen in / context:**
- Receiving incomplete exam questions or exercises
Initial review of problem sets
Quality assurance of educational materials

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Attempting to infer a problem from minimal cues (e.g., just the question number)
Assuming implicit information is present when it is explicitly missing
Spending time trying to 'solve' a non-existent problem

**Notes for generation:**
- This pattern is for identifying malformed or empty problem statements, not solving mathematical problems.
It is crucial for preventing wasted effort on unsolvable or undefined tasks.
Such 'questions' typically indicate an error in material generation or presentation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q3_NSAA_Sec`: The prompt asks to analyze a 'Maths question', but the provided text is a table of contents for an exam paper, not an actual question, problem statement, or numerical data. The core move of recognizing the absence of the problem content directly applies here.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q3_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is labeled as 'QUESTION' but does not contain an explicit problem statement or a question to be answered, which directly matches the core move of identifying missing problem content.

## **M_5a1dd45f. Identifying Biological Structures from Descriptions**

**Core move:** Infer the identity of a biological entity or its observable features by integrating descriptive cues with domain-specific knowledge of structure and scale.

**Seen in / context:**
- Identifying cell types or organelles from microscopic observations.
Determining species based on morphological characteristics.
Relating physiological functions to anatomical structures.
Predicting observable features under different conditions (e.g., magnification, staining).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Ignoring key descriptive terms (e.g., 'large central vacuole' for plant cell).
Confusing the scale of observation (e.g., assuming structures visible by electron microscope are visible by light microscope at low power).
Misidentifying structures due to incomplete knowledge of their typical appearance or presence.
Over-interpreting vague descriptions or assuming structures are present without direct evidence.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide specific visual or functional cues.
Requires recall of known biological structures and their typical characteristics.
May involve understanding the capabilities/limitations of observational tools.
Answers often involve selecting from a list of plausible biological structures.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q62_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q62_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires identifying characteristics of a cell by integrating specific descriptive cues (cell wall, cell membrane, no mitochondria) with domain-specific knowledge of cell biology to infer a correct statement about its nature, which perfectly aligns with the core move of inferring identity or features from descriptions.

## **M_5d190467. Solving Constant Acceleration Kinematics**

**Core move:** Apply the relevant kinematic equations to calculate unknown motion parameters (displacement, velocity, acceleration, time) for objects undergoing constant acceleration.

**Seen in / context:**
- Projectile motion (e.g., stones thrown upwards, objects falling)
Uniformly accelerating/decelerating vehicles
Situations involving gravity as the sole acceleration

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting positive and negative directions for vectors (displacement, velocity, acceleration).
Incorrectly assuming initial or final conditions (e.g., initial velocity is zero, final velocity is zero).
Choosing the wrong kinematic equation for the given knowns and unknowns.
Neglecting the effect of gravity or other constant accelerations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the identification of knowns (s, u, v, a, t) and the desired unknown.
Problems can often be solved by choosing a consistent coordinate system (e.g., upward positive, downward positive).
Consider breaking complex motions into simpler phases if necessary (e.g., up to peak, then down).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q84_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q74_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying a specific kinematic equation (v² = u² + 2as) to find the acceleration, given initial velocity, final velocity, and displacement, which directly aligns with the core move of applying relevant kinematic equations to calculate unknown motion parameters under constant acceleration.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q90_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires calculating the total time of flight for the first object using kinematic equations under constant acceleration (gravity). This time is then used to determine the duration of fall for the second object, from which its release height can be calculated, again using kinematic equations for constant acceleration. This directly aligns with applying kinematic equations to find unknown motion parameters.

## **M_5dff36d3. Ordering Elements by Periodic Property**

**Core move:** Apply knowledge of periodic trends and their exceptions to order chemical elements based on a specified atomic or chemical property.

**Seen in / context:**
- First ionization energy
Atomic radius
Electronegativity
Electron affinity
Metallic character
Reactivity trends

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Applying general periodic trends without accounting for known exceptions (e.g., drops in ionization energy from Group 2 to Group 13, or Group 15 to Group 16).
Incorrectly recalling the direction of a trend (e.g., whether a property increases or decreases across a period or down a group).
Misidentifying the group or period of an element in the periodic table.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide a set of elements and a corresponding set of numerical values for the property.
The elements selected should allow for the demonstration of both general trends and key exceptions.
The task typically involves matching elements to their values or ordering them correctly.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q32_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_6029763c. Applying Conservation of Momentum in Collisions**

**Core move:** Apply the principle of conservation of linear momentum to a system of colliding objects to determine unknown velocities or impulses.

**Seen in / context:**
- Collisions (elastic or inelastic) between two or more objects
Problems involving impulse and force over a duration
One-dimensional or multi-dimensional collision scenarios

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming kinetic energy is conserved in inelastic collisions
Forgetting to treat velocity as a vector quantity (especially when directions are opposite or change)
Incorrectly summing initial or final momenta of the system
Confusing impulse with force, or miscalculating impulse (e.g., F*t vs change in momentum)

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure a clear system is defined for momentum conservation.
Provide initial masses and velocities for all objects involved.
The duration of the collision can be used to calculate the average force or impulse.
Can be extended to scenarios where objects combine or separate.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q76_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_612ed4aa. Analyzing Physical Variation Models**

**Core move:** Interpret and apply a given mathematical function to model the variation of a physical quantity, either by calculating specific values or analyzing its functional behavior.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing how a physical quantity (e.g., resistivity, temperature, pressure, concentration) varies with another (e.g., depth, time, distance).
Calculating specific values of a physical quantity using the model at given conditions.
Analyzing the behavior of the model, such as identifying maximum/minimum values, intercepts, or rates of change.
Understanding the physical implications of the mathematical form of the model.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting the variables or constants in the formula.
Incorrectly substituting numerical values into the formula.
Failing to understand the physical meaning of the mathematical expression (e.g., what the model predicts at certain boundary conditions).
Assuming linearity or other simple relationships when the model is complex.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often require both mathematical manipulation and physical interpretation.
Ensure the model is clearly defined and all variables and constants are explained.
Can involve algebraic manipulation to solve for unknown variables or interpret parameters.
May involve calculus if questions about rates of change or turning points are asked.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_622816e1. Simplifying Rational Algebraic Expressions**

**Core move:** Combine or reduce rational expressions by finding a common denominator, performing operations, and factoring.

**Seen in / context:**
- Adding and subtracting algebraic fractions
Multiplying and dividing algebraic fractions
Solving equations involving algebraic fractions
Calculus (simplifying before differentiation/integration)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly finding the lowest common multiple (LCM) of denominators
Errors in expanding or factorizing polynomials
Making sign errors when combining terms, especially after subtraction
Forgetting to simplify the final expression by cancelling common factors
Not recognizing common algebraic identities like difference of squares

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the importance of factorizing all denominators first to easily determine the LCM.
Questions often involve common factorizations like difference of squares (a^2 - b^2) or perfect square trinomials.
Ensure the final simplified expression is presented in its most reduced form.
Consider mentioning restrictions on the variable (i.e., denominators cannot be zero).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_622b9d44. Predicting Mass Spectrum Peaks from Isotopic Composition**

**Core move:** Calculate all possible molecular masses of a compound by considering the isotopic variations of its constituent atoms, and determine the relative intensities of the corresponding molecular ion peaks based on the natural abundances of these isotopes.

**Seen in / context:**
- Interpreting mass spectrometry data for compounds with multi-isotopic elements.
Determining the number and m/z values of molecular ion peaks.
Predicting the isotopic distribution patterns in mass spectra.
Qualitative analysis in analytical chemistry to confirm compound identity.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to account for all possible combinations of isotopes in a given molecular formula.
Incorrectly calculating the molecular mass for each isotopic combination.
Neglecting the stoichiometric coefficients of elements when determining isotopic combinations (e.g., for BrCl3, considering Br, Cl, Cl, Cl independently).
Miscalculating the relative abundances by not multiplying the probabilities of individual isotopes for each combination or ignoring binomial probabilities.
Confusing atomic numbers with mass numbers or relative atomic masses.
Assuming only the most abundant isotope exists for each element, thus missing other peaks.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure that elements with significant isotopic variations (e.g., Cl, Br, S, C, O, N) are central to the question.
Provide necessary isotopic masses and their natural abundances, or context to derive them (e.g., number of neutrons).
Vary the complexity by changing the number of multi-isotopic atoms in the compound.
For low-resolution mass spectrometry, emphasize that m/z values are typically integer mass numbers.
The 'most intense peak(s)' requires a calculation of relative abundances for all combinations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_624e224a. Calculating Unknown Dimensions in General Triangles**

**Core move:** Apply the Sine Rule or Cosine Rule to determine unknown side lengths or angles in non-right-angled triangles.

**Seen in / context:**
- Solving problems in triangle geometry
Surveying and navigation calculations
Physics problems involving vector components at angles

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the Pythagorean theorem to non-right-angled triangles
Confusing the Sine Rule with the Cosine Rule, or vice-versa
Using an angle that is not opposite the required side in the Cosine Rule
Errors in algebraic manipulation of trigonometric expressions

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly specifies which sides and angles are known and which are to be found.
Vary the required unknown (side length, angle, or area related to these).
Provide diagrams to clarify the relationships between sides and angles.
Consider scenarios where one rule (e.g., Sine Rule) might lead to an ambiguous case, or where only one rule is applicable based on the given information.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q61_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_628a6f0a. Integrating Energy Conservation and Kinematics**

**Core move:** Apply the principle of conservation of mechanical energy or the work-energy theorem in conjunction with kinematic equations to determine unknown quantities in motion problems.

**Seen in / context:**
- Projectile motion problems where initial or final velocities are derived from energy considerations.
Motion along a path where changes in height and speed are related by energy, and time/distance by kinematics.
Problems involving objects launched or dropped where the trajectory depends on both initial energy and gravitational effects.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly defining the initial and final states for energy conservation, often neglecting potential or initial kinetic energy.
Mixing horizontal and vertical components incorrectly in kinematic equations for projectile motion.
Forgetting to convert units (e.g., mass from grams to kilograms).
Confusing the initial horizontal velocity with the total initial velocity if the launch angle is not purely horizontal.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all forms of energy relevant to the problem are accounted for (kinetic, potential).
Emphasize the independence of horizontal and vertical motion in projectile problems.
Questions can vary the unknown, asking for initial velocity, height, time, or range, requiring different sequences of application of energy and kinematic principles.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_6293bac3. Determining Specific Coefficients in Polynomial Expansions**

**Core move:** Apply algebraic identities and the binomial theorem to efficiently identify and calculate the coefficient of a specified term in a polynomial expansion.

**Seen in / context:**
- Binomial theorem applications.
Problems involving products of polynomials.
Series expansions where a specific power's coefficient is sought.
Combinatorial counting related to polynomial terms.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to simplify the expression algebraically before applying the binomial theorem.
Incorrectly identifying the power of 'x' in the general term of the expansion (e.g., confusing x^r with x^(2r)).
Errors in calculating the binomial coefficient (nCr).
Sign errors when one of the terms in the binomial is negative.
Attempting full expansion when only a specific coefficient is needed, leading to unnecessary calculations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Always look for opportunities to simplify the base of the binomial expansion using algebraic identities (e.g., difference of squares, perfect squares/cubes).
Pay close attention to the power of the variable within the binomial term (e.g., (1+ax^k)^n will have terms of x^(kj)).
A coefficient can be zero if the desired power of x does not appear in the expansion.
Be mindful of multiple factors and how they contribute to the desired power of x if simplification is not possible.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q85_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_6338643e. Determine Shape Dimensions from Partial Vertices**

**Core move:** Apply coordinate geometry and geometric principles to identify all valid configurations of a shape given partial vertex information, then calculate and compare their dimensions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding side lengths, areas, or perimeters of squares, rectangles, or triangles given two or three vertices.
Problems involving circles defined by points on their circumference or center.
Determining the range of possible sizes for a geometric shape based on limited vertex information.
Geometric locus problems where points define a boundary.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming a single configuration for how the given points relate to the shape (e.g., always adjacent vertices, or always opposite diagonal points).
Incorrectly applying distance formulas or fundamental geometric relationships (e.g., diagonal-to-side ratio in a square).
Making algebraic errors when simplifying radicals or comparing numerical magnitudes.
Failing to consider all possible valid geometric arrangements of the given points within the specified shape.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage visualizing or sketching the different possible geometric configurations to aid understanding.
Ensure all possible interpretations of the given points relative to the shape's definition are systematically considered.
The calculations typically involve the distance formula, sometimes midpoint formula, slopes, and basic Pythagorean relationships or trigonometric ratios for common geometric shapes.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_63470767. Quantifying and Comparing Biological Properties**

**Core move:** Compute specific numerical values for biological components by applying given rates and proportions, and simultaneously evaluate qualitative comparisons between biological states using factual knowledge.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating molecular counts or concentrations in cells.
Determining population demographics or disease prevalence rates.
Comparing physiological parameters (e.g., heart rate, blood oxygen) across different conditions or locations in the body.
Quantifying energy flow in ecosystems and comparing efficiencies.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Arithmetic errors, especially with large numbers or scientific notation.
Misinterpreting percentages or ratios.
Applying incorrect biological facts for comparisons (e.g., reversing the order of saturation).
Failing to break down the problem into distinct quantitative and qualitative parts.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often combine a direct calculation with a conceptual understanding check rooted in biology.
The calculation part can involve simple multiplication, division, or percentages.
The comparison part relies on core biological knowledge, often related to physiological processes or anatomy.
Ensure both aspects of the question are addressed for a complete answer.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_63a3ced2. Relating Dimensions of 3D Shapes via Properties**

**Core move:** Formulate and solve an algebraic equation by applying established geometric formulas for surface area or volume of 3D objects, relating their dimensions according to given conditions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Comparing surface areas or volumes of different 3D shapes
Problems involving scaling dimensions and observing effects on area/volume
Optimization problems where a relationship between dimensions needs to be established

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrect recall or application of surface area/volume formulas (e.g., cylinder lateral area, cube face area)
Algebraic errors in simplifying or solving the equation for the unknown dimension
Confusing surface area with volume or vice versa
Incorrectly handling constants like pi or squared terms

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all relevant geometric formulas are either provided or are expected prior knowledge.
The algebraic manipulation should be challenging enough to test precision but not overly complex.
Often involves expressing one variable in terms of another, leading to a ratio or an expression.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying established geometric formulas for the surface areas of a sphere and a cylinder, setting up an algebraic equation to relate their dimensions based on the given condition (equal surface areas), and then solving this equation for R in terms of r. This directly aligns with the core move of schema M_63a3ced2.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires using the properties of similar objects, specifically the relationship between their surface areas and volumes (Area ratio = k^2, Volume ratio = k^3), to establish an algebraic equation relating the volumes and then solving it. This directly aligns with 'Formulate and solve an algebraic equation by applying established geometric formulas for surface area or volume of 3D objects, relating their dimensions according to given conditions.'
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q38_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying established geometric formulas for surface area and volume of a 3D object (a tube/cylinder) and then relating these properties (SA and V) by calculating their ratio. This directly aligns with the core move of applying formulas for 3D objects and relating their dimensions/properties.

## **M_66cabcc8. Quantifying Reaction Products via Calorimetry and Stoichiometry**

**Core move:** Determine the quantity (mass or volume) of a specific reaction product by first calculating the heat exchanged using calorimetric data, then relating this heat to the moles of reactant via the given enthalpy change, and finally applying reaction stoichiometry and appropriate conversion factors.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chemical reactions where heat is absorbed or released (exothermic/endothermic).
Calorimetry experiments where temperature changes in a surrounding medium are measured.
Calculations involving gas volumes at specified conditions (e.g., RTP).
Problems combining thermochemistry and stoichiometry.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the sign of enthalpy change (ΔH) or confusing heat released by reaction with heat absorbed by water.
Failing to write or balance the chemical equation correctly, leading to incorrect mole ratios.
Using incorrect units for heat capacity, mass, temperature, or molar volume, or failing to convert between J and kJ.
Mixing up the reactant for which ΔH is given with another substance when determining moles.
Ignoring the assumptions made in the problem (e.g., all heat transferred to water only).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary physical constants (specific heat capacity, molar volume of gas) are provided or can be assumed from context.
The reaction's stoichiometry must be deducible from the information given or general chemical knowledge (e.g., group number of a metal).
Emphasize careful unit tracking throughout the calculation process.
Questions can vary the unknown, asking for ΔH, initial mass of reactant, or final temperature, but the core sequence of steps remains similar.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_66dd1b9b. Inferring Quantity from Combinatorial Probability**

**Core move:** Derive an unknown quantity within a set by forming and solving an equation based on a given probability calculated from combinations of selections without replacement.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving drawing items (e.g., sweets, balls, cards) from a finite collection.
Situations where the total number of items or the number of items of a specific type is unknown.
Questions where a probability of a specific outcome of selection (without replacement) is given, and an initial quantity needs to be determined.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing combinations (order doesn't matter) with permutations (order matters).
Incorrectly setting up the probability fraction (e.g., numerator and denominator errors).
Making algebraic errors when solving the resulting equation, particularly quadratic equations.
Assuming independence of events when selections are made without replacement.
Forgetting that the inferred quantity must be a non-negative integer.

**Notes for generation:**
- The derived probability expression will typically involve combinations (nCr).
The equation formed by equating the derived probability to the given probability often results in a quadratic or higher-order polynomial equation.
Ensure the question clearly states 'at random' and implies 'without replacement' for this schema to apply.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q15_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_67471921. Quantitative Chemical Calculation and Qualitative Classification**

**Core move:** Apply specific chemical formulas or principles to calculate quantitative values and determine qualitative features of chemical species or reactions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating thermodynamic properties (e.g., lattice enthalpy, enthalpy of reaction, bond energy) using empirical or theoretical equations.
Determining oxidation states of elements within compounds.
Classifying chemical reactions (e.g., redox, acid-base, precipitation) based on changes in properties or composition.
Using given data (radii, charges, concentrations) to feed into chemical models.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly substituting values into a given formula or using the wrong constants.
Errors in basic arithmetic or unit conversions during calculations.
Misidentifying oxidation states due to incorrect charge balancing or knowledge of common ion charges.
Confusing definitions for different types of chemical reactions (e.g., oxidation vs. reduction).

**Notes for generation:**
- The necessary formula or principle is usually provided or is a standard one expected to be known at the relevant educational level.
Accuracy in calculation and clear demonstration of steps are often required.
Classification tasks require precise application of chemical definitions and rules.
Parts of the question may build upon each other (e.g., calculation followed by interpretation) or be somewhat independent.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_6959e5e9. Analyzing Surface Area to Volume Ratio Scaling**

**Core move:** Explain how the surface area to volume ratio of 3D objects changes with their linear dimensions, applying scaling principles derived from geometric formulas.

**Seen in / context:**
- In biology, understanding metabolic rates, heat regulation, nutrient absorption, and waste excretion in relation to organism size.
In engineering, designing structures where surface area for exchange (e.g., heat sinks, catalysts) is critical relative to volume.
In physics, studying general scaling laws and their implications.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming that both surface area and volume increase proportionally with size, leading to a constant ratio.
Confusing the direct proportionality of surface area to L^2 and volume to L^3 with the inverse proportionality of the ratio SA:V to L.
Failing to link the mathematical scaling to its biological or physical implications.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions typically require a comparison between objects of different sizes or shapes.
The answer may be qualitative (e.g., 'smaller organisms have a higher ratio') or quantitative if specific dimensions are given.
Often involves interpreting or explaining a phenomenon based on this mathematical principle.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q39_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_69fc19a4. Calculating Enthalpy Changes Using Hess's Law**

**Core move:** Manipulate given standard enthalpy changes of constituent reactions to determine the enthalpy change of a target reaction using Hess's Law.

**Seen in / context:**
- Thermochemistry problems
Energy cycles (Hess cycles)
Born-Haber cycles
Calculating standard enthalpy of formation, combustion, or reaction from other known values

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly reversing the sign of ΔH when reversing a reaction
Forgetting to multiply ΔH when multiplying the stoichiometric coefficients of a reaction
Adding instead of subtracting, or vice-versa, when combining reactions in the cycle
Not ensuring all intermediate species cancel out to leave only the target reaction

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the target equation is clearly identified.
Emphasize the systematic manipulation (reversing, multiplying) of given equations and their enthalpy changes.
The use of a Hess cycle diagram or step-by-step algebraic manipulation of equations should be demonstrated.
Values should allow for clear calculation, possibly resulting in whole numbers or simple fractions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: Part k) of the question explicitly asks to calculate a standard enthalpy change by constructing an energy cycle, which is a direct application of Hess's Law. This perfectly matches the core move of manipulating standard enthalpy changes to determine a target reaction's enthalpy change.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires the application of Hess's Law to combine given standard enthalpy changes of constituent reactions to determine the enthalpy change for a target reaction, which perfectly matches the core move described in schema M_69fc19a4.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q34_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires calculating the enthalpy change for the sublimation of iodine by combining the given standard enthalpy of formation of hydrogen iodide (from solid iodine), and the enthalpy of reaction for hydrogen iodide formation (from gaseous iodine) which is derived from bond enthalpy values. This process involves manipulating these constituent enthalpy changes using Hess's Law to determine the target enthalpy change.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the enthalpy change of formation for calcium chloride by manipulating a series of given enthalpy changes for constituent reactions and then summing them, which is a direct application of Hess's Law as described in the core move of M_69fc19a4.

## **M_6b0a0838. Analyzing Mendelian Ratios with Selection**

**Core move:** Apply Mendelian inheritance principles, incorporating selection pressures (e.g., lethal alleles), to determine the altered genotype and phenotype ratios in a surviving population.

**Seen in / context:**
- Genetics problems involving lethal alleles
Scenarios where certain genotypes fail to develop or reproduce
Calculating population allele frequencies after selection events

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to exclude non-surviving genotypes from ratio calculations
Applying standard Mendelian ratios (e.g., 3:1 or 1:2:1) without adjustment for selection
Confusing sex-linkage with autosomal inheritance when given specific information
Incorrectly calculating allele frequencies from genotype frequencies in a selected population

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure to clearly identify the genotypes that are removed from the population due to selection
Ratios and percentages must be recalculated based on the *remaining* viable genotypes/phenotypes
Pay attention to whether the question asks about individuals (genotypes/phenotypes) or alleles within the population
Be mindful of how gene location (autosomal vs. sex chromosome) impacts inheritance patterns and ignore irrelevant gender information if the gene is autosomal

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q69_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q77_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying Mendelian inheritance principles (heterozygous cross) to evaluate possible genotype and phenotype ratios within a resulting 'young adult fly population'. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of analyzing Mendelian ratios in a surviving population, even if specific selection pressures are not explicitly detailed, the 'could be correct' phrasing implies considering real-world outcomes that might deviate from ideal ratios, often due to factors like selection.

## **M_6ba9782e. Interpreting Structured Numerical Data**

**Core move:** Systematically navigate a given grid or table of numbers to extract specific values or enumerate elements based on defined numerical or positional criteria.

**Seen in / context:**
- Identifying elements by atomic number or position on a periodic table
Retrieving specific values from a data matrix or spreadsheet
Counting items within a specified range or category in a table
Answering questions about sequences or patterns within a dataset

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Miscounting due to irregular table structure (e.g., skipping lanthanoids/actinoids or the 57-71/89-103 numbering)
Incorrectly applying range or criteria for extraction
Assuming patterns where none exist or misinterpreting explicit rules
Overlooking the explicit 'atomic number' and 'symbol' labels

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly state what information needs to be extracted or how it should be counted/analyzed
The dataset itself is the primary source of information, requiring careful reading
Can be combined with other mathematical operations once data is extracted
Ensure the complexity is appropriate for a 'Maths' question, e.g., asking for simple counting, range, or sequence properties, rather than pure chemical properties

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_6c393b6b. Stoichiometric Analysis in Titrations**

**Core move:** Determine unknown reactant properties or identities by interpreting quantitative titration data, and then apply stoichiometric principles to calculate quantities of products or other reactants in related reactions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Acid-base titrations to find unknown concentrations, volumes, or molar ratios.
Precipitation titrations or redox titrations where stoichiometry is key.
Problems requiring the identification of an unknown substance (e.g., monoprotic vs. diprotic acid, unknown metal) from titration results.
Multi-step problems where initial titration data informs subsequent calculations.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating moles (e.g., forgetting to convert cm³ to dm³ or using incorrect units).
Misinterpreting the stoichiometric ratio from the balanced equation or titration data.
Assuming a 1:1 molar ratio when the actual reaction stoichiometry differs.
Failing to correctly identify the nature of the unknown reactant (e.g., mistaking a diprotic acid for a monoprotic one).
Applying an incorrect formula for mole calculations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure distinct stages of calculation and deduction are required.
Include an unknown aspect (reactant identity, stoichiometry) that needs to be determined from the initial data.
The problem should then leverage this determined information for a subsequent calculation.
Answers often involve specific quantities of products, requiring precise calculations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q50_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_6d49e166. Comparing Biological Dimensions with Unit Conversion and Scaling**

**Core move:** Convert biological dimensions across various units and scales, including magnification, to accurately compare and identify structures.

**Seen in / context:**
- Microscopy calculations (determining actual size from magnified images)
Comparing the relative sizes of different biological structures (e.g., cells, organelles, blood vessels)
Interpreting anatomical measurements presented in diverse units.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Errors in unit conversion (e.g., confusing µm with mm, or incorrect powers of 10)
Incorrectly applying magnification (e.g., multiplying by magnification instead of dividing to find actual size, or vice versa)
Failing to convert all given values to a common unit before making comparisons.
Misinterpreting radius versus diameter in calculations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure a range of common units are used (e.g., m, cm, mm, µm, nm).
Include scenarios where both radius and diameter are mentioned to test careful reading.
Magnification factors should span several orders of magnitude.
The biological context should be clear but not overly complex, allowing the focus to remain on the dimensional calculations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q55_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_6d4ca1f8. Deduce Organic Structure from Quantitative Reactions**

**Core move:** Deduce the structure or molecular formula of an unknown organic compound by quantitatively analyzing its reactions and applying principles of stoichiometry, redox chemistry, and functional group behavior.

**Seen in / context:**
- Identifying unknown compounds from experimental data (e.g., combustion analysis, reactions with specific reagents, titration results).
Calculating molar mass or empirical/molecular formula using gas volumes, masses, or concentrations.
Determining the extent of oxidation/reduction based on reactant quantities (e.g., amount of oxidizing agent consumed).
Linking stoichiometric ratios to the specific reactivity of functional groups.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying stoichiometric ratios (e.g., for gas evolution or electron transfer).
Making errors in calculations involving moles, mass, volume, or concentration.
Misinterpreting the reaction behavior or oxidation state changes of organic functional groups.
Failing to integrate all pieces of quantitative and qualitative information to narrow down possibilities.
Neglecting to consider all possible isomers that fit partial criteria.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions typically provide multiple pieces of experimental data that must be reconciled.
Requires a strong understanding of both quantitative chemical calculations and qualitative organic reaction principles.
Options often include compounds with similar molecular formulas or partial reactivities, requiring precise analysis.
Often involves interpreting electron changes in redox reactions for organic compounds.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q39_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_6d926509. Balancing Moments for Equilibrium**

**Core move:** Apply the principle of moments (sum of clockwise moments equals sum of anticlockwise moments) to determine unknown forces, masses, or positions required for rotational equilibrium.

**Seen in / context:**
- Levers and beams in static equilibrium
Finding the centre of mass of an irregular object by balancing
Problems involving torque and rotational equilibrium

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the pivot point.
Calculating distances from the wrong reference point (e.g., from an end of the rule instead of the pivot).
Forgetting to account for the mass of the rule itself (if uniform).
Mixing up clockwise and anticlockwise moments.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the rule/lever's own weight/mass is considered if it's uniform or has a known centre of mass.
Clearly define the pivot point for moment calculations.
Units must be consistent (e.g., cm for distance, kg for mass, Nm for moment).
Questions can be extended to include multiple forces or objects.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q9_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_6e1820ed. Identifying Biological Structures from Diagrams**

**Core move:** Accurately name specific anatomical or biological structures by visually recognizing them in a provided diagram.

**Seen in / context:**
- Anatomy and Physiology exams
Practical lab identification tasks
Questions requiring recall of biological morphology

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing similar-looking structures
Misinterpreting the orientation or context of the diagram
Lacking foundational knowledge of biological terminology

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure diagrams are clear and unambiguous.
Vary the level of detail required for identification (e.g., whole organ vs. specific tissue/cell type).
Can be combined with questions about function or location.
Questions might present structures with numerical labels and ask to match them to a list of names.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q70_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_6e4fd9a6. Analyzing Circuit Duration from Cell Energy Capacity**

**Core move:** Calculate the power dissipated in various circuit configurations and use the total energy capacity of the power sources to determine the operational duration.

**Seen in / context:**
- Circuits with combinations of series and parallel resistors and cells.
Cells with a specified total energy capacity.
Problems comparing the lifetime of different circuit arrangements.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating equivalent resistance for series or parallel components.
Incorrectly calculating total voltage or current for series/parallel cells.
Misunderstanding how to combine the energy capacity of multiple cells (e.g., if two cells each have E energy, total available is 2E).
Confusing total power for the circuit with power dissipated by a single resistor.
Applying the wrong power formula (e.g., P=IR instead of P=I²R or P=V²/R).
Failing to connect power (rate of energy use) to total energy and time (E=Pt).

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly define voltage (V), resistance (R), current (I), power (P), energy (E), and time (t) for calculations.
Pay attention to whether cells are in series (voltages add) or parallel (voltage remains the same, current capacity adds/shares the load).
Ensure consistent units throughout the calculations.
The problem often involves comparing relative durations, so a base case (e.g., t1) can be defined and other times expressed as multiples or fractions of it.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q11_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_70189334. Identifying Missing Mathematical Problems**

**Core move:** Determine if the provided text constitutes a complete and solvable mathematical problem suitable for analysis.

**Seen in / context:**
- Reviewing exam questions for validity
Automated problem classification and parsing
Initial assessment of problem statements

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Attempting to extract numerical information from non-problematic text (e.g., page numbers, dates)
Mistaking formatting, headers, or instructions for the actual problem content
Inferring a problem when explicit details are entirely absent

**Notes for generation:**
- This schema is a meta-level check, preceding the application of specific mathematical problem-solving schemas.
It addresses cases where the input is clearly not a mathematical question or is incomplete.
Requires clear criteria for what constitutes a 'valid' problem statement.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q80_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is presented as a 'Maths question' but is in fact a set of administrative instructions for an exam paper, not a solvable mathematical problem. This directly aligns with the core move of determining if the provided text constitutes a complete and solvable mathematical problem suitable for analysis.

## **M_7027d4f3. Pythagorean Theorem in Related Right Triangles**

**Core move:** Apply the Pythagorean theorem to multiple interconnected right-angled triangles to establish and solve a system of algebraic equations for unknown side lengths.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving geometric figures composed of several right-angled triangles sharing common sides or vertices.
Questions where side lengths are related by given ratios, sums, or differences across different right-angled triangles.
Situations requiring the calculation of unknown dimensions in multi-stage right-angled triangle constructions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the hypotenuse or legs in each right-angled triangle.
Making algebraic errors when setting up or solving the system of equations.
Failing to recognize all relevant right-angled triangles within the given figure.
Ignoring or misinterpreting the given relationships (e.g., ratios) between segments.
Confusing side lengths between different triangles.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem necessitates at least two distinct applications of the Pythagorean theorem.
Incorporate a clear relationship (e.g., a ratio, a sum, a difference) between common or related side lengths to form simultaneous equations.
Design questions such that the solution involves solving a system of algebraic equations, often quadratic.
Vary the visual complexity of the geometric arrangement of the triangles.
The final answer can be a numerical value, or an expression involving radicals.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_710e5702. Calculating Particle Velocity in Slit Selectors**

**Core move:** Apply kinematic and rotational motion principles to determine the linear velocity of particles filtered by a rotating slit system.

**Seen in / context:**
- Physics problems involving velocity selectors or choppers.
Rotational motion applications in experimental setups.
Molecular beam experiments requiring specific speed selection.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to convert angular displacement from degrees to radians.
Not converting linear distance to consistent SI units (e.g., cm to m).
Confusing frequency (f) with angular frequency (ω) or vice-versa without the 2π factor.
Incorrectly relating the time of flight to the angular rotation of the slits.

**Notes for generation:**
- The problem often requires a clear understanding of the time required for a particle to travel between slits and the corresponding angular rotation of the slits.
Pay attention to the specific geometry and number of slits (e.g., single slit, double slit, multiple slits) as it affects the angular condition.
Ensure all units are consistent before performing calculations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_713af0f5. Calculate Composite Area from Basic Geometric Shapes**

**Core move:** Decompose a complex geometric region into simpler, known shapes (e.g., squares, triangles, sectors, segments) and sum or subtract their individual areas to find the total area.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving shaded regions within larger figures.
Questions requiring the area of a region bounded by a mix of straight lines and curves (e.g., circles, arcs).
Design problems where the area of a non-standard shape needs to be determined.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misidentifying the component shapes or their boundaries.
Using incorrect area formulas for component shapes (e.g., sector area, triangle area).
Double-counting or omitting parts of the area.
Incorrectly identifying radii or angles for circular sectors/segments.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage drawing diagrams and clearly labeling all relevant dimensions and angles.
Often involves subtracting the area of 'unshaded' regions from a larger, simpler containing shape.
Pay close attention to definitions of points (midpoint, center) and the specific nature of arcs (centre, start/end points).
Solutions typically involve constants like π and algebraic terms based on given dimensions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_717df325. Deducing Chemical Formulas from Reaction Stoichiometry**
