**Core move:** Determine the empirical or molecular formula of an unknown substance by quantitatively analyzing a chemical reaction's reactants and known products, applying mole conversions and the conservation of elements.

**Seen in / context:**
- Decomposition or synthesis reactions with incomplete product information.
Experiments involving gravimetric analysis or gas volume measurements.
Problems requiring the determination of empirical formulae from elemental analysis by difference.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Miscalculating moles from given masses or volumes (e.g., using atomic mass instead of molecular mass, incorrect gas molar volume).
Incorrectly applying the law of conservation of mass or elements.
Errors in determining the simplest whole-number ratio for the empirical formula.
Assuming the identity of the unknown without calculation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide sufficient quantitative data (masses, volumes, concentrations) for all known species or for the initial reactant.
Specify conditions for gas measurements (e.g., rtp, stp) if relevant.
The resulting formula should be chemically plausible.
Can be a multi-step problem involving other stoichiometric calculations like balancing equations (as in part b of this question).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q16_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_720e227f. Calculating Total Combinations from Independent Choices**

**Core move:** Determine the total number of possible outcomes by multiplying the number of options for each independent choice or component.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating the number of possible gametes due to independent assortment of chromosomes.
Determining the number of possible sequences or arrangements when each position has independent options (e.g., password combinations, licence plates).
Counting the total number of different selections when making choices from multiple distinct categories (e.g., menu choices, outfit combinations).
Calculating the total number of states in systems with independent binary or multi-state components.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Adding the number of choices for each component instead of multiplying them.
Confusing combinations with permutations when the order of individual choices within the overall outcome does not matter.
Overlooking or miscounting the number of options for each independent choice.

**Notes for generation:**
- This pattern often leads to calculations of the form k^n, where 'k' is the number of options for each independent choice, and 'n' is the number of independent choices.
The key is identifying truly independent choices and their respective number of options.
Distinguish this from situations where selections are made 'without replacement' or where order explicitly matters for the final outcome (permutations).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_72677c80. Applying Periodic Trends for Group Elements**

**Core move:** Infer the relative property of a specific element within a periodic group by applying established periodic trends to its position.

**Seen in / context:**
- Solubility trends of compounds (e.g., Group 2 hydroxides, sulfates).
Reactivity trends of metals (e.g., Group 1, Group 2 with water/oxygen).
Acidity/basicity trends of oxides or hydroxides.
Thermal stability trends of carbonates or nitrates.
Ionization energy or atomic radius trends down a group or across a period.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing or reversing general periodic trends.
Applying trends incorrectly across different properties (e.g., hydroxide solubility vs. sulfate solubility).
Failing to identify the correct element or compound based on the described property.
Ignoring the specified range of elements for comparison.

**Notes for generation:**
- Focus on a specific group or period and a well-defined chemical or physical property.
Ensure the trend is generally consistent, or explicitly mention exceptions if relevant.
Questions can ask to identify an element, predict a property, or explain the underlying reason for the trend.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: The core of this question relies on inferring the elements based on their periodic properties (electron affinity and first ionisation energy) using established periodic trends across periods and down groups. Once the elements are identified, the subsequent steps of determining the product and its molar mass are straightforward calculations.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q31_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires using solubility data of Group 2 sulfates to infer the position of element X relative to Calcium, and then applying periodic trends to determine the relative thermal stability of XCO3. This directly aligns with the core move of inferring relative properties of group elements by applying established periodic trends.

## **M_72682ff8. Identifying Insufficient Problem Information**

**Core move:** Determine if a given mathematical problem contains all the necessary data and a clear query to be solvable.

**Seen in / context:**
- Word problems where a critical variable or quantity is omitted
Geometric problems lacking specific dimensions or angle measures
Mathematical reasoning tasks where conditions for a solution are not fully stated
Analyzing experimental data that is incomplete

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming missing values or relationships without explicit mention
Attempting to solve the problem by introducing arbitrary variables or making unjustified assumptions
Failing to recognize that the problem cannot be solved with the information provided
Confusing a challenging problem with one that is genuinely incomplete

**Notes for generation:**
- The question provided ('30' followed by many lines of dots) is incomplete and does not contain any mathematical problem statement or query to analyze. Therefore, no existing problem-solving schema can be applied.
The generated schema addresses the situation where the core 'problem' itself is missing or critically incomplete.
When generating similar questions, ensure the missing information is critical and not implicitly inferable, making the problem genuinely unsolvable without further data.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_75022e20. Calculating Time of Flight in TOF Mass Spectrometry**

**Core move:** Apply the relationships between kinetic energy, mass, velocity, and distance to determine or compare time of flight for accelerated particles.

**Seen in / context:**
- Time-of-flight mass spectrometry (TOF-MS) calculations.
Comparison of travel times for different ions or isotopes in a drift region.
Problems involving particles accelerated by electric fields where kinetic energy is converted from potential energy.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming time is directly proportional to mass (t ∝ m) or inversely proportional to mass (t ∝ 1/m) instead of the square root.
Incorrectly relating kinetic energy (KE = 1/2 mv^2) to velocity or mass.
Forgetting that all ions of the same charge are typically accelerated to the same kinetic energy in a TOF-MS system (assuming negligible initial kinetic energy).
Making algebraic errors when manipulating square roots in proportionality calculations.

**Notes for generation:**
- The key is to establish that the kinetic energy (KE) gained by all ions with the same charge (q) accelerated through the same potential difference (V) is constant (KE = qV).
Then, derive the velocity v = sqrt(2KE/m) and subsequently the time of flight t = d/v = d * sqrt(m/2KE).
This leads to the crucial proportionality t ∝ sqrt(m) when the distance (d) and kinetic energy (KE) are constant.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q31_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_752d0ebb. Comparing Gas Diffusion and Kinetic Properties**

**Core move:** Apply Graham's Law of Diffusion and the Kinetic Theory of Gases to compare the relative speeds, diffusion times, and average kinetic energies of different gases under varying temperature and mass conditions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Experiments on gas diffusion or effusion rates.
Understanding the molecular basis of gas properties (speed, energy, pressure).
Applications involving ideal gas behavior and molecular motion.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the inverse square root relationship for diffusion rates or direct square root for diffusion times with respect to mass.
Confusing relative atomic mass with relative molecular mass, especially for diatomic gases, when determining particle mass for diffusion calculations.
Misunderstanding the concept of average kinetic energy versus individual particle kinetic energy at a given temperature.
Incorrectly interpreting the effect of temperature on average kinetic energy or particle speeds (e.g., higher temperature leading to lower KE).
Assuming that relative diffusion rates or detection orders change with temperature.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly specifies the mass to be used (atomic or molecular) to avoid ambiguity, as this is a common source of error.
Emphasize that the average kinetic energy of gas particles is directly proportional to the absolute temperature and is independent of the type of gas.
Highlight that the relative order of diffusion rates (and thus detection times) for different gases depends only on their masses and remains constant regardless of the absolute temperature, provided conditions are uniform.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q31_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_7623e2ea. Work Done in Elastic Deformation**

**Core move:** Apply the relationships between stress, strain, Young's modulus, and geometric properties to calculate the work done (or elastic potential energy stored) during the elastic deformation of a material.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating energy stored in springs or wires
Problems involving stress-strain graphs where work done is the area under the curve
Determining the work done by an external force to stretch or compress an elastic object

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing stress with force, or strain with extension
Forgetting the factor of 1/2 in the work done formula (W = 1/2 FΔL or W = 1/2 Stress × Strain × Volume), treating force as constant
Incorrectly converting units (e.g., Pa, m, J, kJ)
Misapplying formulas for work done by constant force instead of work done by a varying elastic force

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all quantities are in consistent SI units before calculation.
The work done is equivalent to the elastic potential energy stored in the material, assuming deformation is within the elastic limit.
Remind students of the definitions: Stress = Force/Area, Strain = Extension/Original Length, Young Modulus = Stress/Strain.
The work done can be calculated as 1/2 * Force * Extension, or 1/2 * Stress * Strain * Volume (where Volume = Area * Original Length).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q0_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the elastic potential energy stored in an elastic cord due to its extension. The extension is determined by geometric properties (the radius of the ring). This directly aligns with the core move of applying relationships (like the elastic potential energy formula E = 1/2 kx^2) and geometric properties to calculate energy stored during elastic deformation.

## **M_76588e58. Inferring Right Triangle Properties for Area**

**Core move:** Deduce implicit properties of a right-angled triangle from partial information to enable calculation of its area using the Pythagorean theorem and the standard area formula.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems where only the hypotenuse or one side and perhaps one angle of a right-angled triangle are explicitly given, but a unique answer is expected.
Multiple-choice questions requiring a unique answer despite seemingly insufficient explicit information.
Geometric problems testing the understanding of implicit assumptions in problem statements.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Not recognizing the implication of 'hypotenuse' for a right-angled triangle.
Assuming arbitrary side lengths for the legs, leading to multiple possible areas when a unique answer is implied.
Incorrectly applying the Pythagorean theorem or the area formula for a triangle.
Errors in surd arithmetic or simplification of square roots.

**Notes for generation:**
- When a unique answer is expected from limited information about a right triangle, common implicit assumptions include: it is an isosceles right triangle (45-45-90), or it is a 30-60-90 triangle (if an angle or specific side ratio is hinted).
Emphasize that the term 'hypotenuse' strictly defines the triangle as being right-angled.
Ensure values for dimensions lead to solvable expressions for area.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_7771ad3d. Applying Biological Definitions to Scenarios**

**Core move:** Evaluate given biological scenarios or situations to determine if they exemplify a specific biological concept or process.

**Seen in / context:**
- Multiple-choice questions asking to identify examples of a process (e.g., natural selection, symbiosis, diffusion)
Questions asking to classify scenarios based on biological principles
Analyzing case studies or descriptions of ecological interactions

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting the core definition of the biological concept
Focusing on superficial elements of a scenario rather than its underlying biological mechanism
Confusing related but distinct biological concepts

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the definition provided (or assumed to be known) is clear and unambiguous.
Scenarios should be varied enough to test understanding beyond rote memorization.
Include distractors that superficially resemble the concept but lack a critical component.
The question should involve qualitative reasoning rather than quantitative calculation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q61_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_78346fd1. Analyzing Ratios and Counts in Biological Samples**

**Core move:** Calculate quantities like total component volume, average cell volume, or concentration within a biological sample using given cell counts, volume ratios, and unit conversions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining the proportion of different components in a biological mixture.
Calculating cell density or concentration in a given volume.
Finding the average size or mass of individual biological units from population data.
Analyzing the composition of bodily fluids like blood based on cell counts and volumetric ratios.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly interpreting ratios (e.g., mistaking a ratio of 2:3 for 2/3 of the total instead of 2/5).
Errors in unit conversions (e.g., between cm³, mm³, μm³).
Mistakes in calculations involving scientific notation.
Confusing the total volume of a cell type with the average volume of a single cell.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should involve at least two types of numerical data (e.g., cell count and a ratio, or two ratios).
Require clear application of proportional reasoning.
Opportunities for unit conversion between different powers of ten (e.g., cm to μm) should be present.
Focus should be on the quantitative aspects, rather than complex biological recall, to distinguish from purely biology schemas.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q42_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_786e1e97. Applying Biological Knowledge of Cells**

**Core move:** Recall and apply factual knowledge about the structural and functional properties of different cell types within a healthy human organism to evaluate given statements.

**Seen in / context:**
- Comparative anatomy/physiology questions at the cellular level
Questions about cell differentiation and specialization
Questions involving commonalities and differences among somatic cells
Evaluating statements on genetic material, organelles, or metabolic processes across different cell types

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing properties of prokaryotic vs. eukaryotic cells when the context is human cells.
Assuming all cells in a healthy human are identical.
Overlooking the 'healthy human' constraint and considering diseased states or mutations (unless explicitly allowed).
Incorrectly applying properties of specialized cells (e.g., neurons, erythrocytes) to generalize for all cell types.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should test understanding of cell theory, organelle functions, and cellular specialization.
Statements should require distinguishing between universal cellular properties and specialized ones.
The 'no mutations' clause simplifies the problem by removing genetic variability as a factor, focusing on normal physiological differences.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q79_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q62_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires recalling and applying specific factual biological knowledge about the cardiovascular system to evaluate given statements. This directly matches the 'Core Move' of 'Recall and apply factual knowledge... to evaluate given statements,' even though the schema title and specific mention in the core move highlight 'cell types,' as knowledge of organ systems often builds upon and involves understanding cellular properties.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q67_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires recalling factual knowledge about the DNA content of different cell types (gametes, somatic cells at various stages of the cell cycle) to interpret the given ratio and deduce the potential state of the removed cell. This directly aligns with applying biological knowledge about cell properties.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q77_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires recalling and applying specific biological factual knowledge about DNA structure (triplets, single-stranded DNA) and its properties to evaluate potential statements, which perfectly aligns with the core move of applying factual biological knowledge to evaluate given statements.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q80_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires recalling specific biological facts about human cell types (plasma cells, red blood cells) and the state of chromosomes during cell division (a cell about to start cytokinesis) to determine the chromosome count for each, and then summing these counts. This directly aligns with applying factual knowledge about cell properties.
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q23_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires recalling and applying factual knowledge about different cell types to identify them from provided figures, which directly aligns with the core move of applying biological knowledge of cells.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q48_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires recalling and applying factual biological knowledge about the relative sizes (diameters) of different structures within the human respiratory system (trachea, bronchus, bronchiole, alveolus) to order them or match them to given diameters. This directly aligns with the core move of applying factual knowledge about structural properties within a healthy human organism.

## **M_7968a109. Calculating Net Effect of Sequential Percentage Changes**

**Core move:** Apply multiplicative factors to a quantity to determine its final value after a series of percentage increases or decreases.

**Seen in / context:**
- Financial calculations involving interest, discounts, or inflation
Population dynamics (growth and decay rates)
Price adjustments in retail or economics
Compounding effects in various domains

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assuming that an increase and subsequent decrease of the same percentage cancel each other out (e.g., +10% then -10% results in no change).
Applying subsequent percentage changes to the original value instead of the new, intermediate value.
Misinterpreting percentage values (e.g., using 0.1 for a 10% increase instead of 1.1, or for a 10% decrease instead of 0.9).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly specifies whether percentages are applied to the original or subsequent values.
Vary the number and type (increase/decrease) of percentage changes.
Can include questions that ask for the original value given the final value and changes.
Can compare outcomes of different sequences of percentage changes.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_7a0da6ae. Analyzing Motion with Variable Acceleration**

**Core move:** Describe the qualitative changes in acceleration, velocity, and displacement by considering the varying net forces acting on an object during its motion.

**Seen in / context:**
- Motion involving elastic forces (springs, bungee ropes) where tension varies.
Motion with changing resistive forces like air resistance.
Conceptual questions requiring a description of how kinematic variables evolve over time or distance due to non-constant forces.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming constant acceleration throughout the entire motion.
Confusing zero acceleration with zero velocity, or vice versa.
Failing to identify critical points where forces or the nature of acceleration change (e.g., rope starts to stretch, maximum speed point, lowest point).
Incorrectly describing the direction of acceleration relative to velocity.

**Notes for generation:**
- Requires a strong understanding of Newton's second law (F=ma) and how varying forces impact acceleration.
Often involves breaking the motion into distinct phases (e.g., free fall, rope stretching).
Questions may be purely descriptive (as in part c) or include quantitative aspects where constant acceleration might apply for a specific phase (as potentially in part d).
Ensure the description covers the initial, intermediate, and final states of acceleration.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_7a67f0df. Analyzing Arithmetic Sequences from Nth Term Formula**

**Core move:** Deduce properties of an arithmetic sequence, such as its common difference and the position of a specific value, directly from its given algebraic nth term formula.

**Seen in / context:**
- Identifying the common difference (term-to-term rule) for linear sequences.
Finding the term number for a given term value.
Calculating a specific term value given its term number.
Basic algebraic manipulation (solving linear equations).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the coefficient of 'n' with the initial term or a different sequence property.
Incorrectly solving the linear equation to find the term number.
Mistaking the common difference for the first term.
Applying rules for non-arithmetic sequences (e.g., geometric or quadratic).

**Notes for generation:**
- The nth term formula for an arithmetic sequence is typically linear in 'n' (e.g., `an + b`), where 'a' is the common difference.
Questions may involve working forwards (finding a term's value) or backwards (finding a term's position).
Emphasize the distinction between the 'nth term formula' and the 'term-to-term rule'.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_7ba6d005. Simplifying Algebraic Rational Expressions**

**Core move:** Factorize quadratic expressions in numerators and denominators, then simplify algebraic fractions by cancelling common factors.

**Seen in / context:**
- High school algebra
Preparing for calculus (simplifying expressions before differentiation/integration)
Solving rational equations (after simplification)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly factoring quadratic expressions (e.g., wrong signs, incorrect coefficients).
Cancelling terms that are not factors (e.g., cancelling 'x' from x+2/x+3).
Errors in manipulating division of fractions (e.g., not inverting the second fraction).
Failing to fully factorize or simplify.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all quadratic expressions are factorable into linear terms.
Design factors to allow for significant cancellation.
Use difference of squares or perfect square trinomials for variety.
Check for restrictions on the variable (denominators cannot be zero).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_7c78e947. Solving for Unknowns in Selection Probabilities**

**Core move:** Formulate a probability equation for successive selections without replacement, often involving combinations, and solve the resulting algebraic equation for an unknown quantity.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving drawing cards from a deck without replacement.
Selecting items from a group where the composition changes after each selection.
Determining unknown group sizes or counts based on a given probability of selection.
Situations where the order of selection does not matter (combinations).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming events are independent (i.e., implicitly replacing items after selection).
Incorrectly calculating combinations or permutations for the numerator or denominator.
Errors in setting up the initial rational equation from the probability expression.
Algebraic mistakes when solving the resulting quadratic or higher-order equation.
Not considering practical constraints on the unknown variable (e.g., must be an integer, positive, within a logical range).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly states 'without replacement' or implies it through context (e.g., 'chosen at random' from a diminishing pool).
Vary the number of items selected and the characteristic being sought (e.g., at least one of a type, exactly one of a type).
The resulting algebraic equation should typically be solvable and yield a sensible answer for the unknown.
Questions can involve setting up the probability using either sequential conditional probabilities or combinations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q15_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_7cf3f483. Analyzing Data Trends in Investigations**

**Core move:** Evaluate statements about an experimental investigation by identifying patterns, trends, and specific values within a given numerical dataset.

**Seen in / context:**
- Experiments recording changes over time (e.g., reaction rates, growth rates, temperature changes).
Data presented as a list, table, or graph.
Questions requiring interpretation of initial/final states, maximum/minimum values, increasing/decreasing trends, or plateaus.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Extrapolating beyond the given data without justification.
Confusing specific data points with general trends or averages.
Assuming cause-and-effect relationships without additional experimental details.
Misinterpreting the meaning of 'arbitrary units' or similar non-standard units.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the dataset clearly illustrates a discernible trend (e.g., increase, decrease, plateau, fluctuation).
Statements should test the understanding of data analysis concepts like initial/final values, maximum/minimum, steady state, or rate of change.
Can be extended to include questions about identifying outliers or inconsistencies in more complex datasets.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q70_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q90_NSAA_Sec`: The question provides numerical data related to a biological process ('blood output in dm3 from the left ventricle of the heart each minute') and asks to evaluate statements based on this data. This directly aligns with the core move of identifying patterns, trends, and specific values within a given numerical dataset from an experimental investigation.

## **M_7dbc7974. Algebraic Probabilities in Overlapping Categories**

**Core move:** Translate given categorical data into algebraic expressions for counts of overlapping and non-overlapping subsets, form a probability ratio, and solve the resulting equation for an unknown variable.

**Seen in / context:**
- Survey data analysis involving choices or characteristics with overlaps (e.g., 'likes A', 'likes B', 'likes both', 'likes neither').
Venn diagram problems where the number of elements in each region is expressed algebraically.
Calculating the probability of specific subgroups when total population and subgroup sizes are dependent on an unknown.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting terminology such as 'only X' versus 'X' (where X includes 'both').
Incorrectly setting up the equations for the different regions of a Venn diagram or the sums of categories.
Algebraic errors when solving the equation derived from the probability statement.
Forgetting to ensure the sum of all disjoint categories equals the total group size.
Failing to recognize that probabilities must be between 0 and 1, leading to an incorrect interpretation of the problem if the result falls outside this range (or identifying a flaw in the problem statement itself).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem statement clearly defines 'total' and the various overlapping/non-overlapping subgroups.
The algebraic expressions should lead to a solvable linear or quadratic equation for the unknown.
When generating such questions, ensure the resulting probability is a valid value between 0 and 1, and that all counts (when the unknown is solved) are non-negative integers.
Can be extended to three categories for increased complexity, requiring careful setup of a three-set Venn diagram.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q14_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_7e1830fc. Counting Solutions Graphically for Transcendental Equations**

**Core move:** Determine the number of intersections of two functions' graphs by analyzing their properties and behavior within a specified interval.

**Seen in / context:**
- Equations mixing polynomial/linear/exponential/logarithmic functions with trigonometric functions (e.g., x = sin x, x^2 = cos x).
Equations where f(x) = g(x) and one or both functions are non-linear and not easily invertible.
Problems asking for the 'number of solutions' rather than the solutions themselves.
Equations involving functions with periodic behavior.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Attempting to solve the equation algebraically, leading to an impasse.
Incorrectly counting intersections due to overlooking periodicity or asymptotes.
Failing to consider the behavior of both functions (e.g., only sketching one).
Ignoring the bounds of the given interval, especially near the endpoints or asymptotes.
Misinterpreting the units of x (e.g., degrees vs. radians) when x appears both as an angle and as a coefficient/variable in a non-trigonometric part.
Missing the x=0 solution, if applicable, or incorrectly assuming symmetry for x=0 when it's not a general rule.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage sketching the graphs of both functions y = f(x) and y = g(x).
Focus on critical points, asymptotes, periodicity, and symmetry of each function.
Break down the interval into smaller, manageable parts (e.g., positive, negative, and zero; or intervals between asymptotes).
Emphasize careful consideration of the slopes and magnitudes of functions to determine if intersections occur in specific regions.
Questions often involve sin x = kx, cos x = kx, tan x = kx, or x^n = sin x, etc.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_7ee16792. Interpreting Qualitative Relationships**

**Core move:** Identify and articulate the directional relationship (positive, negative, or no correlation) between two described variables based on a given qualitative statement.

**Seen in / context:**
- Environmental science observations
Biological processes descriptions
Social science phenomena
Economic trends

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming a specific quantitative relationship (e.g., linear, proportional) when only a qualitative one is stated.
Inferring causation without explicit evidence of a causal link.
Misinterpreting the direction of the relationship (e.g., thinking 'decreases' when 'increases' is stated).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions using this schema should present statements describing how one variable changes in response to another.
The task might involve identifying the type of correlation, predicting a directional change, or summarizing the relationship.
Avoid introducing numbers or units unless the schema is specifically about quantitative relationships.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_7ff16a1f. Deducing Compound Properties and Reactions**

**Core move:** Deduce the chemical formula, bonding type, and electrolytic behavior of a compound based on its formation reaction and observed physical/chemical properties.

**Seen in / context:**
- Interpreting experimental observations (e.g., melting point, conductivity, electrolysis products).
Relating macroscopic properties to microscopic structure and bonding.
Predicting products of chemical reactions, including electrolysis.
Writing balanced chemical equations.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly balancing the chemical equation.
Confusing properties of solids vs. molten states for conductivity.
Misidentifying the type of bonding (e.g., ionic vs. molecular covalent) based on melting point or conductivity.
Incorrectly assigning products to electrodes during electrolysis (anode/cathode confusion).
Assuming all compounds of non-metals are covalent or all compounds involving metals are ionic without considering specific elements or properties.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide sufficient experimental data (e.g., melting point, conductivity, state) to allow for deduction.
The elements involved should give clear clues (e.g., metal + non-metal often implies ionic, but hydrogen can form both covalent and ionic hydrides).
Electrolysis aspects should clearly indicate molten or aqueous states as it affects product prediction.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q15_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q31_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires deducing the chemical formula of the anion (BF₄⁻) from quantitative elemental analysis (mass data), and then using this formula to determine its 'bonding type' (electron configuration for VSEPR) to infer a 'property' (the molecular shape). This directly aligns with the schema's core move of deducing chemical formula and properties based on observed data.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q38_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires deducing the structures of a series of organic compounds (P, Q, S, T, U) by interpreting a sequence of reactions, applying knowledge of functional group transformations, and utilizing observed chemical properties and tests (e.g., Tollens' reagent, sodium carbonate test, stereoisomerism). This directly aligns with the core move of deducing compound properties and reactions based on formation reactions and observed physical/chemical properties.

## **M_81162d8d. Analyzing Data Point Addition on Descriptive Statistics**

**Core move:** Determine the necessary or possible changes in mean, median, and range when a new data point is added to an existing dataset, based on its value relative to the original dataset's statistics.

**Seen in / context:**
- Evaluating the robustness of statistical measures against new data.
Understanding the impact of outliers on central tendency and spread.
Basic data analysis and interpretation exercises.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming all statistical measures (mean, median, range) will change in the same direction or always increase/decrease.
Incorrectly identifying the median after adding a data point, especially for an even number of terms.
Failing to consider all possible scenarios for where the new data point might fit within the existing sorted list (e.g., as a new minimum, new maximum, or somewhere in the middle).
Confusing 'must be true' with 'could be true'.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often require a comparison between the original statistics and the new ones.
A strong grasp of the definitions of mean, median, and range is essential.
It is often helpful to use small example datasets to test different scenarios and eliminate options.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q17_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_81ea8a2d. Comprehensive Stoichiometry with Deductive Reasoning**

**Core move:** Calculate an unknown quantity in a chemical reaction by first inferring the formula of a reactant and the balanced chemical equation from descriptive and quantitative information, then applying stoichiometric ratios and unit conversions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chemistry problems involving reactions of compounds with partially described properties (e.g., percentage composition, reaction products without full formulas).
Problems requiring the construction of chemical equations from verbal descriptions.
Quantitative calculations involving mass, moles, and volume in chemical reactions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly deducing the molecular formula of a compound from given elemental composition or mass percentages.
Failing to correctly balance the chemical equation based on the reaction description.
Errors in calculating molar masses of reactants or products.
Applying incorrect mole ratios from the unbalanced or incorrectly balanced equation.
Mistakes in converting between mass, moles, and volume (e.g., using incorrect density values or units).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all available information (percentage by mass, product descriptions, element conservation) is used to deduce formulas and balance equations.
The deduction of the compound's formula often precedes the balancing of the reaction equation.
Emphasize the step-by-step nature of the calculation, from formula deduction to equation balancing to stoichiometric conversion, and finally unit conversion.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_82f92a71. Analyzing Biomolecule Composition and Sequence Isomers**

**Core move:** Deduce the constituent monomers of a complex biomolecule using stoichiometric data and overall molecular mass, then determine the number of possible sequence isomers.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining amino acid composition of peptides from titration and hydrolysis data.
Analyzing carbohydrate composition from reaction data.
Calculating possible sequences for short polymers (peptides, oligonucleotides).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating moles or stoichiometric ratios.
Forgetting to account for water loss/gain during polymerization or hydrolysis.
Misidentifying the type or specific identity of monomers (e.g., acidic vs. neutral amino acids).
Incorrectly calculating permutations/combinations for sequence isomers, especially when identical monomers are present.
Failing to use provided Mr data (e.g., from options) to deduce specific monomer identities.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often require recall of common monomer structures and their molecular weights (or ability to calculate them from given Ar values).
Stoichiometry is crucial for determining the ratio or types of monomers present.
Combinatorics (permutations with repetition) is frequently needed for counting sequence isomers.
Options in multiple-choice questions can sometimes provide critical information (like Mr of the biomolecule) that helps to work backward to monomer identities.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q49_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_83f49809. Stoichiometric Mass-to-Mass Conversions**

**Core move:** Convert the mass of one chemical substance to the equivalent mass of another substance using molar masses and stoichiometric ratios derived from a balanced chemical equation.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chemical yield calculations in industrial processes
Quantitative analysis in chemistry experiments
Determining reactant/product quantities in balanced reactions
Environmental chemistry calculations involving specific compounds

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to balance the chemical equation correctly
Using incorrect molar masses for either substance
Applying the stoichiometric mole ratio inversely or incorrectly
Not converting units consistently (e.g., grams to kilograms)
Confusing empirical formula with molecular formula for molar mass

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary atomic masses are provided or readily calculable.
The relationship between the two substances (e.g., reaction, decomposition) must be clear.
Questions should typically involve multiple conversion steps (mass A -> moles A -> moles B -> mass B).
Vary the complexity by introducing limiting reactants, percentage yield, or impurities.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q14_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_89108f3b. Solving Basic Linear Inequalities**

**Core move:** Algebraically manipulate a given linear inequality to isolate the variable and determine its range.

**Seen in / context:**
- Equations and inequalities in algebra
Number line representation of solutions
Foundation for more complex inequalities (quadratic, rational)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Treating the inequality sign as an equality sign
Forgetting to reverse the inequality sign when multiplying or dividing by a negative number
Errors in basic arithmetic operations
Incorrectly transposing terms across the inequality sign
Misinterpreting the final inequality expression (e.g., confusing 'x < a' with 'x > a')

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the inequality contains only one variable for this basic level.
Operations (addition, subtraction, multiplication, division) are similar to solving linear equations, with the critical distinction for negative multipliers/divisors.
Solutions are typically expressed as a range or an interval.
Question values should lead to clear and unambiguous solutions from the provided options.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires setting up an inequality (nth term of S > nth term of T) and then algebraically manipulating this linear inequality to solve for 'n', ultimately finding the smallest integer value that satisfies the condition. This directly matches the core move of solving basic linear inequalities.

## **M_895cc926. Simplifying Rational Algebraic Expressions**

**Core move:** Factor quadratic or other polynomial expressions in the numerator and denominator, convert division of fractions to multiplication by the reciprocal, and then cancel common factors to simplify the rational expression.

**Seen in / context:**
- Algebraic fractions simplification problems
Solving equations involving rational expressions
Manipulating functions defined as rational expressions
Polynomial factorization

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly factoring quadratic or polynomial expressions (e.g., wrong signs or factor pairs)
Making errors when converting division to multiplication (e.g., not inverting the second fraction)
Attempting to cancel terms that are not common factors (e.g., cancelling 'x' from 'x+a' and 'x+b')
Algebraic errors in distributing or combining terms
Forgetting to consider restrictions on the variable that would make denominators zero

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure that the expressions provided can be factored, often into linear or quadratic factors with integer coefficients.
The problem should be designed so that common factors exist between the numerator and denominator (and across fractions when multiplying/dividing) to allow for simplification.
Vary the complexity by including polynomials of different degrees or those with leading coefficients other than one.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_89da9da1. Circuit Current After Component Change**

**Core move:** Apply Ohm's Law and rules for combining series and parallel resistors to determine the total circuit current after a component alteration.

**Seen in / context:**
- Electrical circuits (series, parallel, mixed configurations)
Analysis of circuit behavior with component changes (adding/removing resistors, changing voltage source)
Physics problems involving basic electricity and electronics

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly combining series and parallel resistors to find total resistance.
Forgetting to recalculate the total resistance after a circuit modification.
Applying Ohm's Law to an individual component's resistance instead of the total circuit resistance when determining total current.
Assuming a change in voltage when only resistance is altered (unless explicitly stated).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the initial and final circuit diagrams or descriptions are fully understood.
Identify which quantities (voltage, individual resistances) remain constant and which change.
Pay attention to units (Ohms, Volts, Amperes) and significant figures in calculations.
Problems often implicitly assume an ideal voltage source.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q15_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q15_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the total equivalent resistance of an infinite parallel combination of resistors (which involves summing an infinite geometric series of reciprocal resistances), and then applying Ohm's Law to find the total current through the cell. This directly aligns with the core move of applying Ohm's Law and rules for combining parallel resistors to determine the total circuit current after a specific component configuration.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q12_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires applying Ohm's Law and series circuit rules to calculate initial circuit parameters, then inferring the new circuit current after a component (LDR) effectively 'alters' its resistance due to changing light intensity, which is then used to find the change in the LDR's resistance.

## **M_8a8a55ac. Solving Systems of Linear Equations**

**Core move:** Formulate and solve a system of linear equations, typically through substitution or elimination, to determine unknown variable values.

**Seen in / context:**
- Word problems involving multiple unknown quantities with given relationships
Finding intersection points of lines in coordinate geometry
Problems in physics or engineering where multiple variables are linked by linear relationships

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly setting up the equations from the problem description
Algebraic errors during substitution or elimination
Solving for the wrong variable (e.g., solving for the cost of an apple when the cost of a pear is requested)
Failing to multiply all terms in an equation when scaling for elimination

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the number of independent equations matches the number of unknown variables for a unique solution
Encourage checking the solution by substituting values back into the original equations
Questions can vary difficulty by increasing the number of equations/variables or by making the setup more complex

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_8cbc919a. Analyzing Tangency Between Lines and Curves**

**Core move:** Apply conditions for tangency (equal gradients and points, or zero discriminant of combined equation) to find unknown parameters.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding the equation of a tangent or normal to a curve.
Determining parameters for which a line is tangent to a curve.
Investigating the nature of intersections between lines and curves (e.g., number of intersection points).
Optimization problems involving the shortest distance from a point to a curve where the tangent condition is implicitly used.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to set the discriminant of the resulting quadratic to zero for tangency.
Incorrectly calculating the derivative (gradient) of the curve.
Setting only the y-values equal without considering the gradient or discriminant.
Algebraic errors when solving the quadratic equation or the discriminant condition.
Confusing conditions for general intersection with conditions for specific tangency.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the curve is typically a quadratic or a function whose derivative is easily manageable for pre-university level questions.
Vary whether the unknown parameter is in the line equation or the curve equation.
Questions can also ask to find the point of tangency, or the equation of the tangent/normal itself.
For higher-degree curves, the equal gradients and equal points method is more general, while the discriminant method is specific to situations resulting in a quadratic equation (often when a line is tangent to a quadratic curve).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q77_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_8cc99940. Analyzing Physiological Changes During Exercise**

**Core move:** Infer changes in molecular concentrations within the circulatory system based on the metabolic demands and byproducts of active tissues.

**Seen in / context:**
- Circulatory system and gas exchange
Cellular respiration (aerobic and anaerobic)
Muscle physiology during strenuous activity
Transport of nutrients and waste products in blood

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing arteries and veins in terms of blood flow direction relative to an organ (e.g., thinking a vein carries blood *to* the muscle)
Incorrectly identifying the products or reactants of respiration (e.g., thinking oxygen concentration increases in venous blood from muscle)
Ignoring the difference between resting and actively contracting muscle metabolism
Forgetting about anaerobic respiration byproducts during intense exercise

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions can vary by type of tissue (e.g., lungs, liver, kidney) or by metabolic state (e.g., fed vs. fasted, rest vs. exercise).
Focus on molecules involved in energy production, waste removal, and nutrient delivery.
Consider both aerobic and anaerobic pathways, especially for intense activities.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q68_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q67_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires analyzing changes in molecular concentrations (glucose, oxygen, urea) as blood passes through various organs (kidney, small intestine, heart), based on their physiological functions (e.g., absorption, filtration, metabolism). This directly aligns with the core move of inferring changes in molecular concentrations within the circulatory system based on the metabolic demands and byproducts of active tissues/organs, even though the specific context is a meal rather than exercise.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q68_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires interpreting changes in aerobic and anaerobic respiration rates during exercise to infer subsequent physiological changes, such as alterations in molecular concentrations (e.g., lactate, CO2, O2) within the body, which directly aligns with the schema's core move.

## **M_8cf528ca. Mapping DNA Cut Sites from Fragment Lengths**

**Core move:** Deduce the relative positions of restriction enzyme cut sites on a circular DNA molecule by systematically analyzing fragment lengths from single and combined enzyme digests.

**Seen in / context:**
- Molecular biology laboratory techniques
Genetic engineering and biotechnology
Restriction mapping problems in genetics courses

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly determining the number of cut sites from the number of fragments (especially for circular DNA).
Failing to reconcile fragment lengths from single digests with those from double digests.
Misinterpreting how combined enzyme cuts alter existing fragments.
Incorrectly summing fragment sizes or calculating distances between sites.
Assuming linear DNA instead of circular when interpreting fragment data.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure total fragment length sums match the given total DNA length for all digest combinations.
The problem often requires drawing a circular map with labeled distances.
Can be extended to include more enzymes or more complex cutting patterns.
Emphasize the step-by-step process of comparing single and double digest results.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q32_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_8d593ba1. Analyzing Genetic and Phenotypic Outcomes of Cell Division and Mutation**

**Core move:** Deduce the genetic and phenotypic characteristics of daughter cells by applying principles of cell division and subsequent genetic alterations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Understanding the outcomes of mitosis vs. meiosis on genetic content.
Analyzing the impact of mutations on genotype, gene product function, and phenotype.
Distinguishing between genotype and phenotype, and conditions under which they differ.
Relating gene function (e.g., enzyme production) to cellular processes.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the outcomes of mitosis with meiosis (e.g., assuming genetic variation in mitotic daughter cells before mutation).
Assuming all mutations automatically lead to a visible phenotypic change.
Failing to recognize that a mutation occurring in one daughter cell does not inherently affect its sister cell.
Incorrectly defining or applying the concepts of genotype, phenotype, or chromosome structure.
Assuming a change in enzyme function necessitates a change in chromosome number or length.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly define the type of cell division (mitosis/meiosis) and the timing/nature of any mutation.
Emphasize the qualitative comparison of cells (e.g., identical, different, functional, non-functional).
Include conditions where a mutation may or may not affect phenotype (e.g., recessive, silent, no direct observable effect).
Ensure the question requires understanding the sequence of events and their cumulative impact.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q72_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q68_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires understanding how a genetic alteration (gene insertion) in stem cells affects their ability to divide (cell division) and differentiate, leading to a change in the phenotypic outcome (production of functional enzyme, increased white blood cell count). This directly aligns with the schema's core move of deducing genetic and phenotypic characteristics of daughter cells by applying principles of cell division and subsequent genetic alterations.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q66_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires deducing the genetic outcome (e.g., altered chromosome number) of cell division due to a specific chemical intervention that prevents chromosome separation, which directly aligns with applying principles of cell division to determine characteristics of daughter cells.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires deducing the genetic outcome (genotype of tetraploid cells) by applying principles of cell division (mitosis, chromosome doubling) and identifying the stage of cell division affected by a chemical agent, which constitutes analyzing genetic and phenotypic outcomes of cell division and subsequent alterations.

## **M_8dc34125. Identifying Biochemical Compounds via Qualitative Tests**

**Core move:** Infer the presence or absence of specific biomolecules in a solution by interpreting the expected outcomes of standard qualitative biochemical tests after chemical/enzymatic reactions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Enzyme assays to determine substrate/product presence
Food testing for nutrient composition (e.g., carbohydrates, proteins, fats)
Diagnostic tests in biology or medicine (e.g., testing for glucose in urine)
Identifying unknown organic compounds based on functional groups

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting the action of enzymes or other reactants (e.g., assuming amylase breaks down sucrose or that starch remains after complete reaction).
Confusing the positive and negative results/color changes for specific biochemical reagents (e.g., iodine, biuret, Benedict's).
Incorrectly classifying sugars as reducing vs. non-reducing.
Forgetting to account for all components initially present in the mixture or assuming enzymes are consumed in the reaction.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly state the initial components and reaction conditions (e.g., incubation temperature, time).
The expected color changes for positive/negative results of the tests should be standard knowledge or clearly implied.
May involve multiple reaction steps or conditions before the final testing stage.
Ensure a clear logical flow from the initial state, through any reactions, to the final composition, and then to the expected test results.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q41_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_8dd5c131. Predicting Organic Reaction Outcomes and Structures**

**Core move:** Predict the structures of products, intermediates, or conjugate bases in organic reactions or acid-base equilibria by applying principles of electronic effects, reaction mechanisms, and resonance stabilization.

**Seen in / context:**
- Identifying acidic or basic sites in organic molecules
Drawing resonance structures for intermediates or conjugate bases
Determining the major product of a named organic reaction (e.g., rearrangement, substitution)
Explaining regioselectivity or stereoselectivity based on mechanism or stability

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the most acidic proton due to neglecting resonance or inductive effects
Drawing an unstable or incorrect resonance structure for an anion or carbocation
Misapplying the mechanism of a known reaction, leading to an incorrect product structure (e.g., incorrect migratory group in rearrangements)
Failing to draw correct connectivity, formal charges, or stereochemistry in product structures

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should often require both an explanation of the underlying principle and the drawing of the resulting chemical structure(s).
Focus on common reaction types and stability principles taught in introductory to intermediate organic chemistry.
Ensure the complexity of the molecules and reactions is appropriate for the target level.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q16_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires identifying the structural formula of an organic product formed from a given reactant in the haloform reaction. This involves applying knowledge of organic reaction mechanisms and reactivity patterns to predict how the reactant's structure is transformed into the specified product (triiodomethane) and the other co-product (a carboxylic acid upon acidification). This aligns directly with the core move of predicting product structures in organic reactions based on mechanistic understanding.

## **M_8df750f2. Inferring Relative Strengths of Redox Agents**

**Core move:** Infer the relative strengths of oxidizing and reducing agents and their conjugate pairs by interpreting the directionality of a spontaneous redox reaction.

**Seen in / context:**
- Predicting if a redox reaction will occur spontaneously.
Explaining the observed direction of electron transfer in chemical reactions.
Ranking substances in terms of their reactivity as oxidizing or reducing agents.
Understanding the basis for standard electrode potentials and electrochemical series.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing oxidizing agents with reducing agents or vice versa.
Incorrectly identifying conjugate redox pairs.
Assuming that a spontaneous reaction implies the reverse is also spontaneous or equally strong.
Failing to understand that spontaneous reactions proceed from stronger agents to weaker agents.

**Notes for generation:**
- The problem statement must clearly indicate or imply the spontaneity of the reaction.
Options should test understanding of both oxidizing and reducing agent strengths, and their relationship to conjugate pairs.
Can be extended to problems involving standard electrode potentials (E° values) for quantitative comparisons.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q29_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_8e7863b0. Calculating Wave Properties from Relationships**

**Core move:** Apply fundamental equations relating wave speed, wavelength, frequency, period, phase difference, and path difference to solve for unknown wave parameters.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving transverse or longitudinal waves.
Questions about sound waves, light waves, or waves on a string.
Scenarios where multiple wave properties are given and one is unknown.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing period (T) with half-period (T/2) when given intervals between zero displacement or other specific points in the wave cycle.
Incorrectly applying the phase difference formula, e.g., mixing up path difference and time difference.
Using incorrect relationships between wave speed, frequency, wavelength, and period.
Failing to check if calculated values satisfy all given conditions (e.g., wavelength constraint).

**Notes for generation:**
- Carefully interpret descriptive information to derive fundamental wave properties (e.g., interval between zero displacements implies T/2).
Ensure consistent units for all calculations.
Commonly used formulas include v = fλ, f = 1/T, v = λ/T, Δφ = (2π/λ)Δx, and Δφ = (2π/T)Δt.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_8f3ec7eb. Analyzing Trends in Biological Data over Time**

**Core move:** Compare and contrast given numerical data points across different categories or time points to identify trends and evaluate the validity of descriptive statements.

**Seen in / context:**
- Changes in biological metrics (e.g., genetic diversity, population size, disease prevalence) over historical periods
Analyzing experimental data presented in tables or graphs to identify patterns
Evaluating the impact of interventions or natural phenomena based on before-and-after data

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting the direction or magnitude of trends (e.g., confusing 'increase' with 'reach highest point')
Extrapolating trends beyond the provided data range without justification
Failing to compare data points to relevant baselines or reference values
Selecting statements that are generally plausible but not specifically supported by the given data
Overlooking specific details or exceptions in the data set

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure numerical data is clearly presented, often in tables or simple graphs.
Include a baseline or reference value for comparison, if relevant (e.g., wild type vs. commercial varieties).
Statements to evaluate should cover comparisons between data points, identification of trends (increase, decrease, stable), and specific values.
Include distractors that are partially true, generally plausible, or misinterpret the data in subtle ways.
The task should primarily involve data interpretation and logical deduction, rather than complex calculations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q58_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_8fa10f58. Predicting and Enumerating Organic Products with Isomerism**

**Core move:** Systematically predict all possible organic products from a given set of reactants and reaction conditions, accounting for all specified types of isomerism.

**Seen in / context:**
- Reactions involving the formation of multiple isomers (e.g., substitution, elimination, addition reactions leading to chiral centers or E/Z isomers).
Questions requiring the counting of distinct organic molecules formed under specific reaction pathways.
Problems assessing comprehensive knowledge of reaction mechanisms, regioselectivity, and stereoselectivity simultaneously.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to identify all possible structural isomers of the starting material.
Missing potential reaction pathways for the given reactants (e.g., only considering substitution and ignoring elimination, or vice-versa).
Incorrectly predicting the structural formulas of the reaction products.
Overlooking or misidentifying E/Z isomers in alkene products.
Incorrectly including or excluding enantiomers when the question specifies 'structural and E/Z isomers only'.
Double-counting identical products or missing unique products during enumeration.

**Notes for generation:**
- The question must clearly define which types of isomerism (e.g., structural, E/Z, enantiomers) are to be considered for the final count.
Vary the complexity by selecting starting materials that can undergo multiple types of reactions or lead to products with diverse isomerism.
Ensure the provided reagent can act in multiple capacities (e.g., as both a nucleophile and a base) to test for comprehensive reaction pathway knowledge.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q27_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_90318e28. Analyzing Proportions from Percentage Distribution**

**Core move:** Calculate specific quantities, proportions, or differences based on percentage distributions of a whole, typically presented in tables or charts.

**Seen in / context:**
- Environmental studies (e.g., carbon emissions by sector)
Financial reports (e.g., budget allocation)
Biological/medical data (e.g., nutrient composition, fluid loss)
Survey results (e.g., responses by category)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting the 'whole' or base amount for the percentages.
Incorrectly summing or subtracting percentages without converting to absolute amounts first, if the bases are different.
Calculation errors when converting percentages to decimals or fractions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the sum of percentages for the 'whole' is clear (e.g., 100%).
Questions can involve finding a missing percentage, calculating an absolute value, finding a difference, or comparing two proportions.
Can be combined with other mathematical operations like ratios or averages after initial calculations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q47_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_907e1d13. Identifying Sensory Detection of Waves**

**Core move:** Identify which specific types of waves or physical phenomena are detected by human sensory organs based on scientific knowledge.

**Seen in / context:**
- Human vision and the electromagnetic spectrum (visible light).
Human hearing and sound waves (mechanical waves).
Distinguishing between electromagnetic and mechanical waves based on their detection methods.
General biology and physics questions regarding sensory perception.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the nature of waves (e.g., categorizing sound as electromagnetic despite it being mechanical).
Misidentifying the specific range of the electromagnetic spectrum detectable by human eyes.
Ignoring explicit constraints or conditions mentioned in the question (e.g., 'unaided', 'using just their eyes and ears').
Assuming all listed options strictly adhere to a general preceding statement, even if one option contradicts it in the context of human senses (e.g., assuming all six waves must be electromagnetic, even if an ear detects a mechanical wave).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly state the sensory organs involved in the detection task.
Options should include relevant distractors, potentially from both electromagnetic and mechanical wave types.
Can be expanded to include other human senses and their corresponding physical or chemical stimuli.
If a general category is initially stated (e.g., 'electromagnetic waves'), question setters should consider if a specific listed option might be an exception to test nuanced understanding, or if the question intends to test the distinction between different wave types.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_9496e8a4. Calculating Probability of Sum from Random Selections**

**Core move:** Determine the total number of ways to select items and the number of selections whose properties sum to a target value, then calculate the probability.

**Seen in / context:**
- Drawing items (e.g., cards, balls, containers) with numerical values (e.g., face value, capacity, weight)
Problems where a specific combined total (sum) of these values is required from a random selection
Scenarios involving combinations or permutations where elements have associated numerical properties

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the total number of possible selections (sample space), often confusing combinations with permutations or overlooking 'without replacement' conditions.
Failing to systematically list or account for all pairs/groups of items that meet the sum condition (favorable outcomes).
Making arithmetic errors when summing the capacities or counting outcomes.
Assuming events are independent when they are dependent (e.g., selecting items without replacement).

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly define the full set of available items and their associated numerical properties.
Determine if the order of selection matters (permutations) or not (combinations) for the specific problem context.
Systematically list or use combinatorial methods to find both the total possible selections and the favorable selections.
Ensure the question provides all necessary numerical data for the items (e.g., capacities of containers).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q12_NSAA_Sec`: Although the question is incomplete, the provided context sets up a scenario with discrete items (storage containers) having specific properties (capacity) and quantities (number available). This data structure is highly indicative of problems requiring the selection of items and calculation of probabilities based on the sum of their properties, which directly aligns with the core move of M_9496e8a4, 'Calculating Probability of Sum from Random Selections'.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires determining the total possible sums from two dice rolls and identifying how many of these sums meet a specific criterion (greater than 9), then calculating the probability based on these counts, which directly aligns with the core move of calculating the probability of a sum from random selections.

## **M_95300376. Recalling Definitions and Explaining Physical Concepts**

**Core move:** State fundamental laws, define physical quantities, and explain scientific concepts.

**Seen in / context:**
- Explaining the meaning of scientific terms (e.g., elastic potential energy, work, power).
Stating fundamental physical laws (e.g., Hooke's Law, Newton's Laws, conservation laws).
Describing physical phenomena, experimental setups, or observations.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Providing incomplete or inaccurate definitions.
Confusing similar terms or laws.
Omitting key aspects of an explanation or description.
Using informal language instead of precise scientific terminology.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often begin with verbs like 'state', 'define', 'explain', 'describe', or 'outline'.
Emphasis is on clarity, precision, and the use of correct scientific terminology.
Such questions frequently serve as introductory parts to more complex calculation-based problems.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q3_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_96ba91cd. Interpreting Exam Paper Instructions**

**Core move:** Recognize and correctly interpret non-mathematical administrative instructions or structural elements presented within an examination paper.

**Seen in / context:**
- Instructions for using blank pages for rough work
Directives on how to fill out personal details (name, candidate number)
Statements regarding copyright or paper numbering
General advice on answering questions (e.g., "Show all your working")
Information on time limits or allowed materials

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Treating administrative text as part of a mathematical problem to be solved
Overlooking crucial instructions about question answering format or time limits
Misinterpreting the purpose of blank pages or specific sections
Attempting to derive a numerical or conceptual answer from non-question text

**Notes for generation:**
- The content is typically explicit and does not require complex deduction or subject-specific knowledge
Questions testing this pattern would involve presenting a snippet of an exam paper and asking what it signifies, its purpose, or what action should be taken by the test-taker
Focus is on test-taking skills, attention to detail, and understanding exam logistics rather than subject matter expertise

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q43_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_98d74330. Determining Function Range via Discriminant**

**Core move:** Transform a given function into a quadratic equation in terms of the independent variable, then apply the discriminant condition for real roots to establish the function's range and identify its extreme values.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding the maximum or minimum value of rational functions, especially those involving quadratic expressions in the numerator and denominator.
Determining the possible set of output values for a function (its range).
Problems where direct differentiation to find extrema is overly complex or algebraically cumbersome.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to consider all cases for the coefficient of the quadratic term (e.g., if it can be zero).
Algebraic errors when expanding the discriminant or solving the resulting inequality.
Not checking if the determined range aligns with the function's behavior at boundaries or asymptotes.

**Notes for generation:**
- This method is highly effective for functions of the form y = (ax^2+bx+c)/(dx^2+ex+f).
Always verify the behavior of the original function as x approaches values that make the denominator zero or as x approaches infinity, especially if the discriminant method yields only part of the range.
The method implicitly finds the minimum/maximum *y* value that allows for a real *x*.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q16_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_99b1a948. Multi-Step Chemical Quantity Conversions**

**Core move:** Execute a sequential series of calculations involving conversions between mass, moles, concentration, and volume using standard chemical definitions and stoichiometric principles.

**Seen in / context:**
- Titration problems to determine unknown concentrations or masses
Gravimetric analysis calculations
Determining the purity or content of a substance in a mixture or solution
Dilution factor calculations combined with concentration/mass determination

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying chemical formulas (e.g., using molar mass for the wrong species, or inverse operations)
Errors in unit conversions (e.g., cm³ to dm³, g to kg, or vice versa)
Failing to account for dilution factors or sample preparation steps when scaling back to the original sample
Prematurely rounding intermediate calculation results
Misinterpreting stoichiometric ratios (if a reaction is involved)

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all relevant chemical definitions and formulas (e.g., n=m/Mr, n=CV) are correctly applied.
Emphasize tracking units throughout the calculation.
Problems often require working backwards from an experimentally determined value to an initial unknown.
Values provided should lead to a clear, logical sequence of steps and reasonable final answers.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: The question primarily involves multi-step calculations to determine chemical quantities. Part (b)(i) requires converting mass to moles, and part (b)(ii) involves a sequence of calculations using concentration, volume, and stoichiometric principles (titration) to find the moles of CO2, directly aligning with the core move of executing a sequential series of calculations involving conversions between mass, moles, concentration, and volume.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires a sequential series of calculations and conversions involving mass, area (which implies a concentration-like quantity), and chemical percentages (derived from molar masses and given values) to determine a final mass. The identification of the correct formulation through gravimetric analysis (mass loss percentage calculation) is a crucial sub-step within this multi-step chemical quantity conversion process, making M_99b1a948 the most appropriate match.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q51_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires a sequential series of calculations involving conversions between concentration, mass, and volume, including unit conversions (cm³ to dm³) and percentage change calculations, which directly aligns with the core move of executing multi-step chemical quantity conversions.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q38_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires a sequential series of calculations converting between mass (of sample, then iron), moles (of Fe, then Fe2+), and finally calculating the concentration of the dichromate solution, incorporating dilution factors and stoichiometric ratios from the provided reaction equations. This directly matches the core move of executing multi-step chemical quantity conversions.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires a multi-step calculation involving converting volume to mass (using density), concentration (ppm) to mass of solute, and then applying stoichiometry and molar masses to convert the required mass of fluoride ions into the mass of various fluoride compounds. This directly aligns with the core move of executing a sequential series of calculations involving conversions between mass, moles, concentration, and volume using standard chemical definitions and stoichiometric principles.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q27_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires a sequential series of calculations: determining initial and target moles from concentration and volume, applying stoichiometry for the precipitation reaction, calculating the molar mass of washing soda, and finally converting moles of washing soda to its mass. This directly aligns with the core move of converting between mass, moles, concentration, and volume using chemical definitions and stoichiometry.

## **M_9c190f2f. Deducing Subgroup Means from Overall Average Changes**

**Core move:** Calculate the total sum of a set of values using its mean and count, then derive the mean or sum of a subset added to or removed from the original set.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving averages (mean, median, mode) where additions or removals change the overall average.
Situations where individual values are not known, but group statistics are provided.
Statistical problems where a new subgroup is added or removed, affecting the overall average.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to update the total count of items when new members join or leave.
Simply averaging the initial mean and the new mean to find the mean of the added/removed group.
Assuming the mean of the added/removed members is the same as the initial mean.
