Confusing the sum of values with the mean value for the subset.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly state initial and final means and counts.
The unknown quantity should be related to the new or removed group.
Can be extended to weighted averages with different group sizes.
Ensure the numbers lead to reasonably simple arithmetic for exam conditions (e.g., integers).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the total sum of a set of numbers using its mean and count, and then expressing this total sum as the sum of its subgroups. This setup leads to an equation which is then solved for 'n', directly matching the core move of deducing relationships between overall and subgroup sums/means.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q11_NSAA_Sec`: The question provides statistics for 'group X' including 'number of students' and 'mean', and explicitly mentions 'two groups of students'. This strongly indicates that the full question will require calculating total sums for groups using their means and counts, likely to derive a combined mean or to work with changes between subsets, which perfectly matches the core move of this schema.

## **M_9d0b506e. Deriving and Applying SHM Frequency in Spring Systems**

**Core move:** Derive the oscillation frequency of a mass-spring system using Newton's second law, Hooke's law, and the SHM acceleration definition, then apply this derivation to calculate frequencies for different spring configurations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Simple harmonic motion problems involving springs.
Calculating equivalent spring constants for parallel and series arrangements.
Derivations of angular frequency and frequency formulas for oscillating systems.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the formulas for springs in parallel versus series.
Incorrectly relating angular frequency (ω) to frequency (f) by missing the factor of 2π.
Failing to equate the net force (from Hooke's law) with ma (from Newton's second law) or with m(-ω²x).
Using kinematic equations for constant acceleration instead of SHM principles.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure students can combine spring constants accurately.
Require both derivation of a general formula and its application to a specific scenario.
Problems can be extended by introducing different oscillating systems or by varying parameters.
Emphasize the understanding of the relationship between acceleration and displacement in SHM.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q11_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_9d48bcd1. Solving for Unknowns in Changing Proportional Relationships**

**Core move:** Establish initial quantities based on a total and ratio, represent a uniform change algebraically, and use the subsequent ratio to form and solve an equation for the unknown change.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving money or items shared in a ratio, where amounts are added or removed equally.
Age problems where the ratio of ages changes after a certain number of years.
Mixture problems where a fixed quantity of each component is added or removed, changing the concentration ratio.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Applying the change directly to the ratio parts instead of the actual quantities.
Incorrectly setting up the algebraic equation from the new ratio.
Arithmetic errors when solving the linear equation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize calculating initial absolute quantities before applying changes.
Ensure the variable represents the 'change' per unit or person, not a multiple of the ratio.
Questions should lead to a solvable linear equation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_9e062dac. Formulating and Solving Simultaneous Equations**

**Core move:** Formulate a system of linear or non-linear equations from given conditions or functional evaluations and solve it to determine unknown parameters.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding coefficients of polynomials given specific function values or points.
Determining unknown variables in physics or engineering problems where multiple conditions apply.
Solving algebraic word problems that translate into multiple related equations.
Analyzing systems where variables are interdependent and constrained by several rules.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Making algebraic errors during substitution or simplification of expressions.
Incorrectly setting up the equations from the problem statement.
Errors in solving the resulting system of equations (e.g., arithmetic mistakes in elimination or substitution).
Misinterpreting the relationship between variables or conditions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the number of independent equations equals the number of unknown variables for a unique solution (in linear systems).
The complexity can vary based on the type of expression (linear, quadratic, cubic) and the number of variables involved.
Solutions may be unique, multiple, or non-existent depending on the system's nature.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q73_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q87_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires evaluating two definite integral conditions to formulate two distinct algebraic equations involving the unknown parameter 'm'. The core task is to derive these equations from the given conditions and then solve for 'm', which aligns directly with the core move of formulating and solving a system of equations to determine unknown parameters, even if the system is for a single variable and might be inconsistent as presented here.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q83_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires identifying the definite integrals as unknown variables and formulating a system of two linear equations based on the given conditions. The core task is then to solve this system to determine the value of the requested integral, which acts as one of the unknown parameters.

## **M_9eb5c094. Predicting Phenotypes from Gene Modifications**

**Core move:** Infer the observable traits or survival outcomes of an organism based on its genetic makeup, including the presence, absence, or modification of specific genes and their functions under defined environmental conditions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Genetic engineering experiments involving gene insertion, deletion, or inactivation.
Selection experiments using antibiotics, toxins, or other selective agents.
Analysis of gene function and its impact on cellular or organismal phenotype.
Understanding the consequences of horizontal gene transfer, such as plasmid uptake.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the function of different genes or the specific resistance conferred by each.
Misinterpreting the effect of gene inactivation (e.g., assuming a gene still functions after insertion).
Incorrectly predicting survival or growth patterns under given selective conditions.
Failing to consider all possible genetic states of the organism (e.g., bacteria without any plasmid).
Confusing the antibiotic name with the resistance gene name.

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly define the function of each gene and the trait it confers.
Explicitly state the type of genetic modification and its specific impact on gene function.
Precisely describe the environmental conditions, especially the selective agents present or absent.
Ensure all possible outcomes based on different genetic configurations are considered.
Can involve prokaryotic or eukaryotic systems, but often focuses on bacteria for antibiotic resistance.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q56_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q54_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires inferring the physiological consequences (phenotype) and potential inheritance patterns of a genetic condition (LPL deficiency) caused by a specific gene mutation (Gly188Glu), based on information about its recessive and autosomal nature, which directly aligns with predicting phenotypes from gene modifications.

## **M_9f3186e8. Calculating Series Coefficients from Integrated Expressions**

**Core move:** Determine specific coefficients in a power series by first expanding an integrand, performing term-by-term integration, and then evaluating the definite integral with variable limits.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems defining a function via a definite integral, where the integrand is a binomial or polynomial.
Finding coefficients of power series that are derived from integration.
Tasks combining algebraic expansion, integral calculus, and series analysis.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to expand the integrand before integration.
Errors in applying the binomial theorem or other algebraic identities.
Incorrectly performing the integration of polynomial terms.
Mistakes in evaluating the definite integral at the upper and lower limits.
Confusing the variable of integration (t) with the variable of the function (x).

**Notes for generation:**
- The integrand is often a binomial power, requiring expansion.
The limits of integration are typically from a constant to a variable (e.g., 0 to x).
The desired coefficient refers to the power series of the resultant function, f(x).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q85_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_a01d483d. Calculating Thermal Equilibrium with Phase Change**

**Core move:** Apply the principle of conservation of energy (heat transfer) to determine the equilibrium temperature of a mixture, accounting for specific heat capacities and latent heats of fusion or vaporization.

**Seen in / context:**
- Mixing substances at different temperatures, where one or more undergo a phase change.
Problems involving melting ice or freezing water.
Problems involving boiling or condensing a substance.
Calorimetry experiments.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Not performing a preliminary check to determine if a phase change completes (e.g., assuming all ice melts when it might not).
Ignoring latent heat during phase changes.
Incorrectly assigning signs to heat gained/lost or using incorrect temperature differences (ΔT).
Mixing units (e.g., grams vs. kilograms, J vs. kJ).
Assuming specific heat capacity is constant across different phases (e.g., water vs. ice).

**Notes for generation:**
- Always perform a preliminary calculation to assess if a phase change will occur and complete. This often involves comparing the maximum heat available from the warmer component to the heat required for the phase change of the colder component.
Break down the heat transfer into distinct stages (e.g., cooling down to phase change temperature, phase change itself, warming/cooling from phase change temperature).
Emphasize that heat lost by one component equals heat gained by the other in an isolated system (Q_lost = Q_gained).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_a208747e. Simplifying Expressions with Indices**

**Core move:** Apply laws of indices (e.g., product, quotient, power of a power) to combine terms with the same base and simplify algebraic or numerical expressions involving exponents.

**Seen in / context:**
- Algebraic simplification problems (e.g., simplify (x^a * x^b)^c / x^d).
Evaluating numerical expressions with large powers (e.g., (2^10 * 4^5) / 8^3).
Solving equations where bases can be made common to equate exponents.
Working with roots as fractional exponents.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly adding/subtracting exponents when multiplying/dividing terms with the same base (e.g., x^a * x^b = x^(a*b)).
Incorrectly multiplying bases (e.g., 2^3 * 2^4 = 4^7).
Not converting all numbers to common bases (e.g., leaving 27 as 27 instead of 3^3).
Misinterpreting negative exponents (e.g., x^-a = -x^a).
Ignoring the order of operations when applying index laws.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure expressions require multiple applications of index laws.
Vary the complexity by including variables, negative exponents, fractional exponents, and different bases that can be converted to common ones.
Can be used to set up equations where the goal is to find an unknown exponent.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_a300484c. Interpreting Initial Physical Conditions**

**Core move:** Identify and categorize all given physical parameters, constraints, and initial states of a system to prepare for subsequent analysis.

**Seen in / context:**
- Setting up mechanics problems (e.g., pendulums, projectiles, forces on inclined planes).
Describing electrical circuits (components, initial charges, switch states).
Chemical reaction setups (initial concentrations, temperature, volume).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Overlooking crucial qualitative details (e.g., 'light string', 'inextensible', 'smooth surface').
Misinterpreting kinematic terms like 'released from rest' or 'constant velocity'.
Ignoring units or performing incorrect unit conversions prematurely.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question provides a rich, detailed setup without necessarily posing an immediate calculation.
Encourage the student to draw diagrams and label all known quantities.
This schema focuses on information extraction and system definition rather than direct computation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q88_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_a48895cd. Explaining Biological Interrelationships and Consequences**

**Core move:** Articulate and link biological processes, mechanisms, and their consequences, demonstrating an understanding of cause-and-effect relationships within biological systems.

**Seen in / context:**
- Discussions on evolution and natural selection
Explanations of physiological adaptations
Analyses of ecological interactions
Describing genetic inheritance and its impact

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Stating facts without explaining the underlying mechanisms.
Failing to connect different parts of the discussion (e.g., discussing variation without linking it to reproduction or survival).
Providing superficial explanations without depth.
Confusing correlation with causation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should require students to synthesize knowledge from multiple biological areas.
Emphasize 'how' and 'why' questions rather than just 'what'.
Marks are often awarded for demonstrating a logical flow of argument and accurate biological terminology.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q29_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q29_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires interpreting molecular evidence to draw conclusions about evolutionary relationships and the reclassification of organisms. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of 'Articulate and link biological processes, mechanisms, and their consequences, demonstrating an understanding of cause-and-effect relationships within biological systems', where the processes are evolution and phylogeny, and the consequences are changes in our understanding of animal relationships and classification.

## **M_a4eca3c4. Multi-step Stoichiometric Yield Calculation**

**Core move:** Calculate the final mass or quantity of a product from a series of sequential chemical reactions, accounting for specified yields, reactant fractions, and limiting reactants at each stage.

**Seen in / context:**
- Industrial synthesis processes where overall efficiency is important
Laboratory synthesis planning, determining maximum theoretical yield
Problems involving consecutive reactions or convergent synthesis

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to apply the yield percentage at each step
Applying the yield percentage incorrectly (e.g., multiplying by 1.X instead of 0.X)
Not correctly accounting for the splitting of intermediate products (e.g., using 1/3 when 2/3 is left for another step)
Failing to identify the limiting reactant in the final step or other steps
Using incorrect relative molecular masses (Mr values) for compounds
Incorrectly identifying the products of the organic reactions described

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all reaction steps are clearly defined and organic products correctly identified based on common reaction types.
Provide all necessary atomic masses or Mr values for calculation.
Vary the number of steps, the fractions of intermediates used, and the percentage yields to adjust difficulty.
Can incorporate different types of organic reactions (e.g., oxidation, reduction, esterification, substitution) to test chemical knowledge.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q29_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_a5791e29. Designing Multi-Step Organic Syntheses**

**Core move:** Construct a detailed, step-by-step synthetic route to a specific organic product, clearly indicating starting materials, reagents, and all necessary intermediate structures.

**Seen in / context:**
- Advanced organic chemistry synthesis problems.
Functional group interconversions.
Retrosynthetic analysis.
Drug synthesis and medicinal chemistry.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Choosing reagents that lead to undesired side reactions or products.
Incorrectly identifying suitable starting materials or intermediates.
Missing key steps or using an illogical sequence of reactions.
Ignoring stereochemical considerations if relevant.
Not providing full displayed formulae or structures for intermediates.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should specify the target molecule and may provide constraints on starting materials.
Ensure the synthesis pathway requires multiple distinct reaction types (e.g., addition, elimination, substitution, oxidation/reduction).
The target molecule should necessitate a logical sequence of functional group transformations.
Consider including isotopic labeling to test understanding of reaction mechanisms.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_a6b48971. Solving Coordinate Geometry Problems with Geometric Constraints**

**Core move:** Convert geometric properties (like shape type, area, orientation, quadrant location) into algebraic equations using coordinate formulas, then solve for unknown coordinates or parameters while ensuring consistency with all constraints.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving polygons defined by coordinates where specific vertices or area are given.
Calculating areas, perimeters, or specific point locations under geometric restrictions.
Questions where geometric constraints (e.g., 'in first quadrant', 'clockwise labelling', 'collinear points') significantly influence the algebraic solution.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting the naming convention of a polygon (e.g., OPQR meaning consecutive vertices versus O,P,R being adjacent to O).
Incorrectly applying area formulas (e.g., using signed area when magnitude is needed, or using vectors that are not adjacent).
Failing to check if derived coordinates or parameters satisfy all given geometric constraints (e.g., a point landing outside the specified quadrant).
Errors in vector addition or subtraction when translating geometric relationships into algebraic equations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Pay close attention to the exact phrasing of polygon naming and vertex order.
Be precise with vector definitions (e.g., vector OP vs. vector PO).
Systematically verify all algebraic solutions against every geometric condition specified in the problem.
For 'clockwise' or 'counter-clockwise' labelling, consider its implication on the sign of determinant-based area formulas, even if only the magnitude is ultimately used.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q8_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_a814b13d. Quantitative Analysis via Multi-step Titration**

**Core move:** Calculate the initial amount of an unknown substance by using titration data to determine the quantity of a reagent in excess, then applying stoichiometry and accounting for dilution steps.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining the purity of a substance
Analyzing the concentration of a reactive component in a mixture (e.g., active ingredient in a tablet, acid in vinegar)
Situations involving back titration where a reactant is added in excess and the unreacted portion is then titrated
Sequential chemical reactions where an intermediate product or remaining reactant needs to be quantified

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to correctly identify the amount of substance that reacted vs. the amount initially added or remaining
Incorrectly applying dilution factors (e.g., multiplying instead of dividing, or vice-versa, or forgetting to apply them)
Using incorrect stoichiometric ratios for the reactions involved
Confusing the concentration/volume of the titrant with the substance being analyzed in the volumetric flask
Arithmetic errors, especially when dealing with multiple steps and significant figures

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly specify which reagents are in excess.
Provide all necessary volumes, concentrations, and molar masses (Mr).
Ensure the stoichiometry of all relevant reactions is either given or easily derivable.
The steps should logically lead back to the initial unknown quantity.
Consider including a 'distractor' like an inert ingredient to ensure students focus on the active component.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q35_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_a9085184. Identifying and Drawing Chemical Isomers**

**Core move:** Systematically identify and depict all possible structural isomers for a given molecular formula, adhering to valency rules and functional group definitions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Organic chemistry isomerism (structural, geometric, optical)
Nomenclature and representation of organic compounds
Understanding molecular structure and bonding

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Missing some possible isomers
Drawing duplicate structures (e.g., rotated or flipped versions of the same molecule)
Violating valency rules (e.g., carbon with 5 bonds)
Incorrectly applying functional group rules (e.g., forming a non-alkene structure)
Confusing different types of isomerism (e.g., structural vs. geometric)

**Notes for generation:**
- Specify the type of isomerism required (e.g., structural, E/Z, enantiomers).
Provide a molecular formula and potentially a functional group class (e.g., alkenes, alcohols).
Indicate acceptable drawing styles (skeletal, displayed, condensed).
Vary the complexity of the carbon chain and the presence of branches or rings.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: The question's core task is to identify and enumerate all relevant structural isomers for a given molecular formula (implied as C4H8) and functional group (alkene), then to determine which of these isomers exhibit a specific type of isomerism (E/Z). This directly matches the 'Core Move' of systematically identifying and depicting isomers.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q9_NSAA_Sec`: The core of this question explicitly asks for the 'total number of isomers that can be represented by the molecular formula of this hydrocarbon', which directly matches the core move of systematically identifying and depicting all possible structural isomers for a given molecular formula.

## **M_a9661615. Predicting Molecular Geometry and Bond Angles**

**Core move:** Apply the VSEPR (Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion) model to predict the electron domain geometry, molecular geometry, and approximate bond angles of molecules or ions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining the number of valence electrons of the central atom.
Drawing Lewis structures.
Identifying bonding and lone electron pairs.
Explaining deviations from ideal bond angles due to lone pair repulsion.
Predicting molecular polarity based on geometry.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the number of valence electrons or lone pairs on the central atom.
Confusing electron domain geometry with molecular geometry.
Forgetting that lone pairs exert greater repulsion than bonding pairs, leading to compressed bond angles.
Assuming ideal bond angles without considering the effect of lone pairs.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the central atom is clearly identifiable.
Provide examples with varying numbers of electron domains (2, 3, 4, 5, 6).
Include examples with lone pairs to test understanding of angle compression and distortion from ideal geometries.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q23_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires two main steps: first, determining the molecular formula of compound X from the given reaction stoichiometry (which could align with M_eb51e883 or M_fd4ee276), and second, applying the VSEPR model to this compound to predict its molecular geometry and bond angles. The final part of the question explicitly states 'Using the VSEPR model, what is the smallest bond angle...'. This makes M_a9661615 the most direct and primary match for the core scientific model and objective explicitly asked for in the question.

## **M_a9bfb5c4. Quantitative Analysis of Multicomponent Mixtures**

**Core move:** Determine the amount of a specific component in a complex mixture by interpreting qualitative observations, applying mass balance, and performing sequential stoichiometric calculations based on products from selective reactions and separations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analysis of unknown mixtures (e.g., minerals, alloys, consumer products).
Problems involving multiple precipitation, gas evolution, or dissolution steps.
Scenarios where one component is quantified to indirectly find another.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the chemical reactions or products from given observations (e.g., precipitate colors, gas evolution).
Misinterpreting stoichiometric ratios in chemical equations (e.g., moles of ammonia from ammonium salt).
Errors in calculating molar masses, especially for hydrated compounds.
Failing to account for all components when applying mass balance equations.
Confusing the mass of a hydrated salt with its anhydrous form, or vice versa.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often involve common cations (e.g., Fe2+, Cu2+, NH4+) and anions (e.g., SO4^2-, Cl-, CO3^2-).
Properties like solubility, characteristic colors of precipitates, and identifiable gas evolutions are key clues for identifying components and reactions.
Calculations frequently involve converting gas volumes at specified conditions (e.g., RTP) to moles, calculating molar masses, and performing mass conversions.
Ensure all steps of the analytical process are logically connected to accurately deduce the quantity of the target component.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_aa526843. Solving Fractional Linear Equations**

**Core move:** Algebraically manipulate an equation containing fractional terms to combine them and isolate the variable.

**Seen in / context:**
- Equations where the variable is in the denominator of simple fractions.
Equations requiring the calculation of common denominators for both variable and constant terms.
Problems aiming to find a unique numerical value for the unknown variable.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly finding a common denominator for either side of the equation.
Algebraic errors when combining numerators or cross-multiplying.
Misinterpreting the structure of fractional terms (e.g., confusing `a/b+c` with `(a/b)+c`).
Arithmetic mistakes during fraction addition/subtraction or multiplication/division.

**Notes for generation:**
- Construct problems where the variable appears in simple fractional forms (e.g., `k/x`).
Ensure the equation has a single, rational solution.
The arithmetic for combining constant fractions should be straightforward.
Consider including both positive and negative coefficients for fractions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q9_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q77_NSAA_Sec`: The question presents a fractional linear equation, and solving it requires algebraic manipulation to combine the fractional terms and isolate the variable, which directly matches the core move of schema M_aa526843.

## **M_aa7ab5b8. Analyzing Projectile Motion**

**Core move:** Apply kinematic equations independently to the horizontal and vertical components of motion, linking them through time and the initial total speed, to determine unknown displacements or velocities.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving objects launched at an angle under constant gravity.
Scenarios where air resistance is negligible.
Calculating range, maximum height, time of flight, final velocity components, or landing coordinates.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assuming a component of initial velocity (e.g., total speed is only horizontal or vertical).
Using total initial speed directly in component equations without resolving.
Applying acceleration due to gravity (g) to horizontal motion.
Confusing vertical displacement (h) with total distance traveled.
Algebraic errors when solving simultaneous equations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the separation of horizontal (constant velocity) and vertical (constant acceleration) motion.
Reinforce the role of time as the common link between the two components.
Highlight the use of Pythagorean theorem for total velocity from components.
Encourage drawing free-body diagrams and sketching the trajectory.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_ab052aa5. Solving for Function Parameters Using Given Points**

**Core move:** Substitute given coordinate points into a function's equation to form a system of simultaneous equations, then solve for the unknown parameters defining the function.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding coefficients of polynomial functions (e.g., quadratic, cubic)
Determining parameters in exponential or logarithmic functions (if linearizable)
Identifying constants in general algebraic relations (e.g., circles, ellipses)
Fitting curves to data points where the function form is known.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly substituting x and y values into the equation.
Making algebraic errors when setting up or solving the simultaneous equations.
Forgetting that each distinct given point generates an independent equation.
Trying to use calculus (differentiation or integration) when simple algebraic substitution is sufficient.

**Notes for generation:**
- The number of given points typically corresponds to the number of unknown parameters needed to be determined.
The system of equations formed is often linear in terms of the unknown parameters, even if the original function is non-linear (e.g., y = ax^2 + bx + c yields linear equations in a, b, and c).
Emphasize clear setup of the simultaneous equations before attempting to solve them.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_ac8895a0. Analyzing Population Density and Growth**

**Core move:** Calculate initial population density or mean, then utilize area and growth/decay rates to project total population changes over time.

**Seen in / context:**
- Ecological studies involving quadrat sampling and population dynamics
Resource management and conservation planning
Epidemiology (modeling disease spread based on density and rates)
General problems involving rates of change in a defined area

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing total count with density (plants per square metre).
Incorrectly calculating the total area or volume.
Failing to account for the starting population or density when calculating the required growth.
Errors in unit conversion (e.g., square metres to hectares).
Misapplying multiplication instead of division (or vice versa) when working with rates.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often involve a two-step process: first determining an initial average/density (e.g., from quadrat data), then using this to project future states.
Ensure all necessary parameters (initial count/density, area, growth/decay rate, target state) are clearly identified.
Can be extended to include compound growth/decay or multiple phases of change.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_ad19373c. Interpreting Biological Mechanisms from Text**

**Core move:** Analyze descriptive text to identify, compare, and differentiate between observed biological phenomena and their underlying mechanistic explanations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Explaining physiological processes and their causes.
Understanding the effects of environmental factors on biological systems.
Analyzing experimental observations to deduce underlying mechanisms.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing a general observation or symptom with a specific causative mechanism.
Failing to recognize the level of detail provided (e.g., macroscopic vs. molecular).
Misinterpreting key biological terms or processes described in the text.

**Notes for generation:**
- Provide multiple statements or descriptions related to a single biological event.
Ensure some descriptions focus on observable outcomes, while others detail molecular or cellular processes.
The question should implicitly or explicitly require students to evaluate, compare, or select the most accurate/complete explanation among the given statements.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q75_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_ae71e3d6. Solving for Sequence Parameters from Term Conditions**

**Core move:** Translate given conditions about specific terms of sequences into an algebraic equation, solve for an unknown parameter, and then use the refined sequence definition to calculate another specific term.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving sequences (arithmetic, geometric, or general formula) where a parameter needs to be determined.
Questions where relationships between terms from different sequences or between terms and other constants are provided.
Algebra problems requiring sequential substitution and equation solving.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly substituting 'n' values into the nth term formulas.
Errors in setting up the algebraic equation from the given conditions.
Algebraic mistakes when solving for the unknown parameter.
Calculating the wrong term at the final step.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure clarity on which 'n' corresponds to which term.
The complexity can be varied by introducing more terms, multiple unknowns, or more complex relationships (e.g., ratios, sums).
Can involve different types of sequences to mix concepts.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_aec9c7d9. Calculating Work Done by a Position-Dependent Force**

**Core move:** Calculate the work done by integrating a force function that depends on position over a specified displacement range.

**Seen in / context:**
- Stretching or compressing springs that do not obey Hooke's Law (F=kx).
Forces varying with distance, such as electrical or gravitational forces between objects.
Calculating work done against varying resistance over a path.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming the force is constant and using W = FΔx, instead of integration.
Incorrectly determining the limits of integration (e.g., using absolute lengths instead of extensions, or misinterpreting starting/ending points).
Algebraic or calculus errors during the integration process or when solving for constants.
Confusing the definition of 'extension' with total length from a different reference point.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the force F is expressed as a function of the displacement variable (e.g., x).
Carefully define the initial and final displacement limits for the integral based on the problem statement and the reference point for the force function.
Pay close attention to units and unit consistency throughout the calculation, especially when finding unknown constants.
This pattern directly involves the definition of work as the integral of F.dx, contrasting with scenarios where work is inferred from changes in kinetic or potential energy for non-conservative forces.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q15_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_aefb1bb2. Deducing Unknown Counts from Categorized Totals**

**Core move:** Use given overall totals and partial counts within categories to logically deduce missing specific counts through addition and subtraction.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving survey or observational data with distinct categories.
Demographic analysis where a population is divided into subgroups based on multiple attributes.
Situations that can be naturally represented and solved using a contingency table (e.g., 2x2 table).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting negation (e.g., confusing 'did not pass' with 'passed') leading to incorrect deductions.
Incorrectly assigning a given numerical value to the wrong category or subcategory.
Attempting to apply advanced statistical methods (like proportions or percentages) when simple arithmetic deduction is sufficient and more direct.
Failing to systematically organize the given information, leading to confusion and errors in calculation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should be structured to require at least two sequential deductions to arrive at the final answer, ensuring it's not a trivial one-step calculation.
The numerical values should allow for straightforward arithmetic without a calculator, focusing on the logical deduction process.
Can be extended to problems with more categories or conditions, increasing the complexity of the deduction path.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_af9b2fdd. Interpreting Structure of Scientific Data Tables**

**Core move:** Infer the underlying organizational principles and relationships within a complex scientific data table by analyzing numerical sequences, categorical groupings, and positional information.

**Seen in / context:**
- Periodic Tables in Chemistry
Biological classification tables (e.g., phylogenetic trees, species classification)
Astronomical catalogs organized by stellar properties
Geochemical tables classifying minerals or rock types

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming purely mathematical patterns (e.g., arithmetic or geometric progressions) without considering scientific context.
Overlooking hierarchical relationships or implicit groupings within the data.
Failing to differentiate between primary organizing principles (e.g., atomic number) and secondary characteristics (e.g., state at room temp).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions using this schema often require understanding scientific classification systems.
Focus on how different attributes (e.g., atomic number, symbol, group, period) interrelate.
May involve identifying trends or anomalies based on the table's structure.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_afe61d24. Deducing Chemical Structure from Stability Data**

**Core move:** Relate the magnitude of a given thermodynamic stability value (e.g., enthalpy of hydrogenation) to the relative stability and specific structural features of chemical isomers.

**Seen in / context:**
- Organic chemistry problems comparing the stability of different isomers (e.g., cis/trans alkenes).
Interpreting calorimetric data to infer molecular properties.
Thermodynamics problems where specific enthalpy changes are characteristic of particular structures.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the relationship between enthalpy of hydrogenation magnitude and stability (e.g., assuming more negative means more stable).
Ignoring the common typical values for standard isomers if not explicitly stated.
Attempting to use unrelated thermodynamic data provided as distractors.
Misinterpreting the sign convention of enthalpy changes (exothermic vs. endothermic).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often rely on prior knowledge of general chemical principles affecting stability (e.g., hyperconjugation, steric hindrance).
The provided values are typically distinct enough to differentiate between common isomers.
It requires a comparative analysis of values rather than complex calculations, often matching given data to known characteristic values.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_b0db7d23. Solving Rational and Quadratic Inequalities**

**Core move:** Algebraically manipulate a given rational inequality to transform it into a standard polynomial inequality, identify critical points, and determine the solution set by testing intervals.

**Seen in / context:**
- Inequalities involving variables in the denominator.
Inequalities that simplify to quadratic or higher-order polynomials.
Problems requiring case analysis based on the sign of a term.
Determining domains of functions involving square roots or logarithms.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Multiplying by a variable term without considering its sign, leading to incorrect inequality direction.
Incorrectly identifying critical points (e.g., forgetting points where the denominator is zero).
Errors in testing intervals or interpreting the results on a number line.
Assuming an inequality can be solved by simply finding roots and using x < root1 or x > root2 (without proper interval testing).
Forgetting to exclude values that make the denominator zero from the solution set.

**Notes for generation:**
- Always aim to get zero on one side of the inequality.
Combine terms into a single fraction before finding critical points.
Critical points include roots of the numerator and roots of the denominator.
Use a sign table or number line to test intervals for the expression.
Be mindful of strict vs. non-strict inequalities when forming the final solution (open vs. closed intervals).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q87_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires solving a rational inequality by identifying critical points from the numerator and denominator and then testing intervals to determine the solution set, which directly aligns with the core move of this schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: The question presents a rational inequality that requires algebraic manipulation to combine terms, identify critical points (zeros of numerator and denominator), and test intervals to find the solution set, which perfectly aligns with the core move of solving rational and quadratic inequalities.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q89_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires solving a polynomial inequality (a cubic one), which involves identifying critical points (roots of the polynomial) and determining the solution set by testing intervals, precisely matching the core move of schema M_b0db7d23 for solving inequalities.

## **M_b18f37e0. Balancing Redox Chemical Equations**

**Core move:** Systematically construct a balanced chemical equation by identifying half-reactions, balancing atoms and charge, and combining them, considering the reaction environment.

**Seen in / context:**
- Redox reactions in acidic, basic, or neutral solutions.
Stoichiometric calculations involving balanced equations.
Electrochemistry and predicting cell reactions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to correctly identify oxidation states or the individual half-reactions.
Incorrectly balancing oxygen atoms (e.g., not using H2O).
Incorrectly balancing hydrogen atoms (e.g., not using H+ in acidic solutions or OH-/H2O in basic solutions).
Not balancing the charges in each half-reaction using electrons.
Not ensuring the electrons are cancelled out when combining half-reactions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Always specify the reaction environment (acidic/basic) as it affects H and O balancing.
Emphasize the step-by-step process of balancing half-reactions before combining them.
Ensure all original reactants and products mentioned in the question are accounted for.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q23_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: The question explicitly requires writing a balanced chemical equation for a redox reaction, including balancing for both atoms and charge in an acidic solution, which directly matches the core move of systematically constructing a balanced chemical equation in a given environment.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires first deducing the chemical formula of an unknown reactant (Q) based on its properties and the oxidation state of one of its elements, then using this information to systematically balance a complex redox chemical equation, and finally extracting a specific stoichiometric coefficient. The core task of balancing the entire equation, which involves identifying oxidation state changes, balancing atoms, and adjusting coefficients, directly aligns with the 'Balancing Redox Chemical Equations' schema.

## **M_b28adbcd. Calculating Unknown Rate in Multi-Segment Journeys**

**Core move:** Determine an unknown rate for one segment of a multi-segment journey by leveraging the relationship between total distance, total time, and overall average rate, alongside known parameters of other segments.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving average speed over a journey with varying speeds in different segments.
Situations where an object travels to a destination and returns by the same path at a different speed.
Calculating a missing rate (speed, flow rate, production rate) when overall average and other segment rates are given.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the average speed as the arithmetic mean of individual speeds, rather than total distance divided by total time.
Failing to convert units consistently (e.g., minutes to hours).
Miscalculating total distance or total time by not accounting for all segments of the journey.
Confusing time for one segment with the total journey time.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the fundamental relationship: Average Rate = Total Quantity / Total Time (or equivalent, e.g., Speed = Distance / Time).
Ensure all quantities are expressed in consistent units before calculation.
This pattern often involves setting up equations for total distance and total time based on individual segment parameters.
Can be extended to problems with more than two segments or different 'quantities' besides distance.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q12_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_b2af9803. Evaluating Complex Fractional Expressions**

**Core move:** Systematically apply the order of operations and rules for arithmetic with fractions to reduce a given expression to its simplest numerical or algebraic form.

**Seen in / context:**
- Simplifying numerical expressions involving multiple fraction operations.
Evaluating parts of algebraic equations or inequalities.
Problems testing fundamental arithmetic skills with fractions.
Pre-algebra and basic algebra curricula.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the order of operations (e.g., PEMDAS/BODMAS).
Errors in fraction arithmetic (e.g., finding common denominators for addition/subtraction, inverting for division).
Misinterpreting the grouping of terms or implicit operations, especially from ambiguous notation.
Failure to recognize or correctly handle cases of division by zero.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should involve at least two different arithmetic operations (e.g., subtraction and division, addition and multiplication) with fractions.
Can include parentheses/brackets or a clear structure to define the order of operations.
The expression should ideally evaluate to a defined, unique numerical or algebraic answer, unless the specific test is about identifying undefined expressions.
Vary the complexity by using mixed numbers, improper fractions, or multiple levels of nesting.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q73_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_b2cc14c8. Recognizing Non-Problematic Exam Content**

**Core move:** Differentiate between actual problems requiring mathematical or scientific solution and administrative or structural text within an exam paper.

**Seen in / context:**
- Blank pages for rough working or notes.
Copyright statements and page numbers.
General instructions about the exam format, duration, or allowed materials.
Section headings or disclaimers.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Attempting to derive a solution from administrative text.
Confusing instructions with problem statements.
Ignoring administrative information relevant to exam strategy (e.g., time per question, total marks).

**Notes for generation:**
- This pattern tests meta-cognitive skills related to exam comprehension rather than subject-specific knowledge application.
The 'question' itself is the administrative text. The 'thinking' is to understand its nature and purpose.
When generating questions, ensure the administrative text is clearly distinct from actual problem statements.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_b46d0f8e. Analyzing Breakup During Trajectory**

**Core move:** Apply conservation of momentum to determine the velocities of fragments immediately after an in-flight breakup or internal collision, integrating these new velocities with kinematic principles for the subsequent motion of each fragment.

**Seen in / context:**
- A projectile or object in motion (e.g., launched, orbiting) splits into multiple pieces.
An internal explosion causes an object to break into fragments while in motion.
Analyzing the final landing points or speeds of fragments after a mid-air event.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to calculate the velocity of the original object at the exact point of the breakup/collision.
Assuming total kinetic energy is conserved during an explosion or inelastic collision.
Not applying conservation of momentum vectorially (e.g., only considering magnitude instead of components).
Ignoring the continued effect of external forces (like gravity) on the fragments after the breakup.
Using initial launch parameters for momentum conservation instead of parameters immediately prior to the breakup.

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly define the system and the point in time for applying momentum conservation.
Remember that external forces (like gravity) generally do not affect momentum conservation during the instant of the internal event but do affect the motion before and after.
Break the problem into distinct kinematic stages (e.g., initial launch, motion to event, event, motion after event).
Horizontal momentum is typically conserved if air resistance is negligible, while vertical momentum is conserved only if no external vertical forces (like gravity) have a significant impulse during the event (which is generally true for instantaneous explosions).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_b5b682df. Calculating Competitive Binding in Equilibrium**

**Core move:** Apply equilibrium constant expressions and relative affinity ratios to determine unknown concentrations or proportions of species in a competitive binding system.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chemical equilibrium in biological systems (e.g., ligand-receptor binding, gas transport)
Competitive binding scenarios involving multiple ligands for a single site
Calculating relative concentrations or proportions based on given equilibrium constants and ratios

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing equilibrium constants with reaction rates or half-lives.
Incorrectly setting up the ratio of equilibrium constant expressions or concentration terms.
Failing to account for all given numerical relationships (e.g., relative binding efficiency, percentage ratios).
Misinterpreting the relationship between gas concentration in air and dissolved concentration in a solvent (e.g., blood), especially with assumptions like 'same molar solubility'.
Errors in unit conversion, particularly when expressing final answers in parts per million (ppm).

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly define variables for each species and its associated equilibrium constant.
Ensure all given ratios and constants are correctly incorporated into the algebraic setup of the problem.
Pay close attention to units and significant figures throughout the calculation, especially for ppm conversions.
Problems may involve simplifying assumptions about the system (e.g., only one binding site, constant partial pressure of one gas).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_b827ad50. Analyzing Ecological Impact of Pollutants**

**Core move:** Predict the cascading biological and chemical effects within an ecosystem resulting from the introduction of a specific pollutant.

**Seen in / context:**
- Eutrophication in aquatic systems
Impact of industrial discharge on water quality
Consequences of nutrient runoff in agricultural areas
Effects of oil spills on marine life

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the direct effect of the pollutant with secondary, ecosystem-level changes.
Ignoring the role of decomposers and their oxygen demand in organic pollution scenarios.
Failing to consider how environmental factors (e.g., flow rate, temperature) might modify the impact.
Attributing effects that are characteristic of different types of pollution (e.g., heavy metals vs. organic waste).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly specify the type of pollutant and the affected environment.
The correct answer often involves a chain of cause-and-effect relationships within the ecosystem (e.g., organic material -> bacterial decomposition -> increased BOD -> oxygen depletion -> impact on aerobic organisms).
Focus on well-established ecological principles related to pollution and ecosystem responses.
Avoid ambiguity in the nature of the pollutant or the resulting effects.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q69_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_b8c55487. Solving Proportionality Problems**

**Core move:** Derive an algebraic relationship from a proportionality statement, determine the constant of proportionality, and apply it to find unknown values.

**Seen in / context:**
- Direct proportionality (linear, quadratic, cubic, root)
Inverse proportionality (linear, quadratic, cubic, root)
Compound proportionality involving multiple variables or types of proportionality
Problems in physics or chemistry where quantities are related proportionally

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing direct and inverse proportionality (e.g., using multiplication instead of division for inverse)
Algebraic errors when solving for the constant of proportionality or the final unknown
Incorrectly applying powers or roots (e.g., forgetting to square/square root the result)
Misinterpreting the proportionality statement (e.g., 'square root of y' vs 'square of y')

**Notes for generation:**
- Vary the type of proportionality (direct, inverse, to a power, to a root, or combinations).
Ensure the initial conditions allow for a straightforward calculation of the constant.
The numbers should ideally lead to reasonable or integer results for simplicity in exam settings.
Can include real-world contexts or units to add complexity.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the angular displacement of two clock hands, each moving at a constant angular speed, which is a direct proportional relationship with time. The core move of deriving a proportionality and applying it to find unknown values (angular positions) is central to solving this problem.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q14_NSAA_Sec`: The question explicitly states that the cost of manufacturing is directly proportional to the volume of the cake. The solution requires deriving this proportional relationship using the large cake's cost and volume (even if implicitly through ratios) and then applying it to determine the manufacturing cost of the smaller cakes based on their scaled volume, which perfectly aligns with the core move of solving proportionality problems.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: The question establishes a direct proportionality relationship, requires finding the constant of proportionality using given values, and then applying this established relationship to determine an unknown variable under new conditions.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: The question provides a density value (4 g cm–3), which acts as the constant of proportionality relating mass and volume. The task is to find the mass (an unknown value) by applying this constant within the algebraic relationship (Mass = Density × Volume) that is derived from the proportionality between mass and volume. This aligns with the schema's core move of applying a constant of proportionality to find an unknown value.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q9_NSAA_Sec`: The question presents a length scale, which is a direct proportional relationship, and requires deriving the corresponding volume scale factor (cubed proportionality) to find the model's volume. It then implies using the proportional relationship between mass, density, and volume (mass = density × volume) to find a final quantity, directly aligning with the core move of applying derived proportional relationships.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires understanding that the volume of a sphere is proportional to the cube of its radius (V ∝ r³). Given a percentage reduction in the radius, one must apply this proportional relationship to the original volume to find the new volume. This aligns with deriving and applying an algebraic relationship from a proportionality statement to find an unknown value.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: The question establishes a direct proportionality relationship (resistance ∝ speed²), which requires deriving an algebraic relationship (R=kv²) and then applying it to find the percentage increase in resistance when speed changes. This directly matches the core move of solving proportionality problems.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: The question provides data describing a specific rate of carbon dioxide uptake per unit area per unit time. This implicitly establishes a proportional relationship. Any subsequent problem based on this information would require deriving a constant of proportionality (the uptake rate) and applying it to find unknown values, directly matching the core move of solving proportionality problems.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires applying Ohm's Law (I=V/R), which defines a proportional relationship between current (I) and voltage (V), with resistance (R) serving as the constant of proportionality. Given the resistance (20 Ω) and an implicit or explicitly provided voltage (which would be inferred from a complete problem context, likely leading to one of the options), the core task is to apply this proportional relationship to find the unknown current, aligning with the core move of M_b8c55487.

## **M_b985a0a7. Calculating Momentum from Time-Varying Force**

**Core move:** Apply the impulse-momentum theorem by integrating the time-dependent net force to determine the change in an object's momentum.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving forces that are functions of time, position, or velocity.
Situations where the momentum or velocity of an object needs to be calculated from a given force profile over a period.
Questions that blend Newton's laws with integral calculus.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying F=ma directly when force is not constant, without considering integration.
Forgetting or misinterpreting the initial conditions (e.g., initial momentum or velocity).
Errors in performing the definite integral of the force function.
Confusing momentum (p) with impulse (J) or force (F), or not recognizing that F = dp/dt.
Ignoring the vector nature of force and momentum in multi-dimensional problems (though not critical in this 1D case).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the force function F(t) is integrable over the given time interval.
Clearly define the initial state (momentum or velocity) at t=0.
The result of ∫F dt represents the change in momentum, so the initial momentum must be added (or subtracted from) to find the final momentum.
This pattern can be extended to finding velocity by then dividing by mass, or position by integrating velocity.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q14_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_ba18fd00. Solving for Unknowns in Proportional Relationships**

**Core move:** Establish an algebraic proportionality equation from a verbal description, determine the constant of proportionality using given values, and then apply this constant to calculate an unknown quantity.

**Seen in / context:**
- Direct proportionality (y = kx)
Inverse proportionality (y = k/x)
Proportionality to powers or roots (e.g., y = kx^2, y = k/√x)
Joint proportionality (e.g., y = kxz)
Physics problems involving relationships like F=ma, V=IR, P=IV, or gravitation F = Gm1m2/r^2

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly translating the verbal proportionality into an algebraic equation (e.g., using direct instead of inverse, or incorrect power).
Errors in calculating the constant of proportionality due to algebraic mistakes or miscalculation with scientific notation.
Algebraic errors when isolating and solving for the unknown variable.
Ignoring constraints on the variables (e.g., positive numbers).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem clearly defines the type of proportionality and the variables involved.
Provide a set of initial conditions to allow for the determination of the constant of proportionality.
The final goal should be to find a specific numerical value, not just a relational expression.
Can involve scientific notation and require careful algebraic manipulation of powers and roots.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q14_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires establishing algebraic proportionality equations from verbal descriptions for two separate relationships (y to x, and z to x), determining their respective constants of proportionality using given values, and then applying these constants sequentially to calculate the final unknown quantity (z).
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: The question first requires calculating a constant of proportionality (Equation 1) using given absorbance and concentration values, and then applying this constant to determine an unknown concentration from a new absorbance value. This directly aligns with the core move of establishing a proportional equation, determining the constant, and then using it to find an unknown.
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires identifying a proportional relationship (tension to extension, F=kx), calculating the constant of proportionality (k) from given values (mass to find tension, lengths to find extension), and then applying this calculated constant (k) to determine another unknown quantity (elastic potential energy, E_p = 1/2 kx^2). This directly aligns with the core move of schema M_ba18fd00.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires establishing a proportional relationship between the extensions of the two springs based on Hooke's Law and the property of springs in series (equal force). Specifically, x_P * k_P = x_Q * k_Q leads to x_P = (k_Q/k_P) * x_Q, which is a direct proportionality. This relationship is then used with the total extension to solve for the unknown extension of spring P, directly matching the core move of solving for unknowns in proportional relationships.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q16_NSAA_Sec`: The question heavily relies on establishing and manipulating multiple proportional relationships (e.g., mass to radius, force to velocity, power to force and velocity, and eventually power to mass) to determine how a quantity changes when another is scaled. This aligns perfectly with the core move of solving for unknowns in proportional relationships.

## **M_ba24d775. Identifying Exam Paper Administrative Pages**

**Core move:** Recognize the purpose and function of non-question pages within an exam paper.

**Seen in / context:**
- Exam instruction pages
Cover pages with candidate information
Blank pages for rough working or notes
Copyright or pagination pages
Formula sheets or data tables

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing a blank page for a missing question
Attempting to derive mathematical information from administrative text like copyright notices or page numbers
Misinterpreting the purpose of a dedicated rough working page

**Notes for generation:**
- The task involves textual analysis of the page's content, not mathematical calculation.
Questions should present a snippet or image of such a page and ask for its function or implications.
Focus on explicit textual cues like 'intentionally left blank' or 'for rough working'.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q16_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q80_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is an instruction page from an exam paper, outlining rules, structure, and administrative details rather than presenting a mathematical problem. This directly matches the core move of recognizing the purpose and function of non-question pages.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q80_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is an instruction page for an exam, detailing rules, structure, and administrative information, rather than a mathematical problem. This directly aligns with recognizing the purpose and function of non-question pages within an exam paper.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q60_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text consists of 'INSTRUCTIONS TO CANDIDATES' for an exam, which is a classic example of an administrative page. The core move involves recognizing its purpose and function as non-question content, directly matching schema M_ba24d775.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q60_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is clearly an administrative section of an exam paper, containing instructions to candidates. Recognizing its purpose and function as a non-question page directly aligns with the core move of identifying exam paper administrative pages.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q60_NSAA_Sec`: The provided text is clearly an 'INSTRUCTIONS TO CANDIDATES' section, which is a non-question, administrative page within an exam paper. Its purpose is to inform candidates about exam procedures, structure, and rules, directly matching the core move of recognizing the purpose and function of such pages.

## **M_bb2e7b0f. Analyzing Size-Dependent Physiological Constraints and Adaptations**

**Core move:** Explain how changes in organism size impact fundamental physiological processes, particularly substance exchange, and identify specific adaptations that address these constraints.

**Seen in / context:**
- Gas exchange in animals (e.g., lungs, gills)
Nutrient absorption in digestive systems
Waste excretion in excretory systems
Thermoregulation in endotherms and ectotherms
Transport systems in plants and animals

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing absolute size with the surface area to volume ratio.
Describing adaptations without explicitly linking them to the physiological constraints caused by size.
Overlooking the role of diffusion distance and time.
Focusing only on one type of substance exchange (e.g., only gas exchange) when a broader discussion is required.
Failing to discuss the implications for metabolic rate or heat loss/gain.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the explanation clearly links size to surface area to volume ratio.
Require specific biological examples of adaptations.
Encourage discussion of both the 'problem' (constraints) and the 'solution' (adaptations).
Questions may involve comparing organisms of different sizes or discussing evolutionary pressures.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q41_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q56_NSAA_Sec`: The question investigates how changes in the size of an agar cube (a model system) affect its efficiency to absorb and distribute a dye. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of explaining how changes in organism size impact fundamental physiological processes, specifically substance exchange.

## **M_bb902164. Quantifying Biological Structures from Given Data**

**Core move:** Derive specific biological parameters through calculation from provided numerical data and utilize these parameters to evaluate statements regarding biological structures.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining average length or mass per unit (e.g., per nucleotide, per cell).
Calculating concentrations or densities of biological components.
Estimating the total number of molecules or structures based on given dimensions or counts.
Relating macroscopic dimensions to molecular properties.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrect unit conversions (e.g., metres to nanometres, grams to picograms).
Errors in scientific notation arithmetic.
Misinterpreting biological terms (e.g., nucleotides vs. base pairs, single-stranded vs. double-stranded).
Failing to connect calculated values back to the biological context for statement evaluation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure questions provide clear numerical data for calculation.
Statements to be evaluated should require direct calculation or interpretation of derived quantities.
Specify units clearly and expect unit conversions where appropriate.
Avoid ambiguity in biological terms crucial for calculation (e.g., 'nucleotides' meaning total bases on both strands).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q58_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_bdd9d9c6. Analyzing Spring Systems in Static Equilibrium**

**Core move:** Apply Hooke's law, the formula for elastic potential energy, and conditions for static equilibrium to solve for unknown parameters in a spring-mass system.

**Seen in / context:**
- Masses suspended from springs at rest.
Calculating stored elastic potential energy in a spring.
Determining spring constant, extension, or mass in an equilibrium setup.
Problems involving density and volume calculations to find the mass of an object.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing elastic potential energy (1/2 kx²) with force (kx) or work done (Fx).
Failing to convert units consistently (e.g., cm to m, g to kg).
Incorrectly calculating the volume or mass of the object.
Mistaking static equilibrium for dynamic scenarios (e.g., SHM, freefall).
Incorrectly applying the gravitational field strength.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all physical quantities are expressed in consistent units (e.g., SI units).
Emphasize that in equilibrium, the net force on the object is zero (upward spring force equals downward weight).
The problem may require multiple steps involving calculation of intermediate quantities like mass, weight, or extension.
Consider variations where the spring might already have an initial extension or compression.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks for the energy stored in a spring, which directly requires applying the formula for elastic potential energy (E = 0.5 * k * x^2). This is explicitly mentioned as part of the core move in schema M_bdd9d9c6, even if the values for 'k' and 'x' are implied to be provided in an accompanying diagram or text.

## **M_bf8670d1. Quantitative Redox Stoichiometry in Acidic/Basic Solution**

**Core move:** Apply balanced redox equations, including explicit H+/OH-/H2O, to perform stoichiometric calculations for reagent quantities in specified acidic or basic conditions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Titration calculations involving redox reactions.
Determining required volumes or masses of reactants in redox reactions.
Balancing complex redox half-equations in acidic or basic media.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly balancing half-equations (especially H+/OH-/H2O/e-).
Ignoring the stoichiometry of H+ (or OH-) from the acid/base.
Forgetting to account for the number of H+ ions provided by polyprotic acids (e.g., H2SO4 provides 2H+).
Miscalculating moles, concentrations, or volumes.
Failing to resolve ambiguity in chemical species notation by considering common redox pathways and given options.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem clearly states the reaction conditions (acidic/basic).
Provide clear chemical species names or formulas; if ambiguity is intended, it should be resolvable through logical chemical reasoning and given options.
Vary the complexity of the redox half-equations and the stoichiometric ratios.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q37_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c074acc8. Quantifying Cellular Changes During Cleavage**

**Core move:** Apply the principles of cell division without growth to determine the number and individual volume of daughter cells after multiple rounds of cleavage.

**Seen in / context:**
- Early embryonic development (cleavage)
Mitotic division where total volume is conserved
Problems involving changes in cell properties (volume, surface area, number) after division

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming cells grow between divisions, thus increasing total volume.
Incorrectly calculating the exponential increase in cell number (e.g., n * 2 instead of 2^n).
Confusing individual cell volume with the original parent cell's volume.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem clearly states 'no growth' or 'cleavage'.
May require basic geometric formulas (e.g., volume of a sphere) if radius/surface area are involved.
Focus on the conservation of the initial total volume and its distribution among daughter cells.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q59_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q52_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the final number of cells using the principles of binary fission (exponential growth) over a given time period. It then uses the individual cell volume to determine the total volume of cells. This process directly aligns with the core move of applying cell division principles to quantify cellular changes (number and total volume) after multiple rounds of division, even though it extends to a nutrient percentage calculation afterwards.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q54_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the total number of cells after multiple rounds of division and then determining the number of chromosomes per individual cell. This directly aligns with applying principles of cell division to quantify cellular changes, specifically determining the number and characteristics of daughter cells.

## **M_c1739cfa. Determining Function Parameters from Extrema Properties**

**Core move:** Utilize the conditions for local extrema (first derivative equals zero at specific points) to establish and solve a system of equations for unknown coefficients in a function's definition.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding coefficients of polynomial functions given critical points.
Analyzing properties of functions where parameters need to be determined from specific conditions (e.g., tangent lines, points of inflection).
Inverse problems in differential calculus where function form is known but coefficients are not.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the original function with its derivative.
Incorrectly calculating the derivative of the function.
Making algebraic errors when solving the resulting system of simultaneous equations.
Attempting to use the second derivative condition (for max/min type) to set up equations instead of the first derivative (for critical point location).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the derivative is correctly computed.
The number of unknown parameters typically corresponds to the number of independent conditions provided (e.g., two critical points for two parameters).
This pattern often involves polynomial functions but can apply to other differentiable functions as well.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q75_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c29e8e81. Calculating Point Coordinates via Vector Relationships**

**Core move:** Apply vector addition, subtraction, and scalar multiplication to determine unknown point coordinates based on their relative positions and given vector ratios.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding coordinates of points that divide a line segment in a given ratio (internal or external).
Calculating unknown vertices of geometric figures using vector relationships.
Problems involving displacement vectors between points.
Determining final positions after a series of relative movements.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying vector addition/subtraction signs (e.g., confusing P = X + XP with X = P - XP).
Reversing or incorrectly applying the ratio when dividing a line segment (e.g., XP : PY = 1 : 2 meaning PY = 1/2 XP instead of PY = 2 XP).
Errors in scalar multiplication of vector components.
Confusing point coordinates (position vectors) with displacement vectors.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure clarity in defining the start and end points of each vector for correct algebraic manipulation.
Problems can involve 2D or 3D coordinate systems.
Vary the complexity by requiring multiple steps of vector calculation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q17_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c3ba4033. Solving for Variables in Exponential Relations**

**Core move:** Isolate and determine an unknown variable within a given exponential or logarithmic functional relationship by applying substitution and algebraic manipulation, often involving conversion of units.

**Seen in / context:**
- Population growth/decay models
Radioactive decay
Compound interest problems
Physics equations involving exponential relationships (e.g., drag, charging/discharging capacitors, heat transfer)
Logarithmic scales (e.g., pH, decibels, Richter scale)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying logarithm properties (e.g., log(a-b) != log a - log b)
Mistakes in algebraic rearrangement (e.g., order of operations, sign errors)
Failing to convert units consistently before calculation (e.g., grams to kilograms)
Errors in calculator use for logarithms or powers
Misinterpreting the meaning of percentage relationships or terminal values

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the variable to be solved is correctly identified and isolated.
Pay close attention to units and perform necessary conversions at the beginning of the calculation.
Remind students of logarithm definitions and properties (e.g., log_b(x) = y <=> b^y = x).
Calculators will typically be required for non-exact logarithmic values or large powers.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c4961b0e. Extracting Information from Structured Documents**

**Core move:** Locate and retrieve specific data points by matching keywords within a structured document's index or table.
