**Seen in / context:**
- Navigating multi-part exam papers or textbooks to find relevant sections.
Interpreting data presented in tables, lists, or indices.
Extracting specific values or references from structured text.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing similar-sounding entries (e.g., 'Mathematics' versus 'Advanced Mathematics').
Misinterpreting the type of information being presented (e.g., a page number as a question number).
Failing to read the explicit labels or categories accurately.
Making assumptions about information not directly stated in the structure.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should require identifying a specific piece of information (e.g., a page number, a section title, a category).
The source material should be a clearly structured list, table, or index.
Vary the complexity by including distractors or requiring precise keyword matching (e.g., 'Physics' vs 'Advanced Physics').
Ensure the question clearly states what information needs to be extracted.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q71_NSAA_Sec`: The question presents a table showing concentrations and specifies a location ('bacterial cell cytoplasm'). Even though the question is incomplete, the evident task is to locate and retrieve specific data points (concentration values) associated with different locations from the structured table provided, which directly matches the core move of schema M_c4961b0e.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q69_NSAA_Sec`: The question explicitly states that 'The table shows the relationship...' and provides headers, indicating that the primary task will involve locating and extracting specific data points from this structured document (the table) to understand the relationship between heart rate and blood output.

## **M_c502b15a. Calculating Ratios from Equilibrium Constants**

**Core move:** Manipulate the equilibrium constant expression to determine the ratio of product to reactant concentrations (or partial pressures) at equilibrium, given the equilibrium constant and other relevant equilibrium concentrations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Directly applying the equilibrium constant definition (Kc or Kp).
Problems where all necessary values for direct calculation (Kc and some equilibrium concentrations) are provided.
Analyzing the relative prevalence of products versus reactants under specified equilibrium conditions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Errors in setting up the Kc expression (e.g., incorrect exponents, including solids/liquids, incorrect product/reactant placement).
Algebraic mistakes when isolating the desired ratio.
Incorrectly applying the given concentration to the wrong part of the expression.

**Notes for generation:**
- This pattern applies when an ICE (Initial, Change, Equilibrium) table is not necessary.
Questions should provide the equilibrium constant (Kc or Kp) and all but one piece of information, where the unknown is typically a ratio of concentrations/pressures.
Stoichiometric coefficients from the balanced equation become exponents in the equilibrium constant expression.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c6780ea0. Analyzing Evolutionary Divergence**

**Core move:** Assess and validate statements concerning the evolutionary pathways, adaptive radiations, and speciation events of organisms based on given biological data and established evolutionary principles.

**Seen in / context:**
- Questions involving adaptive radiation from a common ancestor.
Analysis of species diversification and niche specialization.
Evaluation of evidence for natural selection and speciation.
Interpretation of phylogenetic relationships or evolutionary diagrams.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing convergent evolution with divergent evolution.
Misinterpreting the role of environmental pressures in adaptation.
Failing to recognize the importance of reproductive isolation in speciation.
Assuming 'less evolved' or 'more evolved' species.
Incorrectly inferring direct lineage from shared characteristics rather than common ancestry.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question provides sufficient descriptive data (e.g., habitat, diet, morphology) to support evaluation of evolutionary claims.
Statements to be evaluated should cover various aspects of evolution (e.g., adaptation, speciation, common ancestry, ecological niche).
Can include diagrams of species, environments, or simplified phylogenetic trees.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q68_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c6bb1156. Chain Rule in Kinematics and Energy**

**Core move:** Apply the chain rule of differentiation to determine the time rate of change of physical quantities (e.g., energy, momentum) that depend on other variables (e.g., position, velocity) which are themselves functions of time.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating power as the rate of change of work or energy.
Deriving relationships between forces, energy, and motion using calculus.
Analyzing systems where potential or kinetic energy is expressed as a function of position or velocity, and these variables change over time.
Problems involving the conservation of energy where the rate of change of total energy is considered.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to apply the chain rule, treating the intermediate variable as a constant during differentiation with respect to time.
Incorrectly recalling or applying fundamental physics formulas (e.g., for kinetic or potential energy).
Confusing variables and their derivatives (e.g., substituting 'v' for 'a' or vice versa).
Making algebraic errors during the differentiation process.

**Notes for generation:**
- Explicitly state the formula for the physical quantity or allow students to infer/recall common ones (e.g., kinetic energy, spring potential energy).
Ensure the problem clearly defines the rates of change of intermediate variables (e.g., dx/dt = v, dv/dt = a).
Can be extended to include product rule applications if the physical quantity is a product of time-dependent variables.
May require conceptual understanding of physics principles (e.g., conservation of energy) alongside the calculus.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q9_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the rate of change of momentum. Momentum (p = mv) depends on velocity (v), and velocity (v = dx/dt) depends on position (x), which is given as a function of time (x = 4.0 t^3). The core move of applying differentiation to determine the time rate of change of a physical quantity (momentum) that depends on other variables (velocity) which are themselves functions of time, is a direct match.

## **M_c74f01ab. Deducing Genotypes from Pedigrees**

**Core move:** Infer the genotypes of individuals in a pedigree by applying Mendelian inheritance patterns and analyzing phenotypic expression across generations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing autosomal dominant or recessive traits.
Determining carrier status of individuals.
Identifying individuals who must be heterozygous or homozygous based on family history.
Predicting inheritance risks for future offspring.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing dominant and recessive phenotypes or their inheritance patterns.
Incorrectly assigning genotypes to individuals (e.g., assuming an unaffected individual is always homozygous dominant when they could be heterozygous).
Ignoring the implications of offspring phenotypes on parental genotypes.
Assuming X-linked inheritance when autosomal is specified (or vice-versa if not specified).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure family trees provide sufficient information for unambiguous deduction.
Clearly define dominant/recessive alleles and their corresponding phenotypes.
Questions can range from determining possible genotypes to necessary genotypes, or identifying the mode of inheritance itself.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q80_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c75e8ef8. Interpreting Exam Paper Directives**

**Core move:** Discern the purpose and implications of explicit instructions or structural declarations presented within an examination paper.

**Seen in / context:**
- Instructions for rough working pages or specific sections.
General exam instructions regarding time limits, allowed materials, or question selection.
Statements about copyright, page numbering, or intentional blank pages.
Section headers or introductory texts explaining question types.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Attempting to solve a mathematical problem on a page designated for notes or rough work.
Ignoring or misinterpreting crucial administrative instructions for the exam procedure.
Confusing descriptive or instructional text with actual question content.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions testing this schema should present a piece of administrative text or a structural element from an exam paper.
The expected 'answer' is an understanding of exam logistics, format, or instructions, not a subject-specific solution.
Complexity can range from simple declarations (like a blank page notice) to multi-part procedural rules.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q34_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c7cb64a6. Solving for Equilibrium Amounts using Kc**

**Core move:** Construct an ICE (Initial, Change, Equilibrium) table to express equilibrium concentrations in terms of initial conditions and reaction extent, substitute these into the equilibrium constant (Kc) expression, and solve the resulting algebraic equation.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chemical equilibrium calculations in general chemistry.
Determining concentrations/moles of reactants/products at equilibrium.
Problems involving homogeneous and heterogeneous equilibria.
Applications of the law of mass action.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to convert moles to concentrations by dividing by volume (V) before using Kc.
Incorrectly applying stoichiometry in the 'Change' row of the ICE table (e.g., using 'x' instead of '2x' for products with coefficients of 2).
Including concentrations of solids or pure liquids in the Kc expression.
Algebraic errors when solving the resulting equation (e.g., quadratic formula mistakes).
Incorrectly calculating initial concentrations or changes.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often involve setting up and solving quadratic equations.
Pay attention to units and ensure consistency (e.g., Kc in mol dm-3).
Clearly define the extent of reaction variable (x).
Remember to check for multiple valid solutions if a quadratic equation yields more than one physically possible answer (e.g., x cannot be negative or lead to negative concentrations).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q34_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c7dd820a. Constrained Geometric Optimization**

**Core move:** Formulate an objective function for a geometric property of a shape under a specific constraint, and use calculus or algebraic methods to determine the dimensions that yield its maximum or minimum value.

**Seen in / context:**
- Optimizing surface area for a fixed volume (or vice versa) of 3D objects (cuboids, cylinders, cones).
Finding optimal dimensions for constructing containers or enclosures.
Maximizing or minimizing the area or perimeter of 2D shapes under given conditions.
Problems involving rates of change of geometric properties related to optimization.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly setting up the objective function or the constraint equation.
Errors in differentiation or algebraic manipulation when finding critical points.
Failing to express the objective function in terms of a single independent variable.
Not verifying whether a critical point corresponds to a maximum or minimum (e.g., using the second derivative test or analyzing function behavior).
Forgetting to calculate the final required quantity (e.g., finding x when the area is asked).

**Notes for generation:**
- Pay close attention to the wording 'maximum' vs. 'minimum' and ensure the found extremum matches the requirement. If it doesn't, consider implied domain restrictions or potential flaws in the question.
Identify all variables and relationships between them, then reduce the problem to optimizing a single-variable function.
Be mindful of the practical domain of the variables (e.g., dimensions must be positive).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q89_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c82728ff. Quantifying Analytes via Gravimetric Analysis with Error Evaluation**

**Core move:** Perform sequential stoichiometric calculations to determine the mass percentage of an analyte from precipitation data, and infer the impact of interfering substances on the determined value.

**Seen in / context:**
- Gravimetric analysis experiments in analytical chemistry.
Stoichiometry problems involving precipitation reactions.
Questions assessing experimental errors in quantitative methods.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating molar masses or using incorrect stoichiometric ratios.
Failing to understand how an impurity's mass contributes to the measured precipitate, leading to misinterpretation of its effect on the final percentage.
Not distinguishing between the desired analyte and interfering substances when calculating percentage.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide sufficient data for molar mass calculations (e.g., periodic table values or contextually implied common masses).
The error evaluation part often requires a qualitative rather than quantitative answer, focusing on overestimation/underestimation.
Ensure clear links between the quantitative calculation and the qualitative error analysis.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_c8eb441b. Calculating Unknown Bond Energy from Enthalpy Differences**

**Core move:** Apply the definition of enthalpy change using bond energies for multiple reactions, and use the given relationship between their enthalpy changes to form and solve an algebraic equation for an unknown bond energy, often involving cancellation of common terms.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining specific bond strengths when direct experimental data is unavailable but related thermochemical data is provided.
Problems involving comparison of exothermicities or endothermicities between homologous series reactions.
Chemistry questions combining Hess's Law principles with bond energy calculations where certain bond energies cancel out.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly counting the number of bonds broken or formed in reactant/product molecules (e.g., overlooking C-H bonds in a CH3 group vs. a CH2 group).
Confusing the signs for bonds broken (energy input, positive contribution) and bonds formed (energy released, negative contribution) in the overall enthalpy change calculation.
Forgetting to multiply bond counts by stoichiometric coefficients for reactants and products.
Algebraic errors when setting up or solving the equation representing the difference in enthalpy changes, particularly sign errors when translating phrases like 'more exothermic' or 'less endothermic'.
Assuming a required bond energy (e.g., C-O in this problem) is missing and the problem is unsolvable, rather than realizing it might cancel out in the difference between two reactions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure structural formulas of organic compounds are correctly interpreted to accurately count each type of bond present.
Emphasize the fundamental equation ΔH = Σ(Bond energies of bonds broken) - Σ(Bond energies of bonds formed).
Highlight that questions often involve homologous series or similar reaction types where taking the difference between reactions can lead to cancellation of terms, simplifying the problem.
Guide students to carefully translate descriptive relationships between enthalpy changes (e.g., 'X kJ mol⁻¹ more exothermic') into algebraic equations (e.g., ΔH₂ = ΔH₁ - X).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_ca0c8b3c. Solving Kinematic Problems with Constant Acceleration**

**Core move:** Apply constant acceleration (SUVAT) equations to solve for unknown kinematic variables such as displacement, velocity, and time.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving objects under gravity (e.g., projectile motion, free fall)
Motion in a straight line with uniform acceleration
Physics problems in introductory mechanics

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assigning positive and negative signs to displacement, velocity, and acceleration vectors
Using the wrong kinematic equation for the given set of knowns and unknowns
Confusing speed with velocity, especially when direction changes or is relevant
Forgetting that acceleration due to gravity is always downwards
Not breaking down complex motion (e.g., up and down phases) into separate segments if necessary

**Notes for generation:**
- Always define a consistent positive direction (e.g., upwards or downwards) at the start.
List known variables (s, u, v, a, t) and the unknown to be found.
Ensure all units are consistent (e.g., meters, seconds, m/s, m/s²).
Remember that 'g' (gravitational field strength) serves as the acceleration 'a' in these equations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q84_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q78_NSAA_Sec`: The question provides initial speed, constant deceleration, and distance, asking for the final speed. This directly requires the application of constant acceleration (SUVAT) equations to solve for an unknown kinematic variable, which is the core move of schema M_ca0c8b3c.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the displacement and velocity for two objects, one with constant velocity and one with constant acceleration, using kinematic (SUVAT) equations. It then involves solving for a specific time when their displacements are equal, which perfectly aligns with applying constant acceleration equations to solve for unknown kinematic variables like time and displacement.

## **M_cbcfe73f. Solving Geometric Problems with Algebraic Expressions**

**Core move:** Formulate an algebraic equation (often quadratic) by substituting given variable expressions for geometric dimensions into a relevant area or perimeter formula, then solve the equation and use its valid solutions to determine unknown lengths or values.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving areas or perimeters of common 2D shapes (e.g., rectangles, triangles, rhombuses, trapezoids) where dimensions are given as algebraic expressions.
Problems where multiple solutions for a variable might arise, requiring careful selection based on physical constraints (e.g., length must be positive).
Questions that require calculating differences, sums, or products of possible values derived from the solutions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly recalling or applying the specific geometric formula (e.g., area of a rhombus).
Algebraic errors when expanding, simplifying, or solving the resulting quadratic equation.
Not considering both solutions for the variable (if applicable) or failing to check for physical validity (e.g., a dimension cannot be negative).
Calculating an intermediate value (e.g., 'x') instead of the final requested quantity (e.g., difference in lengths).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem clearly states the geometric shape and provides the necessary dimensions as algebraic expressions.
The algebraic equation derived should typically be quadratic, leading to two potential solutions for the variable.
The problem should require an additional step after finding the variable, such as calculating a specific length or the difference between possible lengths.
The given dimensions should be structured such that at least one, if not both, solutions for the variable lead to physically sensible (positive) lengths for the relevant dimensions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_cc06fd65. Analyzing Biological Factors Affecting Plant Traits**

**Core move:** Discuss and explain the impact of various environmental, physiological, and ecological factors on specific plant characteristics or processes, such as spread and photosynthetic rate.

**Seen in / context:**
- Ecology questions involving invasive species dynamics.
Plant physiology questions focusing on growth and metabolism.
Environmental biology questions concerning adaptation and distribution.
Questions requiring an understanding of both abiotic and biotic interactions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Listing factors without explaining the mechanism or impact on the specific process (e.g., how temperature affects photosynthesis).
Focusing exclusively on either spread or photosynthetic rate, neglecting the other.
Providing generic answers not tailored to an 'invasive plant' context where applicable.
Omitting crucial factors (e.g., light intensity, CO2 concentration, water availability, nutrient levels, competition, dispersal vectors).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should clearly specify the biological process or characteristic to be analyzed.
Requires an interdisciplinary understanding of plant biology, ecology, and environmental science.
Answers should demonstrate both breadth (identifying multiple factors) and depth (explaining the 'how' and 'why' for each factor).
Can involve both abiotic (non-living) and biotic (living) factors influencing the plant.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q29_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q35_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires interpreting data (implied from a graph) about CO2 uptake in plants and explaining the biological reasons for observed patterns (plateau, differences between plants). This directly aligns with the core move of discussing and explaining the impact of environmental and physiological factors on plant processes like photosynthetic rate.

## **M_cc09dd19. Geometric Reasoning with Bearings**

**Core move:** Apply principles of geometry, including parallel lines and triangle properties, in conjunction with bearing definitions to determine unknown angles or bearings.

**Seen in / context:**
- Navigation problems (ships, planes, landmarks)
Surveying tasks involving angles and distances
Vector problems in a geographical context

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the definition of a bearing (e.g., measuring from East, or counter-clockwise instead of clockwise from North).
Misidentifying alternate interior, corresponding, or consecutive interior angles when using parallel North lines.
Failing to recognize or correctly apply properties of specific triangles (e.g., isosceles or right-angled triangles).
Incorrectly calculating back bearings (the bearing from point B to point A given the bearing from A to B).
Confusing internal triangle angles with external bearings.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage the drawing of a clear, labelled diagram with North lines at all relevant points.
Remind students to consistently use the convention of measuring angles clockwise from North.
Problems often involve calculating back bearings, which is usually (Original Bearing ± 180°).
Triangle properties (sum of angles, isosceles triangle base angles, sine/cosine rule for more complex problems) are frequently essential.
The solution typically involves a sequence of deductions of angles from the given information.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q12_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires using bearing definitions, properties of parallel lines (North lines), and triangle geometry (isosceles triangle, angles sum to 180 degrees) to determine an unknown bearing. This directly aligns with the core move of applying geometric principles in conjunction with bearing definitions.

## **M_cdbe0132. Evaluating Biological Comparisons for Accuracy**

**Core move:** Assess the accuracy of descriptive statements regarding biological structures, their classification, and functions based on established biological knowledge.

**Seen in / context:**
- Multiple choice questions testing factual recall in biology across different topics (e.g., human physiology, plant biology).
Questions requiring the comparison of structures based on shared or distinct functional, anatomical, or histological features.
Identification of correct classifications for biological entities (e.g., organ vs. tissue, endocrine vs. exocrine gland).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misclassifying biological structures (e.g., mistaking a tissue for an organ, or vice-versa).
Attributing a function or characteristic to a structure that it does not possess.
Failing to verify that the descriptive statement applies accurately to *all* components mentioned in the comparison.
Confusing terms or concepts between different biological kingdoms (e.g., plant vs. animal physiology).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should cover a broad range of biological systems and levels of organization.
Options should include plausible but incorrect statements to test depth of understanding.
The correct comparison must be unequivocally supported by standard biological facts.
The complexity can vary by using structures with multiple functions or subtle distinctions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q64_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_d011e6d4. Calculating Cumulative Probabilities of Sequential Events with Stopping Conditions**

**Core move:** Compute the total probability of a defined outcome occurring across a sequence of trials, where trials stop based on a specific event, by summing individual path probabilities or using complementary events.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving drawing items from a bag without replacement until a specific type of item is drawn.
Scenarios where a process continues or stops based on the outcome of each step.
Situations where the number of trials is not fixed, but determined by an event.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating individual conditional probabilities in sequential draws (e.g., forgetting to adjust the total number of items or the count of specific items after each draw).
Forgetting to sum up *all* relevant paths that satisfy the desired condition.
Not recognizing the possibility of using complementary probability to simplify calculations.
Misinterpreting the stopping condition or the desired outcome (e.g., miscalculating N_G > N_R).

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the dynamic nature of probabilities (denominator and numerator change) in 'without replacement' scenarios.
Encourage breaking down the problem into distinct, mutually exclusive sequences of events.
Highlight the utility of the complementary event approach, especially when many paths lead to the desired outcome.
Ensure the stopping condition is clearly understood and correctly applied to determine the valid sequence lengths.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_d54ff9c5. Manipulating Expressions with Exact Trigonometric Values and Surds**

**Core move:** Simplify complex algebraic expressions by substituting exact trigonometric values, rationalizing denominators, and expanding powers of binomials containing surds.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving exact values of sin, cos, tan for standard angles (e.g., 0°, 30°, 45°, 60°, 90°).
Expressions requiring rationalization of denominators containing surds.
Evaluation of powers of binomial expressions of the form (a ± b√c)^n.
Simplifying expressions involving addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division of surds.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrect recall of exact trigonometric values.
Errors in rationalizing denominators (e.g., forgetting to multiply the numerator, incorrect use of difference of squares).
Mistakes in expanding binomials (e.g., incorrectly assuming (a+b)³ = a³+b³).
Arithmetic errors when combining terms involving surds.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the angles chosen lead to standard exact trigonometric values.
The complexity can be varied by changing the power 'n' or the complexity of the initial expression.
Results should ideally simplify to a recognizable form or match provided options, requiring careful design of initial values.
This pattern assesses fundamental algebraic skills combined with basic trigonometry.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q13_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_d5762991. Determining Arithmetic Progression Parameters**

**Core move:** Formulate and solve a system of linear equations derived from conditions on terms of an arithmetic progression to determine its defining parameters and specific terms.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving relationships between terms in arithmetic sequences.
Determining unknown parameters (first term, common difference) of a sequence.
Calculating specific terms or sums of arithmetic sequences based on given conditions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the formula for the nth term (e.g., a + nd instead of a + (n-1)d).
Algebraic errors when setting up or solving the system of equations.
Misinterpreting the word problem statements (e.g., '1 less than twice' vs 'twice less 1').
Confusing arithmetic progressions with geometric progressions or other sequence types.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure conditions translate directly into distinct equations for the first term ('a') and common difference ('d').
Problems can involve various relationships, including sums of terms or specific term values.
The complexity can be varied by using more terms, more complex relationships, or requiring a sum of terms as the final answer.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q79_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_d6419f29. Biological Rate and Combinatorial Calculations**

**Core move:** Calculate unknown quantities by applying rates, performing unit conversions, and using combinatorial principles within biological contexts.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining synthesis rates (e.g., protein, DNA, RNA)
Calculating time taken for biological processes based on given rates
Analyzing the capacity and redundancy of genetic codes or other biological information systems
Solving hypothetical biological scenarios requiring quantitative assessment

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting the biological units and their relationships (e.g., bases per codon, codons per amino acid)
Incorrectly accounting for initiation or termination signals (e.g., start/stop codons) in calculations
Errors in exponential or power calculations when determining combinatorial possibilities
Mistakes in unit cancellation or conversion factor application leading to incorrect final units or values

**Notes for generation:**
- Problems often embed standard mathematical operations (multiplication, division, powers) within detailed biological descriptions.
Encourage breaking down complex problems into smaller, manageable calculation steps.
Attention to significant figures or specified precision may be required in some questions.
Ensure the biological context is correctly understood before applying mathematical methods.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_d6fc6b1d. Applying Proportionality from Physical Laws**

**Core move:** Infer a direct or inverse proportionality between physical quantities from a governing law, and use a given percentage change in one quantity to determine the new value of the proportionally related quantity.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving Ideal Gas Law variations (e.g., Boyle's Law P∝1/V, Charles's Law V∝T, Gay-Lussac's Law P∝T, and P∝ρ from P=(ρ/M)RT).
Relationships like F=ma, V=IR, density ρ=m/V, where some variables are constant.
Questions where one variable changes by a percentage and the effect on a proportionally related variable is sought.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly deriving the proportionality (e.g., assuming direct instead of inverse).
Not accounting for all constant variables in the physical law.
Forgetting that percentage changes are multiplicative (e.g., adding 0.2P instead of multiplying by 1.2P).
Applying the change to the wrong variable or misinterpreting the direction of change.

**Notes for generation:**
- The question should explicitly state or imply which variables are constant.
The physical law can be a direct formula or require some algebraic rearrangement.
The outcome often involves a simple multiplication or division by a percentage factor.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_d9de3280. Calculating Cylindrical Volume in Context**

**Core move:** Apply the formula for the volume of a cylinder (V = πr²h), ensuring correct unit conversions between different length units (e.g., cm to mm), to determine the capacity or quantity within a cylindrical structure described in an applied scenario.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating the volume of blood in arteries or veins.
Determining the capacity of pipes, tubes, or containers.
Estimating material quantities for cylindrical objects in construction or manufacturing.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Using diameter instead of radius in the volume formula.
Failing to convert units consistently across all dimensions (e.g., mixing mm and cm).
Misidentifying the shape as something other than a cylinder.
Confusing volume with surface area or circumference.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often embed the cylindrical object within a broader scientific or engineering context.
Distractors or additional information might be present that is irrelevant to the volume calculation (e.g., the oxygen level for the renal artery is not mathematically related to the 40% oxygen level given for the right atrium/pulmonary artery).
Vary the units given (e.g., m, cm, mm) to test conversion proficiency.
May require knowledge of constants (e.g., π) or approximations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q47_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: The question snippet provides a linear dimension (7 mm) and contextual information about 'water lost by the shoot' from an 'apparatus'. In typical biological experiments (e.g., using a potometer), water loss is quantified by measuring the change in volume of water in a measuring tube, which is often cylindrical. Calculating this volume requires applying the formula for the volume of a cylinder (V = πr²h) and ensuring correct unit conversions, which directly aligns with the core move of schema M_d9de3280. The given information serves as the necessary parameters for such a calculation.

## **M_dd555495. Explaining the Application of Molecular Tools in Genetic Modification**

**Core move:** Describe the specific function and step-by-step role of molecular tools, such as restriction enzymes or vectors, in the process of genetic engineering or modification.

**Seen in / context:**
- Genetic engineering methodologies (e.g., creating recombinant DNA)
Biotechnology applications (e.g., gene cloning, gene therapy)
Molecular biology techniques (e.g., DNA manipulation, PCR)
Enzyme function in biological processes

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Providing a general definition of genetic engineering without detailing the enzyme's specific role.
Confusing the function of different enzymes (e.g., restriction enzymes vs. ligases).
Omitting crucial steps where the enzyme acts (e.g., cutting DNA at specific sites, creating sticky ends).
Describing the overall outcome without explaining the mechanism of the tool's involvement.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question specifies a particular molecular tool (e.g., EcoRI, ligase, plasmid vector) and a biological process (e.g., genetic engineering, gene cloning).
The answer should require explaining 'how' the tool is used, detailing its mechanism of action within the process.
Questions can vary in scope, from a single enzyme's role to the coordinated action of multiple tools.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q33_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_de6412df. Solving Trigonometric Equations in an Interval**

**Core move:** Algebraically factor and solve a trigonometric equation for general solutions, then identify specific solutions that lie within a given interval.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding roots or specific values of trigonometric functions
Analyzing periodic phenomena in mathematics and physics
Problems in pre-calculus and calculus involving trigonometric functions

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Dividing by a trigonometric term that could be zero, thereby losing potential solutions
Incorrectly determining the general solutions for trigonometric equations (e.g., missing solutions in other quadrants or incorrect periodicity)
Errors in converting general solutions to specific values within the specified domain or interval
Not properly checking the boundaries of the given interval

**Notes for generation:**
- Equations can involve basic trigonometric functions, powers, multiple angles, or require identities (e.g., double angle, Pythagorean).
The interval should be clearly defined and can include or exclude endpoints.
Solutions may need to be expressed in radians or degrees, consistent with the interval.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q75_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_df3b6f0b. Explaining DNA Mutations and Disease Pathogenesis**

**Core move:** Explain various types of DNA sequence alterations and detail how these changes disrupt biological functions, leading to specific diseases.

**Seen in / context:**
- Molecular biology
Genetics
Human diseases (e.g., Sickle Cell Anemia, Cystic Fibrosis)
Gene expression and regulation
Protein structure and function

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing changes in chromosome number/structure with point mutations or small indels.
Describing disease symptoms without linking them to the specific molecular change in DNA.
Providing examples of diseases without explaining the mechanism by which the DNA change causes the disease.
Omitting the different categories of DNA sequence changes (e.g., point mutations, frameshift mutations, larger insertions/deletions, duplications, inversions).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question requires both a description of mutation types and an explanation of the causal link to disease.
Emphasize the 'how' (mechanism) at the molecular and cellular level.
Encourage the use of specific examples to illustrate the concepts.
The explanation should ideally cover the impact on DNA sequence, mRNA, protein sequence/structure, and ultimately cellular/organismal function.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_dffc16c1. Calculating Enthalpy Change from Calorimetry Data**

**Core move:** Apply calorimetric data (mass, specific heat capacity, temperature change) to calculate the heat transferred, then use reaction stoichiometry to relate this heat to the moles of a specific reactant or product to determine the enthalpy change per mole.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining enthalpy of neutralization or dissolution experimentally.
Calorimetry experiments where temperature change is measured to find reaction enthalpy.
Problems involving heat release/absorption and stoichiometry to find molar enthalpy changes.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly determining the moles of the reactant/product for which the enthalpy change is requested, especially if stoichiometry is not 1:1.
Forgetting to convert energy units (e.g., J to kJ) or volume units (e.g., cm³ to dm³).
Using the wrong mass in Q=mcΔT (e.g., only one reactant's mass instead of total solution mass).
Incorrectly assigning the sign of the enthalpy change (positive for exothermic, negative for endothermic, or vice-versa).
Confusing enthalpy change per mole of one substance with enthalpy change per mole of another in a reaction with varying coefficients.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly specifies the substance for which the molar enthalpy change is to be calculated.
Provide necessary constants like density and specific heat capacity, or indicate assumptions.
Emphasize the importance of writing and balancing the chemical equation to establish stoichiometry.
Vary the type of reaction (neutralization, dissolution, combustion) and the given concentrations/volumes.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_e066fa4f. Deducing Organic Structures from Chemical Information**

**Core move:** Infer the specific structural formula of an organic molecule based on its observed chemical reactions or stated structure-property relationships.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining reactant structures from product characteristics in organic reactions.
Identifying isomers that satisfy specific criteria related to reaction outcomes (e.g., number of products).
Applying rules about molecular stability or reactivity to deduce a structure.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying reaction mechanisms (e.g., carbocation rearrangements, Markovnikov's rule).
Misinterpreting isomerism (e.g., failing to distinguish unique structural isomers).
Neglecting to systematically test all relevant possible structures.
Failure to correctly interpret qualitative statements linking structure and property.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions typically involve drawing and naming organic compounds.
The information provided should be sufficient to narrow down to a unique structure.
Often requires knowledge of common functional groups and reaction types.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q27_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q37_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires inferring the specific structural formula of compound W by integrating multiple pieces of chemical information: the empirical formula of V, the mass percentage of nitrogen in compound X (a structure-property relationship), and the observed chemical reactions and lack of reactivity of compounds Y and Z (chemical reactions and structure-property relationships). This directly matches the core move of M_e066fa4f.

## **M_e18cb30e. Calculating Work Done by Non-Conservative Forces**

**Core move:** Apply the work-energy theorem or the principle of conservation of energy to account for work done by non-conservative forces during a change in mechanical energy.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving friction, air resistance, or other dissipative forces.
Situations where an object's mechanical energy is not conserved (e.g., a block sliding down a rough incline, a projectile with air resistance).
Calculating efficiency of systems with energy losses.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming conservation of mechanical energy when non-conservative forces are present.
Incorrectly identifying initial or final states (e.g., initial kinetic energy if starting from rest, final potential energy at ground level).
Sign errors when accounting for work done by or against resistive forces.
Forgetting to convert units (e.g., grams to kilograms).

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the relationship: Initial Mechanical Energy + Work Done by Non-Conservative Forces = Final Mechanical Energy, or Work Done by All Forces = Change in Kinetic Energy.
Questions can vary the unknown (e.g., find height, final speed, or the resistive force if distance is known).
Ensure all necessary physical constants (like 'g') are provided or can be assumed.
Units must be consistent (SI units preferred).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q82_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q15_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks for the work done on the trolley, which requires calculating the change in kinetic energy using the Work-Energy Theorem. This aligns with the core move of applying the work-energy theorem to account for work done, even though specific non-conservative forces are not explicitly mentioned, the net work calculated changes the kinetic energy.

## **M_e28f67a8. Area Between Functions by Integration**

**Core move:** Calculate the definite integral of the difference between two functions over the interval defined by their intersection points to find the area of the enclosed region.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding the area enclosed by a curve and a line.
Finding the area enclosed by two curves.
Calculating areas in coordinate geometry where boundaries are not strictly linear.
Applications of definite integrals in geometry.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to find the intersection points correctly to establish limits of integration.
Integrating the functions individually instead of finding the integral of their difference.
Incorrectly determining which function is the 'upper' or 'lower' function within the integration interval.
Errors in basic integration or algebraic manipulation of the integrand.
Attempting to use standard geometric formulas (e.g., area of a triangle, rectangle) for non-polygonal regions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage sketching the graphs to visualize the region and correctly identify upper/lower functions and integration limits.
Remind students to solve the equations simultaneously to find the x-coordinates of intersection.
Highlight the importance of integrating (upper function - lower function) dx.
Consider symmetry to simplify calculations where applicable (e.g., if the region is symmetric about the y-axis).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q73_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_e30696a8. Calculating Penetrated Volume in 3D Shapes**

**Core move:** Calculate the volume of a region penetrated from the exterior of a 3D geometric shape by subtracting the volume of the unpenetrated inner core from the total volume of the original shape, often requiring consideration of penetration depth from all surfaces.

**Seen in / context:**
- Biological experiments involving diffusion into gels or tissues.
Material science problems involving corrosion, coating penetration, or heat conduction into solids.
Geometric problems involving finding the volume of a shell or a hollowed object.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the dimensions of the unpenetrated core (e.g., subtracting penetration depth only once from each dimension, or from only one dimension).
Calculating the volume of only one 'layer' or surface instead of the entire penetrated region.
Confusing surface area with volume.
Using incorrect volume formulas for the given geometric shape.
Misinterpreting the scientific process (e.g., diffusion vs. osmosis).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the original shape (cube, sphere, cylinder) and the uniform penetration depth are clearly defined.
The calculation typically involves finding the volume of the original object and the volume of a smaller, similar internal object (the unpenetrated core), then subtracting the latter from the former.
Can be combined with conceptual scientific understanding (e.g., type of transport, rate of reaction).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q45_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_e36d3949. Relating 2D Nets to 3D Volumes**

**Core move:** Relate the dimensions of a 2D net or component shape to the geometric properties and formulas of the resulting 3D object to calculate its volume or surface area.

**Seen in / context:**
- Forming cylinders from rectangular sheets where one dimension becomes height and the other circumference.
Constructing cones from circular sectors and triangles.
Calculating surface area or volume of prisms and pyramids from their nets.
Identifying the constituent 2D shapes that form the surface of a 3D object.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assigning the 2D dimensions to the 3D properties (e.g., confusing height with circumference).
Using the wrong geometric formula for volume or surface area.
Algebraic errors when solving for intermediate values like radius.
Failing to understand the transformation from 2D to 3D.

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly specify which edges/dimensions of the 2D shape are joined or form specific parts of the 3D object.
Vary the 3D shapes (cylinders, cones, prisms, pyramids) and the 2D nets involved.
Questions can be reversed, asking for the dimensions of the original 2D sheet given the 3D object's properties.
Ensure numerical values lead to calculable (even if irrational) answers.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the volume and surface area of a 3D object (cuboid) using its given dimensions. This directly aligns with the core move of relating component shapes' dimensions (the faces of the cuboid) to the geometric properties (volume and surface area) of the 3D object.

## **M_e3b89d1b. Recognizing Incomplete Problem Statements**

**Core move:** Identify when a problem statement lacks sufficient information, a clear question, or context to formulate a mathematical approach or solution.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing question papers for completeness.
Reviewing problem sets for clarity and solvability.
Initial assessment of a given task to determine necessary preconditions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Attempting to invent missing data or assume implied questions.
Proceeding with a 'solution' based on insufficient information.
Assuming the question is trivial or has no solution without explicitly identifying the incompleteness.

**Notes for generation:**
- Such a question, if intentional, should explicitly ask the test-taker to identify what is missing.
For general question generation, ensure all numerical information, context, and the explicit query are present.
The 'problem' here is the absence of a problem, requiring a judgment about the input itself rather than a mathematical solution.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_e3d65c6a. Relating Roots and Coefficients of Quadratic Equations**

**Core move:** Apply Vieta's formulas to establish relationships between the roots and coefficients of a quadratic equation, and use these relationships to solve for unknown coefficients or properties of the roots.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding unknown coefficients given information about the roots (e.g., sum, product, difference, ratio).
Determining properties of roots (e.g., real, equal, distinct, sum, product) from coefficients.
Constructing quadratic equations given properties of their roots.
Problems involving transformation of roots.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying 'a', 'b', or 'c' from the general form ax^2 + bx + c = 0.
Sign errors in Vieta's formulas (e.g., x1 + x2 = b/a instead of -b/a).
Algebraic mistakes when solving the system of equations derived from Vieta's formulas and the given root properties.
Forgetting to consider constraints on variables (e.g., 'p' is positive).
Incorrectly expanding (x1 - x2)^2 or confusing it with x1^2 - x2^2.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize understanding the standard form ax^2 + bx + c = 0 and the corresponding Vieta's formulas.
Questions can vary the type of information given about the roots (sum, product, difference, ratio, one root is twice the other, etc.).
Ensure algebraic manipulation to solve for the unknown is straightforward but requires care.
Include conditions on coefficients or roots (e.g., 'p' is positive, roots are real).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q17_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q14_NSAA_Sec`: The question directly asks for a property of the roots (their positive difference) of a quadratic equation, which requires the application of Vieta's formulas to relate the roots to the given coefficients (2, 4, and c).

## **M_e43c9eed. Calculating Ratios After Independent Percentage Changes**

**Core move:** Determine the new ratio between two quantities by applying individual percentage changes to each quantity and then forming the ratio of their modified values.

**Seen in / context:**
- Comparing salaries, prices, populations, or other metrics over time.
Problems involving financial calculations where different assets undergo independent proportional adjustments.
Situations where two or more related quantities undergo independent percentage increases or decreases before a final comparison.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Adding or subtracting percentages directly instead of using multiplicative factors (e.g., 1.15 for a 15% increase).
Incorrectly identifying the base quantity for the initial percentage relationship or for the subsequent percentage changes.
Failing to express the final answer as a percentage when required, or misinterpreting the ratio.
Assuming absolute values are needed for initial quantities, rather than recognizing that they often cancel out when forming a ratio.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage setting up initial relationships using variables (e.g., if A is 80% of B, then A = 0.8B).
Emphasize the use of decimal multipliers for percentage changes (e.g., 1 + p/100 for an increase, 1 - p/100 for a decrease).
Ensure students correctly set up the final ratio (numerator/denominator) as specified by the question.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q9_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_e5a1a321. Resolving Forces into Components**

**Core move:** Decompose a force vector into its perpendicular components using trigonometric functions to analyze its effects along different axes.

**Seen in / context:**
- Physics problems involving forces applied at an angle to motion or surfaces.
Calculating work done by a force that is not parallel to displacement.
Determining normal forces or friction when an applied force has a vertical component.
Analyzing motion on inclined planes.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly using sine for the adjacent component or cosine for the opposite component.
Using the total force magnitude instead of its relevant component in calculations (e.g., work, friction).
Confusing the angle given with the angle relative to the desired axis (e.g., angle with vertical vs. horizontal).
Forgetting to include all forces acting on an object when determining net force or normal force.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly specifies the angle's reference (e.g., 'below the horizontal', 'above the horizontal', 'to the normal').
Provide values that allow for common trigonometric ratios or use a calculator for less common angles.
The question can extend to calculating work done, net force, acceleration, or normal force, once components are found.
This pattern can be applied in 2D or 3D contexts, though 2D is more common at this level.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q76_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q88_NSAA_Sec`: The question presents two perpendicular forces and requires finding their resultant magnitude using vector addition (Pythagoras theorem). This is the inverse operation of decomposing a force into its perpendicular components, and both skills fall under the umbrella of analyzing forces along different axes using vector components.

## **M_e5a53f2b. Relating Quantities Through Proportions and Ratios**

**Core move:** Establish an algebraic relationship between two or more quantities based on given percentages, fractions, or proportional statements, then simplify this relationship to determine their lowest whole-number ratio.

**Seen in / context:**
- Word problems involving 'x percent of A is y percent of B'
Comparing quantities using fractions (e.g., 'one-third of X is half of Y')
Problems requiring conversion between percentages, fractions, and ratios
Financial calculations involving proportional distribution

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly converting percentages to decimals or fractions.
Errors in algebraic manipulation when isolating the ratio.
Failing to simplify the ratio to its lowest whole-number terms.
Misinterpreting the order of the ratio (e.g., 'first to second' vs. 'second to first').

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem clearly defines the relationship between the quantities.
Use distinct variables for each quantity.
The final ratio should always be in its simplest integer form.
Can be extended to involve more than two quantities, leading to extended ratios (e.g., A:B:C).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q12_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q12_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires establishing an algebraic inverse proportionality relationship (B = k/T³) and then applying a percentage increase to one quantity to find a new value, which perfectly aligns with 'Establishing an algebraic relationship between two or more quantities based on given percentages... or proportional statements'.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q14_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires establishing algebraic relationships between three quantities (red, blue, yellow paint) based on their given ratios, and then simplifying these relationships to solve for an unknown variable 'p' within one of the ratios. This aligns with the core move of establishing and simplifying algebraic relationships from proportional statements.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q63_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires using a given percentage (10% adenine) and biological proportional rules (A=T, G=C, and total bases = A+T+G+C) to determine the quantities of all base types. This involves establishing algebraic relationships based on percentages and proportions, and then simplifying these relationships to find the number of each component, which aligns well with the core move of relating quantities through proportions and ratios.

## **M_e8c48f8e. Predicting Boiling Point Order from Structure**

**Core move:** Apply knowledge of intermolecular forces, molecular size, and branching to predict and order the boiling points of organic compounds.

**Seen in / context:**
- Comparing compounds with different functional groups (e.g., alkanes, alcohols, carboxylic acids).
Analyzing the effect of molecular size and chain length on intermolecular forces.
Evaluating the impact of branching on surface area for London dispersion forces.
Predicting trends in other physical properties like melting point, solubility, or viscosity based on similar structural considerations.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly ranking the relative strengths of different types of intermolecular forces (e.g., hydrogen bonding vs. dipole-dipole).
Overlooking the effect of molecular mass/size when comparing compounds with similar intermolecular forces.
Ignoring the influence of molecular branching on London dispersion forces and thus boiling point.
Failing to correctly identify the functional groups or overall structure of the organic compounds.

**Notes for generation:**
- Provide a set of organic compounds, either explicitly or as products of reactions, for comparison.
Ensure the compounds demonstrate a range of relevant structural features (functional groups, chain length, branching).
The correct ordering should be unambiguous based on standard chemical principles.
Can be extended to other physical properties that depend on intermolecular forces.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q4_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_e9d864f6. Recalling Biological Facts and Functional Roles**

**Core move:** Identify specific biological entities, their properties, and their functional significance within a given biological context.

**Seen in / context:**
- Questions about the type of biological molecules (e.g., enzymes, hormones, structural proteins).
Questions about specific bonds or structures within biomolecules (e.g., phosphodiester bonds, peptide bonds, double helix structure).
Questions requiring the identification of characteristics of biological sequences or structures (e.g., palindromic sequences, active sites).
Questions about the specific roles or functions of biological components in cells or organisms (e.g., defense mechanisms, catalytic activity, gene regulation).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the identity of a molecule with its function.
Misidentifying specific bonds or structures.
Failing to connect a characteristic to its functional consequence or aid as requested.
Providing a general answer when a specific biological term or concept is required.

**Notes for generation:**
- These questions often test direct recall of biological knowledge.
Answers typically require precise biological terminology.
May involve explaining 'how' a characteristic contributes to a function.
Can cover various levels of biological organization, from molecular to cellular.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q31_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_eb51e883. Determining Empirical/Molecular Formula from Mass Composition**

**Core move:** Convert the given masses of constituent elements into moles using their respective atomic masses, then determine the simplest whole-number ratio of these moles to deduce the empirical formula.

**Seen in / context:**
- Stoichiometry problems in chemistry
Analyzing the elemental composition of compounds
Deriving chemical formulas from experimental data
Comparing empirical and molecular formulas

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing atomic number with atomic mass (Ar)
Failing to convert mass to moles before finding the ratio
Not dividing by the smallest number of moles to simplify the ratio
Incorrectly calculating the mass of an unstated element in the compound
Assuming the empirical formula is always the molecular formula without checking options or molar mass

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all constituent elements' masses are accounted for.
The resulting mole ratios may need slight rounding to the nearest whole number, especially with experimental data.
If the molar mass of the compound is provided, compare the empirical formula mass to the molar mass to find the scaling factor for the molecular formula. If molecular formula options are given, the empirical formula can often be directly matched or scaled.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q81_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q23_NSAA_Sec`: The initial and most crucial step in solving this problem is to determine the chemical formula of compound X. This involves calculating the moles of each constituent element (potassium, hydrogen, oxygen, and implicitly carbon, which must be deduced from the total mass and the reaction hint), and then finding the simplest whole-number ratio to establish the empirical formula. This process directly aligns with the core move of schema M_eb51e883: 'Convert the given masses of constituent elements into moles using their respective atomic masses, then determine the simplest whole-number ratio of these moles to deduce the empirical formula.' The subsequent steps of identifying reactants and deducing Y rely entirely on the correct determination of X's formula.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the mass of water lost, converting the masses of both the anhydrous salt and the water into moles, and then determining the simplest whole-number ratio between them to find 'x', which is the stoichiometric coefficient in the hydrated formula. This directly aligns with the core move of determining an empirical formula from mass composition by converting masses to moles and finding their ratio.

## **M_ec554dea. Analyzing Number Grid Patterns with Positional Relationships**

**Core move:** Deduce the implicit rule for number arrangement within a grid or spiral, often involving square or triangular numbers, to determine a value at a specific relative position.

**Seen in / context:**
- Number grids or spirals where numbers increase sequentially.
Problems asking for numbers 'below', 'above', 'left', or 'right' of a given number.
Patterns often involve identifying properties of numbers (e.g., square roots) at specific locations, or the 'layer' of the spiral.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming a simple arithmetic progression or fixed common difference.
Misinterpreting 'below' or other positional terms without understanding the underlying grid structure.
Not recognizing the significance of specific numbers provided (e.g., 16 and 196 being square numbers).
Attempting to manually draw out a large section of the grid rather than finding a general algebraic or functional rule.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the numbers provided in the question (like '16' or '196') are key to uncovering the pattern rule, not just arbitrary values or problem numbers.
The derived rule for positional relationships should be consistently applicable across different 'layers' or segments of the pattern.
Questions often involve identifying square roots, powers, or layer-based increments/decrements in the sequence.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q16_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_ef3e0cd6. Sequential Geometric Transformations of Coordinates**

**Core move:** Systematically apply a series of geometric transformation rules (rotation, reflection, translation, dilation) to specific coordinates, calculating the intermediate and final positions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Coordinate geometry problems involving mapping points or shapes
Finding the final position of a point after multiple transformations
Understanding the order of operations for transformations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Applying transformations in the incorrect order
Using the wrong center for rotation or incorrect axis for reflection
Errors in applying the transformation rules (e.g., incorrect signs for rotation, incorrect coordinates for reflection)
Calculation errors when performing addition/subtraction after translation or coordinate changes

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often involve a mix of rotation, reflection, and/or translation.
The order of transformations is crucial and must be followed as stated.
Can ask for the transformation of a single point or all vertices of a shape.
Rotation about a point other than the origin requires a translation, rotation, and then inverse translation sequence, or direct application of the rotation formula about a general point.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q14_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying a sequence of two geometric transformations (rotation followed by translation) to a line segment, specifically focusing on how these transformations affect the coordinates of its midpoint. This directly aligns with the core move of systematically applying a series of geometric transformation rules to calculate intermediate and final positions.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying two successive translation vectors (a type of geometric transformation) to an initial coordinate to find the final coordinate, directly matching the core move of systematically applying sequential geometric transformation rules.

## **M_efaaaeaa. Interpreting Temperature-Rate Relationship in Kinetics**

**Core move:** Assess and interpret the relationship between temperature and reaction rate by applying principles of chemical kinetics to evaluate given statements or predict trends.

**Seen in / context:**
- Evaluating multiple-choice statements regarding temperature's effect on reaction speed.
Predicting changes in reaction time or rate given a temperature alteration.
Explaining the molecular basis (e.g., collision theory, activation energy) for temperature's impact on reaction rates.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing temperature's effect on reaction rate with its effect on chemical equilibrium.
Assuming a simple linear relationship between temperature and reaction rate rather than an exponential one (e.g., Arrhenius equation).
Ignoring the role of activation energy in determining reaction speed.
Failing to consider increased kinetic energy, collision frequency, or the proportion of successful collisions.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often test qualitative understanding of the relationship rather than requiring complex calculations (unless specific kinetic data or the Arrhenius equation is provided).
Emphasize core concepts like kinetic energy distribution, collision theory, and the activation energy barrier.
Ensure statements provided for evaluation cover common scientific principles and potential misconceptions related to temperature and reaction rates.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_efe189a9. Separating Mixture Components by Properties**

**Core move:** Utilize characteristic physical or chemical properties to predict and describe the separation of components in a mixture.

**Seen in / context:**
- Designing chemical separation processes (e.g., filtration, distillation, decantation)
Identifying unknown substances based on their unique properties
Analyzing purification steps in laboratory or industrial settings
Understanding the behavior of mixtures when exposed to solvents or other agents

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly recalling or applying the physical/chemical properties of substances (e.g., solubility, density, boiling point)
Confusing suitable separation techniques for different types of mixtures
Failing to account for all components or impurities in a mixture
Misinterpreting the role of a solvent or reactant in a separation process

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly specify the initial mixture and relevant conditions (e.g., solvent, temperature).
Ensure the question requires the student to either identify properties, choose a method, or describe the outcome of a separation.
Can be extended to quantitative problems involving mass, volume, or concentration changes during separation.
Vary the type of properties to be exploited (e.g., solubility, magnetism, boiling point, density, reactivity).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_f0892922. Selecting Correct Item Combinations**

**Core move:** Evaluate each potential item against specified criteria to form a definitive set, then identify the multiple-choice option that precisely matches this derived set.

**Seen in / context:**
- Multiple-choice questions where options are presented as sets of enumerated statements or conditions.
Logic or reasoning problems requiring the identification of all correct components from a given list.
Assessment questions where several facts or properties need to be confirmed and grouped together.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to individually evaluate every enumerated item against the criteria.
Mistakenly including an item that does not meet the criteria or omitting one that does.
Choosing an option that contains a partial but not exhaustive or accurate set of items.
Misinterpreting the conditions or criteria that govern the selection of items.

**Notes for generation:**
- The question prompt must clearly define the criteria for selecting items.
Each enumerated item (e.g., 1, 2, 7) should be distinct and unambiguous.
Distractor options should include common selection errors, such as nearly correct sets, sets with one extra item, or sets with one missing item.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: The question explicitly asks 'Which of the following is/are correct...', indicating that the core task is to evaluate a set of potential items (statements, options) against specified criteria (the genetic assumptions provided) to identify the correct ones. This perfectly aligns with the core move of selecting correct item combinations.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q17_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires evaluating multiple independent biological criteria (the composition of antibodies, the expected outcome of a serological test for human blood, and the identity of a test species) against a set of options presented in rows. The core move involves assessing each component of a row for correctness based on biological knowledge and then identifying the row where all components are consistent with the implied investigation, which aligns with 'Selecting Correct Item Combinations'.

## **M_f180baad. Interpreting Elemental Data from a Periodic Table**

**Core move:** Extract and synthesize specific chemical information and patterns from the structured arrangement of elements in a periodic table.

**Seen in / context:**
- Identifying elements by symbol, name, or atomic number.
Determining relationships between elements based on their position (groups, periods).
Inferring general properties or trends from an element's position.
Counting elements within defined categories or ranges (e.g., metals, non-metals, specific periods).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting the meaning of atomic number or elemental symbols.
Incorrectly applying positional rules, such as confusing periods with groups.
Assuming knowledge of properties not directly inferable from the table (e.g., number of neutrons without mass number).
Forgetting the special placement or numbering of Lanthanoids and Actinoids.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should require direct lookup, simple counting, or basic pattern recognition based on the periodic table's layout.
This schema can often be combined with other schemas for further calculations or classifications (e.g., applying M_38573e2f for subatomic particle analysis once atomic number is extracted).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_f278af2b. Synchronizing Linear and Periodic Motion**

**Core move:** Coordinate the timing of linear motion events with the periodic cycles of a rotating or oscillating system to satisfy given conditions.

**Seen in / context:**
- Particles falling through rotating apertures or slots.
Objects passing through oscillating gates or mechanisms.
Timing events in systems involving repetitive cycles (e.g., machinery, pendulums).
Problems often involve finding minimum/maximum frequencies, heights, or time intervals.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to account for all possible periodic alignments (e.g., considering only the first period, n=1, when other integer multiples might be valid).
Mistaking period for frequency or vice-versa, or using inconsistent units.
Incorrectly applying kinematic equations for the linear motion (e.g., wrong acceleration or initial velocity).
Not recognizing the necessity for integer multiples of the period for successful synchronization.
Failing to connect the calculated time of linear motion to the periodic time of the rotating/oscillating system correctly.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize translating conditions like 'alignment' or 'passing through' into a specific time constraint, typically involving integer multiples of the period (t = nT or t = n/f).
The problem often requires finding the smallest (or largest) integer 'n' that satisfies the physical constraints.
Ensure consistent units throughout the calculations (e.g., s^-1 for frequency, rad/s for angular velocity, m/s^2 for acceleration due to gravity).
Carefully consider initial conditions, such as 'initially at rest' or 'hole vertically below it'.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q7_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_f320f11e. Quantitative Redox Stoichiometry with Specified Product State**

**Core move:** Calculate the mass or volume of a reactant or product by balancing a redox reaction, using given oxidation states or product descriptions to identify species, and applying stoichiometric ratios with relevant conversion factors.

**Seen in / context:**
- Gravimetric analysis involving redox reactions.
Industrial chemical synthesis calculations.
Environmental chemistry problems involving oxidation/reduction processes and yield.
Stoichiometry questions involving gases at non-standard conditions (like RTP).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to correctly identify the product chemical formula based on the given oxidation state or description.
Incorrectly balancing the redox reaction, leading to erroneous mole ratios.
Errors in converting between gas volume and moles (e.g., using standard molar volume for RTP).
Miscalculations in converting moles to mass using molar mass.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem clearly provides enough information (e.g., oxidation state, descriptive name) to deduce the product formula.
Emphasize the importance of balancing the *entire* chemical equation, not just the redox half-reactions.
May require awareness of common acids/bases or simple inorganic compounds.
The use of molar volume at RTP (or STP) is a common element in such problems.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q36_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_f5c3f33f. Conditions for Polynomial Root Multiplicity**

**Core move:** Employ the relationship between a polynomial and its derivative to establish conditions for specific root multiplicities or the number of distinct real roots.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding conditions for a polynomial to have exactly 'k' distinct real roots.
Determining when a polynomial has a tangent to the x-axis.
Solving for parameters that yield specific root patterns in polynomial equations.
Analyzing the number of real roots of cubic or quartic polynomials.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting that P(x_0) = 0 AND P'(x_0) = 0 implies a repeated root at x_0.
Confusing 'two distinct real roots' (meaning one double root and one single root for a cubic) with 'three distinct real roots' or 'exactly one real root'.
Incorrectly calculating the derivative or solving the resulting system of equations (P(x)=0 and P'(x)=0).
Not considering the second derivative (P''(x_0)=0) if a triple root might be possible.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often involve cubic or quartic polynomials where a specific multiplicity or number of distinct roots is required.
The parameter might affect coefficients of multiple terms in the polynomial.
The discriminant of the derivative P'(x) can indicate the existence of local extrema, which is crucial for determining the number of real roots.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q83_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_f62b2484. Relating Diverse Experimental Measurements**

**Core move:** Convert and interrelate different physical measurements from distinct experimental setups to quantify a shared underlying process or rate.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing experimental data from different apparatuses measuring the same phenomenon (e.g., transpiration, reaction rate).
Problems involving unit conversions between mass, volume, length, and time to calculate a rate or quantity.
Comparing the efficacy or consistency of different measurement techniques.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly converting between units (e.g., volume from mass using density, or length to volume using cross-sectional area).
Misinterpreting the physical quantity measured by each apparatus (e.g., confusing mass loss with volume displacement).
