Failing to account for critical physical properties or constants (e.g., density of water, cross-sectional area of a tube).
Ignoring the time component when calculating rates.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary physical constants (e.g., density of water, π) are either provided or assumed as common knowledge.
Questions should clearly define the measured quantities in each setup and the target quantity or rate to be calculated.
Involve at least two distinct measurement methods or units that require conversion and interrelation.
Vary the complexity by requiring multiple steps of conversion or calculation, potentially over different timeframes.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q51_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_f6b6f2b0. Determining Molecular Dipoles from Structure**

**Core move:** Predict the presence of a permanent molecular dipole by analyzing molecular geometry and the polarity of individual bonds.

**Seen in / context:**
- Chemistry: Chemical bonding and molecular structure
Predicting intermolecular forces
Explaining solubility and miscibility
Spectroscopy (e.g., microwave spectroscopy)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing bond polarity with overall molecular polarity
Incorrectly determining molecular geometry using VSEPR theory
Failing to account for the vector nature of bond dipoles and their cancellation due to symmetry
Ignoring the influence of lone pairs on molecular geometry and hence polarity
Incorrectly assessing bond polarity due to misunderstanding electronegativity differences

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure a variety of molecular shapes are testable (e.g., linear, bent, trigonal planar, pyramidal, tetrahedral)
Include molecules with both polar and nonpolar bonds
Vary the central atom and terminal atoms to change electronegativity differences and lone pair presence
Questions can involve identifying polar/nonpolar molecules or ranking molecules by dipole moment strength

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q33_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_f6dce32b. Interpreting Structured Data Grids**

**Core move:** Extract specific data points or identify positional relationships and local patterns within a structured grid or table.

**Seen in / context:**
- Periodic tables, where elements are arranged by atomic number and chemical properties.
Any grid-based data presentation where rows, columns, or relative positions convey meaning.
Spreadsheets or simple databases where lookups and localized pattern recognition are key.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting row/column headers or legends.
Attempting to deduce global patterns when only local relationships are relevant.
Confusing different types of data presented in the grid.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should involve either direct lookup or simple inferences based on adjacency or relative position within the grid.
The grid can be regular or irregular in shape (like the periodic table).
The underlying 'rules' of the grid's structure might be explicit or implicitly understood (e.g., atomic number increases by 1 for consecutive elements).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_f8656e2b. Analyzing Perpendicular Lines**

**Core move:** Determine the gradients of two straight lines from their equations and apply the condition for perpendicularity (m1 * m2 = -1) to solve for an unknown parameter.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving geometric relationships between lines (parallel, perpendicular, intersecting).
Finding unknown coefficients in line equations.
Coordinate geometry questions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly rearranging the line equations to find the gradient (y = mx + c).
Forgetting or misapplying the condition for perpendicularity (e.g., confusing it with the condition for parallel lines).
Algebraic errors when solving the resulting equation for the parameter.
Not considering all possible values for the parameter if the resulting equation is quadratic or higher order.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the line equations are given in various forms (e.g., standard form Ax + By = C, intercept form x/a + y/b = 1, or gradient-intercept form y = mx + c).
The unknown parameter could be in the x-coefficient, y-coefficient, or constant term.
The condition could also be for parallel lines (m1 = m2) or for a specific angle of intersection.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q89_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q12_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires determining the gradients of two lines (one from two points, the other from its equation) and then applying the condition for perpendicularity (m1 * m2 = -1) to solve for the unknown parameter 'q'. This directly matches the core move of schema M_f8656e2b.

## **M_fb83afb1. Calculating Forces and Power in Equilibrium**

**Core move:** Apply the relationship between power, force, and velocity, alongside Newton's First Law (equilibrium of forces), and resolve forces into components to determine unknown forces or power in scenarios of constant velocity.

**Seen in / context:**
- Objects moving at constant speed on level ground or inclines.
Scenarios involving drag, friction, or other resistive forces.
Problems requiring calculation of power output or driving force.
Situations where forces need to be resolved into components (e.g., gravity on an incline).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing driving force with net force.
Forgetting to resolve weight into components when on an incline.
Incorrectly applying the power formula (e.g., P = F/v or P = Fv^2).
Assuming resistive forces change between different parts of the problem if not explicitly stated.
Failing to equate driving force and resistive force when speed is constant on level ground.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize that 'constant speed' implies zero net force (equilibrium), allowing the sum of forces to be set to zero.
Questions often involve two distinct phases (e.g., level ground then incline) where information from one phase is crucial for the other.
Ensure consistent units (e.g., Watts for power, Newtons for force, m/s for velocity).
Trigonometric functions (sine for parallel component, cosine for perpendicular) are key for resolving forces on inclined planes.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q10_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section2_Q2_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying Newton's First Law (since the block is at rest, a scenario of constant zero velocity) and resolving the weight into components along and perpendicular to the inclined plane to compare the magnitudes of the forces (friction, normal force, and weight). This aligns with the core move of applying equilibrium conditions and resolving forces to determine unknown forces in constant velocity scenarios.

## **M_fbd1e7ab. Applying Trigonometry to Bearings Problems**

**Core move:** Translate navigational bearings into internal angles of a geometric shape, typically a triangle, and then apply trigonometric rules (Sine Rule or Cosine Rule) to determine unknown distances or angles.

**Seen in / context:**
- Navigation and surveying problems involving relative positions and directions.
Calculating distances or angles between points given their bearings from one or more reference points.
Geometric problems requiring the construction and solution of triangles from directional data.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly converting a bearing into an internal angle of the triangle, often by misinterpreting clockwise/counter-clockwise or the reference line (e.g., using East instead of North).
Failing to draw an accurate diagram with North lines, leading to errors in angle determination.
Incorrectly applying the Sine Rule (e.g., mismatching sides and opposite angles) or the Cosine Rule (e.g., using the wrong angle for the given sides).
Assuming the triangle formed is right-angled without proof, when it might not be.

**Notes for generation:**
- Always start by drawing a clear, well-labelled diagram including North lines at each relevant point.
Remember that bearings are measured clockwise from North (0°/360°).
Use properties of parallel lines (North lines) and transversals to find internal angles of the triangle.
Systematically calculate each internal angle of the triangle before applying trigonometric rules.
Check if the problem simplifies to a right-angled triangle, as this often simplifies calculations (SOH CAH TOA).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q6_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_fbf02015. Analyzing Physiological Rates and Mechanisms**

**Core move:** Calculate and compare rates of physiological processes using given dimensional and temporal data, and identify associated biological mechanisms.

**Seen in / context:**
- Nerve impulse speed calculations and comparisons.
Enzyme reaction rates or cellular transport speeds.
Comparing efficiencies or speeds of different biological pathways.
Any scenario requiring quantitative comparison of biological events alongside specific biological recall.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Errors in unit conversion (e.g., cm to m, nm to m).
Mistakes in handling scientific notation during calculations.
Incorrectly calculating ratios or comparisons between the rates.
Misremembering or misunderstanding the fundamental biological mechanism (e.g., active transport vs. diffusion, chemical vs. electrical transmission).
Failing to convert units to a consistent base before performing calculations or comparisons.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem requires multiple calculations of rates/speeds before a final comparison.
Incorporate unit conversions that span several orders of magnitude, often involving scientific notation.
Integrate a clear biological knowledge component that requires recall of specific facts relevant to the calculated processes.
The comparison should typically result in a distinct numerical ratio or order of magnitude difference.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q79_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_fcb67590. Identifying Biomolecular Subunits**

**Core move:** Accurately identify the constituent molecular subunits or groups that combine to form a specific biological macromolecule or structure.

**Seen in / context:**
- Components of nucleotides (phosphate, sugar, base)
Components of proteins (amino acids)
Components of lipids (fatty acids, glycerol)
Components of carbohydrates (monosaccharides)
Structure and components of cell organelles or biological membranes

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing components from different classes of macromolecules (e.g., including amino acids in a nucleotide description).
Including non-constituent or higher-level structures as components (e.g., 'gene' as a component of a nucleotide).
Misidentifying the specific type of sugar or base for a given nucleic acid (e.g., ribose for DNA).
Incorrectly listing the number or arrangement of subunits.

**Notes for generation:**
- Vary the target macromolecule or structure (e.g., DNA, RNA, protein, phospholipid, starch).
Provide plausible distractors that include components of other related biological molecules.
The question can ask for the complete set of components, or identify an incorrect component within a list.
Ensure options clearly distinguish between monomers and polymers, or components and the resulting structure.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q1_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_fd4ee276. Inferring Compound Formula from Reaction Stoichiometry**

**Core move:** Infer the elemental composition and consequently the molecular formula of an unknown compound by applying the principle of conservation of atoms in a given chemical reaction description, then calculate its relative molecular mass.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining formulas of coordination complexes or other reaction products.
Stoichiometry problems where a product's formula is deduced.
Problems requiring calculation of molar/molecular mass from a derived formula.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly accounting for all reactant and byproduct atoms when deriving the unknown's formula.
Making errors in retrieving or applying relative atomic masses from the periodic table.
Failing to apply the correct stoichiometric coefficients for reactants and products.
Assuming a known or simpler formula for the unknown compound without derivation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary reactant formulas are known or implicitly expected.
Clearly state the stoichiometry of the reaction, including any minor products.
Explicitly mention that the reaction is balanced or provide enough information to balance it.
Provide access to relative atomic mass data (e.g., 'see periodic table').

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section2_Q17_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: The question's initial and fundamental task (part d) is to infer the molecular formula of an unknown compound (gas A) directly from the stoichiometric ratios of reacting gases (fluorine and A's product volume) and the information about its elemental composition (single tellurium atom per molecule), which perfectly matches the core move of this schema. Subsequent parts (f, g, h) then involve calculations based on this inferred formula, which often follows the initial formula determination as described in the schema's core move.
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires inferring the relative molecular mass of an unknown acid (part j) and subsequently its elemental composition (m and n) and full molecular formula (part k) by analyzing the mass changes in a series of chemical reactions (oxidation of tellurium, followed by neutralization with KOH). This directly aligns with the core move of applying the principle of conservation of atoms in a given chemical reaction description to deduce the formula.
- `NSAA_2020_Section2_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the moles of bromine to determine the number of C=C double bonds in the unknown acid. This stoichiometric information is then used to infer the molecular formula of the acid by adjusting the hydrogen count based on the degree of unsaturation, directly matching the core move of inferring compound formula from reaction stoichiometry.
- `NSAA_2023_Section2_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires a multi-step deduction of intermediate compound formulas based on reaction outcomes and properties. The final step, however, explicitly asks for the relative molar mass of compound Y, which is formed from a reaction between R and X. This involves inferring the molecular formula of Y by applying the principle of conservation of atoms from the known molecular formulas of R and X in the esterification reaction, and then calculating its relative molecular mass. This aligns perfectly with the core move of schema M_fd4ee276.

## **M_fd91f769. Calculating Net Change from Multiple Discrete Events**

**Core move:** Determine the overall change in a quantity by summing the individual changes resulting from a series of discrete events, each with a defined effect.

**Seen in / context:**
- Nuclear physics (alpha, beta decay affecting protons/neutrons/mass number)
Financial transactions (deposits and withdrawals affecting account balance)
Inventory management (items added and removed affecting stock level)
Population dynamics (births, deaths, immigration, emigration affecting total population)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the change associated with each event type (e.g., confusing alpha with beta decay effects on protons).
Forgetting to account for all events or miscounting them.
Errors in arithmetic when summing positive and negative changes.
Confusing the question asking for 'fewer' or 'more' with the direct calculated change (e.g., if net change is -8, the answer is 8 fewer, not -8).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure each event's effect on the target quantity is clearly defined.
Vary the number and type of events.
Can involve both positive and negative changes.
Questions can ask for the final state or the net difference.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q29_NSAA_Sec`: Calculating the standard enthalpy change of combustion typically involves summing the standard enthalpy changes of formation of products and reactants, or summing bond energies broken and formed, or applying Hess's Law by summing enthalpy changes of component reactions. This directly aligns with the schema's core move of determining an overall change by summing individual changes from discrete events, each with a defined effect.
- `NSAA_2017_Section2_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating an overall enthalpy change (a net change in a quantity) by combining the enthalpy changes of several discrete reactions (events), which involves summing them according to Hess's Law principles. This aligns directly with the core move of determining the overall change by summing individual changes.

## **M_fe1a7cdc. Measuring and Quantifying Biodiversity**

**Core move:** Explain and apply various quantitative and qualitative methods for assessing biodiversity, providing specific examples for each.

**Seen in / context:**
- Ecological studies and environmental impact assessments
Conservation biology and species monitoring
Understanding ecosystem health and stability

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing species richness with species evenness
Not providing concrete examples for each measurement method
Omitting discussion of diversity indices (e.g., Simpson's, Shannon-Wiener)
Simply listing methods without explaining their underlying principles or implications

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure questions require both definition/explanation and application/examples
Can ask for advantages and disadvantages of different measurement methods
Can specify a particular habitat or group of organisms for the examples

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section2_Q31_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_fe9ca981. Applying Wave-Particle Duality to Slit Experiments**

**Core move:** Apply de Broglie's hypothesis to relate particle momentum to wavelength and use wave interference/diffraction principles to determine allowed particle velocities.

**Seen in / context:**
- Double-slit experiment with particles (electrons, molecules, etc.)
Diffraction gratings for matter waves
Determining conditions for observing wave-like properties of particles
Calculating particle properties (speed, energy) based on observed interference/diffraction patterns

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly recalling or applying the de Broglie wavelength formula (λ = h/mv)
Confusing the conditions for constructive and destructive interference (e.g., d sinθ = nλ vs. d sinθ = (n+1/2)λ)
Misinterpreting angular widths or separations in the experimental setup
Failing to convert units appropriately (e.g., angles to radians, mass to kg)
Neglecting the wave nature of particles and attempting to solve using classical mechanics alone

**Notes for generation:**
- Often requires the use of small angle approximations (sin θ ≈ tan θ ≈ θ for small θ in radians).
Students must be comfortable with geometric optics principles applied to wave phenomena.
Physical constants (Planck's constant, mass of relevant particle) are usually provided or assumed known.
Questions may involve calculating specific speeds, or a range of speeds based on a tolerance in angle or position.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section2_Q5_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_ff318c2a. Integrating Variable Density to Find Total Mass**

**Core move:** Apply integral calculus to sum infinitesimal mass contributions from a continuously varying density function over a defined volume to determine the total mass.

**Seen in / context:**
- Calculating total mass of objects with non-uniform density (e.g., planets, stars, engineered materials).
Calculating total charge from a non-uniform charge density.
Calculating moment of inertia for objects with varying density.
Finding centers of mass for non-uniform objects.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming uniform density and multiplying average density by total volume.
Incorrectly identifying the differential volume element (dV).
Setting up the integral with incorrect limits of integration.
Algebraic or calculus errors during the integration process.
Using an incorrect formula for the density function or geometric properties.

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly define the density function, ρ(x,y,z) or ρ(r), and the shape of the object.
The problem should guide the choice of coordinate system (e.g., spherical for spheres, cylindrical for cylinders).
Provide any necessary geometric formulas (e.g., surface area of a spherical shell, volume of a cylindrical shell).
Ensure the integral is manageable for the target audience.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section2_Q0_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **M_ff6d0fef. Applying Linear Scaling to Similar Figures**

**Core move:** Use a given or implied linear scale factor to determine unknown corresponding linear dimensions of similar geometric figures.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving similar 2D shapes (e.g., triangles, rectangles, polygons) where side lengths are scaled proportionally.
Problems involving similar 3D shapes (e.g., cuboids, spheres, cones) where linear dimensions like length, width, height, or radius are scaled proportionally.
Questions where a direct ratio or fractional scale factor for lengths is provided or can be easily inferred.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the linear scale factor with area scale factor (square of linear factor) or volume scale factor (cube of linear factor).
Incorrectly identifying corresponding sides or dimensions between similar figures.
Applying an additive or subtractive relationship instead of a multiplicative one.
Misinterpreting the direction of scaling (e.g., dividing instead of multiplying when scaling up, or vice versa).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem explicitly states or strongly implies that the figures are similar.
Focus exclusively on linear dimensions; ignore area and volume unless the problem explicitly asks for them (which would then involve squaring/cubing the linear factor).
The scale factor might be presented as a fraction, decimal, ratio, or percentage.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q77_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_024e9e9d. Power Scaling in Series Circuits**

**Core move:** Calculate the new total power in a series circuit when the number of active components changes, by relating total power to total resistance and constant supply voltage.

**Seen in / context:**
- Series circuits with identical components (e.g., resistors, lamps).
Connected to a constant voltage supply.
Scenarios where some components fail (e.g., short-circuiting, open-circuiting), altering the total circuit resistance.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming the current remains constant when the total resistance changes (for a constant voltage source).
Incorrectly applying P = I^2R or P = V^2/R; specifically, using P = I^2R when I changes, or using P = V^2/R when V is not the voltage across the specific R being considered (if analyzing individual components). For total power with constant V, P = V^2/R_total is key.
Miscalculating the new total resistance after component failure (e.g., confusing short-circuit with open-circuit).

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the constant supply voltage, as this dictates using P = V^2/R_total for total power.
Can be adapted for scenarios with constant current supplies.
Questions often involve proportional reasoning.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_0f1ea62e. Calculating Velocity via Energy Conservation in Gravitational Fields**

**Core move:** Exploit the conservation of mechanical energy principle (or kinematic equations) to determine an object's velocity given its change in height within a gravitational field, incorporating the relationship between weight, mass, and gravitational acceleration.

**Seen in / context:**
- Free-fall problems where an object starts from rest at a given height and falls under gravity.
Problems involving the interconversion of gravitational potential energy and kinetic energy.
Scenarios where local gravitational acceleration ('g') must be derived from the object's weight and mass.
Problems neglecting non-conservative forces like air resistance.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to correctly relate weight (W) to mass (m) and gravitational acceleration (g) (W=mg).
Ignoring initial conditions, such as starting from rest (initial velocity = 0).
Incorrectly applying the conservation of energy equation (e.g., mgh = 1/2 mv instead of 1/2 mv^2).
Forgetting to take the square root when solving for final velocity (v) from v^2, or misinterpreting options for v versus v^2.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize that both energy conservation (mgh = 1/2 mv^2) and constant acceleration kinematics (v^2 = u^2 + 2gh) are valid approaches.
Ensure the problem explicitly states conditions like 'released from rest' and whether air resistance is negligible.
The derivation of 'g' from given weight and mass (g=W/m) is a crucial prerequisite step to finding velocity.
Options might be presented for v^2 rather than v, requiring careful interpretation of the desired format.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_121b9ac8. Applying Momentum Conservation in Collision Problems**

**Core move:** Apply the principle of conservation of momentum to determine an unknown mass or velocity in a closed system collision.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing collisions (elastic and inelastic)
Recoil problems (e.g., gun recoil, astronaut push-off)
Explosions where an object breaks into multiple parts
Situations involving impulse and average force over a collision time

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Neglecting the vector nature of momentum (e.g., not assigning positive/negative signs for direction).
Incorrectly assuming conservation of kinetic energy for inelastic collisions.
Not defining a consistent positive direction for velocities.
Forgetting to include all objects within the defined system when calculating total momentum.

**Notes for generation:**
- Remind students to define a positive direction for velocities at the start of the problem.
Emphasize that total momentum is always conserved in a closed system, even if kinetic energy is not (in inelastic collisions).
Ensure all masses and velocities are correctly identified for both initial and final states of the collision.
Problems often involve solving for one unknown quantity (e.g., the mass of one object, the final velocity of another).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q31_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_1462623d. Relating Wavelength and Frequency of EM Waves**

**Core move:** Infer the change in one electromagnetic wave property (frequency or wavelength) given a change in the other, utilizing their inverse relationship.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing shifts in the electromagnetic spectrum (e.g., redshift, Doppler effect)
Understanding the properties of different regions of the EM spectrum (e.g., radio waves vs. gamma rays)
Calculations involving the speed of light, frequency, and wavelength (c = fλ)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming frequency and wavelength change in the same direction (e.g., both increasing or both decreasing)
Incorrectly recalling the order of the electromagnetic spectrum (e.g., thinking microwaves have higher frequency than gamma rays)
Forgetting or misapplying the constant speed of light relationship (c = fλ)

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions can be qualitative (describing shifts) or quantitative (calculating values).
Often involves understanding that higher frequency means higher energy (E=hf) and shorter wavelength.
The 'expansion of the universe' context is a common scenario for demonstrating redshift.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: The question provides frequency ranges for different types of electromagnetic waves and the speed of light. It also introduces 'Table 2' which will contain wavelength information. This setup directly points to using the wave equation (speed = frequency × wavelength) to relate frequency and wavelength for EM waves, and then classifying them based on the provided ranges, which perfectly aligns with the core move of relating these two properties.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating frequencies from given wavelengths using the inverse relationship c = fλ, and then inferring the frequency range for ultraviolet radiation based on its position relative to visible light in the electromagnetic spectrum, which represents a change in electromagnetic wave property.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires using the constant speed of electromagnetic waves in a vacuum (c = fλ) to infer the ratio of frequencies given the ratio of wavelengths, directly utilizing the inverse relationship between frequency and wavelength. It also requires understanding that all EM waves travel at the same speed in a vacuum.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q32_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires using the relationship c = fλ to convert a given frequency range into a wavelength range (or vice-versa) and then comparing it to known wavelength/frequency ranges for different types of electromagnetic waves (visible, infrared, ultraviolet). This directly matches the core move of inferring one electromagnetic wave property from another, utilizing their inverse relationship.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q33_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the wavelength of light from its frequency (or vice versa) using the constant speed of light (c=fλ) to verify a statement about its color, which directly aligns with the core move of relating wavelength and frequency of EM waves.

## **P_1561ea33. Deducing Atomic Properties from Subatomic Particles**

**Core move:** Determine the atomic number, mass number, and net charge of an atom or ion by analyzing its constituent protons, neutrons, and electrons.

**Seen in / context:**
- Basic atomic structure and definitions of protons, neutrons, and electrons.
Identifying elements, isotopes, and ions from particle counts.
Calculating mass number, atomic number, and overall charge.
Relating particle counts to neutrality or ionic charge.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Mixing up atomic number (protons) and mass number (protons + neutrons).
Incorrectly calculating net charge (e.g., using only electron count, or adding instead of subtracting).
Assuming 'total particles' refers only to nucleons or only to nuclear components.
Forgetting that a neutral atom has equal numbers of protons and electrons.

**Notes for generation:**
- Present information about subatomic particles (p, n, e) explicitly or implicitly.
Require calculation of one or more atomic properties (element identity, mass, charge).
Can involve comparing multiple species (atoms/ions) to deduce relationships.
Use small, manageable particle counts suitable for common elements (e.g., total particles usually < 50).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q33_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_17a253c9. Applying Newton's Second Law to Net Force**

**Core move:** Apply Newton's Second Law by summing all forces acting on an object along the direction of motion to calculate an unknown quantity like mass, acceleration, or an individual force.

**Seen in / context:**
- Objects experiencing acceleration due to multiple forces (e.g., gravity, air resistance, friction, tension).
Calculations involving forces in vertical or horizontal motion.
Problems where the net force is non-zero, leading to acceleration.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the direction of forces (e.g., air resistance always opposes motion, not necessarily downwards).
Forgetting to include all relevant forces (e.g., not accounting for weight).
Confusing mass with weight or vice-versa.
Incorrectly setting up the equation F_net = ma by not consistently defining positive and negative directions for forces and acceleration.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage drawing a free-body diagram to visualize all forces and their directions.
Stress the importance of defining a consistent positive direction for forces and acceleration.
Ensure all units are consistent before performing calculations (e.g., N, kg, m/s^2).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q32_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks for the 'resultant force' acting on an object, given its change in kinetic energy over a specific time, and states that the force is constant. This explicitly requires applying Newton's Second Law (F=ma) to link the resultant force to the acceleration, which in turn causes the change in kinetic energy over time. The solution involves combining Newton's Second Law with the Work-Energy Theorem (ΔE = Fd) and constant acceleration kinematics (e.g., d = v_avg * t, v_avg = (v1+v2)/2, and v2/v1 derived from E=0.5mv^2). All these relationships are foundational to applying Newton's laws to dynamic systems, directly matching the core move of calculating an unknown force.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the acceleration of a combined system by first determining the net force acting on it. This involves summing the increased driving force and the increased resistive forces, and then applying Newton's Second Law (F=ma) to find the acceleration, which directly aligns with the core move of applying Newton's Second Law to calculate an unknown quantity.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q36_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires calculating the acceleration using kinematic equations first, and then applying Newton's Second Law by summing the gravitational force and the unknown air resistance force to find the average air resistance. This directly aligns with the core move of applying Newton's Second Law to calculate an individual force after determining acceleration.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q31_NSAA_Sec`: This problem requires calculating the average net force using the impulse-momentum theorem (an integrated form of Newton's Second Law, F_net = Δp/Δt) and then summing forces (contact force and gravitational force) to find the specific unknown contact force. This aligns with the core move of applying Newton's Second Law and summing forces to determine an individual force.

## **P_1f0da6ee. Optimizing Conductive Heat Transfer Rate**

**Core move:** Apply Fourier's Law of heat conduction to calculate the rate of thermal energy transfer and identify the conditions that minimize or maximize this rate.

**Seen in / context:**
- Heat transfer through solid materials (conduction)
Insulated systems to focus solely on conduction
Comparing different material dimensions or temperature differences to find an optimal rate
Problems involving thermal resistance

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly recalling or applying Fourier's Law (e.g., mixing up length and area in the formula)
Failing to convert units consistently (e.g., diameter in cm vs. length in m)
Not calculating the cross-sectional area correctly (e.g., using diameter directly instead of radius squared)
Misinterpreting the temperature difference (ΔT) or forgetting to take its absolute value
Mistakenly maximizing the rate when minimization is required, or vice versa

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question provides sufficient data (dimensions, temperatures) or implies constants (thermal conductivity).
Often involves comparing several scenarios or options to find the optimal one.
Pay close attention to unit conversions before calculation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_22bfd9bc. Interpreting Trends in Experimental Data**

**Core move:** Identify and describe the qualitative relationship between an independent variable and a dependent variable from experimental data.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing results from controlled experiments in various scientific fields (Physics, Chemistry, Biology)
Identifying trends in graphs or tables of measured quantities
Drawing conclusions about cause-and-effect relationships from empirical observations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the independent and dependent variables or their roles
Misinterpreting the direction of a trend (e.g., seeing an increase as a decrease)
Assuming a causal link without sufficient evidence or ignoring confounding factors
Extrapolating trends significantly beyond the tested range without justification

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should present an independent variable that is systematically changed and a dependent variable that is measured.
The relationship (e.g., increasing, decreasing, optimal point, no clear trend) should be discernible from the provided data.
Can involve data presented in tables, graphs, or descriptive text.
May require inferring what happens between data points or predicting beyond them, if explicitly asked and justified by the trend.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_2db4e658. Wave Speed from Spatio-Temporal Peak Tracking**

**Core move:** Calculate wave speed by determining the travel time of a single wave feature over a known distance, incorporating the wave's periodic nature to correctly interpret observational data across space and time.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving observers at different locations recording wave arrival times.
Analyzing seismic waves, sound waves, or water waves.
Situations where wave frequency/period is given, which influences the interpretation of 'next peak' or 'previous trough'.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Mistaking the given time difference (e.g., 0.80 s in this problem) as the direct travel time between the two points without accounting for which specific peak is being observed.
Incorrectly calculating the wave period from frequency or vice-versa.
Ignoring the direction of wave travel, leading to incorrect interpretation of temporal relationships between points.
Confusing the time for a wave feature to travel between two points with the time interval between two different features at a single point.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize careful visualization or logical tracking of a specific wave feature (e.g., a peak or trough) as it propagates.
The problem often requires using the wave's period (T = 1/f) to relate observations at different points in space and time.
This type of problem can often be solved by either directly finding Δt for a fixed distance or by calculating the wavelength (λ) using phase concepts and then using v=fλ.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q36_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_31ebc6bd. Analyzing Forces in Constant Velocity**

**Core move:** Calculate an unknown force by equating opposing forces acting on an object experiencing zero acceleration.

**Seen in / context:**
- Objects reaching terminal velocity (e.g., parachutists, falling raindrops).
Objects moving at constant velocity on a surface with friction.
Objects floating or fully submerged in a fluid at rest.
Systems in static equilibrium (e.g., objects suspended by ropes, balanced on a pivot).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to recognize that constant velocity implies zero net force.
Incorrectly identifying or neglecting a relevant force (e.g., air resistance, friction, buoyancy, normal force).
Assigning incorrect directions to forces.
Confusing constant velocity with instantaneous rest.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem statement clearly indicates constant velocity, equilibrium, or terminal velocity.
Require identification of all relevant forces acting on the object.
The calculation typically involves setting the sum of forces in perpendicular directions to zero.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q33_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q32_NSAA_Sec`: The question explicitly states that the resultant vertical force on the wire is zero, implying zero acceleration. To solve it, one must calculate the downward gravitational force and then equate it to the upward magnetic force to find the unknown current, which perfectly matches the core move of calculating an unknown force by equating opposing forces acting on an object experiencing zero acceleration.

## **P_32d02d07. Calculating Work and Power Against Gravity**

**Core move:** Calculate the mechanical work done against gravity and the power exerted, using mass, height, and time.

**Seen in / context:**
- Lifting objects vertically at a constant speed
Pumping fluids to a higher elevation
Climbing stairs or hills

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to convert mass to weight (force) when calculating work (i.e., W = mgh, not just mg or mh)
Not converting time to standard units (seconds) for power calculations
Using total distance traveled instead of vertical height for work against gravity
Confusing work with power

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary quantities (mass, height, time) are provided or derivable.
Vary the object (e.g., water, bricks, people) and the lifting mechanism.
Can extend to efficiency calculations if input energy is given.
Units should be varied (kg, g, m, cm, s, min, J, kJ, W, kW).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_3336d531. Calculating Total Distance for Oscillating Particles**

**Core move:** Calculate the total distance travelled by an oscillating particle over a given time, utilizing its amplitude and frequency.

**Seen in / context:**
- Waves (transverse or longitudinal, for particle motion)
Simple Harmonic Motion (SHM)
Oscillations and vibrations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the distance travelled by a particle with the distance travelled by the wave itself.
Using wavelength or wave speed in the calculation for particle distance.
Calculating displacement instead of total distance.
Incorrectly calculating the distance travelled in one oscillation (e.g., 2A instead of 4A).
Units conversion errors (e.g., minutes to seconds).

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the distinction between wave propagation and particle oscillation.
Ensure the time duration is clearly specified.
Values should allow for straightforward calculation of total oscillations and distance.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q27_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q29_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks for the total distance travelled by an oscillating particle in a wave, given its amplitude and the wave's frequency over a specific time. This precisely matches the core move of schema P_3336d531, which is to calculate the total distance travelled by an oscillating particle over a given time, utilizing its amplitude and frequency.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q19_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires determining the amplitude of an oscillating particle by relating the given total distance travelled during one complete oscillation (5.6 cm) to 4 times the amplitude (4A). This is an inverse application of the core move, which focuses on calculating total distance using amplitude. Both require understanding the relationship that a particle travels 4A in one full oscillation.

## **P_3416318a. Calculating Electrical Parameters in Electromechanical Systems with Efficiency**

**Core move:** Calculate an electrical parameter (current, voltage, or power) or a mechanical parameter (force, height, time, or power) by applying the concept of efficiency to convert between mechanical work/power and electrical work/power.

**Seen in / context:**
- Electric motors lifting loads against gravity
Generators producing electricity from mechanical input (e.g., wind turbines)
Pumps moving fluids (linking electrical power to fluid potential energy)
Any system involving energy conversion between electrical and mechanical forms with a given efficiency.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing electrical input power/work with mechanical output power/work.
Incorrectly applying the efficiency formula (e.g., multiplying by efficiency when dividing is needed, or vice-versa, when calculating input from output or vice-versa).
Forgetting to include time when converting between work and power, or vice versa.
Miscalculating gravitational potential energy or work done against gravity (e.g., using mass instead of weight as force).

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly identify the 'input' and 'output' components for the efficiency calculation.
Ensure all units are consistent (e.g., Joules for energy, Watts for power, seconds for time, Volts for potential difference, Amperes for current).
Efficiency is typically given as a percentage or a decimal; ensure correct conversion in calculations.
Common formulas involved are: Work = Force x distance (W=Fd), Gravitational Potential Energy = mgh, Power = Work/time (P=W/t), Electrical Power = Voltage x Current (P=VI).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the kinetic energy (a mechanical parameter/work) of a car, starting from electrical parameters (voltage, current, time) of its motor, and explicitly using the efficiency of the overall system to convert between electrical input energy and mechanical output energy.

## **P_3b31da9d. Analyzing Sound Echo Properties with Relative Motion**

**Core move:** Predict changes in amplitude and frequency of a reflected sound wave (echo) by considering energy dissipation and the Doppler effect due to relative motion.

**Seen in / context:**
- Sound waves reflecting from surfaces
Observer or source moving relative to the reflecting surface
Everyday examples of echoes (e.g., mountains, rooms)
Ultrasonography principles

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting that echoes are always quieter due to energy dissipation and spread
Incorrectly applying the Doppler effect (e.g., confusing moving towards with moving away)
Confusing sound waves with electromagnetic waves when considering specific Doppler effect nuances (e.g., medium dependence)
Assuming frequency remains unchanged during reflection or echo formation

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the two distinct physical phenomena: energy loss for amplitude and relative motion for frequency.
Clearly define the reference frames for Doppler effect calculations (source, medium, observer, reflector).
Questions can vary the direction of motion (towards/away) for both source and observer/reflector, leading to different frequency shifts.
Amplitude reduction is a fundamental property of echoes due to absorption, scattering, and geometric spreading of wave energy.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_42ff888f. Relating Momentum and Kinetic Energy**

**Core move:** Combine the definitions of momentum and kinetic energy to solve for an unknown kinematic property (mass, velocity, momentum, or kinetic energy) of a single object.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving a single object's motion.
Scenarios where two of momentum, kinetic energy, mass, or velocity are known, and a third needs to be found.
Direct application of fundamental mechanics definitions.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly substituting variables between the two equations.
Algebraic errors when rearranging the combined formula.
Confusing units or using incorrect units in calculations.
Trying to apply conservation laws when the problem is about a single object's state.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure a clear understanding of the relationships p=mv and KE=0.5mv².
Emphasize the importance of algebraic manipulation.
Questions can ask for mass, velocity, momentum, or kinetic energy.
Values should ideally lead to straightforward calculations or integer/simple decimal answers.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_43f3e028. Determining Unknown Time in Segmented Motion**

**Core move:** Calculate an unknown time or speed component within a multi-segment journey by applying the definition of average speed as total distance over total time, and expressing total distance as the sum of distances for each segment.

**Seen in / context:**
- Kinematics problems involving objects moving at different speeds over distinct time intervals.
Scenarios requiring the calculation of average speed/velocity for a journey composed of multiple parts.
Problems where a specific duration, speed, or distance for one segment is unknown but the overall average speed is given.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Calculating the simple arithmetic mean of the speeds instead of using total distance / total time.
Incorrectly setting up the total distance equation by not accounting for each segment's time and speed.
Making algebraic errors when solving for the unknown variable.
Confusing average speed (total distance / total time) with average velocity (total displacement / total time) when direction changes are relevant, though not an issue in this specific problem.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the total distance is accurately represented as the sum of (speed × time) for each individual segment.
Emphasize that average speed is not generally the average of individual speeds unless the time intervals are equal.
Can be adapted to solve for an unknown speed or total distance if other variables are provided.
The problem structure often involves setting up an equation with the unknown variable, then solving it algebraically.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_4a4629d5. Aggregating Mass from Multiple Material Densities**

**Core move:** Calculate the total mass of a composite system by determining the volume occupied by each component type and applying their respective densities.

**Seen in / context:**
- Packing problems involving different materials within a container.
Mixture problems where individual component masses are required for a total.
Determining the average density of a composite material or object.
Material science applications involving multi-component structures with known volumes and densities.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly calculating the number of individual components that fit into the total volume.
Misinterpreting or misapplying the ratio of different material types.
Confusing the densities of different materials or applying the wrong density to a component type.
Calculating volume incorrectly (e.g., using area instead of volume for 3D objects, or incorrect units).
Failing to sum the masses of all components to get the total mass.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure clear dimensions are provided for both the container and the individual components.
Provide distinct densities for each material type.
Include a ratio or proportion for the different components to determine their quantities.
Units for dimensions, density, and final mass should be consistent or require conversion steps.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q33_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_4a9e7a6d. Combining Gas Laws with Hydrostatic Pressure**

**Core move:** Apply an appropriate ideal gas law to calculate a gas property, and then use the hydrostatic pressure formula to relate it to fluid depth or external pressure.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving submerged containers with trapped gases (e.g., diving bells, submarines).
Calculating the depth of objects based on changes in trapped gas volume.
Situations where gas properties (pressure, volume) are affected by external fluid pressure.
Analyzing systems under isothermal conditions (Boyle's Law) or other specified gas processes.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to include atmospheric pressure when calculating total external pressure.
Incorrectly applying the ideal gas law (e.g., using PV=nRT for a constant temperature process instead of P1V1=P2V2).
Confusing initial and final states for pressure or volume.
Errors in calculating hydrostatic pressure (ρgh) or using incorrect units for density, gravity, or depth.
Assuming temperature changes when the problem states it remains constant.

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly identify the initial and final states of the gas.
Ensure all pressure components acting on the gas (atmospheric, hydrostatic) are accounted for.
Pay attention to the specific gas law applicable (e.g., Boyle's Law for constant temperature).
The unknown variable can be initial volume, final pressure, or the depth itself.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_4b1d7a8a. Quantifying Energy Transformation and Dissipation**

**Core move:** Calculate energy transferred, converted, or lost within a system or component by applying the principle of energy conservation, definitions of work, and efficiency.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing the performance of machines (motors, generators, engines).
Energy conversion processes (e.g., electrical to mechanical, chemical to thermal).
Problems involving work done against forces (gravitational, frictional, elastic).
Determining energy dissipation due to inefficiencies.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the efficiency formula (e.g., (1-η) for useful output, or vice versa).
Failing to distinguish between different types of energy (input, useful output, wasted).
Neglecting intermediate energy losses between components in a multi-stage process.
Errors in calculating specific types of work (e.g., mgh, Fd).
Inconsistent unit usage (e.g., Joules vs. kiloJoules).

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the relationship: Energy Input = Useful Energy Output + Wasted Energy.
Efficiency = (Useful Energy Output / Energy Input) * 100%.
Questions may require calculating useful energy output (e.g., gravitational potential energy) first, then using this to deduce efficiency or input/lost energy, or vice versa.
Clarify whether efficiency applies to a component or the entire system to avoid ambiguity in 'total loss' questions.
Ensure values used are appropriate for the specific stage of energy conversion being considered.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q23_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q23_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating the energy lost/wasted by a component given its input power and efficiency over a specific time, which directly aligns with the core move of quantifying energy dissipation within a system using efficiency.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the energy transferred (lost) from the tip to the surroundings by applying the principle of energy conservation: total energy supplied equals energy absorbed by the tip plus energy lost to the surroundings. This directly aligns with the core move of calculating energy transferred or lost within a system by applying energy conservation.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q27_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the percentage efficiency of an electrical transmission system and the amount of energy wasted. This directly involves quantifying energy transferred (output power), energy supplied (input power), and energy lost (wasted energy), by applying the definition of efficiency and the principle of energy conservation in the context of power. This aligns perfectly with the core move of calculating energy transferred, converted, or lost within a system and applying efficiency.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q31_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks to calculate the amount of kinetic energy transferred to other forms of energy in a collision, which directly aligns with the core move of quantifying energy transferred, converted, or lost within a system. While an intermediate step involves applying momentum conservation to find the final velocity, the ultimate goal and the specific quantity requested (energy lost/transferred) fall under the umbrella of energy transformation and dissipation.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q38_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires calculating heat energy transfer (including phase change) and applying the principle of energy conservation (heat lost = heat gained) to find the final equilibrium temperature, which directly aligns with the core move of quantifying energy transformation and dissipation within a system.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q40_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks for the efficiency of a transformer, which is a direct quantification of how effectively electrical energy is transformed from input to output and how much is dissipated. This aligns perfectly with the schema's title 'Quantifying Energy Transformation and Dissipation' and its core move of calculating energy transferred, converted, or lost, as efficiency is the ratio of useful energy converted to total energy transferred.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the energy wasted (lost/dissipated) by an electric motor over a given time, using its input power and efficiency, which directly aligns with the core move of quantifying energy transferred and lost within a system using efficiency.

## **P_4fdfe2c2. Wavelength from Relative Wave Encounter**

**Core move:** Apply relative velocity principles to determine the effective speed at which an observer encounters wave crests, then use this relative speed and the observed period to calculate the wave's actual wavelength.

**Seen in / context:**
- Objects moving into or away from a propagating wave
Calculating observed frequency or period for a moving observer
Problems involving relative motion in wave phenomena

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to calculate the correct relative speed between the observer and the wave (e.g., adding instead of subtracting, or vice-versa)
Using the wave's intrinsic speed instead of the relative speed with the observed period/frequency
Confusing the observed period/frequency with the wave's actual period/frequency in the medium

**Notes for generation:**
- The relative speed dictates the rate at which wave features are encountered by the moving observer.
The observed period (or frequency) is inversely proportional to this relative speed for a given wavelength.
Consider scenarios where the observer moves with or against the wave direction.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_65208a7f. Balancing Nuclear Decay Equations**

**Core move:** Apply conservation laws for mass number and atomic number to identify emitted particles in radioactive decay chains.

**Seen in / context:**
- Alpha decay (emission of _2^4 He)
Beta-minus decay (emission of _-1^0 e)
Beta-plus decay (emission of _+1^0 e)
Gamma decay (emission of _0^0 γ)
Positron emission and Electron capture

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assigning the change in mass number (A) or atomic number (Z) for a given decay particle.
Forgetting that Z changes for beta decay but A does not.
Not considering that multiple decay steps or combinations of particles might be required.
Incorrectly balancing Z for beta-minus decay (e.g., subtracting 1 instead of adding 1 to the product's Z).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the sum of mass numbers (A) on both sides of the reaction is equal.
Ensure the sum of atomic numbers (Z) on both sides of the reaction is equal.
Questions can involve a single decay or a series of decays to reach the final nuclide.
Can be extended to predict daughter nuclei given parent and emissions, or parent given daughter and emissions.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q27_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying the conservation laws for mass number and atomic number to track changes during alpha and beta-minus decay. This allows for determining the possible intermediate nuclides in a decay chain, which directly matches the core move of applying these conservation laws in radioactive decay.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q33_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires applying the conservation of mass number and atomic number to balance both the nuclear fission reaction (to find the number of neutrons emitted) and the subsequent beta decay equation (to find the number of protons in the product nuclide). This directly matches the core move of balancing nuclear decay/reaction equations using conservation laws.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires determining the cumulative change in mass number and atomic number after a series of alpha and beta decays. This is achieved by applying the conservation laws for mass number and atomic number, which is the core move of schema P_65208a7f. Specifically, one must calculate the number of alpha and beta particles emitted and then sum their individual effects on A and Z to find the final values for isotope Y.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires determining the number of beta particles emitted in a radioactive decay series by applying the conservation laws for mass number and atomic number, which perfectly aligns with the core move of balancing nuclear decay equations.
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q27_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires determining the properties of a daughter nuclide after a beta decay, which directly involves applying the conservation laws for mass number and atomic number to balance the nuclear decay equation.

## **P_659cfb3f. Identifying Proportional Relationships for Physical Laws**

**Core move:** State or identify the direct or inverse proportional relationships between physical quantities as defined by fundamental laws or equations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Heat conduction (Fourier's Law)
Electrical resistance (R = ρL/A)
Fluid dynamics (Poiseuille's Law for flow rate)
Gravitational force (Newton's Law of Universal Gravitation)
Capacitance of parallel plate capacitors (C = εA/d)
Magnetic force on a current-carrying wire

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing direct proportionality with inverse proportionality.
Misplacing variables in the numerator or denominator.
Incorrectly applying squared or inverse-squared relationships where linear relationships exist, or vice-versa.
Forgetting which variables influence the dependent quantity.

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions often present scenarios and ask for the general form of the relationship (P ∝ X/Y or P ∝ XY).
Tests recall of standard physics formulas and conceptual understanding of how quantities interact.
Avoid providing options that are dimensionally incorrect, unless as a clear distractor.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_7ea33353. Combining Dynamics and Kinematics**

**Core move:** Integrate Newton's Second Law (F=ma) with kinematic equations to determine motion parameters (like displacement, final velocity, or time) for objects undergoing constant acceleration due to a net force.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving constant forces causing acceleration or deceleration.
Scenarios like braking vehicles, objects launched against gravity with air resistance, or objects pulled/pushed for a certain duration/distance.
Problems where initial/final velocities, displacement, and time are involved alongside mass and force.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly identifying the net force or its direction.
Forgetting the sign conventions for acceleration, velocity, and displacement.
Using inappropriate kinematic equations (e.g., assuming constant velocity when there's acceleration).
Confusing initial and final conditions.
Arithmetic errors in calculating acceleration or solving kinematic equations.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the acceleration is constant throughout the motion described.
Clearly define a positive direction and consistently apply it to forces, velocities, and displacements.
Questions can vary by asking for any of the variables (F, m, u, v, a, s, t).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_828c0407. Equating Two Radioactive Decay Processes**

**Core move:** Solve for the time required for the quantities of two radioactive isotopes, each decaying with a distinct half-life and initial amount, to become equal.

**Seen in / context:**
- Comparing the remaining amounts or activities of different isotopes over time.
Determining the time for a specific ratio or equality between two decaying substances.
Problems involving simultaneous exponential decay processes with different parameters.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly setting up the exponential decay equation (e.g., mixing half-lives or initial amounts).
Errors in manipulating logarithmic or exponential functions to solve for time.
Assuming a linear decay model instead of an exponential one.
Forgetting to simplify initial conditions before solving (e.g., canceling common terms).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure students write out the decay equation for each isotope: N(t) = N_0 * (1/2)^(t/T).
Emphasize the importance of algebraic manipulation, especially using logarithms to isolate 't'.
Questions can vary the initial relationship (e.g., N_X = 2N_Y, or N_X + N_Y = constant) and the final condition (e.g., N_X = N_Y, or N_X = 0.5 N_Y).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_836f71b8. Solving for Unknowns Using Paired Measurements**

**Core move:** Exploit differences in a measured property across two scenarios to isolate and determine an unknown quantity that is common to both, often involving a system of equations.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining the mass of a container or an unknown volume using measurements with two different substances (e.g., liquids with different densities).
Finding the specific heat capacity of a calorimeter or an unknown mass/volume using two different liquids or materials in calorimetry experiments.
Calculating an unknown resistance or voltage source by taking two sets of measurements in a circuit.
Determining the volume or density of an object from its apparent weight in two different fluids (Archimedes' principle).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly formulating the equations, misattributing masses or volumes to the wrong components.
Failing to recognize the common unknown (e.g., the volume V or the cylinder mass) that links the two scenarios.
Making algebraic errors when solving the system of simultaneous equations.
Not correctly applying the density formula (mass = density × volume).

**Notes for generation:**
- Identify the quantities that remain constant (e.g., volume of liquid poured, mass of the container) and those that change (e.g., density of liquid, total mass measured).
Set up one equation for each scenario based on the given information.
Often, subtracting one equation from the other will simplify the problem by eliminating one of the unknowns, making it easier to solve for the other.
Ensure unit consistency throughout the calculations.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_8ab88df3. Explaining Convection Mechanisms**

**Core move:** Describe the sequence of physical changes (e.g., heating, expansion, density alteration, buoyancy) that drive convection currents in fluids.

**Seen in / context:**
- Heat transfer in liquids and gases (e.g., boiling water, atmospheric circulation, hot air balloons)
Understanding fluid dynamics related to temperature and density differences
Explaining natural phenomena driven by fluid movement

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing convection with conduction or radiation as the primary mode of heat transfer in fluids
Incorrectly identifying the direction of fluid movement based on temperature or density (e.g., hot fluid sinks, cold fluid rises)
Misunderstanding the role of density changes as the driving force for buoyancy and fluid movement
Failing to connect temperature changes to volume and density changes

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should require an understanding of the cyclical nature of convection.
Can involve identifying correct statements, ordering steps in the process, or explaining cause-and-effect relationships.
Often tests the link between temperature, volume, density, and buoyancy principles.
May require distinguishing between specific heat capacity and latent heat if phase changes are involved, though less common for basic convection.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_8df37512. Applying Work-Energy Theorem with Non-Conservative Forces**

**Core move:** Apply the Work-Energy Theorem, accounting for changes in gravitational potential energy, kinetic energy, and the work done by non-conservative forces like drag or friction.

**Seen in / context:**
- Objects falling or moving through resistive mediums (air, water).
Situations where initial and final mechanical energy differ due to friction, drag, or applied external forces.
Calculating work done by or against non-conservative forces.
Problems involving changes in height and speed simultaneously.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Ignoring the change in kinetic energy.
Ignoring the change in gravitational potential energy.
Confusing work done *by* a force with work done *against* a force (sign error).
Assuming mechanical energy is conserved when non-conservative forces are present.
Incorrectly applying initial/final conditions for height or speed.

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize identifying all forms of initial and final mechanical energy (KE, GPE).
Clearly distinguish between work done *by* a non-conservative force and work done *against* it (W_against = -W_by).
The Work-Energy Theorem can be stated as: W_non-conservative = ΔKE + ΔGPE, or E_initial + W_non-conservative = E_final.
Ensure units are consistent throughout the calculation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q37_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: The question asks for the work done against resistive forces, which is directly addressed by applying the Work-Energy Theorem. The solution involves calculating the change in kinetic energy and the work done by the applied force, then using these values to find the work done by the non-conservative resistive forces (friction). This aligns perfectly with the core move of applying the Work-Energy Theorem and accounting for changes in kinetic energy and work done by non-conservative forces.

## **P_91f4f2e6. Calculate Geometric Dimensions from Properties**

**Core move:** Determine an unknown linear dimension of a polygon by applying its specific geometric properties and relevant theorems like the Pythagorean theorem.

**Seen in / context:**
- Finding side lengths or diagonals of rhombuses, squares, rectangles, or other polygons from given internal features (e.g., diagonals, heights).
Calculating missing lengths in right-angled triangles formed within geometric shapes.
Problems involving specific geometric shapes where knowledge of properties (e.g., diagonals bisecting at right angles, or relationships between sides and angles) is crucial.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misremembering or incorrectly applying geometric properties (e.g., assuming properties for one shape apply to another, or misinterpreting angles).
Calculation errors when applying algebraic or trigonometric theorems (e.g., Pythagorean theorem, sine/cosine rule).
Failing to correctly identify or construct the relevant triangles or sub-shapes within the main figure.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question provides sufficient geometric information (e.g., lengths of diagonals, specific angles, types of shapes).
Can be extended to 3D shapes where 2D cross-sections reveal similar geometric problems.
Be mindful of truncated or incomplete questions (like the 'enlargement' part in this example) and focus the schema on the calculable core task.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q17_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_92052111. Analyzing Mirror and Reflected Ray Rotations**

**Core move:** Determine mirror rotation or reflected ray deviation by applying the law of reflection and the principle that a fixed incident ray's reflection rotates by twice the mirror's rotation angle.

**Seen in / context:**
- Single plane mirror reflection with rotation
Multiple plane mirror reflections with one or more mirror rotations
Periscope systems and optical instruments involving multiple reflections

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the mirror rotation angle with the reflected ray rotation angle (e.g., assuming they are equal).
Incorrectly determining the direction of rotation (clockwise vs. anticlockwise).
Failing to account for the change in the incident ray on a subsequent mirror when a preceding mirror rotates.
Incorrectly applying angular relationships or the law of reflection (angle of incidence equals angle of reflection) in complex setups.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage drawing clear diagrams and consistently defining positive/negative angles (e.g., clockwise vs. anticlockwise).
The key principle: if the incident ray is fixed, rotating the mirror by an angle α rotates the reflected ray by 2α in the same direction.
For problems where the final reflected ray is fixed, and the incident ray on a mirror changes, it is often useful to track the required rotation of the mirror's normal (which bisects the angle between incident and reflected rays) to find the mirror's rotation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q29_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_935b7b5d. Relating Wave Speed and Wavelength Across Mediums**

**Core move:** Utilize the constant frequency of a wave and the wave equation (v=fλ) to determine changes in its speed and wavelength as it transitions between two different mediums.

**Seen in / context:**
- Water waves encountering a change in depth.
Sound waves propagating from one material to another (e.g., air to water).
Light waves refracting between mediums with different refractive indices.
Seismic waves passing through different geological layers.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming wave speed remains constant across the boundary.
Assuming wavelength remains constant across the boundary.
Incorrectly assuming frequency changes across the boundary, rather than remaining constant (a common misconception, especially with light waves where colour is associated with frequency).
Setting up incorrect proportionality relationships between speed and wavelength.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly states what remains constant (typically frequency).
Provide initial conditions (e.g., initial wavelength, speed, or frequency) and information about the change (e.g., reduction in speed, new wavelength).
Units for speed and wavelength must be consistent.
Can ask for any of the wave properties (initial speed, final speed, initial wavelength, final wavelength, or the change in speed/wavelength).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q32_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_95d8eb0b. Analyzing Non-Ideal Transformer Efficiency**

**Core move:** Calculate or infer an electrical output parameter (voltage, current, or power) of a transformer by applying turns ratio relationships and accounting for power losses using the concept of efficiency.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving primary/secondary voltage, current, power, and turns ratio for ideal and non-ideal transformers.
Calculations where transformer efficiency is explicitly given or its effect needs to be conceptually understood.
Qualitative analysis of how non-ideality affects output parameters.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming ideal transformer behavior (P_out = P_in) when the transformer is explicitly stated as non-ideal.
Incorrectly applying the turns ratio for current (e.g., I_s/I_p = N_s/N_p instead of N_p/N_s).
Confusing primary and secondary values or their relationships.
Neglecting to convert units (e.g., kW to W).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions may require the realization that a numerical answer cannot be determined without a specific efficiency value if 'non-ideal' is stated.
Often, 'non-ideal' implies that the output power will be less than the input power, meaning output current will be less than ideal if output voltage follows the ideal turns ratio.
Ensure the distinction between voltage and current relationships based on turns ratio, and how efficiency applies to power.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q23_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_99b4d196. Solving Sequence Properties via Simultaneous Equations**

**Core move:** Formulate and solve a system of linear equations using the general term formula of an arithmetic or geometric sequence to determine unknown sequence parameters.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving terms and common difference/ratio of arithmetic sequences.
Problems involving terms and common difference/ratio of geometric sequences.
Determining unknown initial values or rates in linear or exponential growth/decay models.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misapplying the general term formula (e.g., confusing n with n-1).
Algebraic errors during substitution or elimination.
Incorrectly interpreting the relationship between terms (e.g., 'twice the 8th term' vs. '2nd term is 8 times another value').

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem provides at least two independent conditions to establish a solvable system.
Vary the complexity by asking for different terms or sums of terms.
Can be extended to non-linear sequences (e.g., quadratic) for higher difficulty, requiring non-linear systems.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q18_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_9c0e5b25. Calculating Radioactive Decay Products**

**Core move:** Determine the remaining amount of a radioactive isotope and the quantity of its stable decay product after a given number of half-lives.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving mixed radioactive sources
Calculating the number of undecayed nuclei or atoms
Scenarios where total atom count is conserved after decay into a stable product
Radiometric dating calculations

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the initial number of radioactive atoms with the total number of atoms at a later time
Incorrectly calculating the number of half-lives passed (e.g., dividing half-life by time elapsed)
Miscalculating the fraction remaining (e.g., using linear subtraction instead of exponential decay)
Forgetting to sum the products from all relevant decay chains when multiple sources are present
Assuming the total number of atoms decreases when a stable product is formed (as each parent atom is replaced by a daughter atom)

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the question clearly states whether the decay product is stable or further decays.
Pay attention to the initial conditions (e.g., initial ratio of different isotopes, initial absence of products).
