Calculations often involve powers of 1/2 (for half-life method) or exponential functions (for decay constant method).
Questions may require calculating fractions, ratios, or absolute amounts.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q34_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q35_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the count rate from a radioactive isotope at different times, which is directly proportional to the 'remaining amount of a radioactive isotope' after a given number of half-lives. This involves applying the half-life concept to exponential decay. The additional step of isolating background radiation through simultaneous equations is an algebraic extension to this core physical concept.
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q37_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires determining the number of atoms of stable decay products (R and S) formed from their parent nuclides (X and Y) after a specific time, given their half-lives. This directly matches the core move of calculating the quantity of stable decay products after a given number of half-lives.

## **P_9d738079. Apply Fundamental Electrical Formulas**

**Core move:** Calculate an unknown electrical quantity by sequentially applying fundamental definitions (e.g., current, charge, power) and Ohm's Law.

**Seen in / context:**
- Simple DC circuits.
Problems calculating resistance, current, voltage, charge, time, or power.
Questions involving basic series or parallel components where the quantities for a single component are sought.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying or rearranging Ohm's Law (e.g., R=V/I vs R=I/V).
Using inconsistent units (e.g., time in minutes instead of seconds, charge not in Coulombs).
Confusing charge (Q) with current (I).
Performing calculations in the wrong order (e.g., trying to find R before finding I).

**Notes for generation:**
- Questions should provide enough information to calculate intermediate variables.
Can involve two or three steps.
Can be extended to include power calculations (P=VI, P=I^2R, P=V^2/R).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q22_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: The problem requires calculating the power dissipated in the cables. This involves first using the fundamental transformer turns ratio to find the secondary current, and then applying the fundamental power dissipation formula (P = I^2R) to calculate the heat generated. This is a sequential application of fundamental electrical formulas.
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires first calculating the individual resistance using the total power dissipated by two parallel resistors and the battery voltage, then using this resistance with a new voltage to find the current via Ohm's Law, and finally calculating the total charge using the definition of current (Q=It). This is a sequential application of fundamental electrical formulas and Ohm's Law to find an unknown electrical quantity.

## **P_acb9793b. Isolate Reversible Electromagnetic Force from Differential Measurement**

**Core move:** Determine the magnitude of an electromagnetic force by analyzing the difference in measured mechanical quantities (e.g., balance readings) when the electromagnetic interaction is reversed.

**Seen in / context:**
- Measuring magnetic field strength using a current-carrying wire and a balance.
Quantifying forces in situations where a constant baseline force (e.g., weight) is present and an additional reversible force acts on the system.
Applications involving forces on current-carrying conductors or moving charges in magnetic fields where the direction can be switched.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly setting up the force equations, leading to addition instead of subtraction (or vice-versa) of the magnetic force relative to the baseline force.
Forgetting to account for Newton's third law, incorrectly applying the force to the wrong part of the system or with the wrong direction.
Failing to convert units (e.g., grams to kilograms, cm to meters) before calculations.
Ignoring the factor of two that arises when subtracting the two balance readings (e.g., (W+F) - (W-F) = 2F).

**Notes for generation:**
- This pattern typically involves two measurements where a reversible force adds to or subtracts from a constant background force, allowing the background force to be eliminated through subtraction.
Emphasize the careful application of force direction and Newton's third law.
Ensure all variables are in consistent SI units.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q29_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_ad97dfb5. Scaling quantities with power law relationships**

**Core move:** Calculate the resultant value of a dependent physical quantity by applying given scaling factors to its independent variables in a power law relationship.

**Seen in / context:**
- Stefan-Boltzmann law (P = σAT⁴ or P = kR²T⁴)
Gravitational force/potential (F = Gm₁m₂/r²)
Electric force/potential (F = kq₁q₂/r²)
Intensity of waves (I ∝ 1/r²)
Any physical law where a quantity depends on powers of other variables.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying the exponents to the scaling factors (e.g., multiplying by 2 for R² instead of 2²).
Misinterpreting the scaling factor (e.g., 'decrease by a factor of two' means multiplying by 1/2, not subtracting 2).
Performing absolute calculations when a ratio-based approach would be simpler and less error-prone.
Errors in manipulating scientific notation or powers of 10.

**Notes for generation:**
- Encourage the use of ratios (e.g., P_final/P_initial) to cancel out constants and original values, simplifying the calculation.
Ensure the definition of 'factor of X' increase/decrease is consistent and clearly understood.
Can involve multiple variables changing simultaneously, requiring careful application of all scaling factors and exponents.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q28_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires calculating the ratio of densities (a dependent physical quantity) by applying the given ratio of radii (an independent variable) using a power law relationship (density is inversely proportional to the cube of the radius, ρ ∝ r⁻³). This directly matches the core move of schema P_ad97dfb5.

## **P_ae6afb3b. Calorimetry and Thermal Energy Conservation**

**Core move:** Apply the principle of conservation of thermal energy by equating the heat lost by hotter components to the heat gained by cooler components (Q_lost = Q_gained) using specific heat capacity and temperature changes.

**Seen in / context:**
- Determining initial or final temperatures in mixtures reaching thermal equilibrium
Finding specific heat capacities of unknown materials through mixing
Calculating mass of components in calorimetry experiments
Problems involving phase changes (latent heat) alongside specific heat capacity

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly assigning positive/negative signs to heat changes, leading to subtraction instead of equating magnitudes of heat transfer
Forgetting to convert units (e.g., grams to kilograms) to match the units of specific heat capacity
Using the specific heat capacity of the wrong material for a given component
Confusing initial and final temperatures when calculating temperature change (ΔT)

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all mass units are consistent with the specific heat capacity units (e.g., kg with J kg⁻¹ °C⁻¹).
Clearly identify which component loses heat and which gains heat.
Temperature change (ΔT) for a component should be calculated as (final temperature - initial temperature); however, when equating 'heat lost' and 'heat gained', it's often more intuitive to use the magnitude of temperature change for each part: Q = mc|ΔT|.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q35_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_b1db46d2. Relate Time Difference to Source Position**

**Core move:** Calculate the displacement of a source from a central point based on the time difference of simultaneously emitted signals detected at symmetrical points, utilizing the signal speed.

**Seen in / context:**
- Medical imaging (e.g., PET scans, ultrasound localization)
Sound source localization (e.g., echo ranging, animal hearing)
Ranging and positioning systems (e.g., sonar, seismic surveys)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the total path length difference with the displacement from the center.
Incorrectly setting up the distance equations for each path, especially when dealing with a midpoint.
Forgetting to divide the total distance difference (derived from the time difference) by two to get the displacement from the midpoint.
Incorrectly applying the speed, distance, time relationship (e.g., d = v/t).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the detectors are equidistant from the central reference point (or the initial symmetrical point).
The signals must be emitted simultaneously from the source.
The speed of the signal is assumed to be constant within the medium.
The problem often involves inferring a 'distance difference' from a 'time difference', and then using this to find a 'position offset' or 'displacement'.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2018_Section1_Q36_NSAA_Sec`: This question requires relating the time difference in detecting a signal and the signal speed to infer the relative distances of detectors from the source. Specifically, the fact that X and Y detect simultaneously implies they are equidistant from the epicentre, and Z detecting later implies it is further away by a distance calculated from the time difference and wave speed (Δd = v × Δt). This directly aligns with the core move of relating time differences to source/detector positions using signal speed.

## **P_b51132c8. Predicting Observed Light Properties with Relative Motion**

**Core move:** Predict the observed frequency, wavelength, and speed of electromagnetic radiation by integrating the Doppler effect with the constancy of the speed of light in a vacuum.

**Seen in / context:**
- Relative motion between a light source and an observer in a vacuum.
Redshift and blueshift phenomena in astrophysics.
Fundamental postulates of special relativity regarding the speed of light.
Relationship between wave speed, frequency, and wavelength (c = fλ).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Assuming the speed of light changes due to the relative motion of the source or observer.
Confusing the direction of frequency/wavelength shift for approaching versus receding sources.
Applying the classical Doppler effect formula directly to light without considering relativistic effects on speed.
Mistaking light traveling through a vacuum for light traveling through a medium where speed might vary.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the problem explicitly states the medium (vacuum is key for constant c).
Clearly specify the relative motion (approaching or receding).
Can be extended to calculate specific values or fractional changes, not just qualitative comparisons.
Avoid ambiguity about which frame of reference the initial frequency/wavelength is given in.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_be6e7503. Multi-Phase Kinematic Optimization for Travel**

**Core move:** Calculate total time or distance by segmenting motion into distinct phases of constant acceleration, constant velocity, and constant deceleration, applying kinematic equations to each segment and often optimizing for a specific parameter.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving vehicles (trains, cars, rockets) starting from rest, accelerating, cruising at a maximum speed, and then decelerating to a stop.
Optimizing travel time or minimizing fuel consumption under varying acceleration and speed limits.
Scenarios where an object undergoes sequential changes in its rate of change of velocity.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Failing to check if the maximum speed is actually reached given the total distance and acceleration/deceleration rates.
Incorrectly applying kinematic equations (e.g., using positive acceleration for deceleration without explicit sign convention).
Omitting one or more phases of motion (e.g., the constant velocity phase) or incorrectly assuming a phase does not exist.
Making unit conversion errors (e.g., km to m, km/h to m/s, or vice versa).
Arithmetic mistakes in calculating intermediate times or distances for each segment.

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly define the initial and final velocities, acceleration, and time for each distinct phase of motion.
Break down the problem into usually 2-3 phases: acceleration, constant velocity (if maximum speed is reached), and deceleration.
Utilize the standard SUVAT equations: v = u + at, s = ut + 0.5at², v² = u² + 2as, s = 0.5(u+v)t.
Consider edge cases, such as when the object cannot reach its maximum speed within the given distance, requiring a modification where it accelerates then immediately decelerates.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q34_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_c51df105. Calculating Ratios of Geometric Volumes**

**Core move:** Calculate the ratio of volumes of different geometric solids by first expressing each volume using standard formulas and given dimensional relationships, then simplifying the resulting algebraic expression.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving comparing the sizes or capacities of different geometric objects.
Questions requiring substitution of algebraic expressions into standard geometric formulas.
Any scenario where relative proportions of objects' volumes are needed.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Incorrectly applying or misremembering the volume formulas (e.g., using volume of sphere for hemisphere without dividing by 2).
Errors in algebraic substitution or simplification (e.g., misinterpreting the given ratio of height to radius, or incorrect squaring/cubing).
Failing to express all dimensions in terms of common base variables before forming the ratio.
Incorrectly simplifying the final algebraic fraction or canceling terms.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all necessary geometric formulas are provided or are expected to be known.
Vary the types of solids (cones, cylinders, spheres, pyramids, prisms) and the relationships between their dimensions.
Can be extended to surface area ratios or ratios of other geometric properties.
Ensure the final expression for the ratio is fully simplified.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_c6f35a7a. Analyze Proportional Relationships in Physical Laws**

**Core move:** Exploit direct and inverse proportional relationships within a given physical law to predict changes in one variable based on changes in others.

**Seen in / context:**
- Thermal conduction (Fourier's Law)
Electrical resistance (R = ρL/A)
Ohm's Law (V=IR, P=IV)
Ideal Gas Law (PV=nRT)
Newton's Second Law (F=ma)
Gravitational Force (F = GMm/r^2)

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing direct and inverse proportionality relationships.
Incorrectly applying changes (e.g., adding instead of multiplying, or vice versa).
Failing to recognize which variables are kept constant or how they interact.
Miscalculating the change in a derived quantity (e.g., temperature difference).

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the fundamental physical law governing the scenario is correctly identified.
Pay close attention to how each independent variable (e.g., A, l, ΔT) relates to the dependent variable (e.g., rate of conduction).
Questions often involve comparing an initial state to a modified state, asking for conditions where a specific outcome (e.g., 'same rate') is achieved.
When temperature changes occur at both ends, the key is to recalculate the temperature difference (ΔT) accurately.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
- `NSAA_2017_Section1_Q35_NSAA_Sec`: The question explicitly states a direct proportional relationship between the speeds of car X and car Y ('Car X is travelling at 1.5 times the speed of car Y'). Although incomplete, any further calculation involving physical laws (e.g., kinetic energy, momentum) would require exploiting this proportional relationship, matching the core move of analyzing proportional relationships to predict changes in variables.
- `NSAA_2019_Section1_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: The question explicitly states that the spring obeys Hooke's law and supports a range of loads. Hooke's law describes a direct proportional relationship between force and extension. Evaluating statements about this system requires analyzing these proportional relationships to predict changes in extension based on changes in load, which directly matches the core move of schema P_c6f35a7a.
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q30_NSAA_Sec`: The question requires establishing the stoichiometric ratio between the volumes of acid and base from a balanced chemical equation, given they are of the same concentration. This is a direct proportional relationship between the volumes and their stoichiometric coefficients. The task is to analyze this relationship, which aligns with the core move of exploiting direct proportional relationships within a scientific law (stoichiometry in this case) to understand the given quantities.

## **P_c7c6e68d. Average Force from Momentum Change**

**Core move:** Apply the impulse-momentum theorem to determine the average force exerted during an interaction, using the change in momentum over a given time interval.

**Seen in / context:**
- Collisions and impacts between objects
Calculating forces during braking or acceleration
Interactions where force acts over a specific duration

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to convert units (e.g., grams to kilograms)
Incorrectly calculating the change in velocity (final - initial)
Using initial velocity instead of the change in velocity
Confusing impulse (change in momentum) with average force
Misinterpreting 'comes to rest' as non-zero final velocity

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all quantities are converted to consistent SI units before calculation.
The problem typically provides mass, initial velocity, final velocity, and time.
Can be solved by first calculating acceleration (a = Δv/Δt) and then using F=ma, or directly using F_avg = (mΔv)/Δt.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q25_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_d4f05622. Determine Magnetic Force on Current Wire**

**Core move:** Calculate the magnetic force acting on a current-carrying wire by integrating current, wire length, and magnetic field strength, often requiring prior calculation of current from charge and time.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving current-carrying conductors in uniform magnetic fields.
Calculations where current is not directly provided but derivable from fundamental definitions (e.g., charge and time).
Scenarios where the orientation of the wire relative to the magnetic field is specified (e.g., perpendicular, parallel, at an angle).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Forgetting to convert units (e.g., minutes to seconds, cm to meters).
Using the formula for magnetic force on a moving point charge (F=qvB) instead of a current-carrying wire (F=BILsinθ).
Ignoring or incorrectly applying the sine of the angle between the current and magnetic field vectors.
Confusing magnetic field strength (B) with magnetic flux or other related quantities.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure all quantities are converted to consistent SI units before calculation.
The initial calculation of current (I=Q/t) is a crucial prerequisite step.
The problem typically focuses on the magnitude of the force, but the direction could be a follow-up concept (e.g., using the right-hand rule).

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2022_Section1_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_d96051e3. Interconnecting Mechanical Properties for Solids**

**Core move:** Determine an unknown mechanical property of a solid by chaining together the definitions of pressure, force, mass, and density, carefully managing units and relationships.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving solids resting on surfaces, where weight exerts pressure.
Calculations where pressure, force, mass, volume, area, and density are linked.
Situations requiring conversion between mass and weight (force) using gravitational field strength.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing mass and weight (force) or using incorrect units for them.
Incorrectly applying the formula for pressure (e.g., inverting F/A).
Errors in unit conversions (e.g., cm² to m², kg to g, N cm⁻² to Pa) without consistency.
Misinterpreting the given area (e.g., using total surface area instead of contact area).

**Notes for generation:**
- Emphasize the importance of clear unit tracking and conversion at each step.
Ensure all necessary physical constants (like gravitational field strength, g) are provided.
The problem can be structured to find any of the variables: pressure, force, mass, density, volume, or area.
Often involves calculating a property of the object itself (like density or mass) from the pressure it exerts.

**Exemplar questions:**

## **P_db0a126f. Combining Statistical Measures of Datasets**

**Core move:** Calculate the combined mean and determine the possible range of a merged dataset from its constituent groups' statistical properties.

**Seen in / context:**
- Analyzing pooled experimental data from multiple independent trials or groups.
Aggregating results from different samples in surveys or studies.
Determining overall central tendency and spread when combining distinct datasets.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Calculating the combined mean as a simple average of individual means without weighting by group size.
Averaging the individual ranges, or summing them, instead of identifying the true overall minimum and maximum values.
Confusing the properties of individual groups (mean, range) with the properties of the combined group.
Assuming the combined range must be greater than the sum of individual ranges.

**Notes for generation:**
- The combined mean is always a weighted average based on the number of data points in each group.
The combined range is determined by the lowest overall minimum value and the highest overall maximum value across all groups. It will always be greater than or equal to the largest individual range.
Questions often require inferring individual group minimum and maximum values if only mean and range are given for each group.
Pay close attention to the interpretation of symbols for inequalities or exact values in the options.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2020_Section1_Q21_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_dbae2352. Analyze Isotope Properties and Neutron Counts**

**Core move:** Derive unknown atomic, mass, or neutron numbers for isotopes using definitions and given relationships between their properties.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving multiple isotopes of the same element.
Questions that provide relationships between the number of neutrons in different isotopes.
Interpreting nuclide notation (e.g., A/Z X, X-A, or potentially ambiguous forms).

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Misinterpreting the definitions of mass number (A), atomic number (Z), and neutron number (N).
Forgetting that isotopes of the same element must have the same atomic number (Z).
Incorrectly setting up algebraic equations based on the relationships between nuclide properties (e.g., N = A - Z).
Confusing mass number (A) with atomic number (Z) in ambiguous notation.

**Notes for generation:**
- Ensure the notation for nuclides (A, Z, N) is clearly understood or inferred from context.
Emphasize the constant atomic number (Z) for all isotopes of a given element.
Questions should provide sufficient information to establish an algebraic relationship between the properties of the isotopes.
The calculated values for atomic number, mass number, and neutron number must always be positive integers.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2021_Section1_Q26_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_e5000b48. Multi-stage Conditional Probability with Transfers**

**Core move:** Calculate the overall probability of an event by combining probabilities of intermediate outcomes in a sequential process involving transfers between distinct sets.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving drawing items from multiple containers with transfers.
Scenarios where the composition of a set changes based on a prior random event.
Determining unknown quantities in probability distributions given overall event probabilities.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Not considering all possible outcomes from the first stage (e.g., forgetting to account for transferring different colored counters).
Incorrectly updating the total number of items or specific item counts in the second bag after a transfer.
Confusing conditional probabilities with joint probabilities.
Failing to use the law of total probability correctly when combining probabilities from different branches.

**Notes for generation:**
- Often best approached using a probability tree diagram.
Ensure that the denominator for probabilities in the second stage reflects the new total number of items after the transfer.
Questions may ask for an unknown initial quantity, requiring algebraic manipulation of the probability equation.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2023_Section1_Q20_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_ec2e8bdd. Variable Resistor Circuit Analysis**

**Core move:** Calculate the range of a specific circuit parameter (current, voltage, or power) by evaluating its value at the minimum and maximum resistance settings of a variable component in a series or parallel circuit.

**Seen in / context:**
- Problems involving variable resistors (rheostats, potentiometers) in series or parallel circuits.
Questions requiring the determination of the maximum or minimum possible value of a circuit quantity.
Applications like dimmer switches, volume controls, or motor speed controls where resistance changes a circuit's behavior.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Only calculating the circuit parameter for a single resistance value of the variable resistor, ignoring its range.
Incorrectly assuming that maximum resistance always leads to maximum current or power (or vice-versa), without considering the specific circuit configuration and formula.
Errors in calculating the total equivalent resistance for series or parallel combinations.
Misapplying Ohm's Law (V=IR) or power formulas (P=IV, P=I²R, P=V²/R) to the wrong components or total circuit.

**Notes for generation:**
- Clearly define the circuit configuration (series or parallel) and the components involved.
Provide explicit minimum and maximum resistance values for the variable component.
The question should prompt for a range of a quantity, or its maximum/minimum possible value, making the variable nature critical to the solution.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q24_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.

## **P_f4c1e46f. Thevenin Equivalent for Fixed Load Power**

**Core move:** Apply Thévenin's theorem to simplify a complex source circuit and then calculate the power dissipated in a specified fixed load resistor.

**Seen in / context:**
- Circuits containing multiple resistors and ideal voltage/current sources.
Problems requiring the calculation of power dissipation in a specific component.
Questions where a 'maximum power' is mentioned for a fixed load, suggesting the need to model the source with its Thévenin equivalent.

**Possible wrong paths:**
- Confusing the Maximum Power Transfer Theorem (which maximizes power delivered to a variable load) with calculating power in a fixed load connected to a source with internal resistance.
Incorrectly determining the Thévenin equivalent voltage (V_Th) or resistance (R_Th) of the source circuit.
Forgetting to deactivate independent sources (voltage sources become short circuits, current sources become open circuits) when calculating R_Th.
Misinterpreting 'negligible battery resistance' as implying zero overall source resistance (R_Th=0), when other resistors in the circuit can contribute to R_Th.

**Notes for generation:**
- When finding the Thévenin equivalent, always ensure it is from the perspective of the load terminals.
The 'maximum' in the question's phrasing, when applied to a fixed load, usually refers to the highest possible power under the given source configuration, or that the source configuration itself is optimized.
The final power calculation for the fixed load R_L should use P = I_L^2 * R_L or P = V_L^2 / R_L, where I_L and V_L are the current through and voltage across the load resistor, respectively, in the simplified Thévenin circuit.

**Exemplar questions:**
- `NSAA_2016_Section1_Q15_NSAA_Sec`: Initial exemplar for new schema.
